Dharma › Theravāda › Visuddhimagga › Paññābhūminiddesa
Chapter 17 · Paññābhūminiddesa · Complete

The Ground of Wisdom, Completed

This page carries Ru-Yi's translation of the Visuddhimagga's chapter 17, Paññābhūminiddesa ("The Description of the Ground of Wisdom") — by far the treatise's longest and most technically demanding chapter, completing wisdom's six-part "ground" with a full treatment of dependent origination (paṭiccasamuppāda). It works through the twelve-factor formula link by link, applies the Paṭṭhāna's complete twenty-four conditions to a single worked example, walks through the precise psychology of dying and rebirth-linking, mounts the tradition's classic philosophical defense against a transmigrating self, and closes with the famous "wheel of becoming" and its two great chains of similes.

Status: chapter 17 complete — full treatment of dependent origination Translator: Ru-Yi Meditation Center Source: Vism XVII, Paññābhūminiddesa (VRI/CST numbering) Author: Bhadantācariya Buddhaghosa, 5th c. CE

Translation Method

The Pāli source is the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana (Sixth Council) romanized edition maintained by the Vipassana Research Institute (VRI, tipitaka.org), cross-checked against the Pali Text Society's Rhys Davids edition (1920–21) as digitized on GRETIL. The VRI text is used under its stated terms — freely available for non-commercial use, with attribution to the Vipassana Research Institute — while the GRETIL/PTS digitization, which carries its own CC BY-SA licence, is used only to verify readings, never copied.

The English translation is Ru-Yi's own, made directly from the Pāli. No existing English translation of the Visuddhimagga — including Bhikkhu Ñāṇamoli's copyrighted The Path of Purification — is consulted, paraphrased, or used as a guide for wording or structure. Reading notes are grounded only in the text's own internal citations and in the general, uncontested conventions of the Pāli commentarial (aṭṭhakathā) genre — never in a modern scholar's specific interpretation. Each translated passage carries a short vocabulary list for readers learning Pāli alongside the text, continuing the same format established on this site's earlier Visuddhimagga chapters.

What Is Dependent Origination, What Are Dependently Arisen States

The seventeenth chapter opens by picking up exactly where the sixteenth left off: of the six categories named back in chapter fourteen as wisdom's "ground" — aggregates, sense bases, elements, faculties, truths, and dependent origination — only the last remains. Buddhaghosa begins with the plainest possible distinction, drawn straight from the Buddha's own words: "dependent origination" names the twelve conditioning states themselves, from ignorance to aging-and-death, while "dependently arisen states" names those same twelve states considered instead as things that arise, are impermanent, and pass away. The same list of states is being looked at through two different lenses — as cause, and as caused.

Pali Source

Idāni "khandhāyatanadhātuindriyasaccapaṭiccasamuppādādibhedā dhammā bhūmī"ti evaṃ vuttesu imissā paññāya bhūmibhūtesu dhammesu yasmā paṭiccasamuppādoceva, ādisaddena saṅgahitā paṭiccasamuppannā dhammā ca avasesā honti, tasmā tesaṃ vaṇṇanākkamo anuppatto.

Tattha avijjādayo tāva dhammā paṭiccasamuppādoti veditabbā. Vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā – "katamo ca, bhikkhave, paṭiccasamuppādo? Avijjāpaccayā, bhikkhave, saṅkhārā, saṅkhārapaccayā viññāṇaṃ, viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpaṃ, nāmarūpapaccayā saḷāyatanaṃ, saḷāyatanapaccayā phasso, phassapaccayā vedanā, vedanāpaccayā taṇhā, taṇhāpaccayā upādānaṃ, upādānapaccayā bhavo, bhavapaccayā jāti, jātipaccayā jarāmaraṇaṃ sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā sambhavanti. Evametassa kevalassa dukkhakkhandhassa samudayo hoti. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, paṭiccasamuppādo"ti.

Jarāmaraṇādayo pana paṭiccasamuppannā dhammāti veditabbā. Vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā – "katame ca, bhikkhave, paṭiccasamuppannā dhammā? Jarāmaraṇaṃ, bhikkhave, aniccaṃ saṅkhataṃ paṭiccasamuppannaṃ khayadhammaṃ vayadhammaṃ virāgadhammaṃ nirodhadhammaṃ. Jāti, bhikkhave…pe… bhavo… upādānaṃ… taṇhā… vedanā… phasso… saḷāyatanaṃ… nāmarūpaṃ… viññāṇaṃ… saṅkhārā… avijjā, bhikkhave, aniccā saṅkhatā paṭiccasamuppannā khayadhammā vayadhammā virāgadhammā nirodhadhammā. Ime vuccanti, bhikkhave, paṭiccasamuppannā dhammā"ti.

Ayaṃ panettha saṅkhepo. Paṭiccasamuppādoti paccayadhammā veditabbā. Paṭiccasamuppannā dhammāti tehi tehi paccayehi nibbattadhammā. Kathamidaṃ jānitabbanti ce? Bhagavato vacanena. Bhagavatā hi paṭiccasamuppādapaṭiccasamuppannadhammadesanāsutte – "katamo ca, bhikkhave, paṭiccasamuppādo? Jātipaccayā, bhikkhave, jarāmaraṇaṃ, uppādā vā tathāgatānaṃ anuppādā vā tathāgatānaṃ ṭhitāva sā dhātu dhammaṭṭhitatā dhammaniyāmatā idappaccayatā. Taṃ tathāgato abhisambujjhati abhisameti, abhisambujjhitvā abhisametvā ācikkhati deseti paññapeti paṭṭhapeti vivarati vibhajati uttānīkaroti, passathāti cāha. Jātipaccayā, bhikkhave, jarāmaraṇaṃ. Bhavapaccayā, bhikkhave, jāti…pe… avijjāpaccayā, bhikkhave, saṅkhārā uppādā vā tathāgatānaṃ…pe… uttānīkaroti passathāti cāha. Avijjāpaccayā, bhikkhave, saṅkhārā. Iti kho, bhikkhave, yā tatra tathatā avitathatā anaññathatā idappaccayatā. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, paṭiccasamuppādo"ti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now, it was said thus: "the states classed as aggregates, sense bases, elements, faculties, truths, dependent origination, and so on, are the ground." Among these states forming the ground of this understanding, dependent origination itself, together with the dependently arisen states included under the word "and so on," are what remain; the order of exposition has therefore now arrived at them.

Herein, the states beginning with ignorance should first be understood as "dependent origination." For this was said by the Blessed One — "and what, bhikkhus, is dependent origination? Dependent on ignorance, bhikkhus, are formations; dependent on formations, consciousness; dependent on consciousness, name-and-form; dependent on name-and-form, the sixfold base; dependent on the sixfold base, contact; dependent on contact, feeling; dependent on feeling, craving; dependent on craving, clinging; dependent on clinging, being; dependent on being, birth; dependent on birth, aging-and-death, sorrow, lamentation, pain, displeasure, and despair come to be. Thus is the arising of this whole mass of suffering. This, bhikkhus, is called dependent origination."

But aging-and-death and the rest should be understood as "dependently arisen states." For this was said by the Blessed One — "and what, bhikkhus, are dependently arisen states? Aging-and-death, bhikkhus, is impermanent, conditioned, dependently arisen, subject to destruction, subject to falling away, subject to fading, subject to cessation. Birth, bhikkhus … being … clinging … craving … feeling … contact … the sixfold base … name-and-form … consciousness … formations … ignorance, bhikkhus, is impermanent, conditioned, dependently arisen, subject to destruction, subject to falling away, subject to fading, subject to cessation. These, bhikkhus, are called dependently arisen states."

This, herein, is the summary: "dependent origination" should be understood as the conditioning states; "dependently arisen states" as the states brought into being by those various conditions. How is this to be known? By the Blessed One's own word. For the Blessed One, in the discourse teaching dependent origination and dependently arisen states, said — "and what, bhikkhus, is dependent origination? Dependent on birth, bhikkhus, is aging-and-death. Whether Tathāgatas arise or do not arise, that property still stands — the stableness of the Dhamma, the fixed course of the Dhamma, specific conditionality. The Tathāgata awakens to this and breaks through to it; having awakened to it and broken through to it, he explains it, teaches it, proclaims it, establishes it, discloses it, analyzes it, makes it plain, and says: 'See!' Dependent on birth, bhikkhus, is aging-and-death. Dependent on being, bhikkhus, is birth … dependent on ignorance, bhikkhus, are formations. Whether Tathāgatas arise or do not arise … he says: 'See!' Dependent on ignorance, bhikkhus, are formations. Thus, bhikkhus, the suchness therein, the unfailingness, the not-otherwiseness, the specific conditionality — this, bhikkhus, is called dependent origination."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

Chapter seventeen carries a heavier weight than any before it: it is the last of the six categories named as wisdom's "ground" back in chapter fourteen, and by the tradition's own account one of the four things in the world hardest to see and hardest to teach — a claim the chapter itself will quote directly within a few paragraphs. Buddhaghosa opens, characteristically, not with drama but with the plainest possible distinction: the same twelve-term list can be named "dependent origination" (looking at each term as a condition for what follows) or "dependently arisen states" (looking at the very same term as an impermanent, conditioned thing that itself arose). Nothing new has been added — only the angle of view has shifted.

The third quotation, from the discourse specifically contrasting these two framings, adds one further point that becomes central later in the chapter: this causal patterning — "dependent on birth is aging-and-death" — is said to hold whether or not a Buddha ever appears to discover it. The Buddha does not invent the law of conditionality; he discovers, penetrates, and then discloses a "stableness" and "fixed course" that was already so.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
paṭiccasamuppāda

dependent origination — paṭicca (having depended on) + samuppāda (arising-together); the chapter's own subject and title-word, given several distinct etymological treatments as the chapter proceeds.

idappaccayatā

specific conditionality — idam (this) + paccayatā (the state of being a condition); "this-conditionality," the abstract principle that a given effect depends on just these particular conditions, neither more nor fewer.

ṭhitāva sā dhātu dhammaṭṭhitatā dhammaniyāmatā

"that property still stands, the stableness of the Dhamma, the fixed course of the Dhamma" — the famous formula asserting that causal law is discovered, not invented, by a Buddha's awakening.

Suchness, Unfailingness, Not-Otherwiseness, Specific Conditionality

Buddhaghosa now unpacks the four synonyms the Buddha himself used for dependent origination in the sutta just quoted, showing each to capture a distinct facet of the same fact: a result arises through its own precise conditions (suchness), never fails to arise once those conditions have assembled (unfailingness), never arises from someone else's conditions (not-otherwiseness), and is, in the most literal sense, simply "conditioned by this" (specific conditionality).

Pali Source

Evaṃ paṭiccasamuppādaṃ desentena tathatādīhi vevacanehi paccayadhammāva paṭiccasamuppādoti vuttā. Tasmā jarāmaraṇādīnaṃ dhammānaṃ paccayalakkhaṇo paṭiccasamuppādo, dukkhānubandhanaraso, kummaggapaccupaṭṭhānoti veditabbo.

So panāyaṃ tehi tehi paccayehi anūnādhikeheva tassa tassa dhammassa sambhavato tathatāti, sāmaggiṃ upagatesu paccayesu muhuttampi tato nibbattadhammānaṃ asambhavābhāvato avitathatāti, aññadhammapaccayehi aññadhammānuppattito anaññathatāti, yathāvuttānaṃ etesaṃ jarāmaraṇādīnaṃ paccayato vā paccayasamūhato vā idappaccayatāti vutto.

Tatrāyaṃ vacanattho, imesaṃ paccayā idappaccayā. Idappaccayā eva idappaccayatā. Idappaccayānaṃ vā samūho idappaccayatā. Lakkhaṇaṃ panettha saddasatthato pariyesitabbaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

In teaching dependent origination in this way, it is precisely the conditioning states themselves that are called "dependent origination" under such synonyms as "suchness." Therefore dependent origination should be understood as having, for its characteristic, the being-a-condition of such states as aging-and-death; for its function, the yoking of beings to suffering; and for its manifestation, a wrong path.

And this is called "suchness," because each and every such state comes to be through its own respective conditions, neither less nor more; it is called "unfailingness," because, once the conditions have arrived at concurrence, there is not, even for a moment, any failure of the states produced from them to arise; it is called "not-otherwiseness," because there is no arising of one state through conditions belonging to another; and, with reference to these same states just spoken of — aging-and-death and the rest — by way of their condition, or by way of the collection of their conditions, it is called "specific conditionality."

Herein this is the literal sense: the conditions of these states are "conditions-for-this." The very state of being conditions-for-this is "specific conditionality" — or, the collection of the conditions-for-this is "specific conditionality." The precise grammatical derivation here, however, should be sought from the treatises on word-formation.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The four synonyms sort neatly into two pairs, one about arising and one about non-arising: "suchness" and "unfailingness" both say the result reliably does come, exactly proportioned to its causes; "not-otherwiseness" says the result never comes from the wrong causes. "Specific conditionality" then names the whole relation itself, cause and result together, rather than either side alone — which is why Buddhaghosa treats it separately, as a term needing its own grammatical unpacking (idam + paccayatā) rather than a further description of what happens.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
tathatā / avitathatā / anaññathatā

suchness / unfailingness / not-otherwiseness — three of the four canonical synonyms for dependent origination, each isolating a distinct guarantee about how conditions produce their results.

kummaggapaccupaṭṭhāna

manifesting as a wrong path — ku- (bad) + magga (path); dependent origination "manifests" experientially as saṃsāra's wrong, painful course, in contrast to the noble eightfold path.

Not Mere Arising, and Not a Change of State

Two wrong theories are now cleared away, in Buddhaghosa's characteristic style of naming a rival position, then dismantling it point by point. The first claims dependent origination is simply "arising" itself, stripped of any reference to a non-Buddhist first cause; four separate objections show this cannot be right — no discourse says so, it contradicts two specific discourses that do exist, it could not sustain the "profound method" the Buddha explicitly claimed for dependent origination, and it produces a plain grammatical mismatch when the two words are actually combined. The second theory claims "specific conditionality" names some internal alteration undergone by ignorance and the other causes, rather than those causes themselves — refuted simply by pointing to the Buddha's own words, which call ignorance, not some change within it, the cause.

Pali Source

Keci pana paṭicca sammā ca titthiyaparikappitapakatipurisādikāraṇanirapekkho uppādo paṭiccasamuppādoti evaṃ uppādamattaṃ paṭiccasamuppādoti vadanti, taṃ na yujjati. Kasmā? Suttābhāvato, suttavirodhato, gambhīranayāsambhavato, saddabhedato ca.

"Uppādamattaṃ paṭiccasamuppādo"ti hi suttaṃ natthi. Taṃ "paṭiccasamuppādo"ti ca vadantassa padesavihārasuttavirodho āpajjati. Kathaṃ? Bhagavato hi "atha kho bhagavā rattiyā paṭhamaṃ yāmaṃ paṭiccasamuppādaṃ anulomapaṭilomaṃ manasākāsī"ti ādivacanato paṭiccasamuppādamanasikāro paṭhamābhisambuddhavihāro, padesavihāro ca tassekadesavihāro. Yathāha "yena svāhaṃ, bhikkhave, vihārena paṭhamābhisambuddho viharāmi, tassa padesena vihāsi"nti. Tatra ca paccayākāradassanena vihāsi, na uppādamattadassanenāti. Evaṃ uppādamattaṃ "paṭiccasamuppādo"ti vadantassa padesavihārasuttavirodho āpajjati. Tathā kaccānasuttavirodho.

Kaccānasuttepi hi "lokasamudayaṃ kho, kaccāna, yathābhūtaṃ sammappaññāya passato yā loke natthitā, sā na hotī"ti anulomapaṭiccasamuppādo lokapaccayato "lokasamudayo"ti ucchedadiṭṭhisamugghātatthaṃ pakāsito, na uppādamattaṃ. Na hi uppādamattadassanena ucchedadiṭṭhiyā samugghāto hoti. Paccayānuparamadassanena pana hoti, paccayānuparame phalānuparamatoti.

Gambhīranayāsambhavatoti vuttaṃ kho panetaṃ bhagavatā "gambhīro cāyaṃ, ānanda, paṭiccasamuppādo gambhīrāvabhāso cā"ti. Gambhīrattañca nāma catubbidhaṃ, taṃ parato vaṇṇayissāma. Taṃ uppādamatte natthi. Catubbidhanayapaṭimaṇḍitañcetaṃ paṭiccasamuppādaṃ vaṇṇayanti, tampi nayacatukkaṃ uppādamatte natthīti gambhīranayāsambhavatopi na uppādamattaṃ paṭiccasamuppādo.

Saddabhedatoti paṭiccasaddo ca panāyaṃ samāne kattari pubbakāle payujjamāno atthasiddhikaro hoti. Seyyathidaṃ, "cakkhuñca paṭicca rūpe ca uppajjati cakkhuviññāṇa"nti. Idha pana bhāvasādhanena uppādasaddena saddhiṃ payujjamāno samānassa kattu abhāvato saddabhedaṃ gacchati, na ca kiñci atthaṃ sādhetīti saddabhedatopi na uppādamattaṃ paṭiccasamuppādoti.

Tattha siyā – "hoti-saddena saddhiṃ yojayissāma 'paṭiccasamuppādo hotī'ti", taṃ na yuttaṃ. Kasmā? Yogābhāvato ceva, uppādassa ca uppādapattidosato. "Paṭiccasamuppādaṃ vo, bhikkhave, desessāmi. Katamo ca, bhikkhave, paṭiccasamuppādo…pe… ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, paṭiccasamuppādo"ti. Imesu hi padesu ekenapi saddhiṃ hoti-saddo yogaṃ na gacchati, na ca uppādo hoti. Sace bhaveyya, uppādassāpi uppādo pāpuṇeyyāti.

Yepi maññanti "idappaccayānaṃ bhāvo idappaccayatā, bhāvo ca nāma yo ākāro avijjādīnaṃ saṅkhārādipātubhāve hetu, so. Tasmiñca saṅkhāravikāre paṭiccasamuppādasaññā"ti, tesaṃ taṃ na yujjati. Kasmā? Avijjādīnaṃ hetuvacanato. Bhagavatā hi "tasmātiha, ānanda, eseva hetu, etaṃ nidānaṃ, esa samudayo, esa paccayo jarāmaraṇassa yadidaṃ jāti…pe… saṅkhārānaṃ, yadidaṃ avijjā"ti evaṃ avijjādayova hetūti vuttā, na tesaṃ vikāro. Tasmā "paṭiccasamuppādoti paccayadhammā veditabbā"ti iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, taṃ sammā vuttanti veditabbaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

But some say that dependent origination is mere arising — arising that occurs dependently and rightly, without depending on such causes as a "primordial nature" or a "creator person" as imagined by sectarian teachers. This is untenable. Why? Because there is no discourse saying so, because it contradicts other discourses, because the profound method could then not arise, and because of a difference in grammatical construction.

For there is no discourse that says "dependent origination is mere arising." And for one who calls that "dependent origination," a contradiction arises with the Discourse on the Partial Dwelling. How so? From the Blessed One's own statement — "then, in the first watch of the night, the Blessed One attended to dependent origination in forward and reverse order" — attention to dependent origination was the dwelling of the newly, fully awakened One, and the "partial dwelling" spoken of elsewhere was a dwelling in one part of that very dwelling. As it is said: "bhikkhus, in whatever dwelling I dwelt when newly, fully awakened, in a part of that dwelling I dwelt." And there he dwelt by seeing the mode of the conditions, not by seeing mere arising. Thus, for one who calls mere arising "dependent origination," a contradiction with the Discourse on the Partial Dwelling arises. Likewise a contradiction with the Discourse to Kaccāna.

For in the Discourse to Kaccāna, too, it is said: "Kaccāna, for one who sees, with right understanding, the arising of the world as it really is, the notion of 'there is not,' with respect to the world, does not occur." This proclaims dependent origination in forward order — "the arising of the world" — by way of the world's condition, for the purpose of uprooting the view of annihilation; it does not proclaim mere arising. For it is not by seeing mere arising that the view of annihilation is uprooted, but by seeing the non-continuation of the conditions — since, when the conditions do not continue, the result does not continue either.

As for "because the profound method could then not arise": this was said by the Blessed One — "profound, Ānanda, is this dependent origination, and profound is its appearance." Now, profundity is of four kinds, which we shall describe further on; this is not found in mere arising. And this dependent origination is described as adorned with a fourfold method; that fourfold method, too, is not found in mere arising. Therefore, since the profound method is not found there either, dependent origination is not mere arising.

As for "because of a difference in grammatical construction": the gerund "having depended on" [paṭicca], when used with a single agent for both actions and referring to a prior time, does yield sense — as in "and having depended on the eye and forms, eye-consciousness arises." But here, when used together with the noun "arising" [uppāda], it results in a grammatical mismatch, since there is no single agent performing both the depending and the arising; and it establishes no sense at all. So, by reason of this difference in grammatical construction too, dependent origination is not mere arising.

Here it might be said: "we shall construe it together with the verb 'is' — 'dependent origination is [mere arising].'" That will not do. Why? Because no such construction is ever used, and because it would entail the fault that arising itself would then have to have an arising. "Bhikkhus, I shall teach you dependent origination. And what, bhikkhus, is dependent origination? … this, bhikkhus, is called dependent origination." In none of these passages is the word "is" construed together with it in this way, nor does "arising" occur as a separate event alongside it. If it did occur separately, then arising itself would have to have an arising.

There are also those who think: "the being-thus of the conditions-for-this is specific conditionality; and 'being-thus' here means that particular mode, belonging to ignorance and the other factors, which is the cause of the appearance of formations and the rest — so it is this alteration within the formations and so forth that is meant by 'dependent origination.'" This too is untenable. Why? Because ignorance and the rest are spoken of as the cause itself. For the Blessed One said: "therefore, Ānanda, this itself is the cause, this the source, this the origin, this the condition of aging-and-death — namely birth … of formations — namely ignorance." Thus it is ignorance and the other factors themselves that are called the cause, not some alteration belonging to them. Therefore it should be understood that the statement "'dependent origination' should be understood as the conditioning states" was rightly made.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The four-part refutation of "mere arising" is a small masterclass in the commentarial method: rather than simply asserting the correct view, Buddhaghosa tests the rival theory against everything it would have to be compatible with — silence in the canon, direct contradiction with two named discourses, the loss of a specific quality (profundity) the Buddha explicitly attributed to the real teaching, and finally a purely grammatical failure, since Pāli's gerund "paṭicca" requires a single agent doing both the depending and the resulting action, which "arising" alone cannot supply. Four independent lines of failure, from scripture, doctrine, and grammar alike, converge on the same verdict.

The Kaccāna-sutta point is worth lingering on: the Buddha there is specifically defending against annihilationism, the view that a person simply ceases at death with nothing continuing. Buddhaghosa's point is that merely watching phenomena "arise" says nothing about whether anything continues afterward — what actually refutes annihilationism is seeing that when conditions persist, so does what depends on them, and only when conditions genuinely cease does the dependent state cease too. It is the conditioning relationship itself, not the raw fact of arising, that carries the weight of the teaching.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
uppādamatta

mere arising — the rejected theory that "dependent origination" refers to nothing more than bare, uncaused-by-any-external-agent arising, without the full network of specific conditioning relationships.

padesavihārasutta

the Discourse on the Partial Dwelling — a short text describing the Buddha, freshly awakened, dwelling for a time in only "a part" of the dwelling he had experienced under the Bodhi tree; Buddhaghosa reads "that dwelling" as full contemplation of dependent origination.

ucchedadiṭṭhi

the view of annihilation — the wrong view that a being is simply cut off, with nothing continuing, at death; the Kaccāna Sutta's target, invoked here to show what dependent origination is actually for.

The Etymology of "Paṭiccasamuppāda"

Having cleared the ground of wrong theories, Buddhaghosa now gives the word itself three separate etymological treatments, each cast as its own verse, unpacking "paṭicca" (depending) and "samuppāda" (rightly co-arising) from slightly different angles — as something worth relying on, as causes turned "face to face" with one another, and as a set of conditions that can only produce their result together, never singly or in sequence. A closing verse then draws out the payoff: the very structure of the compound word, taken as a whole, rules out both eternalism and annihilationism at once, and stakes out the Buddha's own "middle way" between them.

Pali Source

Yā panettha "paṭiccasamuppādo"ti imāya byañjanacchāyāya uppādoyevāyaṃ vuttoti saññā uppajjati, sā imassa padassa evamatthaṃ gahetvā vūpasametabbā. Bhagavatā hi, "dvedhā tato pavatte, dhammasamūhe yato idaṃ vacanaṃ; tappaccayo tatoyaṃ, phalopacārena iti vutto."

Yo hi ayaṃ paccayatāya pavatto dhammasamūho, tattha paṭiccasamuppādoti idaṃ vacanaṃ dvidhā icchanti. So hi yasmā patīyamāno hitāya sukhāya ca saṃvattati, tasmā paccetumarahanti naṃ paṇḍitāti paṭicco. Uppajjamāno ca saha sammā ca uppajjati, na ekekato, nāpi ahetutoti samuppādo. Evaṃ paṭicco ca so samuppādo cāti paṭiccasamuppādo. Apica saha uppajjatīti samuppādo, paccayasāmaggiṃ pana paṭicca apaccakkhāyāti evampi paṭiccasamuppādo. Tassa cāyaṃ hetusamūho paccayoti tappaccayattā ayampi, yathā loke semhassa paccayo guḷo semho guḷoti vuccati, yathā ca sāsane sukhappaccayo buddhānaṃ uppādo "sukho buddhānaṃ uppādo"ti vuccati, tathā paṭiccasamuppādo icceva phalavohārena vuttoti veditabbo.

Atha vā, "paṭimukhamitoti vutto, hetusamūho ayaṃ paṭiccoti; sahite uppādeti ca, iti vutto so samuppādo." Yo hi esa saṅkhārādīnaṃ pātubhāvāya avijjādiekekahetusīsena niddiṭṭho hetusamūho, so sādhāraṇaphalanipphādakaṭṭhena avekallaṭṭhena ca sāmaggiaṅgānaṃ aññamaññena paṭimukhaṃ ito gatoti katvā paṭiccoti vuccati. Svāyaṃ sahiteyeva aññamaññaṃ avinibbhogavuttidhamme uppādetīti samuppādotipi vutto. Evampi paṭicco ca so samuppādo cāti paṭiccasamuppādo.

Aparo nayo – "paccayatā aññoññaṃ, paṭicca yasmā samaṃ saha ca dhamme; ayamuppādeti tatopi, evamidha bhāsitā muninā." Avijjādisīsena niddiṭṭhapaccayesu hi ye paccayā yaṃ saṅkhārādikaṃ dhammaṃ uppādenti, na te aññamaññaṃ apaṭicca aññamaññavekalle sati uppādetuṃ samatthāti. Tasmā paṭicca samaṃ saha ca na ekekadesaṃ, nāpi pubbāparabhāvena ayaṃ paccayatā dhamme uppādetīti atthānusāravohārakusalena muninā evamidha bhāsitā, paṭiccasamuppādotveva bhāsitāti attho.

Evaṃ bhāsamānena ca, "purimena sassatādīna, mabhāvo pacchimena ca padena; ucchedādivighāto, dvayena paridīpito ñāyo." Purimenāti paccayasāmaggiparidīpakena paṭiccapadena pavattidhammānaṃ paccayasāmaggiyaṃ āyattavuttittā sassatāhetuvisamahetuvasavattivādappabhedānaṃ sassatādīnaṃ abhāvo paridīpito hoti. Pacchimena ca padenāti dhammānaṃ uppādaparidīpakena samuppādapadena paccayasāmaggiyaṃ dhammānaṃ uppattito vihatā ucchedanatthikaakiriyavādāti ucchedādivighāto paridīpito hoti. Dvayenāti sakalena paṭiccasamuppādavacanena tassā tassā paccayasāmaggiyā santatiṃ avicchinditvā tesaṃ tesaṃ dhammānaṃ sambhavato majjhimā paṭipadā, "so karoti so paṭisaṃvedeti, añño karoti añño paṭisaṃvedetī"ti vādappahānaṃ, janapadaniruttiyā anabhiniveso, samaññāya anatidhāvananti ayaṃ ñāyo paridīpito hotīti ayaṃ tāva paṭiccasamuppādoti vacanamattassa attho.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now, the impression may arise that this word "paṭiccasamuppāda," by the shading of its literal form, refers to nothing but "arising." That impression should be laid to rest by grasping the meaning of this term as follows. For the Blessed One [meant it thus] — "since this word is used for a collection of states occurring in two ways from that condition, it is said, by a figure of speech transferring the name of the result, to be 'that whose condition is this.'"

For they intend this word "paṭiccasamuppāda" in a twofold sense, with reference to that very collection of states occurring by way of conditionality. First: because, when relied upon, it conduces to welfare and happiness, the wise consider it worthy of being relied upon — hence "paṭicca," the depended-upon. And it arises together and rightly, not singly one by one, and not without a cause — hence "samuppāda," the rightly-co-arisen. Thus, being both "paṭicca" and "samuppāda," it is "paṭiccasamuppāda." Further, it is "samuppāda" because it arises together; and it is also "paṭiccasamuppāda" in the sense that it occurs only in dependence on the concurrence of conditions, never apart from them. And since this very collection of causes is itself the condition for that co-arising, on account of being its condition, it is called, in this further sense too, "paṭiccasamuppāda" — just as, in ordinary usage, the cause of phlegm, namely raw sugar, is called "phlegm-sugar," and just as, in the Teaching, the arising of Buddhas, being the cause of happiness, is called "the happy arising of Buddhas" — so too, it should be understood, "paṭiccasamuppāda" is spoken of by a figure of speech naming the cause after its result.

Or again — "this collection of causes is called 'paṭicca' because it is said to have gone, from here, face to face; and it is called 'samuppāda' because it makes conditions that are together arise." For this collection of causes, set out under the heading of each single cause beginning with ignorance for the appearance of formations and the rest, is called "paṭicca," taking it that, in the sense of jointly producing a shared result, and in the sense of no member being deficient, each member of this concurrence has "gone face to face" with the others. And this same collection, since it produces, only in their togetherness, states that occur inseparably from one another, is also called "samuppāda." Thus, being both "paṭicca" and "samuppāda" in this sense as well, it is "paṭiccasamuppāda."

Yet another method — "since this condition-state produces states only in dependence on one another, equally and together, therefore it has here been spoken of thus by the Sage." For among the conditions set out under the headings of ignorance and the rest, whichever conditions give rise to a state such as the formations are incapable of doing so without depending on one another, when any one of them is lacking. Therefore, since this condition-state gives rise to states only in dependence on one another, equally and together — not singly, one part at a time, nor by way of a before-and-after sequence — the Sage, skilled in usage that follows the meaning, spoke of it thus in this connection: that is, he spoke of it precisely as "paṭiccasamuppāda."

And by speaking in this way — "by the former term, the non-existence of eternalism and the like is shown; by the latter term, the refutation of annihilationism and the like; by the two together, the method is made clear." "By the former term": by the term "paṭicca," which shows the concurrence of conditions — since occurring states depend for their occurrence on the concurrence of conditions — the non-existence of eternalism, of the causeless-cause doctrine, of the unequal-cause doctrine, and of the controlling-agent doctrine, divisions of "eternalism and the like," is shown. "And by the latter term": by the term "samuppāda," which shows the arising of states — since states arise from the concurrence of conditions — the doctrines of annihilation, of non-existence after death, and of the inefficacy of action are refuted; thus the refutation of annihilationism and the like is shown. "By the two together": by the whole expression "paṭiccasamuppāda," since those very states come to be through the uninterrupted continuity of their respective concurrences of conditions, the middle way is shown — the abandoning of the doctrine "the one who acts is the one who experiences the result" and "one acts, another experiences," the not settling into a fixed local idiom, and the not overstepping the bounds of conventional designation: this method is thereby made clear. This, then, is the meaning of the mere word "paṭiccasamuppāda."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

Three etymologies for one compound word may look like overkill, but each earns its place by answering a different question: the first explains why the word is worth using at all (because relying on this understanding brings welfare); the second explains the internal mechanics of how the causes relate to each other (face to face, none deficient); the third explains why the causes must act together rather than one after another. None of the three contradicts the others — they simply illuminate the same compound from different angles, exactly the kind of layered, non-exclusive analysis this commentarial genre favors over picking a single "correct" derivation.

The closing point is the philosophical payoff of the whole exercise: the two halves of the compound word each do independent doctrinal work. "Paṭicca" (depending) rules out eternalism, because a truly eternal, self-existent thing could not depend on anything. "Samuppāda" (arising) rules out annihilationism, because if states genuinely arise from conditions, nothing simply winks out of existence uncaused when those conditions persist. Held together, the single word "paṭiccasamuppāda" is the Buddha's middle way in miniature — neither an eternal self undergoes experience, nor does one thing simply vanish while an unrelated thing appears, but a genuine, conditioned continuity runs through, without needing either an eternal soul or an absolute break.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
paṭimukhaṃ ito gato

"gone, from here, face to face" — the image behind the second etymology: each condition in a concurrent set is pictured as turning to face the others, none of them able to produce the shared result alone.

so karoti so paṭisaṃvedeti / añño karoti añño paṭisaṃvedeti

"the one who acts is the one who experiences" / "one acts, another experiences" — the two extreme wrong views (roughly, eternalism and annihilationism restated in terms of personal identity) that the middle-way reading of dependent origination is said to avoid.

semhassa paccayo guḷo, semho guḷo

"the cause of phlegm, namely raw sugar, is called 'phlegm-sugar'" — Buddhaghosa's homely illustration of naming a cause after its effect, used to justify calling the conditions themselves "paṭiccasamuppāda," a word whose surface form more naturally suggests the result.

"I Find No Foothold, as Though Plunged into the Ocean"

Before attempting the detailed commentary, Buddhaghosa pauses for one of the Visuddhimagga's most personal moments — a direct admission, echoed by generations of commentators after him, that dependent origination is simply too deep to explain with confidence. He quotes the ancient teachers naming it one of four things in the world "hard to see and hard to teach," then confesses, in his own verse, to feeling like a man plunged into the open ocean with no foothold beneath him — before resolving to proceed anyway, leaning on both scripture and the unbroken line of earlier teachers.

Pali Source

Yā panāyaṃ bhagavatā paṭiccasamuppādaṃ desentena "avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā"tiādinā nayena nikkhittā tanti, tassā atthasaṃvaṇṇanaṃ karontena vibhajjavādimaṇḍalaṃ otaritvā ācariye anabbhācikkhantena sakasamayaṃ avokkamantena parasamayaṃ anāyūhantena suttaṃ appaṭibāhantena vinayaṃ anulomentena mahāpadese olokentena dhammaṃ dīpentena atthaṃ saṅgāhentena tamevatthaṃ punarāvattetvā aparehipi pariyāyantarehi niddisantena ca yasmā atthasaṃvaṇṇanā kātabbā hoti, pakatiyāpi ca dukkarāva paṭiccasamuppādassa atthasaṃvaṇṇanā. Yathāhu porāṇā – "saccaṃ satto paṭisandhi, paccayākārameva ca; duddasā caturo dhammā, desetuṃ ca sudukkarā"ti.

Tasmā aññatra āgamādhigamappattehi na sukarā paṭiccasamuppādassatthavaṇṇanāti paritulayitvā, "vattukāmo ahaṃ ajja, paccayākāravaṇṇanaṃ; patiṭṭhaṃ nādhigacchāmi, ajjhogāḷhova sāgaraṃ. Sāsanaṃ panidaṃ nānā, desanānayamaṇḍitaṃ; pubbācariyamaggo ca, abbocchinno pavattati. Yasmā tasmā tadubhayaṃ, sannissāyatthavaṇṇanaṃ; ārabhissāmi etassa, taṃ suṇātha samāhitā."

Vuttañhetaṃ pubbācariyehi – "yo koci maṃ aṭṭhikatvā suṇeyya, labhetha pubbāpariyaṃ visesaṃ; laddhāna pubbāpariyaṃ visesaṃ, adassanaṃ maccurājassa gacche"ti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now, as for this teaching which the Blessed One, in teaching dependent origination, set down by the method beginning "dependent on ignorance are formations" — one composing a commentary on its meaning must do so having descended into the sphere of the analytical method, without misrepresenting the teachers, without straying into one's own school's dogma, without dragging in other schools' doctrines, without contradicting the discourses, in conformity with the discipline, having regard for the great standards of reference, illuminating the Dhamma, drawing the sense together, and — having brought the very same meaning round again — setting it out also by other, further methods of exposition. For these reasons a commentary on its meaning must be composed with such care; and, in its own nature too, a commentary on the meaning of dependent origination is exceedingly difficult. As the ancient teachers have said: "truth, a being, relinking, and the mode of conditions — these four things are hard to see, and hard indeed to teach."

Therefore, having carefully weighed the fact that a commentary on the meaning of dependent origination is not easy except for those who have attained both to scriptural learning and to personal realization — "wishing today to speak a commentary on the mode of conditions, I find no foothold, as though plunged into the ocean. Yet this Dispensation is adorned with manifold methods of exposition, and the path of the earlier teachers still flows on, unbroken. Therefore, relying on both of these, I shall begin a commentary on the meaning of this teaching; listen to it with minds composed."

For this was said by the earlier teachers — "whoever should listen to me, giving due weight to what is said, would gain a distinction over what came before; and having gained that distinction over what came before, would go to where the Lord of Death cannot see."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

This is one of the very few places in the entire Visuddhimagga where Buddhaghosa steps out from behind the systematic voice of the treatise and speaks in the first person about his own limits — "I find no foothold, as though plunged into the ocean" is not false modesty so much as an honest acknowledgment, shared by the whole commentarial tradition he is quoting, that this particular teaching resists being fully mastered even by its most careful students. The four "hard to see" things named in the ancient verse — truth (in the sense of the ultimate nature of things), a being (rightly understood as no more than a conventional label over process), relinking (how one life connects to the next without a soul crossing over), and the mode of conditions (this very chapter's subject) — form a natural set, since each one is exactly the point at which ordinary, self-based thinking most wants to smuggle in a fixed, essential "thing" where none exists.

Notice, too, the specific method he commits himself to before proceeding: neither pure scriptural citation nor pure independent reasoning, but both together — "relying on both of these." This is the working method of the entire commentarial genre in miniature, and it explains why the chapter to follow constantly interleaves canonical quotation with the commentator's own logical argument, rather than choosing one mode of exposition and sticking to it.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
ajjhogāḷhova sāgaraṃ

"as though plunged into the ocean" — ajjhogāḷha (plunged, submerged) + sāgara (ocean); Buddhaghosa's own image for the sheer difficulty of commenting on dependent origination, among the most quoted lines in the whole treatise.

saccaṃ satto paṭisandhi paccayākāro

truth, a being, relinking, the mode of conditions — the ancient teachers' list of four things "hard to see and hard to teach," each a place where deep analysis and ordinary intuition most sharply diverge.

pubbācariyamaggo abbocchinno

"the path of the earlier teachers, unbroken" — Buddhaghosa's appeal to an unbroken line of prior commentators as one of the two supports (alongside scripture) he relies on to attempt this commentary at all.

Four Ways to Gather the Vine

The famous simile of four vine-gatherers now explains why the Buddha taught dependent origination in four different sequences across different discourses — forward from the beginning, backward from the middle, backward from the very end, and backward from the middle a second way — matching four different starting points a listener might need, depending on what confuses them and what stage of realization they have already reached.

Pali Source

Iti "avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā"tiādīsu hi āditoyeva tāva, desanābhedato attha, lakkhaṇekavidhādito. Aṅgānañca vavatthānā, viññātabbo vinicchayo. Tattha desanābhedatoti bhagavato hi vallihārakānaṃ catunnaṃ purisānaṃ valligahaṇaṃ viya ādito vā majjhato vā paṭṭhāya yāva pariyosānaṃ, tathā pariyosānato vā majjhato vā paṭṭhāya yāva ādīti catubbidhā paṭiccasamuppādadesanā.

Yathā hi vallihārakesu catūsu purisesu eko valliyā mūlameva paṭhamaṃ passati, so taṃ mūle chetvā sabbaṃ ākaḍḍhitvā ādāya kamme upaneti, evaṃ bhagavā "iti kho, bhikkhave, avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā…pe… jātipaccayā jarāmaraṇa"nti ādito paṭṭhāya yāva pariyosānāpi paṭiccasamuppādaṃ deseti.

Yathā pana tesu purisesu eko valliyā majjhaṃ paṭhamaṃ passati, so majjhe chinditvā uparibhāgaññeva ākaḍḍhitvā ādāya kamme upaneti, evaṃ bhagavā "tassa taṃ vedanaṃ abhinandato abhivadato ajjhosāya tiṭṭhato uppajjati nandī. Yā vedanāsu nandī, tadupādānaṃ. Tassupādānapaccayā bhavo, bhavapaccayā jātī"ti majjhato paṭṭhāya yāva pariyosānāpi deseti.

Yathā ca tesu purisesu eko valliyā aggaṃ paṭhamaṃ passati, so agge gahetvā aggānusārena yāva mūlā sabbaṃ ādāya kamme upaneti, evaṃ bhagavā "jātipaccayā jarāmaraṇanti iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ, jātipaccayā nu kho, bhikkhave, jarāmaraṇaṃ no vā kathaṃ vo ettha hotīti? Jātipaccayā, bhante, jarāmaraṇaṃ. Evaṃ no ettha hoti jātipaccayā jarāmaraṇanti. Bhavapaccayā jāti…pe… avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārāti iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ, avijjāpaccayā nu kho, bhikkhave, saṅkhārā no vā kathaṃ vo ettha hotī"ti pariyosānato paṭṭhāya yāva āditopi paṭiccasamuppādaṃ deseti.

Yathā panetesu purisesu eko valliyā majjhameva paṭhamaṃ passati, so majjhe chinditvā heṭṭhā otaranto yāva mūlā ādāya kamme upaneti, evaṃ bhagavā "ime ca, bhikkhave, cattāro āhārā kinnidānā, kiṃsamudayā, kiṃjātikā, kiṃpabhavā? Ime cattāro āhārā taṇhānidānā, taṇhāsamudayā, taṇhājātikā, taṇhāpabhavā. Taṇhā kinnidānā… vedanā… phasso… saḷāyatanaṃ… nāmarūpaṃ… viññāṇaṃ… saṅkhārā kinnidānā…pe… saṅkhārā avijjānidānā…pe… avijjāpabhavā"ti majjhato paṭṭhāya yāva ādito deseti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Thus, in such expressions as "dependent on ignorance are formations," from the very outset — "by way of the division of the teaching, by meaning, by characteristic and by kind, and by the determination of the factors — the discernment should be understood." Herein, "by way of the division of the teaching": just as, among four men gathering a creeper, one may proceed from the beginning, or from the middle, up to the end, or, likewise, from the end, or from the middle, back to the beginning — so too the teaching of dependent origination is fourfold.

Just as, among the four men gathering the creeper, one first sees only the root of the creeper; he cuts it at the root, pulls out the whole of it, and takes it to his work — so too the Blessed One teaches dependent origination in full, from the very beginning up to the very end: "thus, bhikkhus, dependent on ignorance are formations … dependent on birth is aging-and-death."

But just as, among those men, one first sees the middle of the creeper; he cuts it in the middle and pulls out only the upper portion, taking it to his work — so too the Blessed One teaches, starting from the middle up to the very end: "in one who delights in, welcomes, and remains holding to that feeling, delight arises. Whatever delight there is in feelings, that is clinging. Dependent on his clinging is being, dependent on being is birth."

And just as, among those men, one first sees the tip of the creeper; taking hold of the tip, and following it, he pulls out the whole of it right back to the root, taking it to his work — so too the Blessed One teaches, starting from the very end back to the very beginning: "now it was said, 'dependent on birth is aging-and-death' — is it, bhikkhus, that aging-and-death is dependent on birth, or is it not so, or how do you understand this?" "Aging-and-death, venerable sir, is dependent on birth; thus do we understand this — that aging-and-death is dependent on birth." "Dependent on being is birth … dependent on ignorance are formations — now it was said, 'dependent on ignorance are formations' — is it, bhikkhus, that formations are dependent on ignorance, or is it not so, or how do you understand this?"

And just as, among those men, one first sees only the middle of the creeper; he cuts it in the middle and, descending downward, pulls it out right back to the root, taking it to his work — so too the Blessed One teaches, starting from the middle back to the beginning: "and these four kinds of nutriment, bhikkhus — what is their source, what their origin, from what are they born, from what do they arise? These four kinds of nutriment have craving as their source, craving as their origin; they are born from craving, they arise from craving. And craving — what is its source? … feeling … contact … the sixfold base … name-and-form … consciousness … formations — what is their source? … formations have ignorance as their source … they arise from ignorance" — thus he teaches, starting from the middle back to the beginning.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The vine-gatherers' simile earns its lasting popularity by turning an apparent inconsistency in the canon — the same twelve-factor chain quoted in four different orders across different suttas — into evidence of the Buddha's own teaching skill rather than a puzzle to be explained away. Each of the four men simply happened upon the vine at a different point, and each still ends up with the same whole vine in hand; likewise, each discourse simply enters the same causal structure from a different point suited to that particular audience, without any of the four versions being more "correct" than the others.

It is worth noting exactly which discourse each direction is drawn from: the forward-from-the-beginning version is the standard formula familiar from countless other contexts; the middle-to-end version comes from analyses of feeling and craving specifically; the end-to-beginning version is a Socratic, question-and-answer catechism confirming each link one at a time; and the middle-to-beginning version traces backward specifically from the four kinds of nutriment, a starting point distinct from any of the twelve factors themselves. Four genuinely different pedagogical situations, four genuinely different points of entry.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
vallihāraka

a vine-gatherer, creeper-collector — valli (creeper, vine) + hāraka (one who carries off); the four men of the chapter's central simile, each entering the same vine from a different point.

anuloma / paṭiloma

forward order / reverse order — the two basic directions of reciting the twelve-factor chain, already familiar from the very first quotation of this chapter describing the Buddha's night of awakening.

āhāra

nutriment — the fourth of the four kinds of nutriment (edible food, contact, mental volition, and consciousness) that anchor the "middle-to-beginning" version of the teaching, tracing all four back to craving and, ultimately, to ignorance.

Why Ignorance Comes First: The Cow Driven to Slaughter

Why does the standard formula always begin with ignorance rather than some other factor — and is ignorance itself an uncaused, ultimate first cause of the world, the way rival schools imagined a "primordial nature" to be? Buddhaghosa answers no on both counts: ignorance does have its own cause (the taints), but it can still serve as the practical "head" of any given account of the round of rebirth, alongside a second candidate, the craving for being. A single vivid simile — one cow, driven to slaughter, choosing between hot water and cold — then explains why these two factors specifically are singled out as heads: each is the decisive trigger behind one of the two basic directions kamma can take, toward a bad destination or toward a good one.

Pali Source

Kasmā panettha avijjā ādito vuttā, kiṃ pakativādīnaṃ pakati viya avijjāpi akāraṇaṃ mūlakāraṇaṃ lokassāti? Na akāraṇaṃ. "Āsavasamudayā avijjāsamudayo"ti hi avijjāya kāraṇaṃ vuttaṃ. Atthi pana pariyāyo yena mūlakāraṇaṃ siyā, ko pana soti? Vaṭṭakathāya sīsabhāvo.

Bhagavā hi vaṭṭakathaṃ kathento dve dhamme sīsaṃ katvā katheti, avijjaṃ vā. Yathāha – "purimā, bhikkhave, koṭi na paññāyati avijjāya 'ito pubbe avijjā nāhosi, atha pacchā samabhavī'ti, evañcetaṃ, bhikkhave, vuccati, atha ca pana paññāyati idappaccayā avijjā"ti. Bhavataṇhaṃ vā. Yathāha – "purimā, bhikkhave, koṭi na paññāyati bhavataṇhāya 'ito pubbe bhavataṇhā nāhosi, atha pacchā samabhavī'ti, evañcetaṃ, bhikkhave, vuccati, atha ca pana paññāyati idappaccayā bhavataṇhā"ti.

Kasmā pana bhagavā vaṭṭakathaṃ kathento ime dve dhamme sīsaṃ katvā kathetīti? Sugatiduggatigāmino kammassa visesahetubhūtattā. Duggatigāmino hi kammassa visesahetu avijjā. Kasmā? Yasmā avijjābhibhūto puthujjano aggisantāpalaguḷābhighātaparissamābhibhūtā vajjhagāvī tāya parissamāturatāya nirassādampi attano anatthāvahampi ca uṇhodakapānaṃ viya kilesasantāpato nirassādampi duggatinipātanato ca attano anatthāvahampi pāṇātipātādiṃ anekappakāraṃ duggatigāmikammaṃ ārabhati. Sugatigāmino pana kammassa visesahetu bhavataṇhā. Kasmā? Yasmā bhavataṇhābhibhūto puthujjano sā vuttappakārā gāvī sītūdakataṇhāya saassādaṃ attano parissamavinodanañca sītūdakapānaṃ viya kilesasantāpavirahato saassādaṃ sugatisampāpanena attano duggatidukkhaparissamavinodanañca pāṇātipātā veramaṇiādiṃ anekappakāraṃ sugatigāmikammaṃ ārabhati.

Etesu pana vaṭṭakathāya sīsabhūtesu dhammesu katthaci bhagavā ekadhammamūlikaṃ desanaṃ deseti. Seyyathidaṃ, "iti kho, bhikkhave, avijjūpanisā saṅkhārā, saṅkhārūpanisaṃ viññāṇa"ntiādi. Tathā "upādāniyesu, bhikkhave, dhammesu assādānupassino viharato taṇhā pavaḍḍhati, taṇhāpaccayā upādāna"ntiādi. Katthaci ubhayamūlikampi. Seyyathidaṃ, "avijjānīvaraṇassa, bhikkhave, bālassa taṇhāya sampayuttassa evamayaṃ kāyo samudāgato. Iti ayañceva kāyo bahiddhā ca nāmarūpaṃ itthetaṃ dvayaṃ. Dvayaṃ paṭicca phasso saḷevāyatanāni, yehi phuṭṭho bālo sukhadukkhaṃ paṭisaṃvedetī"tiādi. Tāsu desanāsu "avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā"ti ayamidha avijjāvasena ekadhammamūlikā desanāti veditabbā. Evaṃ tāvettha desanābhedato viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now, why is ignorance spoken of here as the starting point — is ignorance, like the "primordial nature" of those who hold the primordial-nature doctrine, an uncaused root-cause of the world? It is not uncaused. For it is said: "from the arising of the taints, there is the arising of ignorance" — thus a cause of ignorance is stated. But is there, then, some manner of speaking by which it could be a root-cause? What is it? It is its serving as the head in the discourse on the round.

For when teaching the discourse on the round, the Blessed One speaks by making one of two states the head: either ignorance, as he said — "bhikkhus, no first point of ignorance is discerned, such that one could say, 'before this there was no ignorance, and afterward it came to be'; yet this much is said — it is discerned that ignorance has a specific condition" — or the craving for being, as he said — "bhikkhus, no first point of the craving for being is discerned, such that one could say, 'before this there was no craving for being, and afterward it came to be'; yet this much is said — it is discerned that the craving for being has a specific condition."

But why, in teaching the discourse on the round, does the Blessed One speak by making these two states the head? Because they are the special cause of the kamma that leads to a good or a bad destination. For ignorance is the special cause of kamma leading to a bad destination. Why? Because it is like this: a cow being driven to slaughter, overpowered by the heat of the fire, by blows of the goad, and by exhaustion — that cow, in the feverish state of her exhaustion, drinks hot water, though it gives her no real relish and is, moreover, harmful to her; so too a worldling overpowered by ignorance, overcome by the burning of the defilements, undertakes many kinds of kamma leading to a bad destination — such as the killing of living beings — kamma that gives him no real relish and, since it casts him down into a bad destination, is harmful to him as well. But the craving for being is the special cause of kamma leading to a good destination. Why? Because it is like this: that same cow, out of craving for cool water, drinks cool water, which does give relish and does relieve her exhaustion; so too a worldling overcome by the craving for being, free from the burning of the defilements and finding his own relish, undertakes many kinds of kamma leading to a good destination — such as abstinence from the killing of living beings — by attaining thereby a good destination and relieving his own suffering and exhaustion of a bad destination.

Now, among these two states that serve as the head in the discourse on the round, in some places the Blessed One teaches a discourse rooted in a single factor. For example: "thus, bhikkhus, formations have ignorance as their support; consciousness has formations as its support," and so on. Likewise: "bhikkhus, for one who dwells contemplating gratification in things that can be clung to, craving increases; dependent on craving is clinging," and so on. In some places he teaches a discourse rooted in both. For example: "bhikkhus, for a fool obstructed by ignorance and bound to craving, this body has thus come to be. So there is just this: this body, and name-and-form external to it — this pair. Dependent on this pair is contact; there are just the six sense bases, by which, when touched, the fool experiences pleasure and pain," and so on. Among these teachings, "dependent on ignorance are formations" should be understood as, here, a discourse rooted in a single factor, by way of ignorance. Thus, so far, the discernment by way of the division of the teaching should be understood.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The cow simile is easy to misread on a first pass, since it is compressed to the point of near-riddle in the Pāli: it is the very same cow in both halves, not two different cows. Driven mercilessly toward slaughter, exhausted and overheated, she might drink whatever water is nearest regardless of its quality — hot, foul, and ultimately harmful to her — which stands for kamma driven by sheer unseeing ignorance, taking whatever action promises the nearest relief without any discernment of consequence. Or, still the same exhausted cow, she might instead specifically crave and seek out cool water, which genuinely does relieve her — which stands for kamma driven by the craving for being, still self-interested and still keeping her walking toward the very same slaughterhouse, but at least choosing an action that brings a comparatively better outcome. Neither drink stops the journey to slaughter; the simile's real point is that both bad and good destinations alike are moves within saṃsara, not exits from it — ignorance and the craving for being are simply the two directions that same trapped momentum can take.

This chapter's insistence that ignorance is not, after all, an uncaused first cause is doctrinally load-bearing: it is precisely what keeps dependent origination free of the "primordial nature" and "creator" theories rejected several paragraphs earlier. Ignorance and the craving for being are heads only in the practical, rhetorical sense of where a particular telling of the story happens to begin — never in the sense of being outside the causal web itself.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
vaṭṭakathā

the discourse on the round — vaṭṭa (the round, i.e. of rebirth) + kathā (discourse, discussion); any canonical teaching that surveys the whole cycle of becoming, of which the twelve-factor formula is the primary example.

sīsabhāva

the state of being the head — sīsa (head) + bhāva (state); ignorance's and the craving for being's role as the rhetorical starting point of a given telling of the round, distinguished carefully from being an uncaused ultimate cause.

vajjhagāvī

a cow [being driven] to slaughter — vajjha (to be killed) + gāvī (cow); the chapter's central simile for a worldling driven by exhaustion and burning defilement toward either a harmful or a comparatively better course of action, neither of which escapes the journey itself.

The Meaning of Each Term

Buddhaghosa now works through every one of the twelve factors — and several surrounding operative words — giving each its own etymological derivation, in the treatise's characteristic style of finding the literal sense packed inside each technical term. Ignorance receives an unusually rich, six-part treatment (a term this important gets no less), formations receive a careful sorting into six different technical senses of the word "saṅkhāra" scattered across the canon, and the remaining ten factors each receive a brief, single-line derivation before the whole formula's closing words — "thus is the arising of this whole mass of suffering" — are parsed word by word.

Pali Source

Atthatoti avijjādīnaṃ padānaṃ atthato. Seyyathidaṃ, pūretuṃ ayuttaṭṭhena kāyaduccaritādi avindiyaṃ nāma, aladdhabbanti attho. Taṃ avindiyaṃ vindatīti avijjā. Tabbiparītato kāyasucaritādi vindiyaṃ nāma, taṃ vindiyaṃ na vindatīti avijjā. Khandhānaṃ rāsaṭṭhaṃ, āyatanānaṃ āyatanaṭṭhaṃ, dhātūnaṃ suññaṭṭhaṃ, indriyānaṃ adhipatiyaṭṭhaṃ, saccānaṃ tathaṭṭhaṃ aviditaṃ karotītipi avijjā. Dukkhādīnaṃ pīḷanādivasena vuttaṃ catubbidhaṃ atthaṃ aviditaṃ karotītipi avijjā. Antavirahite saṃsāre sabbayonigatibhavaviññāṇaṭṭhitisattāvāsesu satte javāpetīti avijjā. Paramatthato avijjamānesu itthipurisādīsu javati, vijjamānesupi khandhādīsu na javatīti avijjā. Apica cakkhuviññāṇādīnaṃ vatthārammaṇānaṃ paṭiccasamuppādapaṭiccasamuppannānañca dhammānaṃ chādanatopi avijjā.

Yaṃ paṭicca phalameti, so paccayo. Paṭiccāti na vinā apaccakkhatvāti attho. Etīti uppajjati ceva pavattati cāti attho. Apica upakārakaṭṭho paccayaṭṭho. Avijjā ca sā paccayo cāti avijjāpaccayo. Tasmā avijjāpaccayā.

Saṅkhatamabhisaṅkharontīti saṅkhārā. Apica avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā, saṅkhārasaddena āgatasaṅkhārāti duvidhā saṅkhārā. Tattha puññāpuññāneñjābhisaṅkhārā tayo, kāyavacīcittasaṅkhārā tayoti ime cha avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā. Te sabbepi lokiyakusalākusalacetanāmattameva honti.

Saṅkhatasaṅkhāro, abhisaṅkhatasaṅkhāro, abhisaṅkharaṇakasaṅkhāro, payogābhisaṅkhāroti ime pana cattāro saṅkhāra-saddena āgatasaṅkhārā. Tattha "aniccā vata saṅkhārā"tiādīsu vuttā sabbepi sappaccayā dhammā saṅkhatasaṅkhārā nāma. Kammanibbattā tebhūmakā rūpārūpadhammā abhisaṅkhatasaṅkhārāti aṭṭhakathāsu vuttā, tepi "aniccā vata saṅkhārā"ti ettheva saṅgahaṃ gacchanti. Visuṃ pana nesaṃ āgataṭṭhānaṃ na paññāyati. Tebhūmikakusalākusalacetanā pana abhisaṅkharaṇakasaṅkhāroti vuccati, tassa "avijjāgatoyaṃ, bhikkhave, purisapuggalo puññañceva saṅkhāraṃ abhisaṅkharotī"tiādīsu āgataṭṭhānaṃ paññāyati. Kāyikacetasikaṃ pana vīriyaṃ payogābhisaṅkhāroti vuccati, so "yāvatikā abhisaṅkhārassa gati, tāvatikā gantvā akkhāhataṃ maññe aṭṭhāsī"tiādīsu āgato.

Na kevalañca eteyeva, aññepi "saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ samāpajjantassa kho, āvuso visākha, bhikkhuno paṭhamaṃ nirujjhati vacīsaṅkhāro, tato kāyasaṅkhāro, tato cittasaṅkhāro"tiādinā nayena saṅkhāra-saddena āgatā aneke saṅkhārā. Tesu natthi so saṅkhāro, yo saṅkhatasaṅkhārehi saṅgahaṃ na gaccheyya, ito paraṃ saṅkhārapaccayā viññāṇantiādīsu vuttaṃ vuttanayeneva veditabbaṃ.

Avutte pana vijānātīti viññāṇaṃ. Namatīti nāmaṃ. Ruppatīti rūpaṃ. Āye tanoti āyatañca nayatīti āyatanaṃ. Phusatīti phasso. Vedayatīti vedanā. Paritassatīti taṇhā. Upādiyatīti upādānaṃ. Bhavati bhāvayati cāti bhavo. Jananaṃ jāti. Jiraṇaṃ jarā. Maranti etenāti maraṇaṃ. Socanaṃ soko. Paridevanaṃ paridevo. Dukkhayatīti dukkhaṃ. Uppādaṭṭhitivasena vā dvidhā khaṇatītipi dukkhaṃ. Dummanabhāvo domanassaṃ. Bhuso āyāso upāyāso.

Sambhavantīti abhinibbattanti. Na kevalañca sokādīheva, atha kho sabbapadehi sambhavanti-saddassa yojanā kātabbā. Itarathā hi "avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā"ti vutte kiṃ karontīti na paññāyeyya, sambhavantīti pana yojanāya sati avijjā ca sā paccayo cāti avijjāpaccayo. Tasmā avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā sambhavantīti paccayapaccayuppannavavatthānaṃ kataṃ hoti. Esa nayo sabbattha.

Evanti niddiṭṭhanayanidassanaṃ. Tena avijjādīheva kāraṇehi, na issaranimmānādīhīti dasseti. Etassāti yathāvuttassa. Kevalassāti asammissassa, sakalassa vā. Dukkhakkhandhassāti dukkhasamūhassa, na sattassa, na sukhasubhādīnaṃ. Samudayoti nibbatti. Hotīti sambhavati. Evamettha atthato viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Ru-Yi Translation

"By meaning" means: by the meaning of the terms beginning with ignorance. For example: bodily misconduct and the rest are called "not-to-be-found," in the sense that they are not fit to be pursued — meaning that they ought not to be obtained. And that which finds this not-to-be-found: that is ignorance. Conversely, bodily good conduct and the rest are called "to-be-found"; and that which does not find this to-be-found: that too is ignorance. It is also ignorance in that it makes unknown the sense of "heaping" belonging to the aggregates, the sense of "base-hood" belonging to the sense bases, the sense of "voidness" belonging to the elements, the sense of "dominance" belonging to the faculties, and the sense of "suchness" belonging to the truths. It is also ignorance in that it makes unknown the fourfold sense — stated by way of oppression and the rest — belonging to suffering and the other truths. It is ignorance in that, throughout the beginningless round, it drives beings on through every womb, destination, form of being, station of consciousness, and abode of beings. In the ultimate sense, it is ignorance in that it runs after women, men, and the like, which do not really exist, while it does not run after the aggregates and the like, which do exist. Moreover, it is ignorance also in the sense that it conceals the states that are the physical basis and object of eye-consciousness and the rest, and the states that are dependently arisen and dependently arising.

That, depending on which, the result comes — that is the condition. "Depending on" means: not without it, not having disregarded it. "Comes" means: arises and occurs. Furthermore, the sense of a condition is the sense of being a help. It is both ignorance and a condition — hence "the condition that is ignorance." Hence, "dependent on ignorance."

Those that fashion the conditioned are "formations." Now, formations are twofold: the formations that are "dependent on ignorance," and those that simply come under the word "formation." Herein, the meritorious, demeritorious, and imperturbable formations — three — and the bodily, verbal, and mental formations — three — these six are "the formations dependent on ignorance." All of these are nothing but mundane wholesome and unwholesome volition.

But these four — the conditioned-formation, the produced-formation, the producing-formation, and the exertion-formation — come under the mere word "formation." Herein, all conditioned states, spoken of in such passages as "impermanent indeed are formations," are called "conditioned-formations." The states of form and non-form belonging to the three planes, brought about by kamma, are called, in the commentaries, "produced-formations"; these too are included under this same "impermanent indeed are formations," though no place is found where they are mentioned separately under that name. Wholesome and unwholesome volition belonging to the three planes, however, is called the "producing-formation"; the place where it is so mentioned is found — in such passages as "this person, bhikkhus, gone into ignorance, produces a meritorious formation." And bodily and mental effort is called the "exertion-formation"; it is mentioned in such passages as "having gone as far as the exertion-formation can reach, it stood still, as though struck against something."

And it is not only these: there are also many other formations that come under the word "formation," in the manner of such passages as "friend Visākha, for a bhikkhu entering upon the cessation of perception and feeling, the verbal formation ceases first, then the bodily formation, then the mental formation." Among these, there is no formation that would not be included among the conditioned-formations. What is said hereafter, in such contexts as "dependent on formations, consciousness," should be understood in just the way already stated.

As for the rest, not otherwise mentioned: it cognizes, hence "consciousness." It bends toward its object, hence "name." It is deformed, hence "form." It stretches out and extends what has arisen, or leads onward what has extended, hence "sense base." It touches, hence "contact." It feels, hence "feeling." It thirsts, hence "craving." It grasps, hence "clinging." It comes to be, and it causes kamma to come to be, hence "being." The being-born is "birth." The growing-old is "aging." That by which beings die is "death." The grieving is "sorrow." The lamenting is "lamentation." That which is hard to bear is "pain" — or, by way of its rising and its persisting, that which is torn in two moments is also "pain." The state of a dejected mind is "displeasure." Intense distress is "despair."

"Come to be" means: are produced. And this word "come to be" is to be construed not only with sorrow and the rest, but with every single term. Otherwise, when it is said "dependent on ignorance are formations," it would not be clear what they do; but when the construing is "come to be," the sense is complete — it is both ignorance and a condition, hence "the condition that is ignorance" — so that "dependent on ignorance, formations come to be" establishes the determination of condition and conditioned-arisen. The same method applies throughout.

"Thus" points to the method just set out. By it, it is shown that this comes about through none other than the causes beginning with ignorance, not through a creator-god's fashioning or the like. "Of this" means: of what has just been described. "Whole" means: unmixed with anything else, or entire. "Mass of suffering" means: the aggregate of suffering — not a being, and not an aggregate of pleasure, beauty, and the like. "Arising" means: production. "Comes to be" means: is produced. Thus, herein, the discernment by way of meaning should be understood.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

Ignorance's six-part definition is worth sitting with, since it does not simply say "not-knowing" and move on — it insists ignorance is worse than blank absence. It positively runs after (javati) what does not exist (persons, selves) and fails to run after what does exist (the aggregates); it does not merely fail to reveal, but actively conceals, five distinct technical senses proper to the five categories already treated in earlier chapters (heaping, base-hood, voidness, dominance, suchness); and it drives beings, restlessly and specifically, through the entire machinery of rebirth catalogued in earlier chapters — every womb, destination, plane of being, station of consciousness, and abode. Ignorance is not a passive gap in knowledge; it is an active, propulsive force.

The sixfold breakdown of the mere word "saṅkhāra" is a useful map to keep for the rest of the treatise, since the same Pāli word recurs constantly with different senses depending on context: only the narrow, six-item "formations dependent on ignorance" (three kinds of intentional action, subdivided by body/speech/mind) belong properly to this chapter's twelve-factor chain, while "impermanent indeed are formations" uses the word in its broadest possible sense, covering every conditioned thing whatsoever.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
avindiya / vindiya

not-to-be-found / to-be-found — the pair underlying the chapter's first etymology of avijjā: ignorance "finds" (pursues) what should not be pursued, and fails to "find" what should be pursued.

puññābhisaṅkhāra / apuññābhisaṅkhāra / āneñjābhisaṅkhāra

meritorious, demeritorious, and imperturbable formations — the three kinds of intentional kamma that, together with the three "door" formations (bodily, verbal, mental), make up the narrow, technical "six formations dependent on ignorance" proper to this chain.

payogābhisaṅkhāra

the exertion-formation — one of four broader senses of "saṅkhāra" distinguished here; bodily or mental effort itself, illustrated by the canonical image of a chariot rolling only as far as its momentum allows before coming to a stop "as though struck against something."

Characteristic, Function, Manifestation, and Proximate Cause

Following the same four-part schema used throughout the treatise's earlier chapters, each of the twelve factors now receives its own characteristic, function, manifestation, and proximate cause — with the proximate cause of each term, fittingly, turning out to be simply the factor immediately preceding it in the chain.

Pali Source

Lakkhaṇāditoti avijjādīnaṃ lakkhaṇādito. Seyyathidaṃ – aññāṇalakkhaṇā avijjā, sammohanarasā, chādanapaccupaṭṭhānā, āsavapadaṭṭhānā. Abhisaṅkharaṇalakkhaṇā saṅkhārā, āyūhanarasā, cetanāpaccupaṭṭhānā, avijjāpadaṭṭhānā. Vijānanalakkhaṇaṃ viññāṇaṃ, pubbaṅgamarasaṃ, paṭisandhipaccupaṭṭhānaṃ, saṅkhārapadaṭṭhānaṃ, vatthārammaṇapadaṭṭhānaṃ vā. Namanalakkhaṇaṃ nāmaṃ, sampayogarasaṃ, avinibbhogapaccupaṭṭhānaṃ, viññāṇapadaṭṭhānaṃ. Ruppanalakkhaṇaṃ rūpaṃ, vikiraṇarasaṃ, abyākatapaccupaṭṭhānaṃ, viññāṇapadaṭṭhānaṃ. Āyatanalakkhaṇaṃ saḷāyatanaṃ, dassanādirasaṃ, vatthudvārabhāvapaccupaṭṭhānaṃ, nāmarūpapadaṭṭhānaṃ. Phusanalakkhaṇo phasso, saṅghaṭṭanaraso, saṅgatipaccupaṭṭhāno, saḷāyatanapadaṭṭhāno. Anubhavanalakkhaṇā vedanā, visayarasasambhogarasā, sukhadukkhapaccupaṭṭhānā, phassapadaṭṭhānā. Hetulakkhaṇā taṇhā, abhinandanarasā, atittabhāvapaccupaṭṭhānā, vedanāpadaṭṭhānā. Gahaṇalakkhaṇaṃ upādānaṃ, amuñcanarasaṃ, taṇhādaḷhattadiṭṭhipaccupaṭṭhānaṃ, taṇhāpadaṭṭhānaṃ. Kammakammaphalalakkhaṇo bhavo, bhāvanabhavanaraso, kusalākusalābyākatapaccupaṭṭhāno, upādānapadaṭṭhāno. Jātiādīnaṃ lakkhaṇādīni saccaniddese vuttanayeneva veditabbāni. Evamettha lakkhaṇāditopi viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Ru-Yi Translation

"By characteristic and so forth" means: by the characteristic and so forth of ignorance and the rest. Namely: ignorance has the characteristic of unknowing, the function of bewildering, it manifests as concealment, and has the taints as its proximate cause. Formations have the characteristic of fashioning, the function of accumulating kamma, they manifest as volition, and have ignorance as their proximate cause. Consciousness has the characteristic of cognizing, the function of going before its associated states, it manifests as rebirth-linking, and has formations as its proximate cause — or, alternatively, the physical basis and object as its proximate cause. Name has the characteristic of bending toward its object, the function of conascent association, it manifests as inseparability from its associated states, and has consciousness as its proximate cause. Form has the characteristic of being deformed, the function of scattering, it manifests as indeterminate, and has consciousness as its proximate cause. The sixfold base has the characteristic of being a sense base, the function of seeing and so forth, each according to its own kind, it manifests as the state of being a physical basis and a door, and has name-and-form as its proximate cause. Contact has the characteristic of touching, the function of impinging, it manifests as a coincidence of consciousness, object, and physical basis, and has the sixfold base as its proximate cause. Feeling has the characteristic of experiencing, the function of relishing the flavor of the object, it manifests as pleasure or pain, and has contact as its proximate cause. Craving has the characteristic of being a cause, the function of delighting, it manifests as a state of never being satisfied, and has feeling as its proximate cause. Clinging has the characteristic of grasping, the function of not letting go, it manifests as the firmness of craving and as wrong view, and has craving as its proximate cause. Being has the characteristic of kamma and the result of kamma, the function of causing-to-be and of becoming, it manifests as wholesome, unwholesome, or indeterminate states, and has clinging as its proximate cause. The characteristics and so forth of birth and the rest should be understood in just the way already stated in the discussion of the truths. Thus, herein, the discernment by way of characteristic and so forth should also be understood.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

Reading straight down this list, the "proximate cause" column silently reconstructs the entire twelve-factor chain a second time, in miniature: ignorance's proximate cause is the taints (reaching one step further back than the formula itself usually goes), formations' proximate cause is ignorance, consciousness's is formations, and so on down the line, each factor's proximate cause simply naming the factor immediately before it. The four-part schema, applied mechanically here, ends up quietly re-deriving the causal sequence from a completely different angle than the etymological one just finished.

Two entries repay a closer look. Consciousness is given an alternative proximate cause — "the physical basis and object" — alongside formations, reflecting that at any given moment consciousness depends both on its karmic history (the formations that shaped it) and on its immediate supporting conditions (eye and visible form, for instance, for eye-consciousness). And form's manifestation as "indeterminate" (abyākata) marks its ethical neutrality: unlike volitional formations, physical matter itself is never wholesome or unwholesome, only the mental states that use it are.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
lakkhaṇa / rasa / paccupaṭṭhāna / padaṭṭhāna

characteristic / function / manifestation / proximate cause — the treatise's standard four-part analytical template, applied here to all twelve factors of dependent origination in a single continuous list.

vatthārammaṇapadaṭṭhāna

having the physical basis and object as proximate cause — the alternative proximate cause given for consciousness, alongside formations, reflecting consciousness's dependence on immediate as well as karmic conditions.

One Kind to Six Kinds; Why Exactly Twelve Factors

The opening discussion closes with two final analytical passes. First, a rapid-fire cross-classification: each of the first five factors is shown to admit anywhere from one to six different subdivisions, depending on which distinguishing feature is used to cut across it — a dense, compressed catalogue meant more as a cross-reference into the Abhidhamma's fuller treatments than as a self-contained explanation. Second, the chapter answers a question a careful reader may already be asking: the standard formula lists twelve factors, yet the closing words name five further items — sorrow, lamentation, pain, displeasure, and despair. Why aren't these separately counted as factors thirteen through seventeen? Buddhaghosa's answer folds them back into aging-and-death, since their only role is to demonstrate that the wheel keeps turning without a break, not to add genuinely new links to the chain.

Pali Source

Ekavidhāditoti ettha avijjā aññāṇādassanamohādibhāvato ekavidhā. Appaṭipattimicchāpaṭipattito duvidhā. Tathā sasaṅkhārāsaṅkhārato. Vedanattayasampayogato tividhā. Catusaccapaṭivedhato catubbidhā. Gatipañcakādīnavacchādanato pañcavidhā. Dvārārammaṇato pana sabbesupi arūpadhammesu chabbidhatā veditabbā.

Saṅkhārā sāsavavipākadhammadhammādibhāvato ekavidhā. Kusalākusalato duvidhā. Tathā parittamahaggatahīnamajjhimamicchattaniyatāniyatato. Tividhā puññābhisaṅkhārādibhāvato. Catubbidhā catuyonisaṃvattanato. Pañcavidhā pañcagatigāmito.

Viññāṇaṃ lokiyavipākādibhāvato ekavidhaṃ. Sahetukāhetukādito duvidhaṃ. Bhavattayapariyāpannato, vedanattayasampayogato, ahetukadvihetukatihetukato ca tividhaṃ. Yonigativasena catubbidhaṃ, pañcavidhañca.

Nāmarūpaṃ viññāṇasannissayato kammapaccayato ca ekavidhaṃ. Sārammaṇanārammaṇato duvidhaṃ. Atītādito tividhaṃ. Yonigativasena catubbidhaṃ, pañcavidhañca.

Saḷāyatanaṃ sañjātisamosaraṇaṭṭhānato ekavidhaṃ. Bhūtappasādaviññāṇādito duvidhaṃ. Sampattāsampattanobhayagocarato tividhaṃ. Yonigatipariyāpannato catubbidhaṃ pañcavidhañcāti iminā nayena phassādīnampi ekavidhādibhāvo veditabboti evamettha ekavidhāditopi viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Aṅgānañca vavatthānāti sokādayo cettha bhavacakkassa avicchedadassanatthaṃ vuttā. Jarāmaraṇabbhāhatassa hi bālassa te sambhavanti. Yathāha – "assutavā, bhikkhave, puthujjano sārīrikāya dukkhāya vedanāya phuṭṭho samāno socati kilamati paridevati urattāḷiṃ kandati sammohamāpajjatī"ti. Yāva ca tesaṃ pavatti, tāva avijjāyāti punapi avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārāti sambandhameva hoti bhavacakkaṃ. Tasmā tesaṃ jarāmaraṇeneva ekasaṅkhepaṃ katvā dvādaseva paṭiccasamuppādaṅgānīti veditabbāni. Evamettha aṅgānaṃ vavatthānatopi viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Ayaṃ tāvettha saṅkhepakathā.

Ru-Yi Translation

"By way of being one kind and so forth": herein, ignorance, by its being unknowing, non-seeing, delusion, and the like, is of one kind. By way of non-application and wrong application, it is of two kinds; likewise by way of being accompanied or unaccompanied by instigation. By way of association with the three kinds of feeling, it is of three kinds. By way of obstructing the penetration of the four truths, it is of four kinds. By way of concealing the drawbacks of the five destinations and the rest, it is of five kinds. And by way of door and object, a sixfold nature should be understood, as with all formless states.

Formations, by their being states with taints, resultant states, and the like, are of one kind. By way of wholesome and unwholesome, they are of two kinds; likewise by way of limited and exalted, inferior and medium, fixed-in-wrongness and not-fixed. By way of being meritorious formations and the rest, they are of three kinds. By way of leading to the four kinds of rebirth, they are of four kinds. By way of leading to the five destinations, they are of five kinds.

Consciousness, by its being mundane, resultant, and the like, is of one kind. By way of having roots or being rootless, it is of two kinds. By way of being included in the three kinds of being, by association with the three kinds of feeling, and by being rootless, having two roots, or having three roots, it is of three kinds. By way of womb and destination, it is of four kinds, and of five kinds.
