Name-and-form, by its resting upon consciousness and by its being conditioned by kamma, is of one kind. By way of having an object or being without an object, it is of two kinds. By way of past and so forth, it is of three kinds. By way of womb and destination, it is of four kinds, and of five kinds.

The sixfold base, by its being the meeting-place for what has come into existence, is of one kind. By way of being derived from the elements, or being sensitivity, or being consciousness, and the like, it is of two kinds. By way of having an accessible, an inaccessible, or a common object, it is of three kinds. By way of being included in womb and destination, it is of four kinds, and of five kinds. By this same method, the being-of-one-kind-and-so-forth of contact and the rest, too, should be understood. Thus, herein, the discernment by way of being-one-kind-and-so-forth should also be understood.

"And by the determination of the factors": sorrow and the rest are mentioned here for the purpose of showing that the wheel of becoming has no break in its continuity. For these arise in a fool who is struck by aging-and-death — as it is said: "bhikkhus, when touched by a painful bodily feeling, the uninstructed worldling sorrows, is wearied, laments, beats his breast and wails, and falls into bewilderment." And for as long as these continue to occur, ignorance continues too; and so, once again, the wheel of becoming remains simply the unbroken connection "dependent on ignorance are formations." Therefore it should be understood that, having reduced these to a single grouping together with aging-and-death alone, there are exactly twelve factors of dependent origination. Thus, herein, the discernment by way of the determination of the factors should also be understood.

This, then, so far, is the summary discussion.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The one-to-six-fold catalogue is deliberately compressed almost to the point of shorthand — Buddhaghosa is cross-referencing categories already worked out in full elsewhere in the Abhidhamma rather than re-explaining them here, trusting a well-trained reader to reconstruct, for instance, exactly which "five destinations" or "four kinds of rebirth" are meant from context established many chapters earlier. Its real function is less to teach new content than to show that every factor in the chain can be sliced by the very same standard analytical dimensions — ethical quality, associated feeling, realm, and so on — that structure the whole Abhidhamma system.

The closing point resolves what might otherwise look like an inconsistency between the twelve-factor count this chapter keeps insisting on and the seventeen items actually named in the standard formula. Sorrow, lamentation, pain, displeasure, and despair are not a thirteenth through seventeenth link in the causal chain — they are simply what a fool experiences when struck by aging-and-death, added to make vivid that the round truly has no gap: as long as these five keep occurring, ignorance keeps occurring within them, and the wheel simply starts turning again from "dependent on ignorance are formations." With this settled, the chapter's brief overview — desanā, meaning, characteristic, kind, and factors — is complete, and the detailed, paragraph-by-paragraph commentary can now begin.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
bhavacakka

the wheel of becoming — bhava (becoming) + cakka (wheel); the image of the twelve-factor chain as a continuously turning circle, introduced here and developed fully in the chapter's closing section.

avicchedadassana

showing the absence of a break — a + viccheda (break, interruption) + dassana (showing); the specific purpose Buddhaghosa assigns to naming sorrow, lamentation, and the other closing terms in the standard formula.

saṅkhepakathā

the summary discussion — saṅkhepa (summary, brief) + kathā (discourse); Buddhaghosa's own label for the whole opening section just completed, contrasted with the vitthārakathā ("detailed discussion") the chapter now turns to.

The Detailed Method Begins: Eight Points of Unknowing

Having finished the "summary discussion," Buddhaghosa now opens the chapter's much longer detailed treatment, moving link by link through the standard formula. Ignorance receives a technical refinement here distinct from its earlier six-part definition: the discourses speak of unknowing regarding four points (the four noble truths), while the Abhidhamma speaks of unknowing regarding eight, adding the past, the future, both together, and dependent origination itself. The key clarification is that ignorance is not merely absent knowledge of these eight — it is meant specifically as active concealment, standing over each truth like a lid, preventing its real characteristic from ever being penetrated.

Pali Source

Ayaṃ pana vitthāranayo – avijjāti suttantapariyāyena dukkhādīsu catūsu ṭhānesu aññāṇaṃ, abhidhammapariyāyena pubbantādīhi saddhiṃ aṭṭhasu. Vuttañhetaṃ "tattha katamā avijjā, dukkhe aññāṇaṃ…pe… dukkhanirodhagāminiyā paṭipadāya aññāṇaṃ, pubbante aññāṇaṃ, aparante, pubbantāparante, idappaccayatāpaṭiccasamuppannesu dhammesu aññāṇa"nti. Tattha kiñcāpi ṭhapetvā lokuttaraṃ saccadvayaṃ sesaṭṭhānesu ārammaṇavasena avijjā uppajjati, evaṃ santepi paṭicchādanavaseneva idha adhippetā. Sā hi uppannā dukkhasaccaṃ paṭicchādetvā tiṭṭhati, yāthāvasarasalakkhaṇaṃ paṭivijjhituṃ na deti, tathā samudayaṃ, nirodhaṃ, maggaṃ, pubbantasaṅkhātaṃ atītaṃ khandhapañcakaṃ, aparantasaṅkhātaṃ anāgataṃ khandhapañcakaṃ, pubbantāparantasaṅkhātaṃ tadubhayaṃ, idappaccayatāpaṭiccasamuppannadhammasaṅkhātaṃ idappaccayatañceva paṭiccasamuppannadhamme ca paṭicchādetvā tiṭṭhati. "Ayaṃ avijjā, ime saṅkhārā"ti evaṃ yāthāvasarasalakkhaṇamettha paṭivijjhituṃ na deti. Tasmā dukkhe aññāṇaṃ…pe… idappaccayatāpaṭiccasamuppannesu dhammesu aññāṇanti vuccati.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now, this is the detailed method. "Ignorance," in the suttanta method, is unknowing with respect to four points — suffering and the rest; in the Abhidhamma method, it is unknowing with respect to eight points, together with the past and the rest. For it is said: "and what, herein, is ignorance? Unknowing regarding suffering … unknowing regarding the way leading to the cessation of suffering; unknowing regarding the past; regarding the future; regarding the past and future together; and unknowing regarding specific conditionality and dependently arisen states." Herein, although ignorance can arise, by way of taking an object, with respect to every point other than the two supramundane truths as well, it is nevertheless meant here only in the sense of concealment. For, once arisen, it stands concealing the truth of suffering, and does not allow its true, specific characteristic to be penetrated; likewise it stands concealing the origin, the cessation, the path; the five aggregates of the past, called "the past"; the five aggregates of the future, called "the future"; both of these together, called "the past and future"; and specific conditionality itself together with dependently arisen states, called "states that are specifically conditioned and dependently arisen." It does not allow the true, specific characteristic — "this is ignorance, these are the formations" — to be penetrated here. Therefore it is said: "unknowing regarding suffering … unknowing regarding states that are specifically conditioned and dependently arisen."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

This passage sharpens something easy to miss on a first reading of "ignorance" as simply "not knowing": Buddhaghosa insists the relevant sense here is paṭicchādana, active concealment, not mere absence. Ignorance is compared to something standing in place, holding the truth's own characteristic down and unpenetrated — closer to a hand clamped over the eyes than to an empty room. This matters because it makes ignorance something to be actively removed by seeing through it, rather than a gap that might simply fill itself in with more information over time.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
paṭicchādana

concealment — paṭi (over) + chādana (covering); the specific sense of "ignorance" meant throughout this chain, distinguished from mere absence of information.

pubbanta / aparanta / pubbantāparanta

the past / the future / the past-and-future together — three of the eight points of Abhidhamma-method unknowing, each referring to the five aggregates as they existed, will exist, or existed-and-will-exist.

Formations in Detail: Twenty-Nine Volitions, Sorted Three Ways

The six formations named earlier in summary now receive their full technical count: thirteen wholesome volitions make up the meritorious formation, twelve unwholesome volitions the demeritorious formation, and four formless-sphere volitions the imperturbable formation — twenty-nine volitions in all. A second three-way division, by bodily, verbal, and mental "door," is then shown to simply re-sort these same twenty-nine volitions rather than adding new ones — with two technical exclusions (the volition of direct knowledge and the volition of restlessness) carefully carved out, since neither one actually conditions rebirth-linking consciousness the way the chain requires.

Pali Source

Saṅkhārāti puññādayo tayo kāyasaṅkhārādayo tayoti evaṃ pubbe saṅkhepato vuttā cha, vitthārato panettha puññābhisaṅkhāro dānasīlādivasena pavattā aṭṭha kāmāvacarakusalacetanā ceva bhāvanāvasena pavattā pañca rūpāvacarakusalacetanā cāti terasa cetanā honti. Apuññābhisaṅkhāro pāṇātipātādivasena pavattā dvādasa akusalacetanā. Āneñjābhisaṅkhāro bhāvanāvaseneva pavattā catasso arūpāvacarakusalacetanā cāti tayopi saṅkhārā ekūnatiṃsa cetanā honti.

Itaresu pana tīsu kāyasañcetanā kāyasaṅkhāro, vacīsañcetanā vacīsaṅkhāro, manosañcetanā cittasaṅkhāro. Ayaṃ tiko kammāyūhanakkhaṇe puññābhisaṅkhārādīnaṃ dvārato pavattidassanatthaṃ vutto. Kāyaviññattiṃ samuṭṭhāpetvā hi kāyadvārato pavattā aṭṭha kāmāvacarakusalacetanā, dvādasa akusalacetanāti samavīsati cetanā kāyasaṅkhāro nāma. Tā eva vacīviññattiṃ samuṭṭhāpetvā vacīdvārato pavattā vacīsaṅkhāro nāma. Abhiññācetanā panettha parato viññāṇassa paccayo na hotīti na gahitā. Yathā ca abhiññācetanā, evaṃ uddhaccacetanāpi na hoti. Tasmā sāpi viññāṇassa paccayabhāve apanetabbā, avijjāpaccayā pana sabbāpetā honti. Ubhopi viññattiyo asamuṭṭhāpetvā manodvāre uppannā pana sabbāpi ekūnatiṃsati cetanā cittasaṅkhāroti. Iti ayaṃ tiko purimattikameva pavisatīti atthato puññābhisaṅkhārādīnaṃyeva vasena avijjāya paccayabhāvo veditabbo.

Ru-Yi Translation

"Formations": the three — meritorious and the rest — and the three — bodily formations and the rest — thus six were stated briefly above; in detail, herein, the meritorious formation consists of the eight sense-sphere wholesome volitions occurring by way of giving, virtue, and the like, together with the five form-sphere wholesome volitions occurring by way of development — thirteen volitions in all. The demeritorious formation is the twelve unwholesome volitions occurring by way of killing living beings and the like. The imperturbable formation is the four formless-sphere wholesome volitions occurring by way of development alone. Thus all three formations together amount to twenty-nine volitions.

Among the other three formations: bodily volition is the "bodily formation"; verbal volition is the "verbal formation"; mental volition is the "mental formation." This triad is stated for the purpose of showing the occurrence of the meritorious formation and the rest by way of their door, at the moment of accumulating kamma. For the eight sense-sphere wholesome volitions and the twelve unwholesome volitions, occurring through the bodily door, having given rise to bodily intimation — these twenty volitions together are called the "bodily formation." Those very same volitions, occurring through the verbal door, having given rise to verbal intimation, are called the "verbal formation." Herein, the volition of direct knowledge is not included, since it does not, afterward, serve as a condition for consciousness. And just as with the volition of direct knowledge, so too with the volition of restlessness — therefore it, too, should be excluded from being a condition for consciousness, though all of these are, indeed, conditioned by ignorance. But all twenty-nine volitions whatsoever that arise in the mind-door, without giving rise to either kind of intimation, are the "mental formation." Thus this second triad simply falls within the former triad; and so, in meaning, ignorance's state of being a condition should be understood only by way of the meritorious formation and the rest.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The exclusion of direct-knowledge volition and restlessness volition from the "door" triad is a fine technical point worth pausing on: both of these are genuinely intentional mental events, yet neither one is the kind of volition that shapes a future rebirth, so neither counts as a "formation" in the sense this chain requires — a reminder that "saṅkhāra" here is doing very specific causal work (producing rebirth-linking consciousness), not simply naming every act of will whatsoever.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
kāyaviññatti / vacīviññatti

bodily intimation / verbal intimation — the specific bodily or vocal "signal" (a gesture, a spoken word) through which an intention becomes an outward, communicable act, distinguishing the bodily and verbal formations from the purely internal mental formation.

abhiññācetanā

the volition of direct knowledge — the intentional mental state accompanying a supernormal power such as those catalogued in chapter twelve; excluded here since it does not itself condition rebirth-linking consciousness.

How Do We Know Ignorance Causes This? The Moth, the Honey Knife, the City of Demons

Buddhaghosa now answers a methodological question — how can we actually verify that formations are conditioned by ignorance, rather than simply asserting it? — with the standard Buddhist test of concomitant presence and absence: when unremoved ignorance regarding the four truths is present, all three kinds of formation get produced; when ignorance is removed, they stop. He then illustrates each of the three formations with an unforgettable simile of self-destructive craving: meritorious kamma sought for its own future reward is like a man leaping to his death for a celestial nymph, or a moth flying into a lamp's flame, or someone licking honey off the blade of a knife.

Pali Source

Tattha siyā – kathaṃ panetaṃ jānitabbaṃ "ime saṅkhārā avijjā paccayā hontī"ti? Avijjābhāve bhāvato. Yassa hi dukkhādīsu avijjāsaṅkhātaṃ aññāṇaṃ appahīnaṃ hoti, so dukkhe tāva pubbantādīsu ca aññāṇena saṃsāradukkhaṃ sukhasaññāya gahetvā tasseva hetubhūte tividhepi saṅkhāre ārabhati. Samudaye aññāṇena dukkhahetubhūtepi taṇhāparikkhāre saṅkhāre sukhahetuto maññamāno ārabhati. Nirodhe pana magge ca aññāṇena dukkhassa anirodhabhūtepi gativisese dukkhanirodhasaññī hutvā nirodhassa ca amaggabhūtesupi yaññāmaratapādīsu nirodhamaggasaññī hutvā dukkhanirodhaṃ patthayamāno yaññāmaratapādimukhena tividhepi saṅkhāre ārabhati.

Apica so tāya catūsu saccesu appahīnāvijjatāya visesato jātijarārogamaraṇādianekādīnavavokiṇṇampi puññaphalasaṅkhātaṃ dukkhaṃ dukkhato ajānanto tassa adhigamāya kāyavacīcittasaṅkhārabhedaṃ puññābhisaṅkhāraṃ ārabhati devaccharakāmako viya maruppapātaṃ. Sukhasammatassāpi ca tassa puññaphalassa ante mahāpariḷāhajanikaṃ vipariṇāmadukkhataṃ appassādatañca apassantopi tappaccayaṃ vuttappakārameva puññābhisaṅkhāraṃ ārabhati salabho viya dīpasikhābhinipātaṃ, madhubindugiddho viya ca madhulittasatthadhārālehanaṃ. Kāmupasevanādīsu ca savipākesu ādīnavaṃ apassanto sukhasaññāya ceva kilesābhibhūtatāya ca dvārattayappavattampi apuññābhisaṅkhāraṃ ārabhati, bālo viya gūthakīḷanaṃ, maritukāmo viya ca visakhādanaṃ. Āruppavipākesu cāpi saṅkhāravipariṇāmadukkhataṃ anavabujjhamāno sassatādivipallāsena cittasaṅkhārabhūtaṃ āneñjābhisaṅkhāraṃ ārabhati, disāmūḷho viya pisācanagarābhimukhamaggagamanaṃ.

Evaṃ yasmā avijjābhāvatova saṅkhārabhāvo, na abhāvato. Tasmā jānitabbametaṃ "ime saṅkhārā avijjāpaccayā hontī"ti. Vuttampi cetaṃ "avidvā, bhikkhave, avijjāgato puññābhisaṅkhārampi abhisaṅkharoti, apuññābhisaṅkhārampi abhisaṅkharoti, āneñjābhisaṅkhārampi abhisaṅkharoti. Yato ca kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhuno avijjā pahīnā, vijjā uppannā; so avijjāvirāgā vijjuppādā neva puññābhisaṅkhāraṃ abhisaṅkharotī"ti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Here it might be asked: how, then, is this to be known — that "these formations are conditioned by ignorance"? By their being present when ignorance is present, and absent when it is absent. For one in whom the unknowing called ignorance, with respect to suffering and the rest, is not abandoned — such a one, through unknowing regarding suffering and, first of all, regarding the past and the rest, grasps the suffering of the round as pleasure, and undertakes, on account of that very misperception, all three kinds of formations. Through unknowing regarding the origin, not seeing that the formations which equip craving are themselves the cause of suffering, he undertakes them, thinking them the cause of happiness. And through unknowing regarding cessation and the path, having become one who perceives some particular destination as the cessation of suffering even though it is not the cessation of suffering at all, and one who perceives sacrifices, austerities, and the like as the path to cessation even though they are not the path to cessation at all, and longing for the cessation of suffering, he undertakes all three kinds of formations by way of such things as sacrifices and austerities.

Moreover, because of this unabandoned ignorance regarding the four truths, not knowing, in particular, as suffering that very suffering — the so-called "fruit of merit," strewn with the many drawbacks of birth, aging, sickness, death, and the rest — he undertakes, for the sake of attaining it, the meritorious formation, divided into bodily, verbal, and mental formations, just like one who, longing for a celestial nymph, leaps to his death, longing for a fall into a divine realm. And even one who does not see, at the end of that fruit of merit accounted as pleasure, the suffering inherent in its very changeability — productive of great anguish — and its lack of true satisfaction, still undertakes, on account of that very craving, that same kind of meritorious formation — just like a moth flying into the flame of a lamp, or one greedy for a drop of honey licking the honey-smeared edge of a knife-blade. And one who does not see the drawback in such things as the pursuit of sensual pleasures, together with their results, undertakes, through the perception of pleasure and through being overpowered by the defilements, the demeritorious formation as well, operating through all three doors — just like a fool playing in filth, or one wishing to die by eating poison. And one who does not understand the suffering inherent in the very changeability of the formations, even within the results of the formless attainments, undertakes, through the distortion of perceiving them as permanent and the like, the imperturbable formation — just like one lost in direction, walking a road that leads straight to a city of demons.

Thus, since the existence of the formations depends precisely on the presence of ignorance, and not on its absence, this should be understood: "these formations are conditioned by ignorance." And this, too, was said: "not knowing, bhikkhus, this person, gone into ignorance, produces even a meritorious formation, produces even a demeritorious formation, produces even an imperturbable formation. But when, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu's ignorance has been abandoned and true knowledge has arisen, then, through the fading away of ignorance and the arising of true knowledge, he no longer produces even a meritorious formation."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The most striking move in this passage is that it does not stop at explaining why demeritorious kamma is ignorance-driven — that would be the easy case — but insists that meritorious kamma is equally ignorance-driven, so long as it is done in pursuit of a future reward rather than for liberation itself. Even a generous, virtuous life aimed only at rebirth in a pleasant realm is, on this analysis, still a form of self-harm rooted in not seeing that realm's own inevitable decay: hence the shocking image of a man leaping to his death chasing a celestial nymph, and the quieter but equally pointed image of licking honey from a blade that will cut the tongue that tastes it.

The imperturbable formation gets its own distinct simile — being lost and walking, in perfect confidence, straight toward a city of demons — which fits its specific error: the formless attainments are so refined and stable that a meditator can easily mistake their long, seemingly changeless duration for actual permanence, precisely the "eternalist" distortion this whole chapter has already spent several paragraphs refuting.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
vipariṇāmadukkhatā

the suffering inherent in changeability — one of the treatise's three kinds of suffering (alongside the suffering of pain itself and the suffering built into all conditioned formations); pleasant results are "suffering" in this specific sense because their very alteration, when it comes, is itself painful.

madhulittasatthadhārālehana

licking the honey-smeared edge of a knife-blade — madhu (honey) + litta (smeared) + sattha (knife) + dhārā (edge) + lehana (licking); the chapter's vivid image for meritorious kamma pursued out of craving for its sweet result, heedless of the cut that inevitably follows.

The Twenty-Four Conditions, and What "Root" Really Means

A sharper question now forces a much deeper technical excursion: granted that ignorance conditions the formations, exactly which formations, and by which of the causal relationships actually available? Buddhaghosa answers by importing, wholesale, the Paṭṭhāna's own master list of twenty-four distinct types of conditioning relation — the analytical toolkit underlying the entire Abhidhamma's treatment of causality — and will spend the whole of this section defining each one before returning, several pages later, to show exactly which of the twenty-four apply to ignorance's conditioning of the formations specifically. The first entry, the root condition, becomes the occasion for a genuine philosophical debate: does a "root" like non-greed determine the wholesome or unwholesome quality of its associated states by its own inherent nature, or does it merely stabilize a quality that is determined elsewhere?

Pali Source

Etthāha – gaṇhāma tāva etaṃ avijjā saṅkhārānaṃ paccayoti, idaṃ pana vattabbaṃ katamesaṃ saṅkhārānaṃ kathaṃ paccayo hotīti? Tatridaṃ vuccati, bhagavatā hi "hetupaccayo, ārammaṇapaccayo, adhipatipaccayo, anantarapaccayo, samanantarapaccayo, sahajātapaccayo, aññamaññapaccayo, nissayapaccayo, upanissayapaccayo, purejātapaccayo, pacchājātapaccayo, āsevanapaccayo, kammapaccayo, vipākapaccayo, āhārapaccayo, indriyapaccayo, jhānapaccayo, maggapaccayo, sampayuttapaccayo, vippayuttapaccayo, atthipaccayo, natthipaccayo, vigatapaccayo, avigatapaccayo"ti catuvīsati paccayā vuttā.

Tattha hetu ca so paccayo cāti hetupaccayo, hetu hutvā paccayo, hetubhāvena paccayoti vuttaṃ hoti. Ārammaṇapaccayādīsupi eseva nayo.

Tattha hetūti vacanāvayavakāraṇamūlānametaṃ adhivacanaṃ. "Paṭiññā, hetū"tiādīsu hi loke vacanāvayavo hetūti vuccati. Sāsane pana "ye dhammā hetuppabhavā"tiādīsu kāraṇaṃ. "Tayo kusalahetū, tayo akusalahetū"tiādīsu mūlaṃ hetūti vuccati, taṃ idha adhippetaṃ. Paccayoti ettha pana ayaṃ vacanattho, paṭicca etasmā etīti paccayo. Apaccakkhāya naṃ vattatīti attho. Yo hi dhammo yaṃ dhammaṃ apaccakkhāya tiṭṭhati vā uppajjati vā, so tassa paccayoti vuttaṃ hoti. Lakkhaṇato pana upakārakalakkhaṇo paccayo. Yo hi dhammo yassa dhammassa ṭhitiyā vā uppattiyā vā upakārako hoti, so tassa paccayoti vuccati. Paccayo, hetu, kāraṇaṃ, nidānaṃ, sambhavo, pabhavotiādi atthato ekaṃ, byañjanato nānaṃ. Iti mūlaṭṭhena hetu, upakārakaṭṭhena paccayoti saṅkhepato mūlaṭṭhena upakārako dhammo hetupaccayo.

So sāliādīnaṃ sālibījādīni viya, maṇipabhādīnaṃ viya ca maṇivaṇṇādayo kusalādīnaṃ kusalādibhāvasādhakoti ācariyānaṃ adhippāyo. Evaṃ sante pana taṃsamuṭṭhānarūpesu hetupaccayatā na sampajjati. Na hi so tesaṃ kusalādibhāvaṃ sādheti, na ca paccayo na hoti. Vuttañhetaṃ "hetū hetusampayuttakānaṃ dhammānaṃ taṃsamuṭṭhānānañca rūpānaṃ hetupaccayena paccayo"ti. Ahetukacittānañca vinā etena abyākatabhāvo siddho, sahetukānampi ca yonisomanasikārādipaṭibaddho kusalādibhāvo, na sampayuttahetupaṭibaddho. Yadi ca sampayuttahetūsu sabhāvatova kusalādibhāvo siyā, sampayuttesu hetupaṭibaddho alobho kusalo vā siyā abyākato vā. Yasmā pana ubhayathāpi hoti, tasmā yathā sampayuttesu, evaṃ hetūsupi kusalāditā pariyesitabbā.

Kusalādibhāvasādhanavasena pana hetūnaṃ mūlaṭṭhaṃ agahetvā suppatiṭṭhitabhāvasādhanavasena gayhamāne na kiñci virujjhati. Laddhahetupaccayā hi dhammā virūḷhamūlā viya pādapā thirā honti suppatiṭṭhitā, ahetukā tilabījakādisevālā viya na suppatiṭṭhitā. Iti mūlaṭṭhena upakārakoti suppatiṭṭhitabhāvasādhanena upakārako dhammo hetupaccayoti veditabbo.

Ru-Yi Translation

Here it might be said: "granted, then, that this ignorance is a condition for the formations" — but this should also be asked: a condition for which formations, and how? Here is the answer. For the Blessed One declared twenty-four conditions: "the root condition, the object condition, the predominance condition, the contiguity condition, the immediate contiguity condition, the conascence condition, the mutuality condition, the dependence condition, the decisive support condition, the prenascence condition, the postnascence condition, the repetition condition, the kamma condition, the result condition, the nutriment condition, the faculty condition, the jhāna condition, the path condition, the association condition, the dissociation condition, the presence condition, the absence condition, the disappearance condition, and the non-disappearance condition."

Herein, "that which is both a root and a condition" is the "root condition" — meaning, a condition by being a root, a condition by way of the state of being a root. The same method applies to the object condition and the rest.

Herein, "root" is a term used for three distinct things: a component of speech, a cause, and a root. For in worldly usage, in such phrases as "a reason for the assertion," a component of an argument is called "hetu." But in the Teaching, in such passages as "those things that spring from a cause," it means a cause generally. In such passages as "the three wholesome roots, the three unwholesome roots," a root is called "hetu" — and that root is what is intended here. As for "condition," this is its literal sense: that from which, having depended on it, a result comes — that is a condition. It means: the result occurs without having disregarded it. For whatever state, without disregarding some other state, persists or arises, that other state is said to be its condition. By characteristic, however, a condition has the characteristic of being a help. For whatever state is a help to another state's persisting or arising, that is called its condition. "Condition," "root," "cause," "source," "origin," "production," and the like are one in meaning, though different in wording. Thus, in the sense of being a root, it is "hetu"; in the sense of being a help, it is "paccaya." In brief: a state that is a help by way of being a root is the "root condition."

It is the opinion of the teachers that this root is what accomplishes the very state of being wholesome and so forth for wholesome and other states, just as rice-seeds and the like accomplish the ripening of rice-plants, and just as the color and the rest of a gem accomplish its own distinctive quality. But if this were so, root-condition-hood would not hold for the matter produced by that same consciousness — for the root does not accomplish the wholesome or other state of that derived matter, yet it is not the case that it is not its condition either. For it is said: "roots are a condition, by way of the root condition, for states associated with the root and for the matter produced by them." And, again, the indeterminate nature of rootless states of consciousness is established without reference to this root; and even for states of consciousness that do have roots, their wholesome or other state is bound up with wise attention and the like, not bound up with the associated root. And if the wholesome or other state of associated roots existed simply by their own inherent nature, then non-greed, when bound up with the associated roots, would have to be either wholesome or indeterminate absolutely — yet since it is found to be both, the wholesome or other quality must be sought in the roots in just the same way as in the associated states.

But if the root's being a "root" is taken, not in the sense of accomplishing the wholesome or other state, but in the sense of accomplishing a well-established condition, nothing is contradicted. For states that have obtained the root condition are firm and well established, like trees whose roots have grown deep; states without a root are not well established, like the moss on a sesame seed or the like. Thus it should be understood: a state that is a help, in the sense of being a root, by way of accomplishing a well-established condition — that is the "root condition."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

This is the moment the chapter opens the Paṭṭhāna's own toolbox directly into the Visuddhimagga — the twenty-four conditions named here are the very same list that fills the seven enormous volumes of the Paṭṭhāna, the last and, by most accounts, most technically demanding book of the Abhidhamma Piṭaka. Buddhaghosa is not inventing new material; he is showing exactly how this specialist machinery gets applied to a single, specific case — how ignorance conditions the formations — as a worked example of a method the reader is assumed to need demonstrated rather than merely described.

The debate over what "root" actually contributes is a genuine fork in Abhidhamma commentarial thinking, and Buddhaghosa's own resolution is subtle: a root does not manufacture the ethical quality of its associated states out of nothing — that quality tracks something else, chiefly wise or unwise attention — but a root does make an already-wholesome or already-unwholesome state firm and stable, the way a deep root system makes an already-growing tree stand fast rather than toppling at the first wind. Rootless consciousness is not thereby unstable in an ethical sense (it is simply indeterminate, neither wholesome nor unwholesome), but it lacks this particular kind of reinforcement.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
catuvīsati paccayā

the twenty-four conditions — catuvīsati (twenty-four) + paccayā (conditions); the Paṭṭhāna's complete taxonomy of causal relationships, imported here wholesale as the analytical framework for the rest of this section.

hetupaccayo

the root condition — the first of the twenty-four; specifically the six roots (greed, hatred, delusion, and their wholesome opposites) acting as a condition, distinguished carefully here from "cause" in any looser, more general sense.

yonisomanasikāra

wise attention — yoniso (by way of the origin, thoroughly) + manasikāra (attention); the factor Buddhaghosa identifies as actually determining a state's wholesome or unwholesome quality, rather than the associated root itself.

Object and Predominance

The next two conditions describe how consciousness needs something to lean on: an object condition, without which no state of consciousness could arise at all — Buddhaghosa's own homely image is a weak man who can only stand by leaning on a stick — and a predominance condition, in which one single factor, out of several candidates, takes charge of a given moment of consciousness as its "chief."

Pali Source

Tato paresu ārammaṇabhāvena upakārako dhammo ārammaṇapaccayo. So "rūpāyatanaṃ cakkhuviññāṇadhātuyā"ti ārabhitvāpi "yaṃ yaṃ dhammaṃ ārabbha ye ye dhammā uppajjanti cittacetasikā dhammā, te te dhammā tesaṃ tesaṃ dhammānaṃ ārammaṇapaccayena paccayo"ti osāpitattā na koci dhammo na hoti. Yathā hi dubbalo puriso daṇḍaṃ vā rajjuṃ vā ālambitvāva uṭṭhahati ceva tiṭṭhati ca, evaṃ cittacetasikā dhammā rūpādiārammaṇaṃ ārabbheva uppajjanti ceva tiṭṭhanti ca. Tasmā sabbepi cittacetasikānaṃ ārammaṇabhūtā dhammā ārammaṇapaccayoti veditabbā.

Jeṭṭhakaṭṭhena upakārako dhammo adhipatipaccayo, so sahajātārammaṇavasena duvidho. Tattha "chandādhipati chandasampayuttakānaṃ dhammānaṃ taṃsamuṭṭhānānañca rūpānaṃ adhipatipaccayena paccayo"tiādivacanato chandavīriyacittavīmaṃsāsaṅkhātā cattāro dhammā adhipatipaccayoti veditabbā, no ca kho ekato. Yadā hi chandaṃ dhuraṃ chandaṃ jeṭṭhakaṃ katvā cittaṃ pavattati, tadā chandova adhipati, na itare. Esa nayo sesesupi.

Yaṃ pana dhammaṃ garuṃ katvā arūpadhammā pavattanti, so nesaṃ ārammaṇādhipati. Tena vuttaṃ "yaṃ yaṃ dhammaṃ garuṃ katvā ye ye dhammā uppajjanti cittacetasikā dhammā, te te dhammā tesaṃ tesaṃ dhammānaṃ adhipatipaccayena paccayo"ti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Beyond that, a state that is a help by way of being an object is the "object condition." Though it begins by being illustrated as "the visible-form base is a condition for the eye-consciousness element," it is finally summed up as "whatever states of consciousness and mental factors arise having taken any given state as their object — that given state is, for those states of consciousness and mental factors, a condition by way of the object condition" — such that there is no state whatsoever that is excluded. For just as a weak man can only rise and stand by leaning on a stick or a rope, so too states of consciousness and mental factors arise and persist only by taking hold of an object such as a visible form. Therefore, it should be understood that all states whatsoever that serve as an object for consciousness and mental factors are the "object condition."

A state that is a help by way of being the chief is the "predominance condition"; it is of two kinds, by way of conascence and by way of being an object. Herein, from such statements as "zeal-as-predominant is a condition, by way of the predominance condition, for the states associated with zeal and for the matter produced by them," the four states called zeal, energy, mind, and investigation should be understood as the predominance condition — but not all at once. For when consciousness occurs having made zeal its task, having made zeal its chief, then zeal alone is the predominant one, not the others. The same method applies to the rest.

But that state which, being given weight, causes formless states to occur — that is their object-predominant condition. Hence it is said: "whatever state, having been given weight, gives rise to whatever states of consciousness and mental factors — that state is, for those states of consciousness and mental factors, a condition by way of the predominance condition."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The predominance condition's rule that only one candidate can hold the "chief" position at a time is easy to miss but doctrinally important: zeal, energy, mind, and investigation are the four factors singled out throughout the treatise (see the earlier chapter on direct knowledges) as the "bases of spiritual power," yet a single moment of consciousness is never driven by all four equally — one of them takes the lead, and which one varies from moment to moment and person to person, which is part of why different meditators are said to progress by different dominant faculties.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
ārammaṇapaccayo

the object condition — any state that serves as the object taken by an act of consciousness; since no consciousness can arise objectless, this condition applies, in principle, without exception.

chanda / vīriya / citta / vīmaṃsā

zeal, energy, mind, investigation — the four "bases of spiritual power" (iddhipāda), each capable of serving as the conascent predominance condition for a moment of consciousness, though never more than one at a time.

Contiguity and Immediate Contiguity

These two conditions describe the strict, unbroken order in which one moment of consciousness gives way to the next — the reason, for instance, eye-consciousness is always followed immediately by mind-element, never by anything else. Buddhaghosa also settles a live dispute among the teachers: some claimed the two terms differ by meaning (one about logical succession, one about temporal succession), but a canonical passage about emerging from the deep meditative attainment of cessation — where a very long stretch of real time intervenes, yet the technical term used is still "immediate contiguity" — proves the difference is one of wording only.

Pali Source

Anantarabhāvena upakārako dhammo anantarapaccayo. Samanantarabhāvena upakārako dhammo samanantarapaccayo. Idañca paccayadvayaṃ bahudhā papañcayanti. Ayaṃ panettha sāro, yo hi esa cakkhuviññāṇānantarā manodhātu, manodhātuanantarā manoviññāṇadhātūtiādi cittaniyamo, so yasmā purimapurimacittavaseneva ijjhati, na aññathā, tasmā attano attano anantaraṃ anurūpassa cittuppādassa uppādanasamattho dhammo anantarapaccayo. Tenevāha – "anantarapaccayoti cakkhuviññāṇadhātu taṃsampayuttakā ca dhammā manodhātuyā taṃsampayuttakānañca dhammānaṃ anantarapaccayena paccayo"tiādi. Yo anantarapaccayo, sveva samanantarapaccayo. Byañjanamattameva hettha nānaṃ, upacayasantatīsu viya adhivacananiruttidukādīsu viya ca. Atthato pana nānaṃ natthi.

Yampi "atthānantaratāya anantarapaccayo, kālānantaratāya samanantarapaccayo"ti ācariyānaṃ mataṃ, taṃ "nirodhā vuṭṭhahantassa nevasaññānāsaññāyatanakusalaṃ phalasamāpattiyā samanantarapaccayena paccayo"tiādīhi virujjhati. Yampi tattha vadanti "dhammānaṃ samuṭṭhāpanasamatthatā na parihāyati, bhāvanābalena pana vāritattā dhammā samanantarā nuppajjantī"ti, tampi kālānantaratāya abhāvameva sādheti. Bhāvanābalena hi tattha kālānantaratā natthīti, mayampi etadeva vadāma. Yasmā ca kālānantaratā natthi, tasmā samanantarapaccayatā na yujjati. Kālānantaratāya hi tesaṃ samanantarapaccayo hotīti laddhi. Tasmā abhinivesaṃ akatvā byañjanamattatovettha nānākaraṇaṃ paccetabbaṃ, na atthato. Kathaṃ? Natthi etesaṃ antaranti hi anantarā. Saṇṭhānābhāvato suṭṭhu anantarāti samanantarā.

Ru-Yi Translation

A state that is a help by way of contiguity is the "contiguity condition." A state that is a help by way of immediate contiguity is the "immediate contiguity condition." These two conditions are elaborated upon at great length elsewhere; but this, herein, is the essence: that fixed order of consciousness whereby, for instance, the mind-element follows immediately upon eye-consciousness, and the mind-consciousness-element follows immediately upon the mind-element — since this order is accomplished only by force of each preceding state of consciousness, and never otherwise — therefore a state capable of giving rise to a suitable state of consciousness immediately following it is the "contiguity condition." Hence it is said: "the contiguity condition is this: the eye-consciousness element and its associated states are, for the mind-element and its associated states, a condition by way of the contiguity condition," and so on. Whatever is a contiguity condition is precisely an immediate contiguity condition as well; the difference here is one of wording only, as with "accumulation" and "continuity," or as with such technical pairs as "designation" and "expression." In meaning, however, there is no difference.

As for the opinion of certain teachers that "it is the contiguity condition by way of contiguity in meaning, and the immediate contiguity condition by way of contiguity in time" — this is contradicted by such passages as: "for one emerging from cessation, wholesome consciousness of the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception is, for the attainment of fruition, a condition by way of the immediate contiguity condition." And what they say there — "the capacity of these states to give rise to a subsequent state is not lost; rather, by the power of development, states are simply prevented from arising immediately" — this very statement establishes precisely the absence of contiguity in time. For we, too, say just this: that, by the power of development, there is no contiguity in time in that case. And since there is no contiguity in time, the state of being an immediate contiguity condition would then not hold — for their own position is that it is by contiguity in time that these states become an immediate contiguity condition. Therefore, without clinging to that view, the distinction here should be accepted as one of wording only, not of meaning. How so? "Contiguous" means: there is no interval between these states. "Immediately contiguous" means: thoroughly contiguous, on account of there being no intervening configuration that could separate them.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The refutation here is a compact piece of Abhidhamma detective work: cessation of perception and feeling (nirodhasamāpatti) is a meditative attainment in which mental activity stops entirely for a stretch of real time that can run to days — yet the canonical texts still describe the consciousness immediately following it, on emergence, using the technical term "immediate contiguity condition," exactly the term the rival theory says should require strict temporal adjacency. If temporal gap were really what distinguished the two conditions, this description would be a flat contradiction; since it isn't treated as one anywhere in the canon, the two terms must simply be near-synonyms, distinguished by wording alone, the way "accumulation" and "continuity" describe the same underlying process from two angles.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
nirodhasamāpatti

the attainment of cessation — the deepest meditative attainment available to non-returners and arahants who have also mastered all eight jhānas, in which perception and feeling stop entirely for a fixed period; central to this chapter's proof that "immediate contiguity" cannot mean strict temporal adjacency.

anantara / samanantara

contiguous / immediately contiguous — an (not) + antara (interval), and the same with the intensifying prefix sam-; two of the twenty-four conditions shown here to differ in wording only, not in underlying meaning.

Conascence, Mutuality, Dependence, and Decisive Support

Four related conditions now describe different ways things can support one another simultaneously or across time: conascence (arising together, like a lamp and its light), mutuality (propping each other up, like three sticks leaned together), dependence (one resting on a foundation, like a tree on the earth or a painting on its canvas), and — in the longest treatment of the four — decisive support, itself divided into three subtypes depending on whether the powerful influence comes through an object dwelt upon, through the momentum of an immediately preceding state, or through a settled personal quality such as faith or virtue built up over a lifetime.

Pali Source

Uppajjamānova saha uppādanabhāvena upakārako dhammo sahajātapaccayo pakāsassa padīpo viya. So arūpakkhandhādivasena chabbidho hoti. Yathāha – "cattāro khandhā arūpino aññamaññaṃ sahajātapaccayena paccayo. Cattāro mahābhūtā aññamaññaṃ, okkantikkhaṇe nāmarūpaṃ aññamaññaṃ, cittacetasikā dhammā cittasamuṭṭhānānaṃ rūpānaṃ, mahābhūtā upādārūpānaṃ, rūpino dhammā arūpīnaṃ dhammānaṃ kiñcikāle sahajātapaccayena paccayo, kiñcikāle na sahajātapaccayena paccayo"ti. Idaṃ hadayavatthumeva sandhāya vuttaṃ.

Aññamaññaṃ uppādanupatthambhanabhāvena upakārako dhammo aññamaññapaccayo aññamaññūpatthambhakaṃ tidaṇḍakaṃ viya. So arūpakkhandhādivasena tividho hoti. Yathāha – "cattāro khandhā arūpino aññamaññapaccayena paccayo. Cattāro mahābhūtā okkantikkhaṇe nāmarūpaṃ aññamaññapaccayena paccayo"ti.

Adhiṭṭhānākārena nissayākārena ca upakārako dhammo nissayapaccayo tarucittakammādīnaṃ pathavīpaṭādayo viya. So "cattāro khandhā arūpino aññamaññaṃ nissayapaccayena paccayo"ti evaṃ sahajāte vuttanayeneva veditabbo. Chaṭṭho panettha koṭṭhāso "cakkhāyatanaṃ cakkhuviññāṇadhātuyā…pe… sota… ghāna… jivhā… kāyāyatanaṃ kāyaviññāṇadhātuyā taṃsampayuttakānañca dhammānaṃ nissayapaccayena paccayo. Yaṃ rūpaṃ nissāya manodhātu ca manoviññāṇadhātu ca vattanti, taṃ rūpaṃ manodhātuyā ca manoviññāṇadhātuyā ca taṃsampayuttakānañca dhammānaṃ nissayapaccayena paccayo"ti evaṃ vibhatto.

Upanissayapaccayoti ettha pana ayaṃ tāva vacanattho, tadadhīnavuttitāya attano phalena nissito na paṭikkhittoti nissayo. Yathā pana bhuso āyāso upāyāso, evaṃ bhuso nissayo upanissayo, balavakāraṇassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Tasmā balavakāraṇabhāvena upakārako dhammo upanissayapaccayoti veditabbo.

So ārammaṇūpanissayo anantarūpanissayo pakatūpanissayoti tividho hoti. Tattha "dānaṃ datvā sīlaṃ samādiyitvā uposathakammaṃ katvā taṃ garuṃkatvā paccavekkhati, pubbe suciṇṇāni garuṃkatvā paccavekkhati, jhānā vuṭṭhahitvā jhānaṃ garuṃkatvā paccavekkhati, sekkhā gotrabhuṃ garuṃkatvā paccavekkhanti, vodānaṃ garuṃkatvā paccavekkhanti. Sekkhā maggā vuṭṭhahitvā maggaṃ garuṃkatvā paccavekkhantī"ti evamādinā nayena ārammaṇūpanissayo tāva ārammaṇādhipatinā saddhiṃ nānattaṃ akatvāva vibhatto. Tattha yaṃ ārammaṇaṃ garuṃkatvā cittacetasikā uppajjanti, taṃ niyamato tesu ārammaṇesu balavārammaṇaṃ hoti. Iti garukattabbamattaṭṭhena ārammaṇādhipati, balavakāraṇaṭṭhena ārammaṇūpanissayoti evametesaṃ nānattaṃ veditabbaṃ.

Anantarūpanissayopi "purimā purimā kusalā khandhā pacchimānaṃ pacchimānaṃ kusalānaṃ khandhānaṃ upanissayapaccayena paccayo"tiādinā nayena anantarapaccayena saddhiṃ nānattaṃ akatvāva vibhatto. Evaṃ santepi attano attano anantarā anurūpassa cittuppādassa pavattanasamatthatāya anantaratā, purimacittassa pacchimacittuppādane balavatāya anantarūpanissayatā veditabbā. Yathā hi hetupaccayādīsu kiñci dhammaṃ vināpi cittaṃ uppajjati, na evaṃ anantaracittaṃ vinā cittassa uppatti nāma atthi. Tasmā balavapaccayo hoti. Iti attano attano anantarā anurūpacittuppādanavasena anantarapaccayo, balavakāraṇavasena anantarūpanissayoti evametesaṃ nānattaṃ veditabbaṃ.

Pakatūpanissayo pana pakato upanissayo pakatūpanissayo. Pakato nāma attano santāne nipphādito vā saddhāsīlādi upasevito vā utubhojanādi. Pakatiyā eva vā upanissayo pakatūpanissayo, ārammaṇānantarehi asammissoti attho. Tassa pakatūpanissayo "saddhaṃ upanissāya dānaṃ deti, sīlaṃ samādiyati, uposathakammaṃ karoti, jhānaṃ uppādeti, vipassanaṃ uppādeti, maggaṃ uppādeti, abhiññaṃ uppādeti, samāpattiṃ uppādeti. Sīlaṃ, sutaṃ, cāgaṃ, paññaṃ upanissāya dānaṃ deti…pe… samāpattiṃ uppādeti. Saddhā, sīlaṃ, sutaṃ, cāgo, paññā saddhāya, sīlassa, sutassa, cāgassa, paññāya, upanissayapaccayena paccayo"tiādinā nayena anekappakārato pabhedo veditabbo. Iti ime saddhādayo pakatā ceva balavakāraṇaṭṭhena upanissayā cāti pakatūpanissayoti.

Ru-Yi Translation

A state that, in the very act of arising, is a help by way of arising together with another — that is the "conascence condition," like a lamp for light. It is of six kinds, by way of the formless aggregates and so forth. As it is said: "the four formless aggregates are, for one another, a condition by way of the conascence condition. The four great elements are, for one another; at the moment of descent into the womb, name-and-form are, for one another; states of consciousness and mental factors are, for the matter produced by consciousness; the great elements are, for derived matter; material states are, for formless states, on some occasions a condition by way of the conascence condition, and on some occasions not." This last is said with reference to the heart-basis alone.

A state that is a help to another by way of mutually giving rise to and supporting one another is the "mutuality condition," like three sticks propping one another up. It is of three kinds, by way of the formless aggregates and so forth. As it is said: "the four formless aggregates are, for one another, a condition by way of the mutuality condition. The four great elements are, for one another; at the moment of descent, name-and-form are, for one another, a condition by way of the mutuality condition."

A state that is a help by way of being a foundation and by way of being a support is the "dependence condition," like the earth and a canvas for a tree and a painting. It should be understood in just the way already stated for the conascence condition: "the four formless aggregates are, for one another, a condition by way of the dependence condition." There is, however, a sixth portion here, distinguished thus: "the eye-base is, for the eye-consciousness element and its associated states, a condition by way of the dependence condition; likewise the ear … the nose … the tongue … the body-base, for the body-consciousness element and its associated states. That matter depending on which the mind-element and the mind-consciousness element occur — that matter is, for the mind-element and the mind-consciousness element and their associated states, a condition by way of the dependence condition."

As for "decisive support condition," this, first, is its literal meaning: because of occurring in dependence on it, the result is depended upon by its own effect, not rejected — this is "dependence." And just as "intense distress" intensified becomes "great distress," so too "dependence" intensified becomes "decisive dependence," a term for a powerful cause. Therefore, a state that is a help by way of being a powerful cause should be understood as the "decisive support condition."

It is of three kinds: decisive support by way of object, decisive support by way of contiguity, and decisive support by way of the natural. Herein, decisive support by way of object is first distinguished, in the manner of such passages as "having given a gift, having undertaken the precepts, having performed the observance-day duties, one reviews that, giving it weight; one reviews, giving weight, good deeds done in the past; having emerged from jhāna, one reviews the jhāna, giving it weight; trainees review the change-of-lineage consciousness, giving it weight; they review purification, giving it weight; trainees, having emerged from the path, review the path, giving it weight" — without, at this point, being distinguished from the object-predominance condition. Herein, whatever object, being given weight, gives rise to states of consciousness and mental factors, that object invariably becomes, among those objects, a powerful object. Thus the distinction between these two should be understood as follows: in the mere sense of being worthy of being given weight, it is the object-predominance condition; in the sense of being a powerful cause, it is decisive support by way of object.

Decisive support by way of contiguity, too, is distinguished, in the manner of such passages as "each preceding wholesome aggregate is, for each succeeding wholesome aggregate, a condition by way of the decisive support condition," without, at this point, being distinguished from the contiguity condition. Even so, it should be understood: contiguity-hood consists in the capacity to give rise to a suitable state of consciousness immediately following one's own occurrence, while decisive-support-by-contiguity-hood consists in the power exercised by a preceding state of consciousness in giving rise to a succeeding one. For just as, in the case of the root condition and the rest, a state of consciousness can arise even without some particular state, there is no such thing, in the case of a contiguous state of consciousness, as a state of consciousness arising without it. Therefore it is a powerful condition. Thus it should be understood: by way of giving rise to a suitable state of consciousness immediately following one's own occurrence, it is the contiguity condition; by way of being a powerful cause, it is decisive support by way of contiguity.

But decisive support by way of the natural: a "natural" state is one either accomplished in one's own mental continuity — such as faith, virtue, and the like — or resorted to, such as climate, food, and the like. Or, alternatively: decisive support that is simply by natural inclination itself is decisive support by way of the natural — meaning, not mixed together with decisive support by way of object or contiguity. Its manifold distinction should be understood in the manner of such passages as: "in dependence on faith, one gives a gift, undertakes the precepts, performs the observance-day duties, gives rise to jhāna, gives rise to insight, gives rise to the path, gives rise to direct knowledge, gives rise to an attainment. In dependence on virtue, learning, generosity, wisdom, one gives a gift … gives rise to an attainment. Faith, virtue, learning, generosity, and wisdom are, for faith, virtue, learning, generosity, and wisdom, a condition by way of the decisive support condition." Thus these states — faith and the rest — being both natural and, in the sense of being a powerful cause, a decisive support, constitute "decisive support by way of the natural."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

Decisive support by natural inclination (pakatūpanissaya) is arguably the most quietly important condition in the entire list for anyone thinking about the actual practice of the path: it is the technical name for exactly what a teacher means by saying that faith, virtue, learning, generosity, and wisdom "support" further faith, virtue, learning, generosity, and wisdom — a settled character trait acting as a genuine causal condition for future wholesome action, not merely a habit of speech. It is also the condition doing the causal work whenever the tradition says a favorable "natural" factor such as good health, good company, or a suitable climate supports meditative progress.

The three-way split between decisive support by object, by contiguity, and by the natural is worth holding onto as a map for later: the same underlying idea — a powerful, not-strictly-necessary-but-highly-influential condition — shows up in three very different guises depending on whether the influence comes from what one is looking at, from the sheer momentum of what one's mind was just doing, or from settled qualities built up over a whole lifetime.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
sahajātapaccayo / aññamaññapaccayo

the conascence condition / the mutuality condition — sahajāta (born together) and aññamañña (mutual); closely related conditions describing simultaneous arising and mutual support, illustrated respectively by a lamp and its light, and by three sticks leaned together for mutual support.

upanissayapaccayo

the decisive support condition — upa- (intensifying) + nissaya (dependence); a "powerful cause," divided into support by object, by contiguity, and by natural inclination (pakatūpanissaya), the last covering settled qualities like faith and virtue.

gotrabhū

change-of-lineage — the pivotal moment of consciousness, mentioned here in passing, that marks a meditator's transition from the worldling's lineage to the noble one, immediately before path-consciousness itself arises.

Prenascence, Postnascence, Repetition, Kamma, and Result

These five conditions describe support across time in different directions: something already present before an event can support it (prenascence — the physical eye supporting eye-consciousness), something arising after can still support what came before it (postnascence, illustrated by a striking image of a vulture chick's hunger nourishing its own already-formed body), repeated practice strengthens what follows (repetition, like drilling a memorized text), volitional action shapes a later result (the kamma condition), and a settled, "effortless" result in turn supports its own associated states (the result condition).

Pali Source

Paṭhamataraṃ uppajjitvā vattamānabhāvena upakārako dhammo purejātapaccayo. So pañcadvāre vatthārammaṇahadayavatthuvasena ekādasavidho hoti. Yathāha – "cakkhāyatanaṃ cakkhuviññāṇadhātuyā taṃsampayuttakānañca dhammānaṃ purejātapaccayena paccayo. Sota…pe… ghāna, jivhā, kāyāyatanaṃ, rūpa, sadda, gandha, rasa, phoṭṭhabbāyatanaṃ kāyaviññāṇadhātuyā taṃsampayuttakānañca dhammānaṃ purejātapaccayena paccayo. Rūpa, sadda, gandha, rasa, phoṭṭhabbāyatanaṃ manodhātuyā. Yaṃ rūpaṃ nissāya manodhātu ca manoviññāṇadhātu ca vattanti, taṃ rūpaṃ manodhātuyā taṃsampayuttakānañca dhammānaṃ purejātapaccayena paccayo. Manoviññāṇadhātuyā taṃsampayuttakānañca dhammānaṃ kiñcikāle purejātapaccayena paccayo. Kiñcikāle na purejātapaccayena paccayo"ti.

Purejātānaṃ rūpadhammānaṃ upatthambhakattena upakārako arūpadhammo pacchājātapaccayo gijjhapotakasarīrānaṃ āhārāsācetanā viya. Tena vuttaṃ "pacchājātā cittacetasikā dhammā purejātassa imassa kāyassa pacchājātapaccayena paccayo"ti.

Āsevanaṭṭhena anantarānaṃ paguṇabalavabhāvāya upakārako dhammo āsevanapaccayo ganthādīsu purimapurimābhiyogo viya. So kusalākusalakiriyajavanavasena tividho hoti. Yathāha – "purimā purimā kusalā dhammā pacchimānaṃ pacchimānaṃ kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ āsevanapaccayena paccayo. Purimā purimā akusalā…pe… kiriyābyākatā dhammā pacchimānaṃ pacchimānaṃ kiriyābyākatānaṃ dhammānaṃ āsevanapaccayena paccayo"ti.

Cittapayogasaṅkhātena kiriyabhāvena upakārako dhammo kammapaccayo. So nānakkhaṇikāya ceva kusalākusalacetanāya sahajātāya ca sabbāyapi cetanāya vasena duvidho hoti. Yathāha – "kusalākusalaṃ kammaṃ vipākānaṃ khandhānaṃ kaṭattā ca rūpānaṃ kammapaccayena paccayo. Cetanā sampayuttakānaṃ dhammānaṃ taṃsamuṭṭhānānañca rūpānaṃ kammapaccayena paccayo"ti.

Nirussāhasantabhāvena nirussāhasantabhāvāya upakārako vipākadhammo vipākapaccayo. So pavatte taṃsamuṭṭhānānaṃ, paṭisandhiyaṃ kaṭattā ca rūpānaṃ, sabbattha ca sampayuttadhammānaṃ paccayo hoti. Yathāha – "vipākābyākato eko khandho tiṇṇannaṃ khandhānaṃ cittasamuṭṭhānānañca rūpānaṃ vipākapaccayena paccayo…pe… paṭisandhikkhaṇe vipākābyākato eko khandho tiṇṇannaṃ khandhānaṃ kaṭattā ca rūpānaṃ. Tayo khandhā ekassa khandhassa. Dve khandhā dvinnaṃ khandhānaṃ kaṭattā ca rūpānaṃ vipākapaccayena paccayo. Khandhā vatthussa vipākapaccayena paccayo"ti.

Ru-Yi Translation

A state that, having already arisen and being present beforehand, is a help — that is the "prenascence condition." It is of eleven kinds, by way of the physical basis and object in the five sense-doors, and by way of the heart-basis. As it is said: "the eye-base is, for the eye-consciousness element and its associated states, a condition by way of the prenascence condition. Likewise the ear-base … the nose-, tongue-, body-base; visible form, sound, odor, taste, and the tangible base are, for the body-consciousness element and its associated states, a condition by way of the prenascence condition. Visible form … the tangible base is, for the mind-element. That matter depending on which the mind-element and the mind-consciousness element occur — that matter is, for the mind-element and its associated states, a condition by way of the prenascence condition. For the mind-consciousness element and its associated states, on some occasions it is a condition by way of the prenascence condition, and on some occasions it is not."

A formless state that is a help, by way of supporting material states that arose before it, is the "postnascence condition," like the longing for food felt by a vulture's fledglings toward their own already-formed bodies. Hence it is said: "states of consciousness and mental factors that arise afterward are, for this body that arose before them, a condition by way of the postnascence condition."

A state that is a help, by way of repeated practice, toward the proficiency and strength of the states immediately following it — that is the "repetition condition," like each preceding round of application in memorizing texts and the like. It is of three kinds, by way of wholesome, unwholesome, and functional impulsion. As it is said: "each preceding wholesome state is, for each succeeding wholesome state, a condition by way of the repetition condition. Each preceding unwholesome state … each preceding functional-indeterminate state is, for each succeeding functional-indeterminate state, a condition by way of the repetition condition."

A state that is a help by way of the activity called the exertion of mind — that is the "kamma condition." It is of two kinds: by way of wholesome and unwholesome volition operating at a different time from its result, and by way of any and all volition operating conascently with its result. As it is said: "wholesome and unwholesome kamma is, for the resultant aggregates and for matter produced by that kamma, a condition by way of the kamma condition. Volition is, for its associated states and for the matter produced by that same volition, a condition by way of the kamma condition."

A resultant state that, being itself effortless and quiescent, is a help toward the effortless, quiescent occurrence of another state — that is the "result condition." In the course of occurrence, it is a condition for the matter it produces; at rebirth-linking, for the matter produced by that same kamma; and, in every case, for its associated states. As it is said: "one resultant-indeterminate aggregate is, for the other three aggregates and for the matter produced by that consciousness, a condition by way of the result condition … at the moment of rebirth-linking, one resultant-indeterminate aggregate is, for the other three aggregates and for the matter produced by that kamma; three aggregates are, for one aggregate; two aggregates are, for the other two aggregates and for the matter produced by that kamma, a condition by way of the result condition. The aggregates are, for the physical basis, a condition by way of the result condition."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The postnascence condition is the one entry in this whole list that runs against ordinary intuitions about cause and effect, and Buddhaghosa's chosen simile earns its keep by making the counterintuitive direction vivid: a vulture chick's felt hunger, arising after its body has already formed, does not create that body, yet it genuinely sustains and nourishes its continuation. In the same way, a later moment of consciousness does not cause an earlier-arisen physical body to exist, but it does condition that body's continued vitality — a support relation running "backward" in arising-order even though, of course, not backward in time itself, since the supporting mental state and the supported body are simply co-present once the mental state has arisen.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
purejātapaccayo / pacchājātapaccayo

the prenascence condition / the postnascence condition — pure- (before) and pacchā- (after) + jāta (born, arisen); a matched pair describing support running respectively from an earlier-arisen state and from a later-arisen one.

āsevanapaccayo

the repetition condition — āsevana (habitual practice, repeated cultivation); the condition by which each earlier moment of a run of similar mental impulsions strengthens the proficiency of those that immediately follow it.

Nutriment, Faculty, Jhāna, and Path

Four further conditions describe more specialized kinds of sustaining and directing influence: physical and mental nutriment sustaining the body and mind, the twenty faculties (excluding the two sex faculties) exercising dominance each within its own specific sphere, the seven jhāna-factors bringing sustained close contemplation onto an object, and the twelve path-factors specifically directing a state "out" toward its goal — the last two conditions being unavailable, Buddhaghosa notes, in the rootless varieties of the five kinds of sense-consciousness.

Pali Source

Rūpārūpānaṃ upatthambhakaṭṭhena upakārakā cattāro āhārā āhārapaccayo. Yathāha – "kabaḷīkāro āhāro imassa kāyassa āhārapaccayena paccayo. Arūpino āhārā sampayuttakānaṃ dhammānaṃ taṃsamuṭṭhānānañca rūpānaṃ āhārapaccayena paccayo"ti. Pañhāvāre pana "paṭisandhikkhaṇe vipākābyākatā āhārā sampayuttakānaṃ khandhānaṃ kaṭattā ca rūpānaṃ āhārapaccayena paccayo"tipi vuttaṃ.

Adhipatiyaṭṭhena upakārakā itthindriyapurisindriyavajjā vīsatindriyā indriyapaccayo. Tattha cakkhundriyādayo arūpadhammānaṃyeva, sesā rūpārūpānaṃ paccayā honti. Yathāha – "cakkhundriyaṃ cakkhuviññāṇadhātuyā…pe… sota… ghāna… jivhā… kāyindriyaṃ kāyaviññāṇadhātuyā taṃsampayuttakānañca dhammānaṃ indriyapaccayena paccayo. Rūpajīvitindriyaṃ kaṭattārūpānaṃ indriyapaccayena paccayo. Arūpino indriyā sampayuttakānaṃ dhammānaṃ taṃsamuṭṭhānānañca rūpānaṃ indriyapaccayena paccayo"ti. Pañhāvāre pana "paṭisandhikkhaṇe vipākābyākatā indriyā sampayuttakānaṃ khandhānaṃ kaṭattā ca rūpānaṃ indriyapaccayena paccayo"tipi vuttaṃ.

Upanijjhāyanaṭṭhena upakārakāni ṭhapetvā dvipañcaviññāṇe sukhadukkhavedanādvayaṃ sabbānipi kusalādibhedāni satta jhānaṅgāni jhānapaccayo. Yathāha – "jhānaṅgāni jhānasampayuttakānaṃ dhammānaṃ taṃsamuṭṭhānānañca rūpānaṃ jhānapaccayena paccayo"ti. Pañhāvāre pana "paṭisandhikkhaṇe vipākābyākatāni jhānaṅgāni sampayuttakānaṃ khandhānaṃ kaṭattā ca rūpānaṃ jhānapaccayena paccayo"tipi vuttaṃ.

Yato tato vā niyyānaṭṭhena upakārakāni kusalādibhedāni dvādasa maggaṅgāni maggapaccayo. Yathāha – "maggaṅgāni maggasampayuttakānaṃ dhammānaṃ taṃsamuṭṭhānānañca rūpānaṃ maggapaccayena paccayo"ti. Pañhāvāre pana "paṭisandhikkhaṇe vipākābyākatāni maggaṅgāni sampayuttakānaṃ khandhānaṃ kaṭattā ca rūpānaṃ maggapaccayena paccayo"tipi vuttaṃ. Ete pana dvepi jhānamaggapaccayā dvipañcaviññāṇāhetukacittesu na labbhantīti veditabbā.

Ru-Yi Translation

The four kinds of nutriment, which are a help in the sense of sustaining material and formless states, are the "nutriment condition." As it is said: "edible food is, for this body, a condition by way of the nutriment condition. The formless kinds of nutriment are, for their associated states and for the matter produced by them, a condition by way of the nutriment condition." In the Chapter of Questions, it is also said: "at the moment of rebirth-linking, the resultant-indeterminate kinds of nutriment are, for their associated aggregates and for the matter produced by that kamma, a condition by way of the nutriment condition."

The twenty faculties, excepting the femininity and masculinity faculties, which are a help in the sense of exercising dominance — these are the "faculty condition." Herein, the eye-faculty and the rest are conditions only for formless states; the remaining faculties are conditions for both material and formless states. As it is said: "the eye-faculty is, for the eye-consciousness element … the ear-, nose-, tongue-, body-faculty is, for the body-consciousness element and its associated states, a condition by way of the faculty condition. The material life-faculty is, for the matter produced by kamma, a condition by way of the faculty condition. The formless faculties are, for their associated states and for the matter produced by them, a condition by way of the faculty condition." In the Chapter of Questions, it is also said: "at the moment of rebirth-linking, the resultant-indeterminate faculties are, for their associated aggregates and for the matter produced by that kamma, a condition by way of the faculty condition."

The seven jhāna-factors, of every ethical kind, which are a help in the sense of close contemplation, excepting the pair of pleasant and painful bodily feeling in the two sets of fivefold sense-consciousness — these are the "jhāna condition." As it is said: "the jhāna-factors are, for their associated states and for the matter produced by them, a condition by way of the jhāna condition." In the Chapter of Questions, it is also said: "at the moment of rebirth-linking, the resultant-indeterminate jhāna-factors are, for their associated aggregates and for the matter produced by that kamma, a condition by way of the jhāna condition."

The twelve path-factors, of every ethical kind, which are a help in the sense of leading out from this or that state — these are the "path condition." As it is said: "the path-factors are, for their associated states and for the matter produced by them, a condition by way of the path condition." In the Chapter of Questions, it is also said: "at the moment of rebirth-linking, the resultant-indeterminate path-factors are, for their associated aggregates and for the matter produced by that kamma, a condition by way of the path condition." It should be understood, however, that neither of these two conditions — the jhāna condition and the path condition — is found in the rootless states of consciousness belonging to the two sets of fivefold sense-consciousness.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The jhāna condition's name is broader than a meditator might expect: "jhāna" here does not mean only the deep absorptions of formal meditation, but names seven specific mental factors — initial thought, sustained thought, joy, ...and more, the same set analyzed in the earlier chapters on concentration — present, in weaker or stronger form, in ordinary unwholesome and wholesome consciousness alike, not only in jhāna proper. This is why the text specifies "of every ethical kind": the jhāna condition operates even in an unwholesome moment of consciousness, just as "close contemplation" of an object happens whether that contemplation is skillful or not.

The final note — that rootless sense-consciousness lacks both the jhāna and path conditions — fits a broader pattern already established: bare seeing, hearing, and the rest are simple, momentary, minimally structured events, too weak to sustain the kind of directed, factor-rich engagement with an object that either jhāna or the path requires.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
itthindriya / purisindriya

the femininity faculty / the masculinity faculty — two of the twenty-two faculties catalogued in chapter sixteen, specifically excluded from the faculty condition here since they do not function as the required kind of dominance-exercising support.

jhānaṅga / maggaṅga

jhāna-factor / path-factor — the seven mental factors of absorption and the twelve factors of ethically-classified path-consciousness respectively, each set capable of serving, as a whole, as its own distinct condition.

Association, Dissociation, and the Four Conditions of Presence

The list closes with six final conditions, the last four of which — presence, absence, disappearance, and non-disappearance — turn out, as Buddhaghosa candidly notes, to overlap heavily with each other and with earlier entries, included mainly for the sake of completeness and pedagogical variety rather than because each marks a wholly distinct causal mechanism.

Pali Source

Ekavatthukaekārammaṇaekuppādekanirodhasaṅkhātena sampayuttabhāvena upakārakā arūpadhammā sampayuttapaccayo. Yathāha – "cattāro khandhā arūpino aññamaññaṃ sampayuttapaccayena paccayo"ti.

Ekavatthukādibhāvānupagamena upakārakā rūpino dhammā arūpīnaṃ dhammānaṃ, arūpinopi rūpīnaṃ vippayuttapaccayo. So sahajātapacchājātapurejātavasena tividho hoti. Vuttañhetaṃ "sahajātā kusalā khandhā cittasamuṭṭhānānaṃ rūpānaṃ vippayuttapaccayena paccayo. Pacchājātā kusalā khandhā purejātassa imassa kāyassa vippayuttapaccayena paccayo"ti. Purejātaṃ pana cakkhundriyādivatthuvaseneva veditabbaṃ. Yathāha – "purejātaṃ cakkhāyatanaṃ cakkhuviññāṇassa…pe… kāyāyatanaṃ kāyaviññāṇassa vippayuttapaccayena paccayo"ti.

Paccuppannalakkhaṇena atthibhāvena tādisasseva dhammassa upatthambhakattena upakārako dhammo atthipaccayo. Tassa arūpakkhandhamahābhūtanāmarūpacittacetasikamahābhūtaāyatanavatthuvasena sattadhā mātikā nikkhittā. Yathāha – "cattāro khandhā arūpino aññamaññaṃ atthipaccayena paccayo, cattāro mahābhūtā, okkantikkhaṇe nāmarūpaṃ aññamaññaṃ. Cittacetasikā dhammā cittasamuṭṭhānānaṃ rūpānaṃ. Mahābhūtā upādārūpānaṃ. Cakkhāyatanaṃ cakkhuviññāṇadhātuyā…pe… taṃsampayuttakānañca dhammānaṃ atthipaccayena paccayo"ti.

Attano anantarā uppajjamānānaṃ arūpadhammānaṃ pavattiokāsadānena upakārakā samanantaraniruddhā arūpadhammā natthipaccayo. Yathāha – "samanantaraniruddhā cittacetasikā dhammā paṭuppannānaṃ cittacetasikānaṃ dhammānaṃ natthipaccayena paccayo"ti.

Te eva vigatabhāvena upakārakattā vigatapaccayo. Yathāha – "samanantaravigatā cittacetasikā dhammā paṭuppannānaṃ cittacetasikānaṃ dhammānaṃ vigatapaccayena paccayo"ti.

Atthi paccayadhammā eva ca avigatabhāvena upakārakattā avigatapaccayāti veditabbā. Desanāvilāsena pana tathā vinetabbaveneyyavasena vā ayaṃ duko vutto, ahetukadukaṃ vatvāpi hetuvippayuttaduko viyāti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Formless states that are a help by way of association — that is, by having one physical basis, one object, one arising, and one ceasing in common — are the "association condition." As it is said: "the four formless aggregates are, for one another, a condition by way of the association condition."

Material states, which are a help to formless states by not having such things as one physical basis in common — and formless states likewise, to material states — are the "dissociation condition." It is of three kinds, by way of conascence, postnascence, and prenascence. For it is said: "conascent wholesome aggregates are, for the matter produced by that consciousness, a condition by way of the dissociation condition. Postnascent wholesome aggregates are, for this prenascent body, a condition by way of the dissociation condition." The prenascent dissociation condition, however, should be understood only by way of the eye-faculty and the other physical bases. As it is said: "the prenascent eye-base is, for eye-consciousness … the body-base is, for body-consciousness, a condition by way of the dissociation condition."

A state that, by the characteristic of being present, is a help to a state of that same kind by supporting it — that is the "presence condition." Its summary heading is set out in seven ways: by way of the formless aggregates, the great elements, name-and-form, consciousness and mental factors, the great elements and derived matter, the sense bases, and the physical basis. As it is said: "the four formless aggregates are, for one another, a condition by way of the presence condition; the four great elements are, for one another; at the moment of descent, name-and-form are, for one another. States of consciousness and mental factors are, for the matter produced by that consciousness. The great elements are, for derived matter. The eye-base is, for the eye-consciousness element … and its associated states, a condition by way of the presence condition."

Formless states that have just ceased, immediately upon their own occurrence, which are a help by giving occasion for the occurrence of formless states now arising immediately after them — these are the "absence condition." As it is said: "states of consciousness and mental factors that have ceased immediately are, for the present states of consciousness and mental factors, a condition by way of the absence condition."

Those very same states, by way of being a help through their having disappeared, are the "disappearance condition." As it is said: "states of consciousness and mental factors that have disappeared immediately are, for the present states of consciousness and mental factors, a condition by way of the disappearance condition."

And the very states that constitute the presence condition, by way of being a help through their not having disappeared, should be understood as the "non-disappearance condition." This pair, however, is stated by way of the exuberance of the teaching, or for the sake of those to be guided who need to be taught in just that way — just as the rootless pair is stated even after the root-dissociated pair has already been stated.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

Buddhaghosa's closing admission is refreshingly candid for a text this systematic: the absence condition and the disappearance condition are, in substance, the very same relationship described twice, and the presence condition and the non-disappearance condition likewise collapse into one another on inspection. He explains this redundancy not as an error but as "the exuberance of the teaching" — the Buddha's willingness to name the same underlying relationship more than once, in slightly different words, for the benefit of listeners who might grasp one phrasing more readily than another. With all twenty-four conditions now defined in full, the chapter is finally ready to return to its original question: which of these twenty-four does ignorance actually use in conditioning the formations?

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
sampayuttapaccayo / vippayuttapaccayo

the association condition / the dissociation condition — sampayutta (yoked together) and vippayutta (unyoked); a matched pair describing, respectively, formless states sharing a single physical basis, object, arising, and ceasing, and material and formless states that do not share these.

desanāvilāsa

the exuberance of the teaching — desanā (teaching) + vilāsa (charm, exuberant display); Buddhaghosa's own term for the Buddha's habit of restating a single underlying point through multiple, slightly different technical formulations, explaining several near-duplicate entries on this list.

Returning to the Question: Which Conditions Does Ignorance Actually Use?

With all twenty-four conditions now on the table, Buddhaghosa finally answers the question posed several pages earlier: ignorance conditions meritorious formations in exactly two ways (as an object contemplated, and as a decisive support for one striving to escape it); it conditions demeritorious formations in a great many ways at once, since virtually the whole toolkit comes into play once lust and the other defilements are actually running; and it conditions the imperturbable, formless-sphere formations in exactly one way — decisive support alone.

Pali Source

Evamimesu catuvīsatiyā paccayesu ayaṃ avijjā, "paccayo hoti puññānaṃ, duvidhānekadhā pana; paresaṃ pacchimānaṃ sā, ekadhā paccayo matāti."

Tattha puññānaṃ duvidhāti ārammaṇapaccayena ca upanissayapaccayena cāti dvedhā paccayo hoti. Sā hi avijjaṃ khayato vayato sammasanakāle kāmāvacarānaṃ puññābhisaṅkhārānaṃ ārammaṇapaccayena paccayo hoti. Abhiññācittena samohacittaṃ jānanakāle rūpāvacarānaṃ. Avijjāsamatikkamatthāya pana dānādīni ceva kāmāvacarapuññakiriyavatthūni pūrentassa, rūpāvacarajjhānāni ca uppādentassa dvinnampi tesaṃ upanissayapaccayena paccayo hoti. Tathā avijjāsammūḷhattā kāmabhavarūpabhavasampattiyo patthetvā tāneva puññāni karontassa.

Anekadhā pana paresanti apuññābhisaṅkhārānaṃ anekadhā paccayo hoti. Kathaṃ? Esā hi avijjaṃ ārabbha rāgādīnaṃ uppajjanakāle ārammaṇapaccayena, garuṃkatvā assādanakāle ārammaṇādhipatiārammaṇūpanissayehi, avijjāsammūḷhassa anādīnavadassāvino pāṇātipātādīni karontassa upanissayapaccayena, dutiyajavanādīnaṃ anantarasamanantaraanantarūpanissayāsevananatthivigatapaccayehi, yaṃkiñci akusalaṃ karontassa hetu sahajāta aññamañña nissaya sampayutta atthi avigatapaccayehīti anekadhā paccayo hoti.

Pacchimānaṃ sā ekadhā paccayo matāti āneñjābhisaṅkhārānaṃ upanissayapaccayeneva ekadhā paccayo matā. So panassā upanissayabhāvo puññābhisaṅkhāre vuttanayeneva veditabboti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Thus, among these twenty-four conditions, this ignorance — "is a condition for meritorious formations in two ways, but for the others in many ways; for the last, it is held to be a condition in one way only."

Herein, "for meritorious formations, in two ways": it is a condition in two ways, by way of the object condition and by way of the decisive support condition. For it is a condition, by way of the object condition, for sense-sphere meritorious formations at the time when ignorance itself is being comprehended by way of its destruction and its fall; and for form-sphere meritorious formations at the time when a deluded state of mind is known by direct-knowledge consciousness.
