But for one who, for the purpose of transcending ignorance, fulfills such sense-sphere meritorious bases of action as giving, and gives rise to form-sphere jhānas, it is a condition, by way of the decisive support condition, for both of these — likewise for one who, being confounded by ignorance, aspires after the attainments of sense-sphere and form-sphere being, and performs precisely those same meritorious deeds for that confounded reason.

"But for the others, in many ways": it is a condition for demeritorious formations in many ways. How so? For it is a condition, by way of the object condition, at the time when lust and the rest arise taking ignorance itself as their object; by way of the object-predominance condition and decisive-support-by-object, at the time of relishing it, having given it weight; by way of the decisive support condition, for one confounded by ignorance and blind to its drawbacks who commits the killing of living beings and the like; by way of the contiguity, immediate contiguity, decisive-support-by-contiguity, repetition, absence, and disappearance conditions, for the second and subsequent moments of impulsion; and by way of the root, conascence, mutuality, dependence, association, presence, and non-disappearance conditions, for one performing any unwholesome deed whatsoever — thus it is a condition in many ways.

"For the last, it is held to be a condition in one way only": for the imperturbable formations, it is held to be a condition in one way only, by way of the decisive support condition alone. Its state of being a decisive support here should be understood in just the way already stated for the meritorious formations.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The asymmetry drawn out here is philosophically pointed: ignorance conditions unwholesome action almost promiscuously, through nearly every mechanism the Paṭṭhāna offers, because unwholesome states arise precisely by ignorance operating unchecked, moment after moment, feeding directly into what follows. But ignorance conditions wholesome and imperturbable formations only indirectly and at arm's length — either as the very thing being examined and seen through (the object condition, at the moment one comprehends ignorance's own impermanence), or as background motivation for someone still confused enough to seek a better rebirth rather than liberation itself, even while doing something genuinely virtuous. Ignorance never becomes a root, an object-predominance, or a conascent condition for good kamma the way it does for bad — it can only ever stand behind good kamma, at a distance, never inside it.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
khayato vayato sammasana

comprehending by way of destruction and fall — the specific insight-meditation mode in which ignorance itself becomes an object of contemplation, one of the two ways ignorance conditions meritorious formations.

javana

impulsion — the seven-fold run of "full" cognitive moments within a single cognitive process, the stage of consciousness where kammically active volition actually operates; several of the twenty-four conditions apply specifically to the second and later impulsion-moments in such a run.

One Cause, One Effect — or Many? The Sprout from Many Seeds

A natural objection follows: if other conditions besides ignorance clearly exist, how can the formula legitimately single out ignorance alone, as though it were the only cause? Buddhaghosa's answer draws a careful distinction — a genuinely singular effect never actually arises from a single cause, nor does anything arise from causes without becoming a rich, many-sided effect — while showing that the formula's habit of naming just "ignorance" and just "formations" is a deliberate teaching device, spotlighting whichever cause is principal, evident, or exclusive for a given audience, not a claim that no other causes are operating.

Pali Source

Etthāha – kiṃ panāyamekāva avijjā saṅkhārānaṃ paccayo, udāhu aññepi paccayā santīti? Kiṃ panettha, yadi tāva ekāva, ekakāraṇavādo āpajjati. Athaññepi santi, "avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā"ti ekakāraṇaniddeso nupapajjatīti? Na nupapajjati. Kasmā? Yasmā – "ekaṃ na ekato idha, nānekamanekatopi no ekaṃ; phalamatthi atthi pana eka-hetuphaladīpane attho."

Ekato hi kāraṇato na idha kiñci ekaṃ phalamatthi, na anekaṃ. Nāpi anekehi kāraṇehi ekaṃ. Anekehi pana kāraṇehi anekameva hoti. Tathā hi anekehi utupathavībījasalilasaṅkhātehi kāraṇehi anekameva rūpagandharasādikaṃ aṅkurasaṅkhātaṃ phalaṃ uppajjamānaṃ dissati. Yaṃ panetaṃ "avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā, saṅkhārapaccayā viññāṇa"nti ekekahetuphaladīpanaṃ kataṃ, tattha attho atthi, payojanaṃ vijjati.

Bhagavā hi katthaci padhānattā, katthaci pākaṭattā, katthaci asādhāraṇattā desanāvilāsassa ca veneyyānañca anurūpato ekameva hetuṃ vā phalaṃ vā dīpeti. "Phassapaccayā vedanā"ti hi padhānattā ekameva hetuphalamāha. Phasso hi vedanāya padhānahetu yathāphassaṃ vedanā vavatthānato. Vedanā ca phassassa padhānaphalaṃ yathāvedanaṃ phassavavatthānato. "Semhasamuṭṭhānā ābādhā"ti pākaṭattā ekaṃ hetumāha. Pākaṭo hi ettha semho, na kammādayo. "Ye keci, bhikkhave, akusalā dhammā, sabbe te ayonisomanasikāramūlakā"ti asādhāraṇattā ekaṃ hetumāha. Asādhāraṇo hi ayonisomanasikāro akusalānaṃ, sādhāraṇāni vatthārammaṇādīnīti. Tasmā ayamidha avijjā vijjamānesupi aññesu vatthārammaṇasahajātadhammādīsu saṅkhārakāraṇesu "assādānupassino taṇhā pavaḍḍhatī"ti ca "avijjāsamudayā āsavasamudayo"ti ca vacanato aññesampi taṇhādīnaṃ saṅkhārahetūnaṃ hetūti padhānattā, "avidvā, bhikkhave, avijjāgato puññābhisaṅkhārampi abhisaṅkharotī"ti pākaṭattā, asādhāraṇattā ca saṅkhārānaṃ hetubhāvena dīpitāti veditabbā. Eteneva ca ekekahetuphaladīpanaparihāravacanena sabbattha ekekahetuphaladīpane payojanaṃ veditabbanti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Here it might be asked: is this ignorance, then, all by itself, a condition for the formations — or are there other conditions as well? What of it, one might say: if it is only this one alone, the doctrine of a single cause would follow; but if there are others as well, then the statement of a single cause, "dependent on ignorance are formations," would not hold. Does it not hold? It does hold. Why? Because — "here it is not one from one, nor many from many, nor one from many; but there is an effect from many causes — yet there is also a point to showing a single cause and a single effect."

For here, no single effect comes from a single cause, nor does a manifold effect. Nor does a single effect come from manifold causes. But a manifold effect does indeed come from manifold causes. Thus, for instance, a manifold effect — the sprout, together with its color, its scent, and its taste — is seen to arise from the manifold causes called season, earth, seed, and moisture. As for this statement, "dependent on ignorance are formations; dependent on formations, consciousness," which shows a single cause and a single effect — there is a point to it; a purpose is served.

For the Blessed One shows a single cause or a single effect in some places because it is the principal one, in some places because it is the evident one, and in some places because it is the exclusive one, in accordance with the exuberance of his teaching and with what suits those to be guided. "Dependent on contact is feeling" states a single cause and a single effect because each is the principal one for the other: contact is the principal cause of feeling, since feeling is determined precisely according to the kind of contact; and feeling is the principal effect of contact, since contact is determined precisely according to the kind of feeling. "Ailments arise from phlegm" states a single cause because it is the evident one: here it is phlegm, not kamma and the rest, that is evident. "Whatever unwholesome states there are, bhikkhus, all of them are rooted in unwise attention" states a single cause because it is the exclusive one: for unwise attention is exclusive to unwholesome states, whereas such things as the physical basis and the object are common to wholesome and unwholesome states alike. Therefore, here, even though other causes of the formations do exist — the physical basis, the object, conascent states, and the rest — this ignorance is shown as the cause of the formations because it is the principal one among the other causes of the formations, such as craving, as stated in "for one who dwells contemplating gratification, craving increases" and "from the arising of the taints comes the arising of ignorance"; because it is the evident one, as in "bhikkhus, this person, gone into ignorance, produces even a meritorious formation"; and because it is the exclusive one. And by this same explanation, given as a way of resolving the apparent difficulty in showing a single cause and a single effect, the purpose of showing a single cause and a single effect should be understood everywhere else in the formula as well.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

This passage is one of the most quietly important in the whole chapter for how to read the twelve-factor formula correctly: it is a permanent standing warning against oversimplifying dependent origination into a single-cause chain. Every link in "dependent on ignorance are formations, dependent on formations, consciousness" is really shorthand for "among many genuinely operating conditions, this is the one worth naming here" — selected for being principal, evident, or exclusive to the case at hand, never because the others are absent. The season-earth-seed-moisture example makes the general principle vivid before it is applied to the specifically doctrinal cases.

The three criteria offered — principal, evident, and exclusive — are worth keeping distinct, since they answer different questions. "Principal" picks out whichever cause does the most causal work (contact is what actually determines what kind of feeling arises). "Evident" picks out whichever cause is most obviously present to observation, even if not doing the most causal work (phlegm is what a physician can see and point to, even though diet, kamma and other factors also contribute to illness). "Exclusive" picks out whichever cause is unique to this particular kind of effect and this kind alone (only unwise attention, among all the shared enabling conditions, is specific to unwholesome states).

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
padhāna / pākaṭa / asādhāraṇa

principal / evident / exclusive — the three reasons Buddhaghosa gives for why a teaching might spotlight a single cause among the several genuinely operating, resolving the apparent tension between "dependent on ignorance are formations" and the acknowledged existence of other conditions.

utupathavībījasalila

season, earth, seed, and moisture — the four ordinary agricultural causes Buddhaghosa cites as together producing one manifold effect (a sprout with its own color, scent, and taste), his chosen illustration of "many effects from many causes."

How Can Blameworthy Ignorance Condition Wholesome Kamma?

A sharper objection follows: since ignorance's result is uniformly undesirable and it is itself blameworthy, how can it possibly serve as a condition for meritorious and imperturbable formations at all — surely sugarcane cannot grow from a neem seed? Buddhaghosa's answer distinguishes several different ways a condition can relate to what it conditions — opposed or unopposed in place, function, and intrinsic nature; similar or dissimilar in kind — none of which requires the condition to resemble, in its own ethical quality, the state it helps produce.

Pali Source

Etthāha – evaṃ santepi ekantāniṭṭhaphalāya sāvajjāya avijjāya kathaṃ puññāneñjābhisaṅkhārapaccayattaṃ yujjati? Na hi nimbabījato ucchu uppajjatīti. Kathaṃ na yujjissati? Lokasmiñhi – "viruddho cāviruddho ca, sadisāsadiso tathā; dhammānaṃ paccayo siddho, vipākā eva te ca na."

Dhammānaṃ hi ṭhānasabhāvakiccādiviruddho cāviruddho ca paccayo loke siddho. Purimacittaṃ hi aparacittassa ṭhānaviruddho paccayo, purimasippādisikkhā ca pacchā pavattamānānaṃ sippādikiriyānaṃ. Kammaṃ rūpassa sabhāvaviruddho paccayo, khīrādīni ca dadhiādīnaṃ. Āloko cakkhuviññāṇassa kiccaviruddho, guḷādayo ca āsavādīnaṃ. Cakkhurūpādayo pana cakkhuviññāṇādīnaṃ ṭhānāviruddhā paccayā. Purimajavanādayo pacchimajavanādīnaṃ sabhāvāviruddhā kiccāviruddhā ca.

Yathā ca viruddhāviruddhā paccayā siddhā, evaṃ sadisāsadisāpi. Sadisameva hi utuāhārasaṅkhātaṃ rūpaṃ rūpassa paccayo, sālibījādīni ca sāliphalādīnaṃ. Asadisampi rūpaṃ arūpassa, arūpañca rūpassa paccayo hoti, golomāviloma-visāṇa-dadhitilapiṭṭhādīni ca dubbā-sarabhūtiṇakādīnaṃ. Yesañca dhammānaṃ te viruddhāviruddhasadisāsadisapaccayā, na te dhammā tesaṃ dhammānaṃ vipākā eva.

Iti ayaṃ avijjā vipākavasena ekantāniṭṭhaphalā, sabhāvavasena ca sāvajjāpi samānā sabbesampi etesaṃ puññābhisaṅkhārādīnaṃ yathānurūpaṃ ṭhānakiccasabhāvaviruddhāviruddhapaccayavasena, sadisāsadisapaccayavasena ca paccayo hotīti veditabbā.

Ru-Yi Translation

Here it might be asked: even so, how can ignorance — whose result is unfailingly undesirable, and which is itself blameworthy — be a condition for meritorious and imperturbable formations? For sugarcane does not grow from a neem seed. How can it not be so? For, in the world — "a condition for states is established as being both opposed and unopposed, and likewise both similar and dissimilar; but those states are not, on that account, the result of that condition."

For, in the world, a condition that is opposed to states, in respect of place, intrinsic nature, function, and the like, and one that is unopposed to them, are both established. A preceding state of consciousness is a condition, opposed in place, for a later state of consciousness; and so too is prior training in some craft or skill, for the craft-related activities carried out afterward. Kamma is a condition, opposed in intrinsic nature, for matter; and so are milk and the like, for curds and the like. Light is a condition, opposed in function, for eye-consciousness; and so are jaggery and the like, for the fermenting agents that turn them into liquor. But visible form and the eye and the rest are conditions unopposed in place for eye-consciousness and the rest; and preceding moments of impulsion and the rest are conditions unopposed in intrinsic nature and in function for succeeding moments of impulsion and the rest.

And just as opposed and unopposed conditions are established, so too are similar and dissimilar ones. For matter classed as season and nutriment is a similar condition for further matter, as are rice seeds and the like for rice grains and the like. Dissimilar matter, too, is a condition for the formless, and the formless for matter — as are cattle hair, sheep's wool, horn, curds, and sesame flour, for various kinds of grass such as dūrvā-grass and reed grass. And states for which these are conditions — opposed, unopposed, similar, and dissimilar — are not, on that account, simply the results of those states.

Thus this ignorance, though its result is unfailingly undesirable, and though it is itself blameworthy by its intrinsic nature, is nonetheless a condition — suitably, by way of conditions opposed and unopposed in place, function, and intrinsic nature, and by way of similar and dissimilar conditions — for all of these, the meritorious formations and the rest, as should be understood.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The decisive move here is the closing sentence's separation of "being a condition for" from "being the result reproduced in": a condition simply has to help another state come about or persist — it never has to share that state's own ethical flavor, or even resemble it in kind. Milk conditions curds despite being a completely different substance; light conditions eye-consciousness despite being physical while consciousness is mental; and, by the very same logic, blameworthy ignorance can condition a wholesome deed without the deed thereby inheriting ignorance's own blameworthiness. The neem-seed objection quietly assumes that causes must resemble their effects in kind or quality, and the whole point of this list of examples is to show that assumption is simply false — even in the everyday, non-doctrinal cases everyone already accepts.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
viruddha / aviruddha paccayo

an opposed / unopposed condition — viruddha (opposed, conflicting); Buddhaghosa's key distinction showing that a condition need not resemble, or even be compatible in kind with, the state it conditions — the technical basis for resolving the neem-seed objection.

nimbabīja

a neem seed — the objector's own image for an intrinsically bitter, "blameworthy" source that could never produce something as sweet and desirable as sugarcane; used here as the foil for the whole passage's argument.

The Blind Man's Wandering: One Further Approach

The section closes with an entirely different angle on the same question — not the mechanics of the twenty-four conditions this time, but a psychological portrait of confusion itself. Buddhaghosa shows exactly how someone confounded about five specific things (passing away, rebirth, the round of existence, the true characteristic of formations, and dependent origination itself) inevitably ends up producing all three kinds of formation, good, bad, and imperturbable alike — not because confusion aims at any of them in particular, but because a mind wandering blind, with no true guide, simply stumbles onto whichever path happens to be underfoot.

Pali Source

Apica ayaṃ aññopi pariyāyo – "cutūpapāte saṃsāre, saṅkhārānañca lakkhaṇe; yo paṭiccasamuppanna-dhammesu ca vimuyhati. Abhisaṅkharoti so ete, saṅkhāre tividhe yato; avijjā paccayo tesaṃ, tividhānampayaṃ tatoti."

Kathaṃ pana yo etesu vimuyhati, so tividhepete saṅkhāre karotīti ce. Cutiyā tāva vimūḷho "sabbattha khandhānaṃ bhedo maraṇa"nti cutiṃ agaṇhanto "satto marati, sattassa dehantarasaṅkamana"ntiādīni vikappeti.

Upapāte vimūḷho "sabbattha khandhānaṃ pātubhāvo jātī"ti upapātaṃ agaṇhanto "satto upapajjati, sattassa navasarīrapātubhāva"ntiādīni vikappeti.

Saṃsāre vimūḷho yo esa, "khandhānañca paṭipāṭi, dhātuāyatanāna ca; abbocchinnaṃ vattamānā, saṃsāroti pavuccatī"ti. Evaṃ vaṇṇito saṃsāro, taṃ evaṃ agaṇhanto "ayaṃ satto asmā lokā paraṃ lokaṃ gacchati, parasmā lokā imaṃ lokaṃ āgacchatī"tiādīni vikappeti.

Saṅkhārānaṃ lakkhaṇe vimūḷho saṅkhārānaṃ sabhāvalakkhaṇaṃ sāmaññalakkhaṇañca agaṇhanto saṅkhāre attato attaniyato dhuvato sukhato subhato vikappeti.

Paṭiccasamuppannadhammesu vimūḷho avijjādīhi saṅkhārādīnaṃ pavattiṃ agaṇhanto "attā jānāti vā na jānāti vā, so eva karoti ca kāreti ca. So paṭisandhiyaṃ upapajjati, tassa aṇuissarādayo kalalādibhāvena sarīraṃ saṇṭhapento indriyāni sampādenti. So indriyasampanno phusati, vediyati, taṇhīyati, upādiyati, ghaṭiyati. So puna bhavantare bhavatī"ti vā, "sabbe sattā niyatisaṅgatibhāvapariṇatā"ti vā vikappeti.

So avijjāya andhīkato evaṃ vikappento yathā nāma andho pathaviyaṃ vicaranto maggampi amaggampi thalampi ninnampi samampi visamampi paṭipajjati, evaṃ puññampi apuññampi āneñjābhisaṅkhārampi abhisaṅkharotīti.

Tenetaṃ vuccati – "yathāpi nāma jaccandho, naro apariṇāyako; ekadā yāti maggena, ummaggenāpi ekadā. Saṃsāre saṃsaraṃ bālo, tathā apariṇāyako; karoti ekadā puññaṃ, apuññamapi ekadā. Yadā ca ñatvā so dhammaṃ, saccāni abhisamessati; tadā avijjūpasamā, upasanto carissatī"ti.

Ayaṃ "avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā"ti padasmiṃ vitthārakathā.

Ru-Yi Translation

There is, moreover, this further approach — "whoever is confounded regarding passing away and rebirth, regarding the round, regarding the characteristic of formations, and regarding dependently arisen states, fashions, precisely on that account, these three kinds of formations; ignorance is their condition — and so it is a condition for these three kinds as well."

But how does one who is confounded regarding these things thereby produce these three kinds of formations? First, one confounded regarding passing away, not grasping passing away as it truly is — "in every case, the breaking up of the aggregates is death" — imagines instead: "a being dies," "a being's crossing over into another body," and the like.

One confounded regarding rebirth, not grasping rebirth as it truly is — "in every case, the appearance of the aggregates is birth" — imagines instead: "a being is reborn," "a being's appearance in a new body," and the like.

One confounded regarding the round — which is thus described: "the unbroken sequential occurrence of the aggregates, elements, and sense bases is called the round" — not grasping the round as so described, imagines instead: "this being goes from this world to another world, and comes from another world to this world," and the like.

One confounded regarding the characteristic of formations, not grasping the intrinsic characteristic and the general characteristic of formations, imagines the formations as self, as belonging to a self, as permanent, as pleasurable, as beautiful.

One confounded regarding dependently arisen states, not grasping the occurrence of the formations and the rest through ignorance and the other conditions, imagines instead: either that "a self knows or does not know; that very self acts and causes to act; it is reborn at rebirth-linking; for it, subtle sovereign forces and the like establish the body in the form of the embryonic clot and the rest, and bring the faculties to completion; endowed with faculties, it touches, feels, thirsts, clings, and strives; it comes to be again in another state of being" — or that "all beings are wholly determined by fate and circumstance."

Blinded by ignorance, imagining in this way, he fashions merit, demerit, or the imperturbable formation, just as a blind man, wandering over the earth, sometimes takes the right path and sometimes the wrong one, sometimes level ground and sometimes rough, sometimes even ground and sometimes uneven.

Hence it is said — "just as a man, blind from birth, without a guide, at one time walks by the right path, and at another by the wrong one — so too the fool, wandering through the round of existence, without a guide, at one time performs merit, and at another, demerit as well. But when he has known the Dhamma and awakened to the truths, then, with the calming of ignorance, he will fare having become calmed."

This is the detailed discussion of the phrase "dependent on ignorance are formations."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

This closing simile reframes the entire discussion in a way worth sitting with: ignorance does not condition the formations by pushing toward any particular one of the three — merit, demerit, or the imperturbable formation — the way an archer aims at a chosen target. It conditions all three indifferently, the way blindness itself is indifferent to which path a wandering man happens to stumble onto. A blind man walking without a guide might, by sheer chance, walk the smooth and level road as easily as the rough one; what makes him blind is not a preference for rough roads, but the simple absence of sight that would let him choose reliably at all. In just this way, a person confounded by ignorance about the five points named here — death, rebirth, the round, the formations' own nature, and dependent conditioning itself — ends up producing wholesome, unwholesome, or formless-sphere kamma more or less at random with respect to their actual value, since none of these actions is being chosen with genuine insight into what it truly is or where it truly leads.

The five items confusion is said to concern are also worth reading as a compact preview of the chapter's remaining structure: the round, the true characteristic of formations, and dependent origination itself have already received extensive treatment; passing away and rebirth specifically will be developed much further as the chapter proceeds through consciousness's role in the rebirth-linking process, in the section immediately following this one. This closes the detailed discussion of "dependent on ignorance are formations" — the chapter now turns, at last, to the second link: "dependent on formations, consciousness."

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
jaccandho apariṇāyako

a man blind from birth, without a guide — jacca (by birth) + andha (blind) + a- (without) + pariṇāyaka (a guide, leader); the chapter's closing image for the worldling wandering saṃsāra, producing good and bad kamma indifferently for lack of any guiding insight.

niyatisaṅgatibhāva

the state of being determined by fate and circumstance — niyati (fate, fixed destiny) + saṅgati (chance conjunction); one of the two named wrong views about dependently arisen states, here representing rigid determinism as opposed to the equally wrong "independent self" view named alongside it.

Thirty-Two Kinds of Resultant Consciousness

The chapter turns to its second link — "dependent on formations, consciousness" — with a full catalogue of every kind of resultant consciousness the three formations can produce: thirty-two in total, sorted precisely by which formation produces which. The meritorious formation alone accounts for twenty-one of these (sixteen sense-sphere, five form-sphere); the demeritorious formation for seven; and the imperturbable formation for the remaining four, formless-sphere states.

Pali Source

Saṅkhārapaccayā viññāṇapade – viññāṇanti cakkhuviññāṇādi chabbidhaṃ. Tattha cakkhuviññāṇaṃ kusalavipākaṃ akusalavipākanti duvidhaṃ hoti. Tathā sotaghānajivhākaāyaviññāṇāni. Manoviññāṇaṃ kusalākusalavipākā dve manodhātuyo, tisso ahetukamanoviññāṇadhātuyo, aṭṭha sahetukāni kāmāvacaravipākacittāni, pañca rūpāvacarāni, cattāri arūpāvacarānīti bāvīsatividhaṃ hoti. Iti imehi chahi viññāṇehi sabbānipi bāttiṃsa lokiyavipākaviññāṇāni saṅgahitāni honti. Lokuttarāni pana vaṭṭakathāya na yujjantīti na gahitāni.

Tattha siyā "kathaṃ panetaṃ jānitabbaṃ idaṃ vuttappakāraṃ viññāṇaṃ saṅkhārapaccayā hotī"ti? Upacitakammābhāve vipākābhāvato. Vipākaṃ hetaṃ, vipākañca na upacitakammābhāve uppajjati. Yadi uppajjeyya sabbesaṃ sabbavipākāni uppajjeyyuṃ, na ca uppajjantīti jānitabbametaṃ saṅkhārapaccayā idaṃ viññāṇaṃ hotīti.

Katarasaṅkhārapaccayā kataraṃ viññāṇanti ce. Kāmāvacarapuññābhisaṅkhārapaccayā tāva kusalavipākāni pañca cakkhuviññāṇādīni, manoviññāṇe ekā manodhātu, dve manoviññāṇadhātuyo, aṭṭha kāmāvacaramahāvipākānīti soḷasa. Yathāha – "kāmāvacarassa kusalassa kammassa kaṭattā upacitattā vipākaṃ cakkhuviññāṇaṃ uppannaṃ hoti… sota… ghāna… jivhā… kāyaviññāṇaṃ… vipākā manodhātu uppannā hoti. Somanassasahagatā manoviññāṇadhātu uppannā hoti. Upekkhāsahagatā manoviññāṇadhātu uppannā hoti. Somanassasahagatā ñāṇasampayuttā. Somanassasahagatā ñāṇasampayuttā sasaṅkhārena. Somanassasahagatā ñāṇavippayuttā. Somanassasahagatā ñāṇavippayuttā sasaṅkhārena. Upekkhāsahagatā ñāṇasampayuttā. Upekkhāsahagatā ñāṇasampayuttā sasaṅkhārena. Upekkhāsahagatā ñāṇavippayuttā. Upekkhāsahagatā ñāṇavippayuttā sasaṅkhārenā"ti.

Rūpāvacarapuññābhisaṅkhārapaccayā pana pañca rūpāvacaravipākāni. Yathāha – "tasseva rūpāvacarassa kusalassa kammassa kaṭattā upacitattā vipākaṃ vivicceva kāmehi paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ…pe… pañcamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharatī"ti. Evaṃ puññābhisaṅkhārapaccayā ekavīsatividhaṃ viññāṇaṃ hoti.

Apuññābhisaṅkhārapaccayā pana akusalavipākāni pañca cakkhuviññāṇādīni, ekā manodhātu, ekā manoviññāṇadhātūti evaṃ sattavidhaṃ viññāṇaṃ hoti. Yathāha – "akusalassa kammassa kaṭattā upacitattā vipākaṃ cakkhuviññāṇaṃ uppannaṃ hoti… sota… ghāna… jivhā… kāyaviññāṇaṃ… vipākā manodhātu vipākā manoviññāṇadhātu uppannā hotī"ti.

Āneñjābhisaṅkhārapaccayā pana cattāri arūpavipākānīti evaṃ catubbidhaṃ viññāṇaṃ hoti. Yathāha – "tasseva arūpāvacarassa kusalassa kammassa kaṭattā upacitattā vipākaṃ sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā ākāsānañcāyatanasaññāsahagataṃ…pe… viññāṇañcā…pe… ākiñcaññā…pe… nevasaññānāsaññāyatanasahagataṃ sukhassa ca dukkhassa ca pahānā catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharatī"ti.

Ru-Yi Translation

In the section "dependent on formations, consciousness": "consciousness" is sixfold — eye-consciousness and the rest. Herein, eye-consciousness is of two kinds, wholesome-resultant and unwholesome-resultant; likewise ear-, nose-, tongue-, and body-consciousness. Mind-consciousness is of twenty-two kinds: the two mind-elements that are wholesome- and unwholesome-resultant, the three rootless mind-consciousness-elements, the eight sense-sphere resultant states of consciousness with roots, the five form-sphere, and the four formless-sphere. Thus, by these six kinds of consciousness, all thirty-two mundane resultant states of consciousness are included. The supramundane ones, however, are not taken, since they do not fit within the discourse on the round.

Here it might be asked: how, then, is this to be known — that consciousness of the kind just described is conditioned by formations? By the absence of the result when accumulated kamma is absent. For this is a result, and a result does not arise when accumulated kamma is absent; if it could arise without it, then every result would arise for everyone — yet they do not so arise. Thus it should be known that this consciousness is conditioned by formations.

If it is asked which formation conditions which consciousness: conditioned by the sense-sphere meritorious formation, first, are the five wholesome-resultant states — eye-consciousness and the rest — together with, in the mind-consciousness category, one mind-element, two mind-consciousness-elements, and the eight sense-sphere great resultant states — sixteen in all. As it is said: "through the having-been-done and having-been-accumulated of sense-sphere wholesome kamma, resultant eye-consciousness arises … ear- … nose- … tongue- … body-consciousness … resultant mind-element arises. Mind-consciousness-element accompanied by joy arises. Mind-consciousness-element accompanied by equanimity arises. Accompanied by joy, associated with knowledge; accompanied by joy, associated with knowledge, with prompting; accompanied by joy, dissociated from knowledge; accompanied by joy, dissociated from knowledge, with prompting; accompanied by equanimity, associated with knowledge; accompanied by equanimity, associated with knowledge, with prompting; accompanied by equanimity, dissociated from knowledge; accompanied by equanimity, dissociated from knowledge, with prompting."

But conditioned by the form-sphere meritorious formation are the five form-sphere resultant states. As it is said: "through the having-been-done and having-been-accumulated of that same form-sphere wholesome kamma, one enters and dwells in the resultant first jhāna, quite secluded from sense pleasures … up to the fifth jhāna." Thus, conditioned by the meritorious formation, consciousness is of twenty-one kinds.

But conditioned by the demeritorious formation are the five unwholesome-resultant states — eye-consciousness and the rest — together with one mind-element and one mind-consciousness-element: consciousness of seven kinds in all. As it is said: "through the having-been-done and having-been-accumulated of unwholesome kamma, resultant eye-consciousness arises … ear- … nose- … tongue- … body-consciousness … resultant mind-element arises, resultant mind-consciousness-element arises."

But conditioned by the imperturbable formation are the four formless-resultant states: consciousness of four kinds. As it is said: "through the having-been-done and having-been-accumulated of that same formless-sphere wholesome kamma, one enters and dwells in the resultant attainment accompanied by the perception of the base of infinite space, through the complete transcending of perceptions of form … the base of infinite consciousness … the base of nothingness … one enters and dwells in the fourth jhāna, accompanied by the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, through the abandoning of pleasure and pain."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

This catalogue is drawn directly from the Dhammasaṅgaṇī's own opening enumeration of resultant states, reproduced here to establish a complete inventory before the chapter goes on to explain how these thirty-two states actually operate — some only in the ordinary business of seeing, hearing, and thinking through a life already begun, others specifically at the pivotal moment of rebirth-linking that opens a new life. The verification method offered — that results simply do not appear without their generating kamma, since otherwise everyone would experience every result indiscriminately — is a plain empirical test rather than an appeal to authority: kamma's reality as a condition is shown by what would follow if it were not real.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
lokiyavipākaviññāṇa

mundane resultant consciousness — lokiya (mundane) + vipāka (result) + viññāṇa (consciousness); the thirty-two states catalogued here, deliberately excluding the supramundane path-and-fruit consciousnesses, which fall outside the round of rebirth this chapter is describing.

sasaṅkhārena / asaṅkhārena

with prompting / without prompting — a standard Abhidhamma distinction (implicit in the unprompted forms listed first) between a mental state arising spontaneously and one arising only after deliberate inducement, applied throughout the eight great sense-sphere resultant states.

The Cognitive Process During Ordinary Life

Buddhaghosa now explains how these thirty-two states of consciousness actually operate, sorting them first into thirteen that occur only during the ordinary "course of life" and nineteen that can also occur at rebirth-linking. The thirteen get a detailed walk-through of the standard cognitive process — sense-consciousness, then receiving, then investigating — the basic machinery by which any single act of seeing, hearing, or otherwise sensing actually unfolds moment by moment.

Pali Source

Evaṃ yaṃ saṅkhārapaccayā yaṃ viññāṇaṃ hoti, taṃ ñatvā idānissa evaṃ pavatti veditabbā – sabbameva hi idaṃ pavattipaṭisandhivasena dvedhā pavattati. Tattha dve pañcaviññāṇāni, dve manodhātuyo, somanassasahagatā ahetukamanoviññāṇadhātūti imāni terasa pañcavokārabhave pavattiyaññeva pavattanti. Sesāni ekūnavīsati tīsu bhavesu yathānurūpaṃ pavattiyampi paṭisandhiyampi pavattanti.

Kathaṃ? Kusalavipākāni tāva cakkhuviññāṇādīni pañca kusalavipākena akusalavipākena vā nibbattassa yathākkamaṃ paripākaṃ upagatindriyassa cakkhādīnaṃ āpāthagataṃ iṭṭhaṃ iṭṭhamajjhattaṃ vā rūpādiārammaṇaṃ ārabbha cakkhādipasādaṃ nissāya dassanasavanaghāyanasāyanaphusanakiccaṃ sādhayamānāni pavattanti. Tathā akusalavipākāni pañca. Kevalañhi tesaṃ aniṭṭhaṃ aniṭṭhamajjhattaṃ vā ārammaṇaṃ hoti. Ayameva viseso. Dasapi cetāni niyatadvārārammaṇavatthuṭṭhānāni niyatakiccāneva ca bhavanti.

Tato kusalavipākānaṃ cakkhuviññāṇādīnaṃ anantarā kusalavipākā manodhātu tesaṃyeva ārammaṇaṃ ārabbha hadayavatthuṃ nissāya sampaṭicchanakiccaṃ sādhayamānā pavattati. Tathā akusalavipākānaṃ anantarā akusalavipākā. Idañca pana dvayaṃ aniyatadvārārammaṇaṃ niyatavatthuṭṭhānaṃ niyatakiccañca hoti.

Somanassasahagatā pana ahetukamanoviññāṇadhātu kusalavipākamanodhātuyā anantarā tassā eva ārammaṇaṃ ārabbha hadayavatthuṃ nissāya santīraṇakiccaṃ sādhayamānā chasu dvāresu balavārammaṇe kāmāvacarasattānaṃ yebhuyyena lobhasampayuttajavanāvasāne bhavaṅgavīthiṃ pacchinditvā javanena gahitārammaṇe tadārammaṇavasena ca sakiṃ vā dvikkhattuṃ vā pavattatīti majjhimaṭṭhakathāyaṃ vuttaṃ. Abhidhammaṭṭhakathāyaṃ pana tadārammaṇe dve cittavārā āgatā. Idaṃ pana cittaṃ tadārammaṇanti ca piṭṭhibhavaṅganti cāti dve nāmāni labhati. Aniyatadvārārammaṇaṃ niyatavatthukaṃ aniyataṭṭhānakiccañca hotīti. Evaṃ tāva terasa pañcavokārabhave pavattiyaññeva pavattantīti veditabbāni.

Ru-Yi Translation

Having thus known which consciousness is conditioned by which formation, its occurrence should now be understood as follows. All of this occurs in two ways: by way of the course of life, and by way of rebirth-linking. Herein, the two sets of fivefold consciousness, the two mind-elements, and the rootless mind-consciousness-element accompanied by joy — these thirteen occur only in the course of life, within the five-constituent mode of being. The remaining nineteen occur, as suits each case, both in the course of life and at rebirth-linking, within the three kinds of being.

How so? First, the five wholesome-resultant states — eye-consciousness and the rest — occur, taking as their object a desirable or a neutral-desirable visible form or other object that has come into range of the eye and the other faculties of one born through a wholesome or unwholesome result, whose faculties have reached maturity in due course, accomplishing, in dependence on the sensitivity of the eye and the rest, the function of seeing, hearing, smelling, tasting, and touching. Likewise the five unwholesome-resultant states — except that their object is, without exception, an undesirable or a neutral-undesirable one; this is the only difference. And these ten, too, have a fixed door, object, physical basis, and place, and a fixed function as well.

After that, immediately following the wholesome-resultant eye-consciousness and the rest, the wholesome-resultant mind-element occurs, taking that same object, accomplishing, in dependence on the heart-basis, the function of receiving. Likewise, immediately following the unwholesome-resultant sense-consciousnesses, the unwholesome-resultant mind-element occurs. This pair has a variable door and object, but a fixed physical basis, place, and function.

But the rootless mind-consciousness-element accompanied by joy, occurring immediately after the wholesome-resultant mind-element, taking that same object, accomplishing, in dependence on the heart-basis, the function of investigating — it is stated in the Majjhima commentary that, with respect to a powerful object in any of the six doors, for sense-sphere beings, it occurs once or twice, by way of registration, upon an object taken by the impulsion at the end of a run of impulsions mostly associated with greed, having cut off the course of the life-continuum. In the Abhidhamma commentary, however, two rounds of consciousness are given for registration. This consciousness, moreover, receives two names: "registration" and "the life-continuum behind the impulsions." It has a variable door and object, a fixed physical basis, and a variable place and function. Thus, so far, these thirteen should be understood to occur only in the course of life, within the five-constituent mode of being.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

This passage is quietly laying out, function by function, what later Abhidhamma manuals systematize as the full "cognitive process" (vīthi): a sense-door consciousness sees, hears, or otherwise senses; an immediately following mind-element simply receives what was sensed; and a rootless mind-consciousness-element then investigates it — three distinct, minimal functions performed by three distinct, minimal moments of consciousness, before the mind ever gets to interpreting, liking, disliking, or acting on what it perceived. The two technical names given to the final moment — "registration" and "the life-continuum behind the impulsions" — capture its double role: it can either register a especially powerful object right after a run of impulsions has already responded to it, or it can simply be a plain resumption of the passive background life-continuum stream, depending on the strength of what was sensed.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
sampaṭicchana / santīraṇa

receiving / investigating — the second and third functions in the standard cognitive process, performed respectively by the resultant mind-element and the rootless mind-consciousness-element, immediately following the initial sense-consciousness.

tadārammaṇa / bhavaṅga

registration / life-continuum — tad- (that) + ārammaṇa (object), and bhava (becoming) + aṅga (factor, limb); two names for one and the same function, describing consciousness either registering a powerful object after a run of impulsions or simply continuing the passive background stream between active cognitive events.

The Remaining Consciousnesses: Functions Across a Whole Lifetime

The other nineteen kinds of consciousness — those capable of occurring at rebirth-linking as well as during life — pick up additional functions once a life is already underway: they can serve as the passive "life-continuum" between active cognitive events, and, at the very end of that life, as the single moment of "decease" consciousness that closes it.

Pali Source

Sesesu ekūnavīsatiyā na kiñci attano anurūpāya paṭisandhiyā na pavattati. Pavattiyaṃ pana kusalākusalavipākā tāva dve ahetukamanoviññāṇadhātuyo pañcadvāre kusalākusalavipākamanodhātūnaṃ anantarā santīraṇakiccaṃ, chasu dvāresu pubbe vuttanayeneva tadārammaṇakiccaṃ, attanā dinnapaṭisandhito uddhaṃ asati bhavaṅgupacchedake cittuppāde bhavaṅgakiccaṃ, ante cutikiccañcāti cattāri kiccāni sādhayamānā niyatavatthukā aniyatadvārārammaṇaṭṭhānakiccā hutvā pavattanti.

Aṭṭha kāmāvacarasahetukacittāni vuttanayeneva chasu dvāresu tadārammaṇakiccaṃ, attanā dinnapaṭisandhito uddhaṃ asati bhavaṅgupacchedake cittuppāde bhavaṅgakiccaṃ, ante cutikiccañcāti tīṇi kiccāni sādhayamānāni niyatavatthukāni aniyatadvārārammaṇaṭṭhānakiccāni hutvā pavattanti.

Pañca rūpāvacarāni cattāri ca āruppāni attanā dinnapaṭisandhito uddhaṃ asati bhavaṅgupacchedake cittuppāde bhavaṅgakiccaṃ, ante cutikiccañcāti kiccadvayaṃ sādhayamānāni pavattanti. Tesu rūpāvacarāni niyatavatthārammaṇāni aniyataṭṭhānakiccāni, itarāni niyatavatthukāni niyatārammaṇāni aniyataṭṭhānakiccāni hutvā pavattantīti evaṃ tāva bāttiṃsavidhampi viññāṇaṃ pavattiyaṃ saṅkhārapaccayā pavattati. Tatrāssa te te saṅkhārā kammapaccayena ca upanissayapaccayena ca paccayā honti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Among the remaining nineteen, there is none that does not occur, in due course, at its own suitable rebirth-linking. But in the course of life, first, the two wholesome- and unwholesome-resultant rootless mind-consciousness-elements, occurring immediately after the wholesome- and unwholesome-resultant mind-elements in the five sense-doors, accomplish the function of registering; in all six doors, in the manner already stated, the function of registration; and, after the rebirth-linking they themselves have given, in the absence of any arising of consciousness that would cut off the life-continuum, the function of the life-continuum; and, at the end, the function of decease — thus, accomplishing four functions, they occur with a fixed physical basis but a variable door, object, place, and function.

The eight sense-sphere states of consciousness with roots, in the manner already stated, accomplish, in the six doors, the function of registration; after the rebirth-linking they themselves have given, in the absence of any consciousness that would cut off the life-continuum, the function of the life-continuum; and, at the end, the function of decease — thus, accomplishing three functions, they occur with a fixed physical basis but a variable door, object, place, and function.

The five form-sphere and four formless attainments occur, after the rebirth-linking they themselves have given, accomplishing, in the absence of any consciousness that would cut off the life-continuum, the function of the life-continuum, and, at the end, the function of decease — two functions. Among these, the form-sphere states occur with a fixed physical basis and object but a variable place and function; the others occur with a fixed physical basis, a fixed object, but a variable place and function. Thus, so far, all thirty-two kinds of consciousness occur, in the course of life, conditioned by the formations. And here, the various formations are their conditions by way of both the kamma condition and the decisive support condition.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The pattern that emerges across these three groups traces a clear hierarchy of stability: the rootless states can perform the widest range of functions (four) but with the least fixed circumstances, since they operate across all six sense-doors; the eight sense-sphere states with roots do slightly less (three functions) with slightly more fixity; and the jhāna-attainment consciousnesses, most refined and stable of all, do the least in terms of variety (only two functions — life-continuum and decease) precisely because they are so fixed to a single, unchanging object throughout an entire lifetime once that jhāna-consciousness becomes one's rebirth-linking and life-continuum. The more refined the consciousness, the narrower and more constant its object, but the fewer distinct jobs it needs to do.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
bhavaṅgupacchedaka

that which cuts off the life-continuum — bhavaṅga (life-continuum) + upacchedaka (cutting off); any active moment of cognitive-process consciousness, contrasted with the passive life-continuum it interrupts whenever a new object is encountered.

cutikicca

the function of decease — cuti (falling away, death) + kicca (function, duty); the very last moment of consciousness in a given lifetime, always performed by the same type of consciousness that opened that same life as rebirth-linking.

Nineteen Rebirth-Linking Consciousnesses, Twenty Rebirth-Linkings

A methodological aside now unpacks the compressed claim that all nineteen remaining consciousnesses occur at rebirth-linking, walking through exactly how many rebirth-linkings exist, which consciousness performs each, and what object each one takes — setting up the extended, vivid case studies of actual death-and-rebirth scenarios that follow.

Pali Source

Yaṃ pana vuttaṃ "sesesu ekūnavīsatiyā na kiñci attano anurūpāya paṭisandhiyā na pavattatī"ti, taṃ atisaṃkhittattā dubbijānaṃ. Tenassa vitthāranayadassanatthaṃ vuccati – kati paṭisandhiyo, kati paṭisandhicittāni, kena kattha paṭisandhi hoti, kiṃ paṭisandhiyā ārammaṇanti?

Asaññapaṭisandhiyā saddhiṃ vīsati paṭisandhiyo. Vuttappakārāneva ekūnavīsati paṭisandhicittāni. Tattha akusalavipākāya ahetukamanoviññāṇadhātuyā apāyesu paṭisandhi hoti. Kusalavipākāya manussaloke jaccandhajātibadhirajātiummattakajātieḷamūganapuṃsakādīnaṃ. Aṭṭhahi sahetukakāmāvacaravipākehi kāmāvacaradevesu ceva manussesu ca puññavantānaṃ paṭisandhi hoti. Pañcahi rūpāvacaravipākehi rūpībrahmaloke. Catūhi arūpāvacaravipākehi arūpaloketi. Yena ca yattha paṭisandhi hoti, sā eva tassa anurūpā paṭisandhi nāma. Saṅkhepato pana paṭisandhiyā tīṇi ārammaṇāni honti atītaṃ paccuppannaṃ navattabbañca. Asaññā paṭisandhi anārammaṇāti.

Tattha viññāṇañcāyatananevasaññānāsaññāyatanapaṭisandhīnaṃ atītameva ārammaṇaṃ. Dasannaṃ kāmāvacarānaṃ atītaṃ vā paccuppannaṃ vā. Sesānaṃ navattabbameva. Evaṃ tīsu ārammaṇesu pavattamānā pana paṭisandhi yasmā atītārammaṇassa vā navattabbārammaṇassa vā cuticittassa anantarameva pavattati. Paccuppannārammaṇaṃ pana cuticittaṃ nāma natthi. Tasmā dvīsu ārammaṇesu aññatarārammaṇāya cutiyā anantarā tīsu ārammaṇesu aññatarārammaṇāya paṭisandhiyā sugatiduggativasena pavattanākāro veditabbo.

Ru-Yi Translation

But the statement "among the remaining nineteen, there is none that does not occur, in due course, at its own suitable rebirth-linking" is difficult to understand, being stated so briefly. Therefore, in order to show it in detail, it is asked: how many rebirth-linkings are there, how many rebirth-linking states of consciousness, by what and where does rebirth-linking occur, and what is the object of rebirth-linking?

Together with rebirth-linking among the non-percipient beings, there are twenty rebirth-linkings. The rebirth-linking states of consciousness, of the kind already stated, are nineteen. Herein: rebirth-linking in the states of misery occurs by way of the unwholesome-resultant rootless mind-consciousness-element. Rebirth-linking as one born blind, deaf, mad, dumb, or a eunuch, and the like, in the human world, occurs by way of the wholesome-resultant rootless mind-consciousness-element. Rebirth-linking for the meritorious, among sense-sphere gods and among human beings, occurs by way of the eight sense-sphere resultant states with roots. Rebirth-linking in the form-sphere Brahmā world occurs by way of the five form-sphere resultant states. Rebirth-linking in the formless world occurs by way of the four formless-resultant states. And whichever rebirth-linking occurs by whichever consciousness and in whichever place — that alone is called the rebirth-linking suitable to that case. In brief, rebirth-linking has three kinds of object: past, present, and "not-so-classifiable." Non-percipient rebirth-linking has no object at all.

Herein, rebirth-linking into the base of infinite consciousness and into the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception has only a past object. That of the ten sense-sphere kinds has either a past or a present object. That of the rest has only a "not-so-classifiable" object. Now, since rebirth-linking, occurring with respect to these three kinds of object, occurs only immediately after a decease-consciousness whose object was either past or "not-so-classifiable" — for there is no such thing as a decease-consciousness with a present object — therefore the manner in which rebirth-linking, with one or another of three kinds of object, occurs immediately after decease, with one or another of two kinds of object, by way of a good or a bad destination, should be understood.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The one extra rebirth-linking beyond the nineteen consciousnesses — bringing the total to twenty — belongs to the non-percipient beings, a class of form-sphere beings who arise with matter alone and no consciousness whatsoever; their "rebirth-linking" is therefore a rebirth-linking without any rebirth-linking consciousness at all, purely a matter of matter arising through kamma, which is why it needs to be tallied separately and why it alone takes no object.

The three possible objects of rebirth-linking consciousness — past, present, and the curious "not-so-classifiable" — map directly onto what kind of thing the dying mind was actually attending to just before death: a kamma or vivid mental sign of kamma (a past event, remembered or re-experienced), an actual currently-present sense object (something relatives arrange for a dying person to see, hear, or smell), or a whole future rebirth-destination or jhāna-attainment itself as an object (which the tradition treats as neither strictly past nor strictly present, hence "not-so-classifiable"). This groundwork is what makes the elaborate death-scene narratives that follow immediately intelligible.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
asaññapaṭisandhi

rebirth-linking among the non-percipient beings — a + saññā (without perception) + paṭisandhi (rebirth-linking); a unique twentieth category of rebirth-linking involving matter alone, with no consciousness and therefore no object whatsoever.

navattabba

"not-so-classifiable" — na (not) + vattabba (to be said, to be classified); the technical label for an object that is neither straightforwardly past nor present, applied to a future destination or a jhāna-attainment taken as the object of certain kinds of rebirth-linking consciousness.

Dying Well, Reborn Badly: Three Scenarios

Buddhaghosa now walks through, moment by moment, exactly what happens in the mind of someone dying, using the standard framework of three possible near-death objects — remembered kamma itself, a vivid mental "sign" of that kamma, or a sign of the destination about to be entered — and the precise sequence of mental events (life-continuum, adverting, the run of impulsions, registration, decease, and finally rebirth-linking) that carries a dying person from one life into the next. The first three scenarios all describe someone who had lived virtuously falling, through some late evil deed or its lingering trace, into a bad rebirth.

Pali Source

Seyyathidaṃ – kāmāvacarasugatiyaṃ tāva ṭhitassa pāpakammino puggalassa "tānissa tasmiṃ samaye olambantī"tiādivacanato maraṇamañce nipannassa yathūpacitaṃ pāpakammaṃ vā kammanimittaṃ vā manodvāre āpāthamāgacchati. Taṃ ārabbha uppannāya tadārammaṇapariyosānāya javanavīthiyā anantaraṃ bhavaṅgavisayaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā cuticittaṃ uppajjati. Tasmiṃ niruddhe tadeva āpāthagataṃ kammaṃ vā kammanimittaṃ vā ārabbha anupacchinnakilesabalavināmitaṃ duggatipariyāpannaṃ paṭisandhicittaṃ uppajjati. Ayaṃ atītārammaṇāya cutiyā anantarā atītārammaṇā paṭisandhi.

Aparassa maraṇasamaye vuttappakārakammavasena narakādīsu aggijālavaṇṇādikaṃ duggatinimittaṃ manodvāre āpāthamāgacchati, tassa dvikkhattuṃ bhavaṅge uppajjitvā niruddhe taṃ ārammaṇaṃ ārabbha ekaṃ āvajjanaṃ, maraṇassa āsannabhāvena mandībhūtavegattā pañca javanāni, dve tadārammaṇānīti tīṇi vīthicittāni uppajjanti. Tato bhavaṅgavisayaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā ekaṃ cuticittaṃ. Ettāvatā ekādasa cittakkhaṇā atītā honti. Athassa avasesapañcacittakkhaṇāyuke tasmiññeva ārammaṇe paṭisandhicittaṃ uppajjati. Ayaṃ atītārammaṇāya cutiyā anantarā paccuppannārammaṇā paṭisandhi.

Aparassa maraṇasamaye pañcannaṃ dvārānaṃ aññatarasmiṃ rāgādihetubhūtaṃ hīnamārammaṇaṃ āpāthamāgacchati. Tassa yathākkamena uppanne voṭṭhabbanāvasāne maraṇassa āsannabhāvena mandībhūtavegattā pañca javanāni, dve tadārammaṇāni ca uppajjanti. Tato bhavaṅgavisayaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā ekaṃ cuticittaṃ. Ettāvatā ca dve bhavaṅgāni, āvajjanaṃ, dassanaṃ, sampaṭicchanaṃ, santīraṇaṃ, voṭṭhabbanaṃ, pañca javanāni, dve tadārammaṇāni, ekaṃ cuticittanti pañcadasa cittakkhaṇā atītā honti. Athāvasesaekacittakkhaṇāyuke tasmiññeva ārammaṇe paṭisandhicittaṃ uppajjati. Ayampi atītārammaṇāya cutiyā anantarā paccuppannārammaṇā paṭisandhi. Esa tāva atītārammaṇāya sugaticutiyā anantarā atītapaccuppannārammaṇāya duggatipaṭisandhiyā pavattanākāro.

Ru-Yi Translation

For example: for a person of evil kamma established in a good sense-sphere destination, lying on the deathbed — as in the passage beginning "those past deeds hang over him at that time" — his previously accumulated evil kamma, or a sign of that kamma, comes into range at the mind-door. Taking that as object, a cognitive process of impulsions ending in registration arises; immediately after it, taking the life-continuum's own object, a decease-consciousness arises. When that has ceased, a rebirth-linking consciousness, belonging to a bad destination, bent by the unabandoned power of the defilements, arises taking as its object that very same kamma or sign of kamma that had come into range. This is rebirth-linking with a past object, occurring immediately after decease with a past object.

For another, at the time of death, by force of kamma of the kind already described, a sign of a bad destination — such as the color of a blazing fire in the hells and the like — comes into range at the mind-door. For him, after the life-continuum has arisen twice and ceased, taking that object, one adverting consciousness arises; then, owing to the diminished momentum brought on by the nearness of death, five impulsions and two registrations — three kinds of cognitive-process consciousness arise in all. After that, taking the life-continuum's own object, one decease-consciousness arises. By this point, eleven mind-moments have elapsed. Then, with a lifespan of the remaining five mind-moments, a rebirth-linking consciousness arises taking that very same object. This is rebirth-linking with a present object, occurring immediately after decease with a past object.

For another, at the time of death, an inferior object, serving as the basis for lust and the rest, comes into range in one or another of the five sense-doors. For him, when that has arisen in due course, ending in determining, then, owing to the diminished momentum brought on by the nearness of death, five impulsions and two registrations arise. After that, taking the life-continuum's own object, one decease-consciousness arises. By this point, two life-continuum moments, adverting, seeing, receiving, investigating, determining, five impulsions, two registrations, and one decease-consciousness — fifteen mind-moments have elapsed. Then, with a lifespan of the remaining single mind-moment, a rebirth-linking consciousness arises taking that very same object. This, too, is rebirth-linking with a present object, occurring immediately after decease with a past object. This, then, is the manner in which rebirth-linking into a bad destination, with a past-or-present object, occurs immediately after decease from a good destination, with a past object.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

These three scenarios are worth reading side by side, since they trace exactly the same underlying story — a good life ending badly — through three different levels of vividness at the deathbed: first, the past evil deed itself simply appears directly to the dying mind as an idea; second, a symbolic sign of the destination about to come (flame-colored light, standing for hell) appears instead, giving the mind something almost sensory to fasten onto; third, an actually present sense object — something seen, heard, or otherwise perceived through the ordinary sense-doors in the final moments of life — triggers desire and becomes, in turn, the object carried into the very next rebirth-linking moment. In every case, the underlying mechanism is identical: whatever the dying mind is last actively engaged with becomes, by strict conditioned succession, the object of the very first moment of the next life.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
kamma / kammanimitta / gatinimitta

kamma / sign of kamma / sign of destination — the three possible near-death objects that can come into range at the mind-door (or, for gatinimitta and ordinary sense objects, at the five sense-doors), determining which rebirth-linking consciousness immediately follows.

cittakkhaṇa

a mind-moment — the smallest unit of mental time in Abhidhamma analysis; the precise counting of mind-moments in these death-scene descriptions (eleven, fifteen, and so on) shows the tradition's characteristic insistence on exact, moment-by-moment causal accounting even at life's most emotionally charged juncture.

Dying Badly Reborn Well, Dying Well Reborn Well, and the Meditator's Death

Buddhaghosa completes the cycle of case studies: a bad life ending in a good rebirth through the same mechanism reversed, a good life continuing into another good rebirth (including the poignant detail of relatives arranging pleasant sights, sounds, and offerings at a dying person's bedside specifically to influence their final mental state), and finally the distinctive death of an accomplished meditator, whose jhāna attainment itself, or a meditation sign such as the earth-kasiṇa, can appear at the deathbed and carry them directly into an exalted form- or formless-sphere rebirth — without ever passing through the registration stage ordinary sense-sphere deaths require.

Pali Source

Duggatiyaṃ ṭhitassa pana upacitānavajjakammassa vuttanayeneva taṃ anavajjakammaṃ vā kammanimittaṃ vā manodvāre āpāthamāgacchatīti kaṇhapakkhe sukkapakkhaṃ ṭhapetvā sabbaṃ purimanayeneva veditabbaṃ. Ayaṃ atītārammaṇāya duggaticutiyā anantarā atītapaccuppannārammaṇāya sugatipaṭisandhiyā pavattanākāro.

Sugatiyaṃ ṭhitassa pana upacitānavajjakammassa "tānissa tasmiṃ samaye olambantī"tiādivacanato maraṇamañce nipannassa yathūpacitaṃ anavajjakammaṃ vā kammanimittaṃ vā manodvāre āpāthamāgacchati. Tañca kho upacitakāmāvacarānavajjakammasseva. Upacitamahaggatakammassa pana kammanimittameva āpāthamāgacchati. Taṃ ārabbha uppannāya tadārammaṇapariyosānāya suddhāya vā javanavīthiyā anantaraṃ bhavaṅgavisayaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā cuticittaṃ uppajjati. Tasmiṃ niruddhe tameva āpāthagataṃ kammaṃ vā kammanimittaṃ vā ārabbha anupacchinnakilesabalavināmitaṃ sugatipariyāpannaṃ paṭisandhicittaṃ uppajjati. Ayaṃ atītārammaṇāya cutiyā anantarā atītārammaṇā vā navattabbārammaṇā vā paṭisandhi.

Aparassa maraṇasamaye kāmāvacaraanavajjakammavasena manussaloke mātukucchivaṇṇasaṅkhātaṃ vā devaloke uyyānavimānakapparukkhādivaṇṇasaṅkhātaṃ vā sugatinimittaṃ manodvāre āpāthamāgacchati, tassa duggatinimitte dassitānukkameneva cuticittānantaraṃ paṭisandhicittaṃ uppajjati. Ayaṃ atītārammaṇāya cutiyā anantarā paccuppannārammaṇā paṭisandhi.

Aparassa maraṇasamaye ñātakā "ayaṃ tāta tavatthāya buddhapūjā karīyati cittaṃ pasādehī"ti vatvā pupphadāmapaṭākādivasena rūpārammaṇaṃ vā, dhammassavanatūriyapūjādivasena saddārammaṇaṃ vā, dhūmavāsagandhādivasena gandhārammaṇaṃ vā, "idaṃ tāta sāyassu tavatthāya dātabbadeyyadhamma"nti vatvā madhuphāṇitādivasena rasārammaṇaṃ vā, "idaṃ tāta phusassu tavatthāya dātabbadeyyadhamma"nti vatvā cīnapaṭṭasomārapaṭṭādivasena phoṭṭhabbārammaṇaṃ vā pañcadvāre upasaṃharanti, tassa tasmiṃ āpāthagate rūpādiārammaṇe yathākkamena uppannavoṭṭhabbanāvasāne maraṇassa āsannabhāvena mandībhūtavegattā pañca javanāni, dve tadārammaṇāni ca uppajjanti. Tato bhavaṅgavisayaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā ekaṃ cuticittaṃ, tadavasāne tasmiññeva ekacittakkhaṇaṭṭhitike ārammaṇe paṭisandhicittaṃ uppajjati. Ayampi atītārammaṇāya cutiyā anantarā paccuppannārammaṇā paṭisandhi.

Aparassa pana pathavīkasiṇajjhānādivasena paṭiladdhamahaggatassa sugatiyaṃ ṭhitassa maraṇasamaye kāmāvacarakusalakamma-kammanimitta-gatinimittānaṃ vā aññataraṃ, pathavīkasiṇādikaṃ vā nimittaṃ, mahaggatacittaṃ vā manodvāre āpāthamāgacchati, cakkhusotānaṃ vā aññatarasmiṃ kusaluppattihetubhūtaṃ paṇītamārammaṇaṃ āpāthamāgacchati, tassa yathākkamena uppannavoṭṭhabbanāvasāne maraṇassa āsannabhāvena mandībhūtavegattā pañca javanāni uppajjanti. Mahaggatagatikānaṃ pana tadārammaṇaṃ natthi, tasmā javanānantaraṃyeva bhavaṅgavisayaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā ekaṃ cuticittaṃ uppajjati. Tassāvasāne kāmāvacaramahaggatasugatīnaṃ aññatarasugatipariyāpannaṃ yathūpaṭṭhitesu ārammaṇesu aññatarārammaṇaṃ paṭisandhicittaṃ uppajjati. Ayaṃ navattabbārammaṇāya sugaticutiyā anantarā atītapaccuppannanavattabbārammaṇānaṃ aññatarārammaṇā paṭisandhi. Etenānusārena āruppacutiyāpi anantarā paṭisandhi veditabbā.

Duggatiyaṃ ṭhitassa pana pāpakammino vuttanayeneva taṃ kammaṃ kammanimittaṃ gatinimittaṃ vā manodvāre. Pañcadvāre vā pana akusaluppatti hetubhūtaṃ ārammaṇaṃ āpāthamāgacchati, athassa yathākkamena cuticittāvasāne duggatipariyāpannaṃ tesu ārammaṇesu aññatarārammaṇaṃ paṭisandhicittaṃ uppajjati. Ayaṃ atītārammaṇāya duggaticutiyā anantarā atītapaccuppannārammaṇāya paṭisandhiyā pavattanākāroti. Ettāvatā ekūnavīsatividhassāpi viññāṇassa paṭisandhivasena pavatti dīpitā hoti.

Ru-Yi Translation

But for one established in a bad destination who has accumulated blameless kamma, in just the way already stated, that blameless kamma, or a sign of that kamma, comes into range at the mind-door — everything should be understood in exactly the same way as before, substituting the bright side for the dark. This is the manner in which rebirth-linking into a good destination, with a past-or-present object, occurs immediately after decease from a bad destination, with a past object.

But for one established in a good destination who has accumulated blameless kamma, lying on the deathbed — as in the passage beginning "those past deeds hang over him at that time" — his previously accumulated blameless kamma, or a sign of that kamma, comes into range at the mind-door. And this is so only for accumulated sense-sphere blameless kamma; for accumulated exalted kamma, only a sign of that kamma comes into range. Taking that as object, a cognitive process of impulsions ending in registration — or a bare cognitive process of impulsions alone — arises; immediately after it, taking the life-continuum's own object, a decease-consciousness arises. When that has ceased, a rebirth-linking consciousness, belonging to a good destination, bent by the unabandoned power of the defilements, arises taking as its object that very same kamma or sign of kamma that had come into range. This is rebirth-linking with a past object, or with a "not-so-classifiable" object, occurring immediately after decease with a past object.

For another, at the time of death, by force of sense-sphere blameless kamma, a sign of a good destination comes into range at the mind-door — such as the color of a mother's womb, in the case of the human world, or the color of a park, a mansion, a wish-fulfilling tree, and the like, in the case of the deva world. For him, by the very same sequence already shown for the sign of a bad destination, a rebirth-linking consciousness arises immediately after the decease-consciousness. This is rebirth-linking with a present object, occurring immediately after decease with a past object.

For another, at the time of death, his relatives, saying, "dear one, this offering to the Buddha is being made for your sake — make your mind serene," present at the five sense-doors: a visible-form object, such as garlands of flowers and banners; or a sound object, such as the hearing of the Dhamma or musical offerings; or a smell object, such as incense and perfume; or, saying, "dear one, taste this — it is an offering to be given for your sake," a taste object, such as honey and molasses; or, saying, "dear one, touch this — it is an offering to be given for your sake," a tangible object, such as Chinese or Somāran cloth. For him, on that visible form or other object that has come into range, when the process has arisen in due course, ending in determining, then, owing to the diminished momentum brought on by the nearness of death, five impulsions and two registrations arise. After that, taking the life-continuum's own object, one decease-consciousness arises; at the end of that, a rebirth-linking consciousness arises taking that very same object, which has a duration of just that one mind-moment. This, too, is rebirth-linking with a present object, occurring immediately after decease with a past object.

But for another, one who has attained exalted consciousness by way of the earth-kasiṇa jhāna and the like, established in a good destination, at the time of death, one or another of the following comes into range at the mind-door: sense-sphere wholesome kamma, a sign of kamma, or a sign of destination; or a sign such as the earth-kasiṇa; or an exalted state of consciousness — or a refined object, serving as the basis for the arising of wholesomeness, comes into range in one or another of the eye or ear doors. For him, when this has arisen in due course, ending in determining, then, owing to the diminished momentum brought on by the nearness of death, five impulsions arise. But for those bound for an exalted destination, there is no registration; therefore, immediately after the impulsions, taking the life-continuum's own object, one decease-consciousness arises. At the end of that, a rebirth-linking consciousness arises, belonging to one or another of the sense-sphere or exalted good destinations, taking one or another of the objects as they have been presented. This is rebirth-linking with one or another of a past, present, or "not-so-classifiable" object, occurring immediately after decease from a good destination, with a "not-so-classifiable" object. By this same method, rebirth-linking immediately after decease from a formless state of being, too, should be understood.

But for one of evil kamma established in a bad destination, in the manner already stated, that kamma, a sign of kamma, or a sign of destination comes into range at the mind-door — or an object serving as the basis for the arising of unwholesomeness comes into range in one of the five sense-doors. Then, for him, in due course, at the end of the decease-consciousness, a rebirth-linking consciousness, belonging to a bad destination, arises, taking one or another of those objects. This is the manner in which rebirth-linking, with a past or present object, occurs immediately after decease from a bad destination, with a past object. By this point, the occurrence of all nineteen kinds of consciousness, by way of rebirth-linking, has been shown.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The scene of relatives gathering flowers, music, incense, and honey at a dying person's bedside, urging them to "make your mind serene," is one of the treatise's most human moments — not a symbolic flourish but a direct, practical application of everything this section has just explained: since whatever object the dying mind is engaged with becomes, by strict causal law, the seed of the next rebirth, deliberately curating a dying person's final sensory environment is a genuinely meaningful act of care, not mere sentiment. This is very likely the doctrinal root of the still-living custom, across Theravāda cultures today, of chanting, offering flowers, or playing recorded Dhamma talks at a deathbed.

The meditator's death stands apart from every other scenario here in one precise technical detail: no registration consciousness ever arises for someone bound for an exalted rebirth. Their impulsions run their course and pass directly into decease, without that extra moment of "registering" a powerful object the way ordinary sense-sphere deaths require — a small but telling sign, within the Abhidhamma's own machinery, of how thoroughly a lifetime of jhāna practice has already refined the mind's ordinary reactive patterns, even in its very last active moments.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
voṭṭhabbana

determining — the cognitive-process moment, following investigation, that decides how the mind will respond to an object, immediately before the run of impulsions begins; mentioned repeatedly throughout these death-scene sequences as the pivot before the final, kammically decisive moments.

pathavīkasiṇa

the earth-kasiṇa — the meditation device treated at length in chapter four of this treatise; named here as a sign that can appear directly at a skilled meditator's deathbed, carrying them straight into an exalted rebirth without the ordinary registration stage.

Classifying Rebirth-Linking: Mixed with Matter, by Realm, by Womb

Having shown that rebirth-linking consciousness is conditioned by kamma in two distinct ways at once — the kamma condition and the decisive support condition operating together — Buddhaghosa now sorts it along several further axes: whether it arises together with matter or not, which of the three realms it belongs to, which of the four wombs it is born through, and, down to a precise minimum, exactly how many "decads" of matter must arise together with the very weakest, smallest possible rebirth-linking.

Pali Source

Tayidaṃ sabbampi evaṃ, "pavattamānaṃ sandhimhi, dvedhā kammena vattati; missādīhi ca bhedehi, bhedassa duvidhādiko."

Idañhi ekūnavīsatividhampi vipākaviññāṇaṃ paṭisandhimhi pavattamānā dvedhā kammena vattati. Yathāsakañhi ekassa janakakammaṃ nānākkhaṇikakammapaccayena ceva upanissayapaccayena ca paccayo hoti. Vuttañhetaṃ "kusalākusalaṃ kammaṃ vipākassa upanissayapaccayena paccayo"ti. Evaṃ vattamānassa panassa missādīhi bhedehi duvidhādikopi bhedo veditabbo.

Seyyathidaṃ – idañhi paṭisandhivasena ekadhā pavattamānampi rūpena saha missāmissabhedato duvidhaṃ. Kāmarūpārūpabhavabhedato tividhaṃ. Aṇḍajajalābuja-saṃsedaja-opapātikayonivasena catubbidhaṃ. Gativasena pañcavidhaṃ. Viññāṇaṭṭhitivasena sattavidhaṃ. Sattāvāsavasena aṭṭhavidhaṃ hoti.

Tattha, "missaṃ dvidhā bhāvabhedā, sabhāvaṃ tattha ca dvidhā; dve vā tayo vā dasakā, omato ādinā saha."

Missaṃ dvidhā bhāvabhedāti yaṃ hetaṃ ettha aññatra arūpabhavā rūpamissaṃ paṭisandhiviññāṇaṃ uppajjati, taṃ rūpabhave itthindriyapurisindriyasaṅkhātena bhāvena vinā uppattito. Kāmabhave aññatra jātipaṇḍakapaṭisandhiyā bhāvena saha uppattito sa-bhāvaṃ, a-bhāvanti duvidhaṃ hoti.

Sabhāvaṃ tattha ca dvidhāti tatthāpi ca yaṃ sa-bhāvaṃ, taṃ itthipurisabhāvānaṃ aññatarena saha uppattito duvidhameva hoti.

Dve vā tayo vā dasakā omato ādinā sahāti yaṃ hetamettha "missaṃ amissa"nti duke ādibhūtaṃ rūpamissaṃ paṭisandhiviññāṇaṃ, tena saha vatthukāyadasakavasena dve vā, vatthukāyabhāvadasakavasena tayo vā dasakā omato uppajjanti, natthi ito paraṃ rūpaparihānīti. Taṃ panetaṃ evaṃ omakaparimāṇaṃ uppajjamānaṃ aṇḍajajalābujanāmikāsu dvīsu yonīsu jātiuṇṇāya ekena aṃsunā uddhaṭasappimaṇḍappamāṇaṃ kalalanti laddhasaṅkhaṃ hutvā uppajjati.

Ru-Yi Translation

All of this, then, is as follows — "occurring at rebirth-linking, it operates in two ways by way of kamma; and, by such divisions as mixed and the rest, it admits a further division, twofold and more."

For this resultant consciousness, of nineteen kinds, occurring at rebirth-linking, operates in two ways by kamma. For its own generative kamma, operating at a different time, is a condition for each single one both by way of the kamma condition and by way of the decisive support condition. For it is said: "wholesome and unwholesome kamma is, for its result, a condition by way of the decisive support condition." And, occurring in this way, its further division — twofold and more — by such divisions as mixed with matter and the rest, should be understood.

For example: though it occurs, by way of rebirth-linking, as a single thing, it is of two kinds by the division of being mixed or unmixed with matter; of three kinds by the division of sense-sphere, form-sphere, and formless being; of four kinds by way of the wombs of the egg-born, the womb-born, the moisture-born, and the spontaneously-born; of five kinds by way of destination; of seven kinds by way of station of consciousness; and of eight kinds by way of abode of beings.

Herein — "the mixed kind is twofold by the division of sex-faculty; the 'with sex-faculty' kind, too, is twofold there; and, by way of the minimum, there are two or three decads, together with more."

"The mixed kind is twofold by the division of sex-faculty": this rebirth-linking consciousness, mixed with matter, arising anywhere other than in formless being, is of two kinds — "with sex-faculty" and "without sex-faculty" — since, in form-sphere being, it arises without the state called the femininity or masculinity faculty, whereas in sense-sphere being, other than in the rebirth-linking of one born a congenital eunuch, it arises together with that state.

"The 'with sex-faculty' kind, too, is twofold there": and, of that, whatever is "with sex-faculty" is itself twofold, since it arises together with one or the other of the feminine or masculine state.

"By way of the minimum, there are two or three decads, together with more": this rebirth-linking consciousness, mixed with matter — the first of the pair "mixed" and "unmixed" just mentioned — arises, at the minimum, together with two decads by way of the physical-basis decad and the body decad, or three decads by way of the physical-basis decad, the body decad, and the sex-faculty decad; there is no lesser measure of matter than this. And this, arising in this minimal measure, arises, in the two wombs called the egg-born and the womb-born, having acquired the designation "the embryonic clot" — of a size comparable to a drop of clarified butter drawn up on a single strand of newborn wool.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The minimal measure of matter at conception — the "embryonic clot," no bigger than a drop of ghee balanced on a single strand of newborn wool — is a striking image precisely because of its combination of scientific-sounding precision (exactly two or three specific decads of matter, no fewer) and total absence of anything resembling a soul entering the picture. What begins a new human or animal life, on this analysis, is not a spirit taking up residence in a body, but simply the minimum viable configuration of matter and consciousness able to satisfy the conditions this whole chapter has been laying out — a strikingly non-mystical account of life's very beginning.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
dasaka

a decad — a group of ten kinds of matter (the four elements, color, smell, taste, nutritive essence, vitality, and one further distinguishing quality) that always arises as an indivisible unit; the basic building block of the Abhidhamma's theory of material rebirth.

kalala

the embryonic clot — the very first, minimal stage of a physical body at conception in the womb-born and egg-born wombs, described here with the vivid ghee-on-a-hair-strand simile for its minute size.

Which Wombs Occur Where, and How Much Matter Each Brings

The classification continues with two further technical verses: which of the four wombs are even possible in which destinations (hell-beings and most gods, for instance, are exclusively spontaneously-born), and the precise range of physical matter — from a maximum of seventy kinds down to a minimum of thirty — that accompanies rebirth-linking consciousness across different classes of beings.

Pali Source

Tattha yonīnaṃ gativasena sambhavabhedo veditabbo. Etāsu hi, "niraye bhummavajjesu, devesu ca na yoniyo; tisso purimikā honti, catassopi gatittaye."

Tattha devesu cāti casaddena yathā niraye ca bhummavajjesu ca devesu, evaṃ nijjhāmataṇhikapetesu ca purimikā tisso yoniyo na santīti veditabbā. Opapātikā eva hi te honti. Sese pana tiracchānapettivisayamanussasaṅkhāte gatittaye pubbe vajjitabhummadevesu ca catassopi yoniyo honti.

Tattha, "tiṃsa nava ceva rūpīsu, sattati ukkaṃsatotha rūpāni; saṃsedupapātayonisu, atha vā avakaṃsato tiṃsa."

Rūpībrahmesu tāva opapātikayonikesu cakkhusotavatthudasakānaṃ jīvitanavakassa cāti catunnaṃ kalāpānaṃ vasena tiṃsa ca nava ca paṭisandhiviññāṇena saha rūpāni uppajjanti. Rūpī brahme pana ṭhapetvā aññesu saṃsedajaopapātikayonikesu ukkaṃsato cakkhusotaghānajivhākāyavatthubhāvadasakānaṃ vasena sattati, tāni ca niccaṃ devesu. Tattha vaṇṇo gandho raso ojā catasso cāpi dhātuyo cakkhupasādo jīvitanti ayaṃ dasarūpaparimāṇo rūpapuñjo cakkhudasako nāma. Evaṃ sesā veditabbā. Avakaṃsato pana jaccandhabadhiraaghānakanapuṃsakassa jivhākāyavatthudasakānaṃ vasena tiṃsa rūpāni uppajjanti. Ukkaṃsāvakaṃsānaṃ pana antare anurūpato vikappo veditabbo.

Ru-Yi Translation

Herein, the distinction in how it comes to be should be understood by way of the destination proper to each womb. For, among these wombs — "in hell, among the gods excepting the earth-bound ones, the first three wombs do not occur; in the remaining three destinations, all four do."

Herein, by the word "and" in "among the gods," it should be understood that, just as in hell and among the gods excepting the earth-bound ones, so too among the scorched-by-craving petas, the first three wombs do not exist — for these beings are exclusively spontaneously-born. But in the remaining three destinations — the animal kingdom, the peta realm, and the human world — and among the earth-bound gods excepted above, all four wombs do occur.

Herein — "thirty-nine kinds of matter among the form-sphere beings; seventy at the maximum; but thirty at the minimum, in the moisture-born and spontaneously-born wombs."

Among the form-sphere Brahmās, first — beings of the spontaneously-born womb — thirty-nine kinds of matter arise together with the rebirth-linking consciousness, by way of four groups: the eye-decad, the ear-decad, the physical-basis-decad, and the life-nonad. But among other beings of the moisture-born and spontaneously-born wombs, excepting the form-sphere Brahmās, at the maximum, seventy kinds of matter arise, by way of the eye-, ear-, nose-, tongue-, body-, physical-basis-, and sex-faculty-decads; and these occur invariably among the gods. Herein, color, smell, taste, nutritive essence, the four elements, eye-sensitivity, and the life-faculty — this group of matter, ten kinds in measure, is called the eye-decad; the rest should be understood in the same way. But at the minimum, thirty kinds of matter arise, by way of the tongue-, body-, and physical-basis-decads, in the case of one born congenitally blind, deaf, without a nose, and sexless. Between the maximum and the minimum, the intermediate variations should be understood accordingly.

Reading note — Ru-Yi
