The exception carved out for form-sphere Brahmās — thirty-nine kinds of matter, capped at eye-, ear-, physical-basis-, and life-decads, never reaching the full seventy some gods and spontaneously-born beings can have — reflects a point made in earlier chapters: Brahmā-world beings have refined bodies of pure light, without a nose, tongue, or body-sensitivity in the ordinary sense, since they have no need to smell, taste, or physically touch anything in that exalted plane of existence. Matter itself, not only consciousness, is graded in refinement according to which realm a being inhabits.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
bhummadeva

an earth-bound god — bhumma (of the earth) + deva (god); a lower class of deity dwelling on or near the earth's surface, distinguished here as an exception to the general rule that gods are exclusively spontaneously-born.

jīvitanavaka

the life-nonad — jīvita (life) + navaka (a group of nine); a nine-fold group of matter centered on the life-faculty, one of the four groups that together make up the minimal thirty-nine kinds of matter arising for a form-sphere Brahmā at rebirth-linking.

Same or Different? Eight Ways Decease and Rebirth-Linking Can Diverge

A crucial verse now makes an observation with enormous philosophical weight: the consciousness that closes one life and the consciousness that opens the next need not resemble each other at all — not in how many aggregates they involve, not in their object, not in their destination, not in whether they have moral roots, not in their accompanying feeling, joy, or the presence of initial and sustained thought. If a fixed self or soul carried over from one life to the next, this radical discontinuity would be inexplicable; the Buddhist account has no difficulty with it, because nothing but a conditioned causal relationship is claimed to hold between the two moments in the first place.

Pali Source

Evaṃ viditvā puna, "khandhārammaṇagatihetu-vedanāpītivitakkavicārehi; bhedābhedaviseso, cutisandhīnaṃ pariññeyyo."

Yā hesā missāmissato duvidhā paṭisandhi, yā cassā atītānantarā cuti, tāsaṃ imehi khandhādīhi bhedābhedaviseso ñātabboti attho.

Kathaṃ? Kadāci hi catukkhandhāya āruppacutiyā anantarā catukkhandhāva ārammaṇatopi abhinnā paṭisandhi hoti. Kadāci amahaggatabahiddhārammaṇāya mahaggataajjhattārammaṇā. Ayaṃ tāva arūpabhūmīsuyeva nayo. Kadāci pana catukkhandhāya arūpacutiyā anantarā pañcakkhandhā kāmāvacarapaṭisandhi. Kadāci pañcakkhandhāya kāmāvacaracutiyā rūpāvacaracutiyā vā anantarā catukkhandhā arūpapaṭisandhi. Evaṃ atītārammaṇāya cutiyā paccuppannārammaṇā paṭisandhi. Ekaccasugaticutiyā ekaccaduggatipaṭisandhi. Ahetukacutiyā sahetukapaṭisandhi. Duhetukacutiyā tihetukapaṭisandhi. Upekkhāsahagatacutiyā somanassasahagatapaṭisandhi. Appītikacutiyā sappītikapaṭisandhi. Avitakkacutiyā savitakkapaṭisandhi. Avicāracutiyā savicārapaṭisandhi. Avitakkāvicāracutiyā savitakkasavicārapaṭisandhīti tassa tassa viparītato ca yathāyogaṃ yojetabbaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

Having understood this, further — "by aggregate, object, destination, root, feeling, joy, initial thought, and sustained thought, the distinction of sameness and difference between decease and rebirth-linking should be fully understood."

The meaning is: with regard to this twofold rebirth-linking — mixed and unmixed with matter — and the decease-consciousness immediately preceding it, the distinction of sameness and difference between them should be known by way of these factors: the aggregates and the rest.

How so? Sometimes, immediately after a four-aggregate decease from a formless state, a four-aggregate rebirth-linking occurs, not even different in its object. Sometimes, immediately after a decease with a non-exalted, external object, a rebirth-linking occurs with an exalted, internal object — this, at least, is the method within the formless planes alone. But sometimes, immediately after a four-aggregate decease from a formless state, a five-aggregate sense-sphere rebirth-linking occurs. Sometimes, immediately after a five-aggregate decease from the sense sphere or the form sphere, a four-aggregate formless rebirth-linking occurs. So too: rebirth-linking with a present object may occur after decease with a past object; rebirth-linking into one kind of bad destination may occur after decease from one kind of good destination; rebirth-linking with roots may occur after a rootless decease; rebirth-linking with three roots may occur after a decease with two roots; rebirth-linking accompanied by joy may occur after a decease accompanied by equanimity; rebirth-linking with joy as a jhāna-factor may occur after a decease without it; rebirth-linking with initial thought may occur after a decease without initial thought; rebirth-linking with sustained thought may occur after a decease without sustained thought; rebirth-linking with both initial and sustained thought may occur after a decease with neither — and this should be applied, as appropriate, in the reverse direction as well, case by case.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

This is one of the chapter's quietest but most consequential passages: it establishes, entirely through Abhidhamma bookkeeping rather than open polemic, that literally none of the properties one might expect a persisting "self" to carry across death — how many aggregates are present, what object the mind holds, which destination it belongs to, whether wholesome roots are present, what feeling and jhāna-factors accompany it — need remain constant from the last moment of one life to the first moment of the next. A being can die in deep formless-jhāna equanimity and be reborn an instant later into an ordinary human rebirth accompanied by joy, or die an ordinary human death and be reborn directly into a formless plane. What connects the two moments is conditioning alone, never a substance carried unchanged between them — which sets up perfectly the explicit statement of that very principle in the verses immediately following.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
catukkhandha / pañcakkhandha

four aggregates / five aggregates — a rebirth-linking or decease-consciousness in a formless plane involves only the four mental aggregates (no form); one in a sense-sphere or form-sphere plane involves all five, including the aggregate of form.

bhedābhedaviseso

the distinction of sameness and difference — bheda (difference) + abheda (sameness) + viseso (distinction); the technical framework this passage uses to compare consecutive moments of consciousness across a rebirth, foreshadowing the "neither the same nor different" formula given explicitly two paragraphs later.

Neither the Same Person Nor a Different One: The Rope Across the Canal

The chapter now delivers its central philosophical statement on personal identity and rebirth — one of the most quoted passages in the entire Theravāda tradition. A "mere phenomenon," having obtained its conditions, passes to another state of being: it neither transmigrates from the old state, nor arises here uncaused. Buddhaghosa illustrates this with the full clinical sequence of an actual human death — the body drying like a leaf in the sun, the faculties failing one by one until only the heart-basis remains — and then with an unforgettable image: consciousness "crossing over" like someone swinging across an irrigation canal on a rope tied to a tree on the near bank, releasing the old support only in the very act of reaching for the new one.

Pali Source

"Laddhapaccayamiti dhammamattametaṃ bhavantaramupeti; nāssa tato saṅkanti, na tato hetuṃ vinā hoti."

Iti hetaṃ laddhapaccayaṃ rūpārūpadhammamattaṃ uppajjamānaṃ bhavantaramupetīti vuccati, na satto, na jīvo. Tassa ca nāpi atītabhavato idha saṅkanti atthi. Nāpi tato hetuṃ vinā idha pātubhāvo. Tayidaṃ pākaṭena manussacutipaṭisandhikkamena pakāsayissāma.

Atītabhavasmiṃ hi sarasena upakkamena vā samāsannamaraṇassa asayhānaṃ sabbaṅgapaccaṅgasandhibandhanacchedakānaṃ māraṇantikavedanāsatthānaṃ sannipātaṃ asahantassa ātape pakkhittaharitatālapaṇṇamiva kamena upasussamāne sarīre niruddhesu cakkhādīsu indriyesu hadayavatthumatte patiṭṭhitesu kāyindriyamanindriyajīvitindriyesu taṅkhaṇāvasesahadayavatthusannissitaṃ viññāṇaṃ garukasamāsevitāsannapubbakatānaṃ aññataraṃ laddhāvasesapaccayasaṅkhārasaṅkhātaṃ kammaṃ, tadupaṭṭhāpitaṃ vā kammanimittagatinimittasaṅkhātaṃ visayaṃ ārabbha pavattati. Tadevaṃ pavattamānaṃ taṇhāvijjānaṃ appahīnattā avijjāpaṭicchāditādīnave tasmiṃ visaye taṇhā nāmeti, sahajātasaṅkhārā khipanti. Taṃ santativasena taṇhāya nāmiyamānaṃ saṅkhārehi khippamānaṃ orimatīrarukkhavinibaddharajjumālambitvā mātikātikkamako viya purimañca nissayaṃ jahati, aparañca kammasamuṭṭhāpitaṃ nissayaṃ assādayamānaṃ vā anassādayamānaṃ vā ārammaṇādīhiyeva paccayehi pavattatīti.

Ettha ca purimaṃ cavanato cuti. Pacchimaṃ bhavantarādipaṭisandhānato paṭisandhīti vuccati. Tadetaṃ nāpi purimabhavā idhāgataṃ, nāpi tato kammasaṅkhāranativisayādihetuṃ vinā pātubhūtanti veditabbaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

"Having thus obtained its conditions, this mere phenomenon goes on to another state of being; there is no transmigration of it from that former state, nor does it come to be here without a cause from there."

Thus, it is said, this mere material or formless phenomenon, having obtained its conditions and arising, "goes on to another state of being" — not a being, not a soul. And there is neither any transmigration of it here from the past state of being, nor any coming-into-being here without a cause from that past state. This we shall make clear by means of the well-known sequence of a human being's decease and rebirth-linking.

For in the past state of being, unable to bear the concurrence of the unbearable, deathly, knife-like pains that cut through every joint of every limb, whether arising spontaneously or through violence, as death draws near, the body drying up by stages like a green palm leaf thrown into the sun's heat, when the faculties beginning with the eye have ceased and only the body-faculty, mind-faculty, and life-faculty, resting on the mere heart-basis, remain established, the consciousness that depends on that same, still-remaining heart-basis of the moment occurs, taking as its object one or another of the following: kamma — meaning a formation that has obtained a remaining condition, either heavily indulged in or done immediately before death — or an object presented by that same kamma, called a sign of kamma or a sign of destination. Occurring in this way, since craving and ignorance have not been abandoned, it "bends" toward that object through craving, which conceals its drawback under ignorance's own concealment, and the conascent formations "toss" it forward. Bent by craving and tossed by the formations in this continuous succession, like one crossing an irrigation channel by grasping a rope tied to a tree on the near bank, it lets go of its former support and, whether relishing or not relishing the further support set up by that kamma, occurs solely through such conditions as the object and the rest.

And herein, the former consciousness is called "decease," from its falling away; the latter is called "rebirth-linking," from its linking on to the beginning of another state of being. This should be understood: it has neither come here from the former state of being, nor has it come to be here without a cause — namely, kamma, the formations, the inclination of consciousness, the object, and the rest — from that former state.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The rope-across-the-canal image repays close attention for exactly what it does and does not claim. A person crossing a canal by rope does not carry the near bank with them, and nothing physical travels continuously through empty space between the two banks — yet the crossing is not uncaused or random either: it happens by a single, unbroken, physically continuous act of swinging, releasing the rope's origin only in the instant of reaching the far side. Consciousness at death behaves the same way: nothing substantial "goes" anywhere, and yet the connection between the last moment of one life and the first moment of the next is genuinely, causally unbroken, mediated entirely by craving's "bending" toward an object and the formations' "tossing" consciousness onward, with no gap in which anything could either persist unchanged or vanish entirely.

The unflinching physical detail of the death description — pain compared to knives severing every joint, the body drying like a leaf in the sun, faculties failing one by one until only the heart-basis remains — is not gratuitous. It grounds an extremely abstract metaphysical claim (no self transmigrates) in the most concrete, undeniable fact available: an actual, ordinary human being's actual, ordinary dying. The philosophy is being tested against the most vivid case its author can supply.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
laddhapaccayaṃ dhammamattaṃ

"this mere phenomenon, having obtained its conditions" — the chapter's own precise, deliberately impersonal description of what actually passes from one life to the next: a conditioned event, never a being or a soul.

orimatīrarukkhavinibaddharajju

a rope tied to a tree on the near bank — orima (near) + tīra (bank) + rukkha (tree) + vinibaddha (tied) + rajju (rope); the chapter's central image for consciousness "crossing over" at death without either transmigrating or arising uncaused.

cuti / paṭisandhi

decease / rebirth-linking — cuti (falling away) and paṭi- (back, again) + sandhi (joining); the two names given to the very same causal transition viewed from either side, the closing moment of one life and the opening moment of the next.

An Echo, Not a Traveler: Neither the Same nor Different

Buddhaghosa reinforces the point with further illustrations — an echo, a lamp's flame passed from wick to wick, a seal-impression, a reflection — each showing an effect that has a cause elsewhere without anything itself literally traveling from that cause. He then states the "middle way" formula explicitly: the relationship between one life and the next is neither strict identity nor strict difference, exactly as milk and the curds that form from it are neither simply the same substance nor two wholly unrelated ones.

Pali Source

"Siyuṃ nidassanānettha, paṭighosādikā atha; santānabandhato natthi, ekatā nāpi nānatā."

Ettha cetassa viññāṇassa purimabhavato idha anāgamane, atītabhavapariyāpannahetūti ca uppāde paṭighosa-padīpa-muddā-paṭibimbappakārā dhammā nidassanāni siyuṃ. Yathā hi paṭighosa-padīpa-muddā-chāyā saddādihetukā honti aññatra agantvā evamevaṃ idaṃ cittaṃ.

Ettha ca santānabandhato natthi ekatā nāpi nānatā. Yadi hi santānabandhe sati ekantamekatā bhaveyya, na khīrato dadhi sambhūtaṃ siyā. Athāpi ekantanānatā bhaveyya, na khīrassādhīno dadhi siyā. Esa nayo sabbahetuhetusamuppannesu. Evañca sati sabbalokavohāralopo siyā, so ca aniṭṭho. Tasmā ettha na ekantamekatā vā nānatā vā upagantabbāti.

Ru-Yi Translation

"Here, illustrations for this could be an echo and the like; by the connection of the continuity, there is neither strict oneness nor strict otherness."

Herein, for this consciousness's not coming here from the former state of being, and yet its arising with a cause included in the former state of being, illustrations such as an echo, a lamp's flame, a seal-impression, and a reflected image could be given. For just as an echo, a lamp's flame, a seal-impression, and a shadow have sound and the like as their cause, without themselves having traveled from elsewhere — just so is this consciousness.

And herein, by reason of the connection of the continuity, there is neither strict oneness nor strict otherness. For if, given the connection of the continuity, there were strict oneness, then curds could not come to be from milk; and if there were strict otherness, then curds would not be dependent on milk in the first place. The same method applies to all cause-and-caused-arisen things. And if it were otherwise, the whole of the world's conventional usage would be destroyed — and that is an undesirable consequence. Therefore, here, neither strict oneness nor strict otherness should be accepted.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The milk-and-curd analogy is doing real philosophical work, not just decorating the point: it is offered as a kind of proof by absurdity of the middle position. If milk and curds were strictly identical, curds could never "come to be" from milk — there would be nothing to come to be, since it would already be there. If milk and curds were strictly, wholly different substances with no real connection, curds could not be said to depend on milk at all, and any milk could turn into any curds arbitrarily. Since neither absurd consequence is acceptable — since the everyday fact that this milk becomes these curds, and no other, must be preserved — neither strict identity nor strict difference can be the right description, and this exact same logic, Buddhaghosa notes, applies to every cause-and-effect relationship in the world, not only to rebirth. Personal continuity across death is simply a particularly emotionally weighted instance of a completely general pattern.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
paṭighosa / padīpa / muddā / paṭibimba

echo / lamp-flame / seal-impression / reflection — four everyday illustrations of an effect that is genuinely caused by something elsewhere without any substance actually traveling from that source to the effect.

ekantamekatā / ekantanānatā

strict oneness / strict otherness — the two extreme positions this passage rules out for the relationship between cause and effect generally, and between one life and the next specifically; the rejected pair mirrors the eternalism/annihilationism pairing refuted earlier in the chapter.

If No Self Transmigrates, Who Experiences the Fruit? The Mango Seed

A sharp objection follows: if the aggregates of this life have simply ceased, and the kamma that conditions a result does not itself travel anywhere, wouldn't the result end up belonging to someone else entirely — and, with no one left to experience it, whose result could it possibly be? Buddhaghosa answers first with the image of seed-cultivation: a mango tree's later fruit belongs to that same seed's own continuity, not to some other seed, precisely because of the connection just established — neither strictly the same thing nor a wholly different one. He then dissolves the demand for a separate "experiencer" altogether: just as we say "the tree bears fruit" the instant its fruit appears, with no further tree-behind-the-tree required, "experiencing" a result simply is that result's arising within a particular continuity — nothing more is needed, and nothing more should be sought.

Pali Source

Etthāha – nanu evaṃ asaṅkantipātubhāve sati ye imasmiṃ manussattabhāve khandhā, tesaṃ niruddhattā, phalapaccayassa ca kammassa tattha agamanato aññassa aññato ca taṃ phalaṃ siyā, upabhuñjake ca asati kassa taṃ phalaṃ siyā, tasmā na sundaramidaṃ vidhānanti. Tatridaṃ vuccati – "santāne yaṃ phalaṃ etaṃ, nāññassa na ca aññato; bījānaṃ abhisaṅkhāro, etassatthassa sādhako."

Ekasantānasmiṃ hi phalaṃ uppajjamānaṃ tattha ekantaekattanānattānaṃ paṭisiddhattā aññassāti vā aññatoti vā na hoti. Etassa ca panatthassa bījānaṃ abhisaṅkhāro sādhako. Ambabījādīnaṃ hi abhisaṅkhāresu katesu tassa bījassa santāne laddhapaccayo kālantare phalaviseso uppajjamāno na aññabījānaṃ, nāpi aññābhisaṅkhārapaccayā uppajjati, na ca tāni bījāni, te abhisaṅkhārā vā phalaṭṭhānaṃ pāpuṇanti, evaṃ sampadamidaṃ veditabbaṃ. Vijjāsipposadhādīhi cāpi bālasarīre upayuttehi kālantare vuḍḍhasarīrādīsu phaladehi ayamattho veditabbo.

Yampi vuttaṃ "upabhuñjake ca asati kassa taṃ phalaṃ siyā"ti, tattha, "phalassuppattiyā eva, siddhā bhuñjakasammuti; phaluppādena rukkhassa, yathā phalati sammuti."

Yathā hi rukkhasaṅkhātānaṃ dhammānaṃ ekadesabhūtassa rukkhaphalassa uppattiyā eva rukkho phalatīti vā phalitoti vā vuccati, tathā devamanussasaṅkhātānaṃ khandhānaṃ ekadesabhūtassa upabhogasaṅkhātassa sukhadukkhaphalassa uppādeneva devo, manusso vā upabhuñjatīti vā, sukhito, dukkhitoti vā vuccati. Tasmā na ettha aññena upabhuñjakena nāma koci attho atthīti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Here it might be asked: is it not so that, given this appearance without transmigration, since the aggregates belonging to this present human state have ceased, and since the kamma that is the condition for the result does not itself go over there, the result would then belong to someone else, and would come from someone else — and, there being no one to experience it, whose would that result be? Therefore this arrangement is not a sound one. To this it is said — "this result belongs to the same continuity, not to another, nor does it come from another; the cultivation of seeds is what establishes the truth of this matter."

For, since a result arising within a single continuity has already had strict sameness and strict difference ruled out with respect to it, that result does not belong to another, nor does it come from another. And the cultivation of seeds is what establishes this very point. For when cultivation has been performed on a mango seed and the like, a distinct fruit, having obtained a condition in the continuity of that same seed, arising after an interval of time, does not arise from other seeds, nor conditioned by the cultivation of others; and neither do those seeds, nor that cultivation, themselves reach the place of the fruit. This matter should be understood by this analogy. And this same point should also be understood by way of learning, skills, medicines, and the like, applied to a child's body, and their fruits appearing, after an interval of time, in the grown body and the rest.

As for what was said — "there being no one to experience it, whose would that result be" — herein: "the convention of 'one who experiences' is established simply by the arising of the result, just as the convention 'the tree bears fruit' is established by the tree's fruit having arisen."

For just as, when the tree-fruit, which is a part of the states called "tree," arises, the tree itself is said to "bear fruit" or to "have borne fruit," so too, simply by the arising of the pleasant or painful result called "experience," which is a part of the aggregates called "god" or "human being," that god or human being is said to "experience" it, or to be "happy" or "suffering." Therefore, there is no need here for any further, separate thing called an "experiencer."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The dissolution of the "experiencer" question is a remarkably economical philosophical move: rather than trying to locate who or what does the experiencing, Buddhaghosa simply points out that the ordinary grammar of "the tree bears fruit" never required a hidden fruit-bearing agent standing behind the tree in the first place — "bearing fruit" just names the event of fruit arising as part of the tree's own process. "A person experiences pleasure" works exactly the same way: it names the arising of a pleasant result within a particular continuity of aggregates, and searching for a further "experiencer" behind that arising is simply a grammatical illusion, mistaking ordinary conventional language for a metaphysical commitment it was never making.

The seed-cultivation analogy is chosen carefully to answer the "whose fruit is it" objection at exactly the right level of specificity: it is not enough to show that seeds in general produce fruits in general — that would allow any seed's cultivation to count toward any fruit. The analogy insists that a mango seed's own cultivation conditions specifically that same seed's own later fruit, and nothing else's — mirroring precisely the claim that this life's kamma conditions specifically this same continuity's own later result, not some other being's.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
santāna

continuity — the technical term for the causally connected series of moments that plays the role a "self" would otherwise play, without requiring any single unchanging substance to run through it.

bhuñjakasammuti

the convention of "one who experiences" — bhuñjaka (experiencer) + sammuti (convention, agreed designation); Buddhaghosa's term for the ordinary, useful, but not metaphysically loaded way of speaking about experience, modeled on "the tree bears fruit."

The Guarantor's Debt: How a Past, Vanished Cause Still Conditions a Present Result

One further objection closes the philosophical discussion: do the formations condition their result while still existing, or after having ceased to exist? Either answer seems to lead somewhere absurd — if while existing, the result would have to appear at the very instant the formation occurs; if while not existing, the formation would have to go on bearing fruit forever, both before and after it occurred. Buddhaghosa's answer is the image of a guarantor: the single act of standing surety, once performed, is what conditions a later repayment — it does not matter whether the guarantor, or the act itself, still "exists" at repayment time, and the guarantor is not bound to keep guaranteeing indefinitely once the debt is settled.

Pali Source

Yopi vadeyya "evaṃ santepi ete saṅkhārā vijjamānā vā phalassa paccayā siyuṃ, avijjamānā vā, yadi ca vijjamānā pavattikkhaṇeyeva nesaṃ vipākena bhavitabbaṃ, atha avijjamānā pavattito pubbe pacchā ca niccaṃ phalāvahā siyu"nti, so evaṃ vattabbo – "katattā paccayā ete, na ca niccaṃ phalāvahā; pāṭibhogādikaṃ tattha, veditabbaṃ nidassanaṃ."

Katattāyeva hi saṅkhārā attano phalassa paccayā honti, na vijjamānattā, avijjamānattā vā. Yathāha – "kāmāvacarassa kusalassa kammassa katattā upacitattā vipākaṃ cakkhuviññāṇaṃ uppannaṃ hotī"tiādi. Yathārahassa ca attano phalassa paccayā hutvā na puna phalāvahā honti vipākattā. Etassa catthassa vibhāvane idaṃ pāṭibhogādikaṃ nidassanaṃ veditabbaṃ. Yathā hi loke yo kassaci atthassa niyyātanatthaṃ pāṭibhogo hoti, bhaṇḍaṃ vā kiṇāti, iṇaṃ vā gaṇhāti, tassa taṃ kiriyākaraṇamattameva tadatthaniyyātanādimhi paccayo hoti, na kiriyāya vijjamānattaṃ, avijjamānattaṃ vā, na ca tadatthaniyyātanādito parampi dhārakova hoti. Kasmā? Niyyātanādīnaṃ katattā. Evaṃ katattāva saṅkhārāpi attano phalassa paccayā honti, na ca yathārahaṃ phaladānato parampi phalāvahā hontīti. Ettāvatā missāmissavasena dvedhāpi vattamānassa paṭisandhiviññāṇassa saṅkhārapaccayā pavatti dīpitā hoti.

Ru-Yi Translation

And if someone should say: "even so, these formations would be a condition for the result either while existing or while not existing; and if while existing, they would have to yield their result at the very moment of their own occurrence; but if while not existing, they would have to be forever fruit-bearing, both before and after their occurrence" — to him this should be said: "these are conditions by virtue of having been done, not by virtue of forever bearing fruit; the illustration of a guarantor and the like should be understood here."

For it is precisely by virtue of having been done that formations become a condition for their own result — not by virtue of existing or not existing. As it is said: "through the having-been-done and having-been-accumulated of sense-sphere wholesome kamma, resultant eye-consciousness arises," and so on. And, having been, as appropriate, a condition for their own result, they do not go on being fruit-bearing thereafter, since the result, once arisen, is simply a result. In clarifying this point, the illustration of a guarantor and the like should be understood. For just as, in the world, one who stands surety for the fulfillment of some obligation on another's behalf — whether by buying goods or by taking a loan — the mere performance of that act alone is a condition for the fulfillment of that obligation and the rest; it does not matter whether the act itself still exists or not; nor is he still bound to guarantee it further, beyond that fulfillment of the obligation. Why? Because the fulfilling and the rest have already been done. In just the same way, formations too become a condition for their own result simply by virtue of having been done, and, having duly given their result, they do not go on bearing further results after that. By this point, the occurrence, conditioned by the formations, of rebirth-linking consciousness — occurring in two ways, mixed and unmixed with matter — has been shown.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The guarantor analogy quietly introduces a distinct causal category that the twenty-four conditions defined earlier in the chapter do not fully capture on their own: a "having-been-done" (katattā) relationship, where the causal work is complete the instant the act itself is finished, and the later effect depends only on that completed fact, never on the act's continued existence. This is precisely why kamma done decades or lifetimes earlier can still condition a result now, without requiring the bizarre and clearly false alternative that the original kamma is somehow still actively "existing" and operating in the present moment, or that it never really finished being a condition at all.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
katattā

by virtue of having been done — kata (done) + -ttā (abstract-noun suffix, "by reason of"); the key phrase resolving the existence/non-existence dilemma about how a past kamma can condition a present result.

pāṭibhoga

a guarantor, one who stands surety — the chapter's chosen everyday illustration for a cause whose causal work is completed by a single past act, requiring no ongoing "existence" of that act to keep conditioning its later effect.

The Complete Cross-Reference: Which Formation, Which Consciousness, Which Realm

The chapter closes its treatment of the second link with a comprehensive summary table, tracing each of the three formations through every realm, womb, destination, station of consciousness, and abode of beings it can possibly produce — a systematic answer, at last, to the question first posed many pages earlier: exactly which of the thirty-two resultant consciousnesses does each formation condition, and under exactly what circumstances.

Pali Source

Idāni sabbesveva tesu bāttiṃsavipākaviññāṇesu sammohavighātatthaṃ, "paṭisandhipavattīnaṃ, vasenete bhavādisu; vijānitabbā saṅkhārā, yathā yesañca paccayā."

Tattha tayo bhavā, catasso yoniyo, pañca gatiyo, satta viññāṇaṭṭhitiyo, nava sattāvāsāti ete bhavādayo nāma. Etesu bhavādīsu paṭisandhiyaṃ pavatte ca ete yesaṃ vipākaviññāṇānaṃ paccayā, yathā ca paccayā honti, tathā vijānitabbāti attho.

Tattha puññābhisaṅkhāre tāva kāmāvacaraaṭṭhacetanābhedo puññābhisaṅkhāro avisesena kāmabhave sugatiyaṃ navannaṃ vipākaviññāṇānaṃ paṭisandhiyaṃ nānakkhaṇikakammapaccayena ceva upanissayapaccayena cāti dvedhā paccayo. Rūpāvacarapañcakusalacetanābhedo puññābhisaṅkhāro rūpabhave paṭisandhiyaṃ eva pañcannaṃ.

Vuttappabhedakāmāvacaro pana kāmabhave sugatiyaṃ upekkhāsahagatāhetumanoviññāṇadhātuvajjānaṃ sattannaṃ parittavipākaviññāṇānaṃ vuttanayeneva dvedhā paccayo pavatte, no paṭisandhiyaṃ. Sveva rūpabhave pañcannaṃ vipākaviññāṇānaṃ tatheva paccayo pavatte, no paṭisandhiyaṃ. Kāmabhave pana duggatiyaṃ aṭṭhannampi parittavipākaviññāṇānaṃ tatheva paccayo pavatte, no paṭisandhiyaṃ. Tattha niraye mahāmoggallānattherassa narakacārikādīsu iṭṭhārammaṇasamāyoge so paccayo hoti, tiracchānesu pana petamahiddhikesu ca iṭṭhārammaṇaṃ labbhatiyeva.

Sveva kāmabhave sugatiyaṃ soḷasannampi kusalavipākaviññāṇānaṃ tatheva paccayo pavatte ca paṭisandhiyañca. Avisesena pana puññābhisaṅkhāro rūpabhave dasannaṃ vipākaviññāṇānaṃ tatheva paccayo pavatte ca paṭisandhiyañca.

Dvādasākusalacetanābhedo apuññābhisaṅkhāro kāmabhave duggatiyaṃ ekassa viññāṇassa tatheva paccayo paṭisandhiyaṃ, no pavatte. Channaṃ pavatte, no paṭisandhiyaṃ. Sattannampi akusalavipākaviññāṇānaṃ pavatte ca paṭisandhiyañca.

Kāmabhave pana sugatiyaṃ tesaṃyeva sattannaṃ tatheva paccayo pavatte, no paṭisandhiyaṃ. Rūpabhave catunnaṃ vipākaviññāṇānaṃ tatheva paccayo pavatte, no paṭisandhiyaṃ. So ca kho kāmāvacare aniṭṭharūpadassanasaddasavanavasena, brahmaloke pana aniṭṭharūpādayo nāma natthi. Tathā kāmāvacaradevalokepi.

Āneñjābhisaṅkhāro arūpabhave catunnaṃ vipākaviññāṇānaṃ tatheva paccayo pavatte ca paṭisandhiyañca.

Evaṃ tāva bhavesu paṭisandhipavattīnaṃ vasena ete saṅkhārā yesaṃ paccayā, yathā ca paccayā honti, tathā vijānitabbā. Eteneva nayena yoniādīsupi veditabbā.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now, in order to dispel confusion regarding all these thirty-two kinds of resultant consciousness together — "by way of rebirth-linking and the course of life, among the states of being and the rest, the formations should be understood, namely which they are conditions for, and how."

Herein, the three states of being, the four wombs, the five destinations, the seven stations of consciousness, and the nine abodes of beings — these are called "the states of being and the rest." Among these states of being and the rest, at rebirth-linking and in the course of life, it should be understood which resultant states of consciousness these formations are conditions for, and how they are conditions.

Herein, among the meritorious formations, first: the meritorious formation consisting of the eight sense-sphere volitions is, without distinction, a condition, in two ways — by the kamma condition operating at a different time and by the decisive support condition — for nine resultant states of consciousness at rebirth-linking in a good sense-sphere destination. The meritorious formation consisting of the five form-sphere wholesome volitions is a condition, in the same two ways, for five resultant states of consciousness, only at rebirth-linking in form-sphere being.

But the sense-sphere formation just described, in a good sense-sphere destination, is a condition, in the same two ways, for seven limited resultant states of consciousness — excepting the rootless mind-consciousness-element accompanied by equanimity — only in the course of life, not at rebirth-linking. That same formation, in form-sphere being, is likewise a condition, in the same way, for five resultant states of consciousness, only in the course of life, not at rebirth-linking. And in a bad sense-sphere destination, that same formation is likewise a condition for all eight limited resultant states of consciousness, only in the course of life, not at rebirth-linking. Herein, in hell, this condition operates on the occasion of an encounter with a desirable object — as when the Elder Mahāmoggallāna made his visits to hell and the like; but among animals and among petas of great power, a desirable object is indeed obtainable more generally.

That same sense-sphere formation, in a good sense-sphere destination, is likewise a condition for all sixteen wholesome-resultant states of consciousness, both in the course of life and at rebirth-linking. And the meritorious formation, without distinction, is likewise a condition, in form-sphere being, for ten resultant states of consciousness, both in the course of life and at rebirth-linking.

The demeritorious formation, consisting of the twelve unwholesome volitions, is likewise a condition, in a bad sense-sphere destination, for one consciousness only at rebirth-linking, not in the course of life; for six, only in the course of life, not at rebirth-linking; and for all seven unwholesome-resultant states of consciousness, both in the course of life and at rebirth-linking.

But in a good sense-sphere destination, that same demeritorious formation is likewise a condition for those same seven, only in the course of life, not at rebirth-linking. In form-sphere being, it is likewise a condition for four resultant states of consciousness, only in the course of life, not at rebirth-linking — and this occurs, in the sense sphere, by way of the seeing of an undesirable visible form or the hearing of an undesirable sound; but in the Brahmā world, there is simply no such thing as an undesirable visible form and the rest — and likewise in the sense-sphere heavens.

The imperturbable formation, in formless being, is likewise a condition for four resultant states of consciousness, both in the course of life and at rebirth-linking.

Thus, so far, by way of rebirth-linking and the course of life within the states of being, it should be understood which these formations are conditions for, and how they are conditions. By this same method, it should be understood also with respect to the wombs and the rest.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

Buried in this dense accounting is a small, vivid anecdote: the Elder Mahāmoggallāna, famed throughout the canon for his supernormal powers, is cited here as the standard example of how a desirable object could ever come into range for a hell-being — since ordinarily hell offers nothing but torment, it takes an extraordinary visitor from outside, such as Moggallāna's own recorded excursions into the hell realms, to give a hell-being even a momentary encounter with something pleasant, which the text notes can then trigger a fleeting wholesome-resultant consciousness even amid otherwise unrelieved suffering.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
viññāṇaṭṭhiti / sattāvāsa

station of consciousness / abode of beings — two of the Abhidhamma's standard classificatory grids (seven and nine categories respectively) for sorting every possible mode of existence, used here alongside state of being, womb, and destination to give an exhaustive cross-reference.

parittavipākaviññāṇa

a limited resultant consciousness — paritta (limited, small) + vipāka (result); the technical term for sense-sphere resultant consciousness generally, as opposed to the "exalted" (mahaggata) resultant consciousness of the form and formless spheres.

The Full Reach of Each Formation: Realms, Wombs, and Abodes Named in Full

A final, still more granular pass names every single realm, womb, destination, station of consciousness, and abode each formation can reach — the meritorious formation ranging across two states of being and twenty-one consciousnesses, the demeritorious formation confined to a single state of being and seven consciousnesses, and the imperturbable formation confined most narrowly of all, to a single state of being, a single womb, a single destination, and just four consciousnesses. This closes the chapter's second major section: the full account of how formations condition consciousness.

Pali Source

Tatridaṃ ādito paṭṭhāya mukhamattapakāsanaṃ – imesu hi saṅkhāresu yasmā puññābhisaṅkhāro tāva dvīsu bhavesu paṭisandhiṃ datvā sabbamattano vipākaṃ janeti. Tathā aṇḍajādīsu catūsu yonīsu, devamanussasaṅkhātāsu dvīsu gatīsu, nānattakāyanānattasaññīnānattakāyaekattasaññī-ekattakāyanānattasaññī-ekattakāyaekattasaññīsaṅkhātāsu catūsu viññāṇaṭṭhitīsu. Asaññasattāvāse panesa rūpamattamevābhisaṅkharotīti catūsuyeva sattāvāsesu ca paṭisandhiṃ datvā sabbamattano vipākaṃ janeti. Tasmā esa etesu dvīsu bhavesu, catūsu yonīsu, dvīsu gatīsu, catūsu viññāṇaṭṭhitīsu, catūsu sattāvāsesu ca ekavīsatiyā vipākaviññāṇānaṃ vuttanayeneva paccayo hoti yathāsambhavaṃ paṭisandhiyaṃ pavatte ca.

Apuññābhisaṅkhāro pana yasmā ekasmiṃyeva kāmabhave catūsu yonīsu, avasesāsu tīsu gatīsu, nānattakāyaekattasaññīsaṅkhātāya ekissā viññāṇaṭṭhitiyā, tādiseyeva ca ekasmiṃ sattāvāse paṭisandhivasena vipaccati, tasmā esa ekasmiṃ bhave, catūsu yonīsu, tīsu gatīsu, ekissā viññāṇaṭṭhitiyā, ekamhi ca sattāvāse sattannaṃ vipākaviññāṇānaṃ vuttanayeneva paccayo paṭisandhiyaṃ pavatte ca.

Āneñjābhisaṅkhāro pana yasmā ekasmiṃyeva arūpabhave, ekissā opapātikayoniyā, ekissā devagatiyā, ākāsānañcāyatanādikāsu tīsu viññāṇaṭṭhitīsu, ākāsānañcāyatanādikesu ca catūsu sattāvāsesu paṭisandhivasena vipaccati, tasmā esa ekasmiṃ bhave, ekissā yoniyā, ekissā gatiyā, tīsu viññāṇaṭṭhitīsu, catūsu sattāvāsesu catunnaṃ viññāṇānaṃ vuttanayeneva paccayo hoti paṭisandhiyaṃ pavatte cāti. Evaṃ, "paṭisandhipavattīnaṃ, vasenete bhavādisu; vijānitabbā saṅkhārā, yathā yesañca paccayāti."

Ayaṃ "saṅkhārapaccayā viññāṇa"nti padasmiṃ vitthārakathā.

Ru-Yi Translation

Herein, this is merely an indication of the general outline, taken from the beginning. For, among these formations: the meritorious formation, first, having given rebirth-linking in two states of being, generates the whole of its own result there; likewise in four wombs beginning with the egg-born, in the two destinations called deva and human, and in the four stations of consciousness called: those with diverse bodies and diverse perceptions, those with diverse bodies and uniform perception, those with uniform bodies and diverse perception, and those with uniform bodies and uniform perception. But in the abode of non-percipient beings, since this formation fashions matter alone, it gives rebirth-linking in exactly four abodes of beings and generates there the whole of its own result. Therefore, in these two states of being, four wombs, two destinations, four stations of consciousness, and four abodes of beings, it is, in the manner already stated, a condition for twenty-one resultant states of consciousness, as appropriate, both at rebirth-linking and in the course of life.

But the demeritorious formation, since it ripens, by way of rebirth-linking, only in a single state of being — the sense sphere — in four wombs, in the remaining three destinations, in the single station of consciousness called "diverse bodies, uniform perception," and in that same single abode of beings — therefore it is, in that one state of being, four wombs, three destinations, one station of consciousness, and one abode of beings, in the manner already stated, a condition for seven resultant states of consciousness, both at rebirth-linking and in the course of life.

But the imperturbable formation, since it ripens, by way of rebirth-linking, only in a single state of being — the formless — in the single spontaneously-born womb, in the single deva destination, in the three stations of consciousness beginning with the base of infinite space, and in the four abodes of beings beginning with the base of infinite space, therefore it is, in that one state of being, one womb, one destination, three stations of consciousness, and four abodes of beings, in the manner already stated, a condition for four kinds of consciousness, both at rebirth-linking and in the course of life. Thus — "by way of rebirth-linking and the course of life, among the states of being and the rest, the formations should be understood, namely which they are conditions for, and how."

This is the detailed discussion of the phrase "dependent on formations, consciousness."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The striking asymmetry across the three formations tells its own story about the shape of saṃsāra: the meritorious formation is by far the most versatile, reaching two entire states of being, four wombs, and four stations of consciousness — reflecting the enormous variety of pleasant destinies wholesome kamma can produce, from ordinary human rebirth to the highest form-sphere Brahmā realms. The demeritorious formation, by contrast, is confined entirely within the sense sphere, never touching form or formless being at all — unwholesome kamma simply cannot produce a Brahmā-world rebirth, however strong it is, since aversion and greed are structurally incompatible with the refined states cultivated in the higher jhānas. And the imperturbable formation is narrowest of all, reaching only the single formless state of being — a fitting image for how specialized and rarefied the meditative attainment behind it actually is. With this exhaustive cross-reference complete, the chapter's second link is fully accounted for, and the exposition moves on to the third: how consciousness itself conditions name-and-form.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
nānattakāya / ekattakāya

diverse bodies / uniform bodies — nānatta (diversity) and ekatta (uniformity) + kāya (body, group); two of the defining criteria distinguishing the seven stations of consciousness, based on whether beings of a given class have varied or identical physical forms.

asaññasattāvāsa

the abode of non-percipient beings — a class of form-sphere beings, reached only by the meritorious formation, who arise with matter alone and no consciousness whatsoever — the same class responsible for the twentieth, objectless rebirth-linking noted earlier in this section.

Name-and-Form in Detail: Counting Matter at the Moment of Rebirth

The third link — "dependent on consciousness, name-and-form" — opens with the familiar distinction of name (the three mental aggregates) and form (the four elements and derived matter), then plunges into an extraordinarily precise counting exercise: exactly how many items of matter and mind arise together at the very instant of rebirth-linking, for each different class of being — from the minimal twenty-three for a sexless womb-born creature, up through forty-two for a spontaneously-born Brahmā, to seventy-three for a fully-formed being with all senses intact. The section closes by tracing matter's four distinct origins — kamma, consciousness, temperature, and nutriment — through an entire lifetime, down to the vivid image of the four kinds mutually propping each other up like reed-bundles in the wind, or shipwrecked sailors clinging together in the open sea.

Pali Source

Viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpapade – "vibhāgā nāmarūpānaṃ, bhavādīsu pavattito; saṅgahā paccayanayā, viññātabbo vinicchayo."

Vibhāgā nāmarūpānanti ettha hi nāmanti ārammaṇābhimukhaṃ namanato vedanādayo tayo khandhā, rūpanti cattāri mahābhūtāni catunnañca mahābhūtānaṃ upādāyarūpaṃ. Tesaṃ vibhāgo khandhaniddese vuttoyevāti. Evaṃ tāvettha vibhāgā nāmarūpānaṃ viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Bhavādīsu pavattitoti ettha ca nāmaṃ ekaṃ sattāvāsaṃ ṭhapetvā sabbabhavayonigativiññāṇaṭṭhitisesasattāvāsesu pavattati, rūpaṃ dvīsu bhavesu, catūsu yonīsu, pañcasu gatīsu, purimāsu catūsu viññāṇaṭṭhitīsu, pañcasu sattāvāsesu pavattati.

Evaṃ pavattamāne ca etasmiṃ nāmarūpe yasmā abhāvakagabbhaseyyakānaṃ aṇḍajānañca paṭisandhikkhaṇe vatthukāyadasakavasena rūpato dvesantatisīsāni, tayo ca arūpino khandhā pātubhavanti, tasmā tesaṃ vitthārena rūparūpato vīsati dhammā, tayo ca arūpino khandhāti ete tevīsati dhammā viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpanti veditabbā.

Yasmā ca opapātikasattesu brahmakāyikādīnaṃ paṭisandhikkhaṇe cakkhusotavatthudasakānaṃ, jīvitindriyanavakassa ca vasena rūpato cattāri santatisīsāni, tayo ca arūpino khandhā pātubhavanti, tasmā tesaṃ vitthārena rūparūpato ekūnacattālīsa dhammā, tayo ca arūpino khandhāti ete bācattālīsa dhammā viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpanti veditabbā.

Kāmabhave pana yasmā sesaopapātikānaṃ, saṃsedajānaṃ vā sabhāvakaparipuṇṇāyatanānaṃ paṭisandhikkhaṇe rūpato satta santatisīsāni, tayo ca arūpino khandhā pātubhavanti, tasmā tesaṃ vitthārena rūparūpato sattati dhammā, tayo ca arūpino khandhāti ete tesattati dhammā viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpanti veditabbā. Esa ukkaṃso. Avakaṃsena pana taṃtaṃrūpasantatisīsavikalānaṃ tassa tassa vasena hāpetvā hāpetvā saṅkhepato vitthārato ca paṭisandhiyaṃ viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpasaṅkhā veditabbā.

Arūpīnaṃ pana tayova arūpino khandhā. Asaññīnaṃ rūpato jīvitindriyanavakamevāti. Esa tāva paṭisandhiyaṃ nayo.

Pavatte pana sabbattha rūpappavattidese paṭisandhicittassa ṭhitikkhaṇe paṭisandhicittena saha pavattaututo utusamuṭṭhānaṃ suddhaṭṭhakaṃ pātubhavati. Paṭisandhicittaṃ pana rūpaṃ na samuṭṭhāpeti. Tañhi yathā papāte patitapuriso parassa paccayo hotuṃ na sakkoti, evaṃ vatthudubbalatāya dubbalattā rūpaṃ samuṭṭhāpetuṃ na sakkoti. Paṭisandhicittato pana uddhaṃ paṭhamabhavaṅgato pabhuti cittasamuṭṭhānaṃ suddhaṭṭhakaṃ, saddapātubhāvakāle paṭisandhikkhaṇato uddhaṃ pavattaututo ceva cittato ca saddanavakaṃ, ye pana kabaḷīkārāhārūpajīvino gabbhaseyyakasattā, tesaṃ, "yañcassa bhuñjati mātā, annaṃ pānañca bhojanaṃ; tena so tattha yāpeti, mātukucchigato naro"ti vacanato mātarā ajjhoharitāhārena anugate sarīre, opapātikānaṃ sabbapaṭhamaṃ attano mukhagataṃ kheḷaṃ ajjhoharaṇakāle āhārasamuṭṭhānaṃ suddhaṭṭhakanti idaṃ āhārasamuṭṭhānassa suddhaṭṭhakassa, utucittasamuṭṭhānānañca ukkaṃsato dvinnaṃ navakānaṃ vasena chabbīsatividhaṃ, pubbe ekekacittakkhaṇe tikkhattuṃ uppajjamānaṃ vuttaṃ kammasamuṭṭhānañca sattatividhanti channavutividhaṃ rūpaṃ, tayo ca arūpino khandhāti samāsato navanavuti dhammā. Yasmā vā saddo aniyato kadācideva pātubhāvato, tasmā duvidhampi taṃ apanetvā ime sattanavuti dhammā yathāsambhavaṃ sabbasattānaṃ viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpanti veditabbaṃ.

Yampanetamettha kammajarūpaṃ, taṃ bhavayonigatiṭhitisattāvāsesu sabbapaṭhamaṃ patiṭṭhahantampi tisamuṭṭhānikarūpena anupatthaddhaṃ na sakkoti saṇṭhātuṃ, nāpi tisamuṭṭhānikaṃ tena anupatthaddhaṃ. Atha kho vātabbhāhatāpi catuddisā vavatthāpitā naḷakalāpiyo viya, ūmivegabbhāhatāpi mahāsamudde katthaci laddhapatiṭṭhā bhinnavāhanikā viya ca aññamaññupatthaddhānevetāni apatamānāni saṇṭhahitvā ekampi vassaṃ dvepi vassāni…pe… vassasatampi yāva tesaṃ sattānaṃ āyukkhayo vā puññakkhayo vā, tāva pavattantīti. Evaṃ bhavādīsu pavattitopettha viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Ru-Yi Translation

In the section "dependent on consciousness, name-and-form": "by the division of name-and-form, by its occurrence among the states of being and the rest, by inclusion, and by the mode of conditioning — the discernment should be understood."

"By the division of name-and-form": herein, "name" is the three aggregates — feeling and the rest — since they bend toward their object; "form" is the four great elements and the matter derived from the four great elements. Their division has already been stated in the discussion of the aggregates. Thus, so far, the discernment by way of the division of name-and-form should be understood.

"By its occurrence among the states of being and the rest": herein, name occurs, excepting one abode of beings, in all states of being, wombs, destinations, stations of consciousness, and the remaining abodes of beings; form occurs in two states of being, four wombs, five destinations, the first four stations of consciousness, and five abodes of beings.

Now, this name-and-form occurring in this way: since, at the moment of rebirth-linking for the sexless womb-born and for the egg-born, two groups of continuity arise from matter, by way of the physical-basis-decad and the body-decad, together with the three formless aggregates — therefore, in detail, twenty items from matter, together with the three formless aggregates: these twenty-three items should be understood as "name-and-form conditioned by consciousness."

And since, for spontaneously-born beings such as those of the Brahmā's retinue, at the moment of rebirth-linking, four groups of continuity arise from matter, by way of the eye-, ear-, and physical-basis-decads, and the life-nonad, together with the three formless aggregates — therefore, in detail, thirty-nine items from matter, together with the three formless aggregates: these forty-two items should be understood as "name-and-form conditioned by consciousness."

But in the sense sphere, since, for the remaining spontaneously-born and moisture-born beings with sex faculty and complete sense bases, at the moment of rebirth-linking, seven groups of continuity arise from matter, together with the three formless aggregates — therefore, in detail, seventy items from matter, together with the three formless aggregates: these seventy-three items should be understood as "name-and-form conditioned by consciousness." This is the maximum. By way of the minimum, however, the count should be understood by successively reducing it according to whichever groups of continuity of matter are lacking in each particular case.

But for formless beings, there are simply the three formless aggregates. For non-percipient beings, there is simply the life-faculty-nonad, from matter. This, then, is the method at rebirth-linking.

But in the course of life, everywhere in the region where matter occurs, at the moment the rebirth-linking consciousness persists, a pure octad of temperature-produced matter arises, occurring together with the rebirth-linking consciousness. The rebirth-linking consciousness itself, however, does not produce matter. For, just as a man who has fallen over a precipice is unable to be a support for another, so too, on account of the weakness of its own weak physical basis, it is unable to produce matter. But from the rebirth-linking consciousness onward, starting with the first life-continuum, a pure octad of consciousness-produced matter arises; and, at the time sound appears, starting from the moment of rebirth-linking onward, a nonad of sound produced by the ongoing temperature and by consciousness. But for those beings born from a womb who live on edible food, it is said: "whatever food, drink, and nourishment his mother partakes of, by that the man in his mother's womb is sustained" — hence, once the body has been reached by the nourishment swallowed by the mother, a pure octad of nutriment-produced matter arises; and, for spontaneously-born beings, when they first swallow their own saliva that has come into their mouth, a pure octad of nutriment-produced matter likewise arises. Thus: the pure octad of nutriment-produced matter, together with, at the maximum, the two nonads of temperature- and consciousness-produced matter, making twenty-six kinds; together with the kamma-produced matter already stated as arising three times in each single mind-moment, of seventy kinds — ninety-six kinds of matter in all, together with the three formless aggregates: ninety-nine items in brief. Or, since sound is irregular, appearing only on occasion, by removing both nonads that include it, these ninety-seven items should be understood, as appropriate, as "name-and-form conditioned by consciousness" for all beings.

As for the kamma-produced matter here: even though it establishes itself first of all, in every state of being, womb, destination, station of consciousness, and abode of beings, it is unable to stand without being supported by the matter produced by the other three sources; nor can that matter produced by the other three sources stand without being supported by it. Rather, like bundles of reeds set upright on four sides, buffeted by the wind, or like shipwrecked sailors who have found some support amid the great ocean, buffeted by the force of the waves — just so, these, mutually supporting one another, stand without falling, and continue for one year, for two years … even for a hundred years, for as long as those beings' lifespan or their merit lasts. Thus, herein, the discernment by way of occurrence among the states of being and the rest should be understood.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

This is Abhidhamma embryology at its most exacting: the tradition insists that even the very first moment of a new life has a precise, countable material composition, varying systematically by womb and by realm — a sexless womb-born creature needs only two "decads" of matter (twenty items) to begin, while a fully-formed being needs seven (seventy items). None of this bookkeeping is decorative; it is the same insistence, seen throughout this chapter, that every step of the causal chain, right down to conception itself, is governed by exact, impersonal, discoverable law rather than by chance or by a soul's arbitrary choice of vessel.

The four-sources-of-matter framework introduced here — kamma, consciousness, temperature, and nutriment — becomes foundational for later chapters on insight meditation, where a meditator is trained to discern exactly which portion of their own body's matter arose from which of these four causes. The reed-bundle and shipwreck-survivor similes make vivid a point with real philosophical weight: none of the four kinds of matter could exist alone; the body a person calls "mine" is, at every moment, a temporary, mutual propping-up of four independently arising streams, none of which is more fundamentally "the body" than the others.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
santatisīsa

a group of continuity — santati (continuity, series) + sīsa (head); a bundled unit of matter (typically a decad) counted as one "head" in these enumerations of what arises at rebirth-linking.

kammaja / cittaja / utuja / āhāraja rūpa

kamma-produced, consciousness-produced, temperature-produced, and nutriment-produced matter — the four distinct origins of physical matter in Abhidhamma theory, all of which must operate together, mutually supporting one another, for a living body to persist.

Even Non-Percipient Beings Are "Conditioned by Consciousness"

A pointed objection now surfaces: how can name-and-form be said to be "conditioned by consciousness" among the non-percipient beings, who by definition have no consciousness at all? Buddhaghosa resolves this by distinguishing two senses of the conditioning consciousness itself — the resultant consciousness present in an ordinary rebirth, and the earlier, non-resultant volitional consciousness that produced the very kamma responsible for a non-percipient rebirth in the first place. The section then moves to a careful, technical accounting of exactly which Paṭṭhāna conditions link consciousness to name and to form, closing with a simple but elegant proof by inference: since consciousness visibly produces matter we can observe during life, it is reasonable to infer that the same consciousness also produces the unseen matter of rebirth-linking itself.

Pali Source

Saṅgahāti ettha ca yaṃ āruppe pavattipaṭisandhīsu, pañcavokārabhave ca pavattiyaṃ viññāṇapaccayā nāmameva, yañca asaññesu sabbattha, pañcavokārabhave ca pavattiyaṃ viññāṇapaccayā rūpameva, yañca pañcavokārabhave sabbattha viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpaṃ, taṃ sabbaṃ nāmañca rūpañca nāmarūpañca nāmarūpanti evaṃ ekadesasarūpekasesanayena saṅgahetvā viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpanti veditabbaṃ.

Asaññesu viññāṇābhāvā ayuttanti ce, nāyuttaṃ. Idampi, "nāmarūpassa yaṃ hetu, viññāṇaṃ taṃ dvidhā mataṃ; vipākamavipākañca, yuttameva yato idaṃ."

Yañhi nāmarūpassa hetu viññāṇaṃ, taṃ vipākāvipākabhedato dvedhā mataṃ. Idañca asaññasattesu kammasamuṭṭhānattā pañcavokārabhave pavattaabhisaṅkhāraviññāṇapaccayā rūpaṃ. Tathā pañcavokāre pavattiyaṃ kusalādicittakkhaṇe kammasamuṭṭhānanti yuttameva idaṃ. Evaṃ saṅgahatopettha viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Paccayanayāti ettha hi, "nāmassa pākaviññāṇaṃ, navadhā hoti paccayo; vatthurūpassa navadhā, sesarūpassa aṭṭhadhā. Abhisaṅkhāraviññāṇaṃ, hoti rūpassa ekadhā; tadaññaṃ pana viññāṇaṃ, tassa tassa yathārahaṃ."

Yañhetaṃ paṭisandhiyaṃ pavattiyaṃ vā vipākasaṅkhātaṃ nāmaṃ, tassa rūpamissassa vā amissassa vā paṭisandhikaṃ vā aññaṃ vā vipākaviññāṇaṃ sahajātaaññamaññanissayasampayuttavipākāhārindriyaatthiavigatapaccayehi navadhā paccayo hoti.

Vatthurūpassa paṭisandhiyaṃ sahajātaaññamaññanissayavipākāhārindriyavippayuttaatthiavigatapaccayehi navadhā paccayo hoti. Ṭhapetvā pana vatthurūpaṃ sesarūpassa imesu navasu aññamaññapaccayaṃ apanetvā sesehi aṭṭhahi paccayehi paccayo hoti.

Abhisaṅkhāraviññāṇaṃ pana asaññasattarūpassa vā pañcavokārabhave vā kammajassa rūpassa suttantikapariyāyato upanissayavasena ekadhāva paccayo hoti. Avasesaṃ paṭhamabhavaṅgato pabhuti sabbampi viññāṇaṃ tassa tassa nāmarūpassa yathārahaṃ paccayo hotīti veditabbaṃ.

Tattha siyā – kathaṃ panetaṃ jānitabbaṃ "paṭisandhināmarūpaṃ viññāṇapaccayā hotī"ti? Suttato yuttito ca. Sutte hi "cittānuparivattino dhammā"tiādinā nayena bahudhā vedanādīnaṃ viññāṇapaccayatā siddhā. Yuttito pana, "cittajena hi rūpena, idha diṭṭhena sijjhati; adiṭṭhassāpi rūpassa, viññāṇaṃ paccayo iti."

Citte hi pasanne appasanne vā tadanurūpāni rūpāni uppajjamānāni diṭṭhāni. Diṭṭhena ca adiṭṭhassa anumānaṃ hotīti iminā idha diṭṭhena cittajarūpena adiṭṭhassāpi paṭisandhirūpassa viññāṇaṃ paccayo hotīti jānitabbametaṃ. Kammasamuṭṭhānassāpi hi tassa cittasamuṭṭhānasseva viññāṇapaccayatā paṭṭhāne āgatāti. Evaṃ paccayanayatopettha viññātabbo vinicchayoti.

Ayaṃ "viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpa"nti padasmiṃ vitthārakathā.

Ru-Yi Translation

"By inclusion": herein, whatever is, at rebirth-linking and in the course of life in formless being, and in the course of life in five-constituent being, "name" alone, conditioned by consciousness; and whatever is, everywhere among non-percipient beings, and in the course of life in five-constituent being, "form" alone, conditioned by consciousness; and whatever is, everywhere in five-constituent being, "name-and-form," conditioned by consciousness — all of this, whether name, or form, or name-and-form, should be understood as "name-and-form conditioned by consciousness," having been included by the method of taking one representative term to stand, in part, for the rest.

If it should be said that this is improper, since there is no consciousness among non-percipient beings — it is not improper. This, too, is answered by the verse: "that consciousness which is the cause of name-and-form is held to be twofold — resultant and non-resultant; hence this is entirely proper."

For that consciousness which is the cause of name-and-form is held to be twofold, by the division of resultant and non-resultant. And this applies here: among non-percipient beings, since their matter is produced by kamma, it is form conditioned by the formation-consciousness that occurred during the previous life; likewise, in five-constituent being, matter produced by kamma at a moment of wholesome or other consciousness during the course of life — this, too, is entirely proper. Thus, herein, the discernment by way of inclusion should be understood.

"By the mode of conditioning": herein — "resultant consciousness is a condition for name in nine ways; for the physical-basis matter, in nine ways; for the remaining matter, in eight ways. The formation-consciousness is a condition for matter in one way; but other consciousness is a condition for this or that, as appropriate."

For that name called "resultant," occurring at rebirth-linking or in the course of life, whether mixed or unmixed with matter — for it, resultant consciousness is a condition in nine ways: by the conascence, mutuality, dependence, association, result, nutriment, faculty, presence, and non-disappearance conditions.

For the physical-basis matter, at rebirth-linking, it is a condition in nine ways: by the conascence, mutuality, dependence, result, nutriment, faculty, dissociation, presence, and non-disappearance conditions. But for the remaining matter, other than the physical-basis matter, removing the mutuality condition from these nine, it is a condition by the remaining eight conditions.

But the formation-consciousness is a condition, in the discourse method, for the matter of non-percipient beings, or for the kamma-produced matter in five-constituent being, in one way only — by the decisive support condition. All the remaining consciousness, from the first life-continuum onward, should be understood as a condition, as appropriate, for this or that name-and-form.

Here it might be asked: how, then, is this to be known — that "rebirth-linking name-and-form is conditioned by consciousness"? By scripture and by reasoning. For in the scriptures, by such passages as "states that follow along with consciousness," the state of being conditioned by consciousness is established, in many ways, for feeling and the rest. But by reasoning: "for it is established here, by consciousness-produced matter that is seen, that consciousness is a condition for matter that is not seen as well."

For, when the mind is serene or unserene, corresponding kinds of matter are seen to arise. And from what is seen, an inference can be drawn to what is not seen. By this reasoning, it should be known that, just as consciousness is a condition for consciousness-produced matter that is here seen, so too consciousness is a condition for rebirth-linking matter, which is not seen, kamma-produced though it is — for its being conditioned by that very same consciousness has come down in the Paṭṭhāna. Thus, herein, the discernment by way of the mode of conditioning should be understood.

This is the detailed discussion of the phrase "dependent on consciousness, name-and-form."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The inference from seen to unseen at the close of this section is a small but methodologically important moment: Buddhaghosa is aware that rebirth-linking matter cannot be directly observed the way a mind's visible effect on the body — a serene face, a tense posture — can be, and rather than simply asserting the doctrine on authority alone, he offers a reasoned argument by analogy for why it should be accepted. This same evidentiary move — inferring the unobservable from a structurally similar observable case — recurs throughout the tradition's defense of kamma and rebirth against skeptical challenge.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
abhisaṅkhāraviññāṇa

the formation-consciousness — abhisaṅkhāra (fashioning, kammic formation) + viññāṇa (consciousness); the earlier, non-resultant volitional consciousness of a past life whose kamma produces a present rebirth, distinguished here from ordinary present-moment consciousness.

diṭṭhena adiṭṭhassa anumāna

"an inference to what is not seen, from what is seen" — the explicit logical principle underlying the argument that consciousness conditions rebirth-linking matter, modeled on the observable fact that a person's mental state visibly affects their body's appearance.

Name-and-Form Conditions the Sixfold Base

The fourth link now receives the same rigorous treatment: name (the three mental aggregates) and form (the elements and physical bases) are traced, condition by condition, to the arising of the sixfold sense base. In formless being, only name conditions the mind-base, since there is no matter there at all; everywhere else, form conditions the five physical sense bases while both name and form together condition the sixth, the mind-base — each relationship precisely catalogued by which of the Paṭṭhāna's twenty-four conditions applies.

Pali Source

Nāmarūpapaccayā saḷāyatanapade – "nāmaṃ khandhattayaṃ rūpaṃ, bhūtavatthādikaṃ mataṃ; katekasesaṃ taṃ tassa, tādisasseva paccayo."

Yañhetaṃ saḷāyatanasseva paccayabhūtaṃ nāmarūpaṃ, tattha nāmanti vedanādikkhandhattayaṃ, rūpaṃ pana sasantatipariyāpannaṃ niyamato cattāri bhūtāni cha vatthūni jīvitindriyanti evaṃ bhūtavatthādikaṃ matanti veditabbaṃ. Taṃ pana nāmañca rūpañca nāmarūpañca nāmarūpanti evaṃ katekasesaṃ chaṭṭhāyatanañca saḷāyatanañca saḷāyatananti evaṃ katekasesasseva saḷāyatanassa paccayoti veditabbaṃ. Kasmā? Yasmā āruppe nāmameva paccayo, tañca chaṭṭhāyatanasseva na aññassa. "Nāmapaccayā chaṭṭhāyatana"nti hi vibhaṅge vuttaṃ.

Tattha siyā – kathaṃ panetaṃ jānitabbaṃ "nāmarūpaṃ saḷāyatanassa paccayo"ti? Nāmarūpabhāve bhāvato. Tassa tassa hi nāmassa rūpassa ca bhāve taṃ taṃ āyatanaṃ hoti, na aññathā. Sā panassa tabbhāvabhāvitā paccayanayasmiṃ yeva āvibhavissati. Tasmā, "paṭisandhiyā pavatte vā, hoti yaṃ yassa paccayo; yathā ca paccayo hoti, tathā neyyaṃ vibhāvinā."

Tatrāyamatthadīpanā – "nāmameva hi āruppe, paṭisandhipavattisu; paccayo sattadhā chadhā, hoti taṃ avakaṃsato."

Kathaṃ? Paṭisandhiyaṃ tāva avakaṃsato sahajātaaññamaññanissayasampayuttavipākaatthiavigatapaccayehi sattadhā nāmaṃ chaṭṭhāyatanassa paccayo hoti.

Pavattepi vipākaṃ vuttanayeneva paccayo hoti, itaraṃ pana avakaṃsato vuttappakāresu paccayesu vipākapaccayavajjehi chahi paccayehi paccayo hoti.

"Aññasmimpi bhave nāmaṃ, tatheva paṭisandhiyaṃ; chaṭṭhassa itaresaṃ taṃ, chahākārehi paccayo."

Āruppato hi aññasmimpi pañcavokārabhave taṃ vipākanāmaṃ hadayavatthuno sahāyaṃ hutvā chaṭṭhassa manāyatanassa yathā āruppe vuttaṃ, tatheva avakaṃsato sattadhā paccayo hoti. Itaresaṃ pana taṃ pañcannaṃ cakkhāyatanādīnaṃ catumahābhūtasahāyaṃ hutvā sahajātanissayavipākavippayuttaatthiavigatavasena chahākārehi paccayo hoti.

"Pavattepi tathā hoti, pākaṃ pākassa paccayo; apākaṃ avipākassa, chadhā chaṭṭhassa paccayo."

Pavattepi hi pañcavokārabhave yathā paṭisandhiyaṃ, tatheva vipākanāmaṃ vipākassa chaṭṭhāyatanassa avakaṃsato sattadhā paccayo hoti. Avipākaṃ pana avipākassa chaṭṭhassa avakaṃsatova tato vipākapaccayaṃ apanetvā chadhā paccayo hoti.

"Tattheva sesapañcannaṃ, vipākaṃ paccayo bhave; catudhā avipākampi, evameva pakāsitaṃ."

Tattheva hi pavatte sesānaṃ cakkhāyatanādīnaṃ pañcannaṃ cakkhupasādādivatthukaṃ itarampi vipākanāmaṃ pacchājātavippayuttaatthiavigatapaccayehi catudhā paccayo hoti. Yathā ca vipākaṃ, avipākampi evameva pakāsitaṃ. Evaṃ tāva nāmameva paṭisandhiyaṃ pavatte vā yassa yassa āyatanassa paccayo hoti, yathā ca paccayo hoti, tathā veditabbaṃ.

"Rūpaṃ panettha āruppe, bhave bhavati paccayo; na ekāyatanassāpi, pañcakkhandhabhave pana."

"Rūpato sandhiyaṃ vatthu, chadhā chaṭṭhassa paccayo; bhūtāni catudhā honti, pañcannaṃ avisesato."

Rūpato hi paṭisandhiyaṃ vatthurūpaṃ chaṭṭhassa manāyatanassa sahajātaaññamaññanissayavippayuttaatthiavigatapaccayehi chadhā paccayo hoti. Cattāri pana bhūtāni avisesato paṭisandhiyaṃ pavatte ca yaṃ yaṃ āyatanaṃ uppajjati, tassa tassa vasena pañcannampi cakkhāyatanādīnaṃ sahajātanissayaatthiavigatapaccayehi catudhā paccayā honti.

"Tidhā jīvitametesaṃ, āhāro ca pavattiyaṃ; tāneva chadhā chaṭṭhassa, vatthu tasseva pañcadhā."

Etesaṃ pana cakkhādīnaṃ pañcannaṃ paṭisandhiyaṃ pavatte ca atthi avigataindriyavasena rūpajīvitaṃ tidhā paccayo hoti. Āhāro ca atthiavigatāhāravasena tividhā paccayo hoti, so ca kho ye sattā āhārūpajīvino, tesaṃ āhārānugate kāye pavattiyaṃyeva, no paṭisandhiyaṃ. Tāni pana pañca cakkhāyatanādīni chaṭṭhassa cakkhu sotaghānajivhākāyaviññāṇasaṅkhātassa manāyatanassa nissayapurejātaindriyavippayuttaatthiavigatavasena chahākārehi paccayā honti pavatte, no paṭisandhiyaṃ. Ṭhapetvā pana pañcaviññāṇāni tasseva avasesamanāyatanassa vatthurūpaṃ nissayapurejātavippayuttaatthiavigatavasena pañcadhā paccayo hoti pavatteyeva, no paṭisandhiyaṃ. Evaṃ rūpameva paṭisandhiyaṃ pavatte vā yassa yassa āyatanassa paccayo hoti, yathā ca paccayo hoti, tathā veditabbaṃ.

"Nāmarūpaṃ panubhayaṃ, hoti yaṃ yassa paccayo; yathā ca tampi sabbattha, viññātabbaṃ vibhāvinā."

Seyyathidaṃ. Paṭisandhiyaṃ tāva pañcavokārabhave khandhattayavatthurūpasaṅkhātaṃ nāmarūpaṃ chaṭṭhāyatanassa sahajātaaññamaññanissayavipākasampayuttavippayuttaatthiavigatapaccayādīhi paccayo hotīti. Idamettha mukhamattaṃ. Vuttanayānusārena pana sakkā sabbaṃ yojetunti na ettha vitthāro dassitoti.

Ayaṃ "nāmarūpapaccayā saḷāyatana"nti padasmiṃ vitthārakathā.

Ru-Yi Translation

In the section "dependent on name-and-form, the sixfold base": "name is held to be the three aggregates; form, the elements, the physical bases, and the rest; that, having been made to stand as one representative term, is a condition for that very same kind of thing."

That name-and-form which is the condition specifically for the sixfold base: herein, "name" is the three aggregates — feeling and the rest; "form," however, is understood to be included within one's own continuity, invariably the four elements, the six physical bases, and the life-faculty. This, whether name, or form, or name-and-form, having been made to stand, by one representative term, for the rest, should be understood as a condition specifically for the sixfold base — itself, likewise, made to stand, whether the sixth base alone or the sixfold base, by one representative term, for the other. Why? Because, in formless being, name alone is the condition, and that only for the sixth base, not for any other. For it is said in the Analysis: "dependent on name, the sixth base."

Here it might be asked: how, then, is this to be known — that "name-and-form is a condition for the sixfold base"? By this or that base's presence depending on the presence of this or that name and form. But this dependent-arising will become clear only within the mode of conditioning itself. Therefore — "whether at rebirth-linking or in the course of life, it should be understood by the discerning one which is a condition for which, and how it is a condition."

Herein, this is the clarification of the meaning — "in formless being, at both rebirth-linking and in the course of life, name alone is a condition, in seven ways, or, at the minimum, in six ways."

How so? At rebirth-linking, first, at the minimum, name is a condition for the sixth base in seven ways: by the conascence, mutuality, dependence, association, result, presence, and non-disappearance conditions.

In the course of life, too, the resultant name is a condition in just the way already stated; but the other, non-resultant name, at the minimum, is a condition by the six conditions already stated, excepting the result condition.

"In another state of being too, name, in just the same way, is a condition, at rebirth-linking, for the sixth base; for the other bases, it is a condition in six ways."

For, other than in formless being — that is, in five-constituent being as well — that resultant name, in partnership with the heart-basis, is a condition for the sixth base, the mind-base, at the minimum, in seven ways, in just the way stated for formless being. But for the other five bases — the eye-base and the rest — in partnership with the four great elements, it is a condition in six ways: by the conascence, dependence, result, dissociation, presence, and non-disappearance conditions.

"In the course of life, too, it is the same: the resultant is a condition for the resultant; the non-resultant is a condition for the non-resultant sixth base, in six ways."

For, in the course of life too, in five-constituent being, resultant name is, at the minimum, a condition for the resultant sixth base in seven ways, just as at rebirth-linking. But non-resultant name is a condition for the non-resultant sixth base, at the minimum, in six ways, removing the result condition from those seven.

"There, too, the resultant is a condition for the remaining five bases in four ways; the non-resultant, too, is declared in just the same way."

For there too, in the course of life, resultant name, as well as the other kind, having as its basis the eye-sensitivity and the rest, is a condition for the remaining five bases — the eye-base and the rest — in four ways: by the postnascence, dissociation, presence, and non-disappearance conditions. And just as the resultant is declared, so too is the non-resultant declared in exactly the same way. Thus, so far, it should be understood which base name alone, at rebirth-linking or in the course of life, is a condition for, and how it is a condition.

"Form, herein, in formless being, is not a condition for even a single base; but in five-aggregate being, it is."

"From matter, at rebirth-linking, the physical basis is a condition for the sixth base in six ways; the elements are, without distinction, a condition for the five bases in four ways."

For, from matter, at rebirth-linking, the physical-basis matter is a condition for the sixth base, the mind-base, in six ways: by the conascence, mutuality, dependence, dissociation, presence, and non-disappearance conditions. But the four elements, without distinction, at rebirth-linking and in the course of life, are, according to whichever base arises, a condition for all five bases — the eye-base and the rest — in four ways: by the conascence, dependence, presence, and non-disappearance conditions.

"The life-faculty is a condition for these in three ways; and nutriment, in the course of life; those same five bases are a condition for the sixth base in six ways; the physical basis is a condition for that same sixth base in five ways."

For these five — the eye-base and the rest — at rebirth-linking and in the course of life, material life is a condition in three ways: by the presence, non-disappearance, and faculty conditions. And nutriment is a condition in three ways: by the presence, non-disappearance, and nutriment conditions — and this only for those beings who live on nutriment, only once nutriment has reached the body in the course of life, not at rebirth-linking. But those same five bases — the eye-base and the rest — are a condition for the sixth base, the mind-base classed as eye-, ear-, nose-, tongue-, and body-consciousness, in six ways: by the dependence, prenascence, faculty, dissociation, presence, and non-disappearance conditions, only in the course of life, not at rebirth-linking. But, excepting the five sense-consciousnesses, the physical-basis matter is a condition for the remaining mind-base itself in five ways: by the dependence, prenascence, dissociation, presence, and non-disappearance conditions, only in the course of life, not at rebirth-linking. Thus, it should be understood which base form alone, at rebirth-linking or in the course of life, is a condition for, and how it is a condition.

"And name-and-form together — which is a condition for which, and how, everywhere — this too should be understood by the discerning one."

For example: at rebirth-linking, first, in five-constituent being, name-and-form — called the three aggregates together with the physical-basis matter — is a condition for the sixth base by such conditions as conascence, mutuality, dependence, result, association, dissociation, presence, and non-disappearance. This, here, is merely the outline. But since it is possible to work out everything in accordance with the method already stated, the full detail is not shown here.

This is the detailed discussion of the phrase "dependent on name-and-form, the sixfold base."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

Buried in this thicket of condition-counting is one clean, memorable structural point: name and form divide their labor with almost architectural precision. Name alone reaches the sixth base — the mind — in every realm, even formless ones where matter is entirely absent; form alone reaches the five physical bases, but only where matter exists at all; and only where both are present together, in five-constituent being, do they jointly condition the mind-base as a genuine partnership, mind supplying the associative and resultant conditions, the physical heart-basis supplying dependence and support. The elaborate arithmetic is the tradition's way of making that clean division fully explicit and checkable, condition by condition, rather than leaving it as an unexamined generality.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
hadayavatthu

the heart-basis — the physical support for mind-element and mind-consciousness-element posited by the Abhidhamma, partnered here with resultant name as a joint condition for the mind-base.

ukkaṃsato / avakaṃsato

at the maximum / at the minimum — a recurring qualifier throughout this section's condition-counting, since the exact number of applicable conditions can vary slightly depending on circumstances such as whether the root or nutriment condition happens to apply in a given case.

The Sixfold Base Conditions Contact

The fifth link shows how contact — thirty-two kinds in detail, mirroring the resultant consciousnesses already catalogued — arises from the sixfold base. A genuine grammatical puzzle opens the discussion: the canonical phrase uses "the sixfold base" in the singular, as though a single set of six bases produced a single contact, when in fact each specific contact (eye-contact, ear-contact, and so on) arises from its own distinct combination of several bases at once — never from all six together, and never from just one alone.

Pali Source

Saḷāyatanapaccayā phassapade – "saḷeva phassā saṅkhepā, cakkhusamphassaādayo; viññāṇamiva bāttiṃsa, vitthārena bhavanti te."

Saṅkhepena hi saḷāyatanapaccayā phassoti cakkhusamphasso, sotasamphasso, ghānasamphasso, jivhāsamphasso, kāyasamphasso, manosamphassoti ime cakkhusamphassādayo cha eva phassā bhavanti. Vitthārena pana cakkhusamphassādayo pañca kusalavipākā, pañca akusalavipākāti dasa, sesā bāvīsati-lokiyavipākaviññāṇasampayuttā ca bāvīsatīti evaṃ sabbepi saṅkhārapaccayā vuttaviññāṇamiva bāttiṃsa honti.

Yaṃ panetassa bāttiṃsavidhassāpi phassassa paccayo saḷāyatanaṃ, tattha, "chaṭṭhena saha ajjhattaṃ, cakkhādiṃ bāhirehipi; saḷāyatanamicchanti, chahi saddhiṃ vicakkhaṇā."
