Tattha ye tāva "upādiṇṇakapavattikathā ayaṃ"ti sakasantatipariyāpannameva paccayaṃ paccayuppannañca dīpenti, te "chaṭṭhāyatanapaccayā phasso"ti pāḷianusārato āruppe chaṭṭhāyatanañca, aññattha sabbasaṅgahato saḷāyatanañca phassassa paccayoti ekadesasarūpekasesaṃ katvā chaṭṭhena saha ajjhattaṃ cakkhādiṃ saḷāyatananti icchanti.

Ye pana paccayuppannameva ekasantatipariyāpannaṃ dīpenti, paccayaṃ pana bhinnasantānampi, te yaṃ yaṃ āyatanaṃ phassassa paccayo hoti, taṃ sabbampi dīpentā bāhirampi pariggahetvā tadeva chaṭṭhena saha ajjhattaṃ bāhirehipi rūpāyatanādīhi saddhiṃ saḷāyatananti icchanti.

Etthāha – na sabbāyatanehi eko phasso sambhoti, nāpi ekamhā āyatanā sabbe phassā, ayañca saḷāyatanapaccayā phassoti ekova vutto, so kasmāti. Tatridaṃ vissajjanaṃ – saccametaṃ, sabbehi eko, ekamhā vā sabbe na sambhonti, sambhoti pana anekehi eko. Yathā cakkhusamphasso cakkhāyatanā rūpāyatanā cakkhuviññāṇasaṅkhātā manāyatanā avasesasampayuttadhammāyatanā cāti evaṃ sabbattha yathānurūpaṃ yojetabbaṃ. Tasmā eva hi, "ekopanekāyatanappabhavo iti dīpito; phassoyaṃ ekavacananiddesenīdha tādinā."

Ekavacananiddesenāti saḷāyatanapaccayā phassoti iminā ekavacananiddesena anekehi āyatanehi eko phasso hotīti tādinā dīpitoti attho. Āyatanesu pana, "chadhā pañca tato ekaṃ, navadhā bāhirāni cha; yathāsambhavametassa, paccayatte vibhāvaye."

Tatrāyaṃ vibhāvanā – cakkhāyatanādīni tāva pañca cakkhusamphassādibhedato pañcavidhassa phassassa nissayapurejātindriyavippayuttaatthiavigatavasena chadhā paccayā honti. Tato paraṃ ekaṃ vipākamanāyatanaṃ anekabhedassa vipākamanosamphassassa sahajātaaññamaññanissayavipākāhāraindriyasampayuttaatthiavigatavasena navadhā paccayo hoti. Bāhiresu pana rūpāyatanaṃ cakkhusamphassassa ārammaṇapurejātaatthiavigatavasena catudhā paccayo hoti. Tathā saddāyatanādīni sotasamphassādīnaṃ. Manosamphassassa pana tāni ca dhammāyatanañca tathā ca ārammaṇapaccayamatteneva cāti evaṃ bāhirāni cha yathāsambhavametassa paccayatte vibhāvayeti.

Ayaṃ "saḷāyatanapaccayā phasso"ti padasmiṃ vitthārakathā.

Ru-Yi Translation

In the section "dependent on the sixfold base, contact": "in brief, contacts are simply six — eye-contact and the rest; in detail, they become thirty-two, just like consciousness."

In brief, "contact conditioned by the sixfold base" is these six alone: eye-contact, ear-contact, nose-contact, tongue-contact, body-contact, and mind-contact. In detail, however, eye-contact and the rest are ten — five wholesome-resultant and five unwholesome-resultant — and the remaining twenty-two are those associated with the twenty-two mundane resultant states of consciousness already described; thus all of them together become thirty-two, just like the consciousness already described as conditioned by the formations.

As for the sixfold base which is the condition for this thirty-two-fold contact: herein — "the wise hold 'the sixfold base' to mean the internal eye and the rest together with the sixth, or, together with the six external ones as well."

Herein, those who explain that "this is a discourse on the occurrence of what has been clung to by kamma," showing the condition and the conditioned-arisen as both included within one's own single continuity — they, following the canonical text "contact conditioned by the sixth base," hold that, in formless being, the sixth base alone, and elsewhere, the sixfold base by way of full inclusion, is the condition for contact; and, having made this a case of one representative term standing for the rest, they wish "the sixfold base" to mean the internal eye and the rest together with the sixth.

But those who explain that only the conditioned-arisen is included within a single continuity, while the condition may belong even to a different continuity — they, explaining every single base whatsoever that is a condition for contact, including even the external ones, wish "the sixfold base" to mean the internal eye and the rest together with the sixth, as well as the external visible-form base and the rest.

Here it might be asked: no single contact comes to be from all the bases together, nor do all contacts come from a single base — so why is it said, as though it were one, "contact conditioned by the sixfold base"? Here is the answer: this is true; not all contacts come to be from all the bases, nor all from a single one — but a single one does come to be from several. Just as eye-contact comes to be from the eye-base, the visible-form base, the mind-base classed as eye-consciousness, and the remaining associated-mental-state base — so it should be construed everywhere, as appropriate. Precisely for this reason — "this contact is shown, by the Blessed One, to arise from several bases, expressed here, by him, in the singular number."

"Expressed in the singular number": the meaning is that, by this singular expression "contact conditioned by the sixfold base," it is shown by the Blessed One that a single contact comes to be from several bases. As for the bases: "five internal bases are, in six ways; from that, one internal base is, in nine ways; the six external ones, as appropriate — this should clarify their status as conditions."

Herein, this is the clarification: the eye-base and the other four bases are, first, a condition for the fivefold contact — eye-contact and the rest — in six ways: by the dependence, prenascence, faculty, dissociation, presence, and non-disappearance conditions. Beyond that, the single resultant mind-base is a condition for the resultant mind-contact of manifold kinds in nine ways: by the conascence, mutuality, dependence, result, nutriment, faculty, association, presence, and non-disappearance conditions. Among the external bases, the visible-form base is a condition for eye-contact in four ways: by the object, prenascence, presence, and non-disappearance conditions. Likewise the sound base and the rest are a condition for ear-contact and the rest. But for mind-contact, those same external bases, and the mental-object base as well, are conditions in the same way, and also simply by way of the object condition alone. Thus the six external bases should clarify, as appropriate, their status as conditions for this.

This is the detailed discussion of the phrase "dependent on the sixfold base, contact."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The two rival interpretations of what "the sixfold base" even refers to here are not a minor technicality but reflect two different, both legitimate, ways of framing dependent origination as a whole: one view keeps every step of the chain strictly internal to a single mental-physical continuity (so only the internal bases count, since the external world doesn't belong to "this" continuity); the other allows a condition to belong to a different continuity from its result (so the external color, sound, and so on that a person actually perceives are included too, since without them no contact could occur at all). Buddhaghosa lets both readings stand rather than declaring a winner — a good example of this commentarial genre tolerating more than one valid analytical lens on the same textual formula.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
ekavacananiddesa

expression in the singular number — eka (one) + vacana (grammatical number) + niddesa (expression); the grammatical puzzle motivating this whole section, since the canonical formula speaks of "the sixfold base" as though it were a single unified condition.

upādiṇṇaka

what has been clung to — upādiṇṇa (clung to, appropriated) + -ka; a technical Abhidhamma term for matter bound up with a living being's own kammically-produced continuity, central to the first interpretive camp's argument that only internal bases count as conditions here.

Contact Conditions Feeling

The sixth link is treated more briefly: feeling, sorted by door into the familiar six kinds and by subdivision into eighty-nine, is narrowed down to the thirty-two resultant feelings relevant to this chain, each traced to its conditioning contact — full, eightfold conascent conditioning in the five sense-doors and at the moment of registration in the mind-door, but only the single, weaker decisive-support condition for feelings occurring during the intervening stages of receiving and investigating.

Pali Source

Phassapaccayā vedanāpade – "dvārato vedanā vuttā, cakkhusamphassajādikā; saḷeva tā pabhedena, ekūnanavutī matā."

Etassapi padassa vibhaṅge "cakkhusamphassajā vedanā. Sota… ghāna… jivhā… kāya… manosamphassajā vedanā"ti evaṃ dvārato saḷeva vedanā vuttā, tā pana pabhedena ekūnanavutiyā cittehi sampayuttattā ekūnanavuti matā.

"Vedanāsu panetāsu, idha bāttiṃsa vedanā; vipākacittayuttāva, adhippetāti bhāsitā."

"Aṭṭhadhā tattha pañcannaṃ, pañcadvāramhi paccayo; sesānaṃ ekadhā phasso, manodvārepi so tathā."

Tattha hi pañcadvāre cakkhupasādādivatthukānaṃ pañcannaṃ vedanānaṃ cakkhusamphassādiko phasso sahajātaaññamaññanissayavipākaāhārasampayuttaatthiavigatavasena aṭṭhadhā paccayo hoti. Sesānaṃ pana ekekasmiṃ dvāre sampaṭicchanasantīraṇatadārammaṇavasena pavattānaṃ kāmāvacaravipākavedanānaṃ so cakkhusamphassādiko phasso upanissayavasena ekadhāva paccayo hoti.

Manodvārepi so tathāti manodvārepi hi tadārammaṇavasena pavattānaṃ kāmāvacaravipākavedanānaṃ so sahajātamanosamphassasaṅkhāto phasso tatheva aṭṭhadhā paccayo hoti, paṭisandhibhavaṅgacutivasena pavattānaṃ tebhūmakavipākavedanānampi. Yā pana tā manodvāre tadārammaṇavasena pavattā kāmāvacaravedanā, tāsaṃ manodvārāvajjanasampayutto manosamphasso upanissayavasena ekadhāva paccayo hotīti.

Ayaṃ "phassapaccayā vedanā"ti padasmiṃ vitthārakathā.

Ru-Yi Translation

In the section "dependent on contact, feeling": "feeling, described by way of door, is simply six — that born of eye-contact and the rest; by way of subdivision, these are held to be eighty-nine."

For this section, too, in the Analysis, it is said: "feeling born of eye-contact. Feeling born of ear-, nose-, tongue-, body-, mind-contact" — thus, by way of door, feeling is said to be simply six; but, by subdivision, since it is associated with eighty-nine kinds of consciousness, it is held to be eighty-nine.

"But among these feelings, here it is the thirty-two feelings associated specifically with resultant consciousness that are meant, so it is said."

"There, in the five sense doors, contact is a condition for the five kinds of feeling in eight ways; for the rest, contact is a condition in one way; in the mind-door too, it is the same."

Herein, in the five sense-doors, contact — eye-contact and the rest — is a condition for the five kinds of feeling having the eye-sensitivity and the other physical bases in eight ways: by the conascence, mutuality, dependence, result, nutriment, association, presence, and non-disappearance conditions. But for the remaining sense-sphere resultant feelings, occurring in each single door by way of receiving, investigating, and registering, that same contact is a condition in one way only, by the decisive support condition.

"In the mind-door too, it is the same": for, in the mind-door too, that conascent mind-contact is likewise a condition, in eight ways, for the sense-sphere resultant feelings occurring by way of registration, and also for the three-plane resultant feelings occurring by way of rebirth-linking, life-continuum, and decease. But for those sense-sphere feelings occurring in the mind-door by way of registration, the mind-contact associated with mind-door-adverting is a condition in one way only, by the decisive support condition.

This is the detailed discussion of the phrase "dependent on contact, feeling."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The sharp contrast between "eight ways" and "one way only" tracks a distinction this chapter has already made much of elsewhere: conascent conditions are powerful and multiple precisely because contact and feeling arise together, in the very same moment, tightly bound by shared basis and object; but where feeling arises at a later stage of the same cognitive process — receiving, investigating, registering — its immediate conascent contact is a different, later moment of contact altogether, so the earlier triggering contact can only reach it indirectly, through the weaker, non-conascent decisive support condition.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
ekūnanavuti

eighty-nine — the full canonical count of consciousness in Abhidhamma classification; feeling, being always associated with some consciousness, is here said to divide into the same eighty-nine kinds before being narrowed to the thirty-two resultant kinds relevant to this chain.

Feeling Conditions Craving: One Hundred and Eight Kinds, Reduced to Three

The seventh link receives a much fuller treatment. Craving for each of the six sense objects further divides into craving for sensual pleasure, craving for being (lust bound up with the eternalist view), and craving for non-being (lust bound up with the annihilationist view) — eighteen kinds, doubled for internal and external objects, tripled again across past, present, and future, yielding the traditional figure of one hundred and eight, before Buddhaghosa reduces it back to its essential three or six. A vivid, homely image — people lavishing honors on painters, musicians, and cooks purely because of the pleasant feelings these providers make possible — grounds the abstract claim that all craving, ultimately, is conditioned by feeling.

Pali Source

Vedanāpaccayā taṇhāpade – "rūpataṇhādibhedena, cha taṇhā idha dīpitā; ekekā tividhā tattha, pavattākārato matā."

Imasmiṃ hi pade seṭṭhiputto brāhmaṇaputtoti pitito nāmavasena putto viya "rūpataṇhā. Sadda… gandha… rasa… phoṭṭhabba… dhammataṇhā"ti ārammaṇato nāmavasena vibhaṅge cha taṇhā dīpitā.

Tāsu pana taṇhāsu ekekā taṇhā pavattiākārato kāmataṇhā, bhavataṇhā, vibhavataṇhāti evaṃ tividhā matā. Rūpataṇhāyeva hi yadā cakkhussa āpāthamāgataṃ rūpārammaṇaṃ kāmassādavasena assādayamānā pavattati, tadā kāmataṇhā nāma hoti. Yadā tadevārammaṇaṃ "dhuvaṃ sassata"nti pavattāya sassatadiṭṭhiyā saddhiṃ pavattati, tadā bhavataṇhā nāma hoti. Sassatadiṭṭhisahagato hi rāgo bhavataṇhāti vuccati. Yadā pana tadevārammaṇaṃ "ucchijjati vinassatī"ti pavattāya ucchedadiṭṭhiyā saddhiṃ pavattati, tadā vibhavataṇhā nāma hoti. Ucchedadiṭṭhisahagato hi rāgo vibhavataṇhāti vuccati. Esa nayo saddataṇhādīsupīti. Etā aṭṭhārasa taṇhā honti.

Tā ajjhattarūpādīsu aṭṭhārasa, bahiddhā aṭṭhārasāti chattiṃsa. Iti atītā chattiṃsa, anāgatā chattiṃsa, paccuppannā chattiṃsāti aṭṭhasataṃ taṇhā honti. Tā puna saṅkheppamāṇā rūpādiārammaṇavasena cha, kāmataṇhādivasena vā tissova taṇhā hontīti veditabbā.

Yasmā panime sattā puttaṃ assādetvā putte mamattena dhātiyā viya rūpādiārammaṇavasena uppajjamānaṃ vedanaṃ assādetvā vedanāya mamattena rūpādiārammaṇadāyakānaṃ cittakāra-gandhabba-gandhika-sūda-tantavāyarasāyanavidhāyakavejjādīnaṃ mahāsakkāraṃ karonti. Tasmā sabbāpesā vedanāpaccayā taṇhā hotīti veditabbā.

"Yasmā cettha adhippetā, vipākasukhavedanā; ekāva ekadhāvesā, tasmā taṇhāya paccayo."

Ekadhāti upanissayapaccayeneva paccayo hoti. Yasmā vā, "dukkhī sukhaṃ patthayati, sukhī bhiyyopi icchati; upekkhā pana santattā, sukhamicceva bhāsitā. Taṇhāya paccayā tasmā, honti tissopi vedanā; vedanāpaccayā taṇhā, iti vuttā mahesinā. Vedanāpaccayā cāpi, yasmā nānusayaṃ vinā; hoti tasmā na sā hoti, brāhmaṇassa vusīmatoti."

Ayaṃ "vedanāpaccayā taṇhā"ti padasmiṃ vitthārakathā.

Ru-Yi Translation

In the section "dependent on feeling, craving": "here, craving is shown by way of the division into craving for visible forms and the rest — six kinds; each single one of these, by way of its mode of occurring, is held to be threefold."

For in this section, just as a son is named after his father — "the merchant's son," "the brahmin's son" — so too, in the Analysis, craving is shown as sixfold, named after its object: "craving for visible forms. Craving for sounds … odors … tastes … tangibles … mental objects."

But among these cravings, each single craving, by way of its mode of occurring, is held to be threefold: craving for sensual pleasure, craving for being, and craving for non-being. For when craving for visible forms occurs, relishing a visible-form object that has come into the eye's range by way of sensual gratification, then it is called "craving for sensual pleasure." When that same object occurs together with the eternalist view occurring as "it is permanent, eternal," then it is called "craving for being" — for lust accompanied by the eternalist view is called "craving for being." But when that same object occurs together with the annihilationist view occurring as "it is cut off, it perishes," then it is called "craving for non-being" — for lust accompanied by the annihilationist view is called "craving for non-being." The same method applies to craving for sounds and the rest. These make eighteen kinds of craving.

These eighteen, with respect to internal visible forms and the rest, and eighteen more, with respect to external ones, make thirty-six. Thus, thirty-six with respect to the past, thirty-six with respect to the future, and thirty-six with respect to the present make one hundred and eight kinds of craving. These, reduced again to their essential measure, should be understood as simply six, by way of the objects — visible forms and the rest — or simply three, by way of craving for sensual pleasure and the rest.

And since these beings, just as a wet-nurse, having relished a child, and out of possessiveness for that child, cares for it devotedly — so too, having relished the feeling that arises by way of a visible-form or other object, and out of possessiveness for that feeling, render great honor to painters, musicians, perfumers, cooks, weavers, and physicians skilled in preparing tonics, and the like — all these providers of a visible-form or other object — therefore it should be understood that all of this craving, in every instance, is conditioned by feeling.

"And since, here, only resultant pleasant feeling is meant — a single kind — it is a condition for craving in one way only."

"In one way": it is a condition solely by the decisive support condition. Or, since — "one who suffers longs for pleasure; one who is happy desires still more; but equanimity, being peaceful, is spoken of as pleasure itself. Therefore, all three feelings alike are conditions for craving; and craving is said, by the Great Sage, to be conditioned by feeling. And since craving, conditioned by feeling, does not occur without the underlying tendency, it therefore does not occur for the brahmin who has completed his task."

This is the detailed discussion of the phrase "dependent on feeling, craving."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The closing verse's final line is easy to read past but carries real weight: craving, conditioned by feeling, "does not occur without the underlying tendency" — the latent, dormant disposition toward lust that an ordinary person carries in every moment, even when not actively feeling desire. This is why an arahant — "the brahmin who has completed his task" — can experience the very same pleasant, painful, and neutral feelings as anyone else, since feeling itself continues as long as the body lives, and yet no craving whatsoever arises in response, because the underlying tendency that would normally convert feeling into craving has been permanently uprooted. Feeling conditions craving only where the latent disposition to crave remains; remove that disposition, and the causal chain simply stops at this very link.

The wet-nurse and honored-craftsmen images work together to make an easily overlooked point: craving is not really about visible forms, sounds, tastes, and the rest at all — people do not, at bottom, want colors or sounds for their own sake, but the pleasant feeling those objects make possible, which is why they will pay lavishly for whoever can reliably produce that feeling, whatever the specific sensory channel happens to be.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
kāmataṇhā / bhavataṇhā / vibhavataṇhā

craving for sensual pleasure / craving for being / craving for non-being — the canonical threefold division of craving, distinguished here by which wrong view, if any, accompanies the underlying lust for a given object.

anusaya

the underlying tendency — a latent, dormant disposition toward a defilement (here, specifically lust) that persists even when the defilement is not actively manifest; its permanent removal is what allows an arahant to feel without craving.

Craving Conditions Clinging: Four Kinds, and Why They Come in This Order

The eighth link works through the four kinds of clinging — to sensual pleasure, to views, to rules and vows, and to a doctrine of self — giving each a careful etymology before asking three further questions any thorough treatment demands: what, in brief and in detail, actually constitutes each one; in what order do they naturally arise within a single life; in what order are they abandoned on the path; and in what order does the Buddha choose to teach them. The chapter's answer to the last question is unexpectedly practical: clinging to sensual pleasure is taught first simply because it is the most obvious and common, associated with the widest range of consciousness, while the subtler doctrine of a self is saved for last.

Pali Source

Taṇhāpaccayā upādānapade – "upādānāni cattāri, tāni atthavibhāgato; dhammasaṅkhepavitthārā, kamato ca vibhāvaye."

Tatrāyaṃ vibhāvanā – kāmupādānaṃ, diṭṭhupādānaṃ, sīlabbatupādānaṃ, attavādupādānanti imāni tāvettha cattāri upādānāni. Tesaṃ ayaṃ atthavibhāgo – vatthusaṅkhātaṃ kāmaṃ upādiyatīti kāmupādānaṃ, kāmo ca so upādānañcātipi kāmupādānaṃ. Upādānanti daḷhaggahaṇaṃ. Daḷhattho hettha upasaddo upāyāsaupakaṭṭhādīsu viya. Tathā diṭṭhi ca sā upādānañcāti diṭṭhupādānaṃ. Diṭṭhiṃ upādiyatīti vā diṭṭhupādānaṃ. "Sassato attā ca loko cā"tiādīsu hi purimadiṭṭhiṃ uttaradiṭṭhi upādiyati. Tathā sīlabbataṃ upādiyatīti sīlabbatupādānaṃ. Sīlabbatañca taṃ upādānañcātipi sīlabbatupādānaṃ. Gosīlagovatādīni hi "evaṃ suddhī"ti abhinivesato sayameva upādānāni. Tathā vadanti etenāti vādo. Upādiyanti etenāti upādānaṃ. Kiṃ vadanti, upādiyanti vā? Attānaṃ. Attano vādupādānaṃ attavādupādānaṃ. Attavādamattameva vā attāti upādiyanti etenāti attavādupādānaṃ. Ayaṃ tāva tesaṃ atthavibhāgo.

Dhammasaṅkhepavitthāre pana kāmupādānaṃ tāva "tattha katamaṃ kāmupādānaṃ? Yo kāmesu kāmacchando kāmarāgo kāmanandī kāmataṇhā kāmasneho kāmapariḷāho kāmamucchā kāmajjhosānaṃ, idaṃ vuccati kāmupādāna"nti āgatattā saṅkhepato taṇhādaḷhattaṃ vuccati. Taṇhādaḷhattaṃ nāma purimataṇhāupanissayapaccayena daḷhasambhūtā uttarataṇhāva. Keci panāhu "appattavisayapatthanā taṇhā andhakāre corassa hatthappasāraṇaṃ viya, sampattavisayaggahaṇaṃ upādānaṃ tasseva bhaṇḍaggahaṇaṃ viya. Appicchatāsantuṭṭhitāpaṭipakkhā ca te dhammā. Tathā pariyesanārakkhadukkhamūlā"ti. Sesupādānattayaṃ pana saṅkhepato diṭṭhimattameva.

Vitthārato pana pubbe rūpādīsu vuttaaṭṭhasatappabhedāyapi taṇhāya daḷhabhāvo kāmupādānaṃ. Dasavatthukā micchādiṭṭhi diṭṭhupādānaṃ. Sīlabbatehi suddhīti parāmasanaṃ pana sīlabbatupādānaṃ. Vīsativatthukā sakkāyadiṭṭhi attavādupādānaṃ. Ayamettha dhammasaṅkhepavitthāro.

Kamatoti ettha pana tividho kamo uppattikkamo pahānakkamo desanākkamo ca. Tattha anamatagge saṃsāre imassa paṭhamaṃ uppattīti abhāvato kilesānaṃ nippariyāyena uppattikkamo na vuccati. Pariyāyena pana yebhuyyena ekasmiṃ bhave attaggāhapubbaṅgamo sassatucchedābhiniveso, tato "sassato ayaṃ attā"ti gaṇhato attavisuddhatthaṃ sīlabbatupādānaṃ, "ucchijjatī"ti gaṇhato paralokanirapekkhassa kāmupādānanti evaṃ paṭhamaṃ attavādupādānaṃ, tato diṭṭhisīlabbatakāmupādānānīti ayametesaṃ ekasmiṃ bhave uppattikkamo.

Diṭṭhupādānādīni cettha paṭhamaṃ pahīyanti sotāpattimaggavajjhattā. Kāmupādānaṃ pacchā, arahattamaggavajjhattāti ayametesaṃ pahānakkamo.

Mahāvisayattā pana pākaṭattā ca etesu kāmupādānaṃ paṭhamaṃ desitaṃ. Mahāvisayaṃ hi taṃ aṭṭhacittasampayogā, appavisayāni itarāni catucittasampayogā, yebhuyyena ca ālayarāmattā pajāya pākaṭaṃ kāmupādānaṃ, na itarāni. Kāmupādāna vā kāmānaṃ samadhigamatthaṃ kotūhalamaṅgalādibahulo hoti, sāssa diṭṭhīti tadanantaraṃ diṭṭhupādānaṃ, taṃ pabhijjamānaṃ sīlabbataattavādupādānavasena duvidhaṃ hoti. Tasmiṃ dvaye gokiriyaṃ kukkurakiriyaṃ vā disvāpi veditabbato oḷārikanti sīlabbatupādānaṃ paṭhamaṃ desitaṃ. Sukhumattā ante attavādupādānanti ayametesaṃ desanākkamo.

"Taṇhā ca purimassettha, ekadhā hoti paccayo; sattadhā aṭṭhadhā vāpi, hoti sesattayassa sā."

Ettha ca evaṃ desite upādānacatukke purimassa kāmupādānassa kāmataṇhā upanissayavasena ekadhāva paccayo hoti, taṇhābhinanditesu visayesu uppattito. Sesattayassa pana sahajātaaññamaññanissayasampayuttaatthiavigatahetuvasena sattadhā vā, upanissayena saha aṭṭhadhā vāpi paccayo hoti. Yadā ca sā upanissayavasena paccayo hoti, tadā asahajātāva hotīti.

Ayaṃ "taṇhāpaccayā upādāna"nti padasmiṃ vitthārakathā.

Ru-Yi Translation

In the section "dependent on craving, clinging": "the clingings are four; these should be clarified by way of the division of their meaning, by their nature in brief and in detail, and by their sequence."

Herein, this is the clarification — clinging to sensual pleasure, clinging to views, clinging to rules and vows, and clinging to a doctrine of self: these, first, are the four clingings here. Their division of meaning is this: it clings to sensual pleasure, meaning the object of desire — hence "clinging to sensual pleasure"; or, it is both sensual desire and clinging — hence, again, "clinging to sensual pleasure." "Clinging" means a firm grasping; the prefix "upa-" has the sense of firmness here, as in "intense distress," "close at hand," and the like. Likewise, it is both a view and a clinging — hence "clinging to views"; or, it clings to a view — hence "clinging to views." For, in such passages as "both self and world are eternal," a later view clings to an earlier view. Likewise, it clings to rules and vows — hence "clinging to rules and vows"; or, it is both a rule-and-vow and a clinging — hence, again, "clinging to rules and vows." For such practices as imitating the behavior and habits of cattle are, by the conviction "purification is thus," themselves clingings. Likewise, that by which they say something is a doctrine; that by which they cling is a clinging. What do they say, or cling to? A self. The clinging to a doctrine of self is "clinging to a doctrine of self"; or, that by which they cling to a mere doctrine of self as "self" is "clinging to a doctrine of self." This, first, is the division of their meaning.

As for the nature of these, in brief and in detail: clinging to sensual pleasure, first, since it comes down as "and what, herein, is clinging to sensual pleasure? Whatever desire for sensual pleasure, lust for sensual pleasure, delight in sensual pleasure, craving for sensual pleasure, affection for sensual pleasure, fever for sensual pleasure, infatuation with sensual pleasure, or attachment to sensual pleasure there is with respect to sensual pleasures — this is called clinging to sensual pleasure" — is said, in brief, to be simply the firmness of craving. "The firmness of craving" means precisely a later craving, made firm by the decisive support condition of an earlier craving. But some say: "craving is the longing for an object not yet obtained, like a thief's reaching out his hand in the dark; clinging is the grasping of an object already obtained, like that same thief's grasping of the goods. And these states are opposed to fewness of desire and contentment; likewise, they are rooted in the suffering of seeking and guarding." The remaining three clingings, in brief, are simply views.

In detail, however, the firmness of that same craving already described, with its hundred-and-eight subdivisions among visible forms and the rest, is clinging to sensual pleasure. Wrong view with ten grounds is clinging to views. The misapprehension that "purification comes through rules and vows" is clinging to rules and vows. Personality-view with twenty grounds is clinging to a doctrine of self. This, herein, is the nature of these, in brief and in detail.

"By sequence": herein, there are three kinds of sequence — the sequence of arising, the sequence of abandoning, and the sequence of teaching. Herein, since there is, in the beginningless round, no such thing as a first arising for this, the sequence of arising is not spoken of in the strict sense for the defilements. But in a looser sense, generally, within a single existence, the adherence to eternalism or annihilationism is preceded by the grasping of a self; from that, for one grasping "this self is eternal," clinging to rules and vows arises for the purpose of purifying the self; for one grasping "it is annihilated," clinging to sensual pleasure arises, having no regard for another world. Thus, first, clinging to a doctrine of self; then clinging to views, to rules and vows, and to sensual pleasure — this is their sequence of arising within a single existence.

And herein, clinging to views and the rest are abandoned first, since they are to be destroyed by the path of stream-entry; clinging to sensual pleasure, afterward, since it is to be destroyed by the path of arahantship. This is their sequence of abandoning.

But, among these, clinging to sensual pleasure is taught first, on account of its wide range and its evident character. For it has a wide range, being associated with eight kinds of consciousness, whereas the others have a narrow range, being associated with only four kinds of consciousness; and clinging to sensual pleasure is evident to people, in general, on account of their delight in attachment, whereas the others are not. Or: for one clinging to sensual pleasure, in order to obtain sensual pleasures, there arises an abundance of things such as excitement over omens and auspicious signs; that itself is a view — hence, immediately after it, clinging to views. That, when it splits apart, becomes twofold: clinging to rules and vows, and clinging to a doctrine of self. Between these two, clinging to rules and vows is taught first, since it can be understood as gross even upon merely seeing someone imitate the behavior of a cow or a dog; clinging to a doctrine of self, being subtle, is taught last. This is their sequence of teaching.

"And craving, here, is a condition for the first of these in one way; it is a condition for the remaining three in seven ways, or even in eight."

And herein, among the four clingings thus taught, craving for sensual pleasure is a condition for the first, clinging to sensual pleasure, in one way only, by the decisive support condition, since it arises within objects already delighted in by craving. But for the remaining three, it is a condition in seven ways — by the conascence, mutuality, dependence, association, presence, non-disappearance, and root conditions — or, together with the decisive support condition, in eight ways as well. And when it is a condition by way of the decisive support condition, it is then not conascent with what it conditions.

This is the detailed discussion of the phrase "dependent on craving, clinging."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The most surprising analytical move here is buried in a single line: clinging to sensual pleasure is defined, in brief, as simply "the firmness of craving" — not as a separate mental factor added on top of craving, but as an intensified, later stage of the very same craving already discussed in the previous link. The unnamed teachers' alternative image sharpens the distinction usefully: craving is a thief reaching in the dark for something not yet grasped; clinging is that same thief's hand actually closing around the goods. Nothing new has entered the picture — only the grip has tightened.

The three different "sequences" (arising, abandoning, teaching) matter because they answer three genuinely different questions and should not be confused: which clinging tends to develop first psychologically within an ordinary life (self-view, then views, then rules-and-vows or sensual clinging, depending on which wrong view one falls into); which is destroyed at which stage of the noble path (the first three go at stream-entry, sensual clinging survives all the way to arahantship); and which the Buddha finds pedagogically easiest to introduce to a new audience (sensual clinging first, since it is everyone's most obvious daily experience, self-view last, since it is the subtlest and hardest to detect).

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
sakkāyadiṭṭhi

personality-view — sakkāya (the existing body, one's own compound of aggregates) + diṭṭhi (view); the twenty-ground wrong view underlying "clinging to a doctrine of self," destroyed at the very first stage of the noble path, stream-entry.

gosīlagovata

the behavior and habits of cattle — go (cow) + sīla/vata (habit, vow); Buddhaghosa's chosen example of "clinging to rules and vows," referencing ascetics who imitated animal behavior in the belief that such austerity alone produces spiritual purification.

Clinging Conditions Being: Kamma-Being and Rebirth-Being

The ninth link divides "being" into two: kamma-being (the volitional act itself, together with its associated mental factors) and rebirth-being (the nine-fold classification of the aggregates it produces, spanning sense-sphere, form-sphere, and formless existence). A careful note explains why the formations already described under the first link resurface here under a new name — not mere repetition, but a genuine shift in reference, from kamma as a condition for the immediate present rebirth to kamma as a condition for a future one.

Pali Source

Upādānapaccayā bhavapade – "atthato dhammato ceva, sātthato bhedasaṅgahā; yaṃ yassa paccayo ceva, viññātabbo vinicchayo."

Tattha bhavatīti bhavo. So kammabhavo upapattibhavo cāti duvidho hoti. Yathāha – "bhavo duvidhena atthi kammabhavo, atthi upapattibhavo"ti. Tattha kammameva bhavo kammabhavo, tathā upapattiyeva bhavo upapattibhavo. Ettha ca upapatti bhavatīti bhavo. Kammaṃ pana yathā sukhakāraṇattā "sukho buddhānaṃ uppādo"ti vutto, evaṃ bhavakāraṇattā phalavohārena bhavoti veditabbanti. Evaṃ tāvettha atthato viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Dhammato pana kammabhavo tāva saṅkhepato cetanā ceva cetanāsampayuttā ca abhijjhādayo kammasaṅkhātā dhammā. Yathāha – "tattha katamo kammabhavo? Puññābhisaṅkhāro apuññābhisaṅkhāro āneñjābhisaṅkhāro parittabhūmako vā mahābhūmako vā, ayaṃ vuccati kammabhavo. Sabbampi bhavagāmikammaṃ kammabhavo"ti. Ettha hi puññābhisaṅkhāroti terasa cetanā. Apuññābhisaṅkhāroti dvādasa. Āneñjābhisaṅkhāroti catasso cetanā. Evaṃ parittabhūmako vā mahābhūmako vāti etena tāsaṃyeva cetanānaṃ mandabahuvipākatā vuttā. Sabbampi bhavagāmikammanti iminā pana cetanāsampayuttā abhijjhādayo vuttā.

Upapattibhavo pana saṅkhepato kammābhinibbattā khandhā, pabhedato navavidho hoti. Yathāha – "tattha katamo upapattibhavo? Kāmabhavo rūpabhavo arūpabhavo saññābhavo asaññābhavo nevasaññānāsaññābhavo, ekavokārabhavo catuvokārabhavo pañcavokārabhavo, ayaṃ vuccati upapattibhavo"ti. Tattha kāmasaṅkhāto bhavo kāmabhavo. Esa nayo rūpārūpabhavesu. Saññāvataṃ bhavo, saññā vā ettha bhave atthīti saññābhavo. Vipariyāyena asaññābhavo. Oḷārikāya saññāya abhāvā sukhumāya ca bhāvā nevasaññā, nāsaññā asmiṃ bhaveti nevasaññānāsaññābhavo. Ekena rūpakkhandhena vokiṇṇo bhavo ekavokārabhavo. Eko vā vokāro assa bhavassāti ekavokārabhavo. Esa nayo catuvokārapañcavokārabhavesu. Tattha kāmabhavo pañca upādiṇṇakkhandhā. Tathā rūpabhavo. Arūpabhavo cattāro, saññābhavo pañca. Asaññābhavo eko upādiṇṇakkhandho. Nevasaññānāsaññābhavo cattāro. Ekavokārabhavādayo ekacatupañcakkhandhā upādiṇṇakkhandhehīti evamettha dhammatopi viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Sātthatoti yathā ca bhavaniddese, tatheva kāmaṃ saṅkhāraniddesepi puññābhisaṅkhārādayova vuttā, evaṃ santepi purime atītakammavasena idha paṭisandhiyā paccayattā, ime paccuppannakammavasena āyatiṃ paṭisandhiyā paccayattāti punavacanaṃ sātthakameva, pubbe vā "tattha katamo puññābhisaṅkhāro? Kusalā cetanā kāmāvacarā"ti evamādinā nayena cetanāva saṅkhārāti vuttā. Idha pana "sabbampi bhavagāmikamma"nti vacanato cetanāsampayuttāpi. Pubbe ca viññāṇapaccayameva kammaṃ "saṅkhārā"ti vuttaṃ. Idāni asaññābhavanibbattakampi. Kiṃ vā bahunā, "avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā"ti ettha puññābhisaṅkhārādayova kusalākusalā dhammā vuttā. "Upādānapaccayā bhavo"ti idha pana upapattibhavassāpi saṅgahitattā kusalākusalābyākatā dhammā vuttā. Tasmā sabbathāpi sātthakamevidaṃ punavacananti evamettha sātthatopi viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Bhedasaṅgahāti upādānapaccayā bhavassa bhedato ceva saṅgahato ca. Yañhi kāmupādānapaccayā kāmabhavanibbattakaṃ kammaṃ karīyati, so kammabhavo. Tadabhinibbattā khandhā upapattibhavo. Esa nayo rūpārūpabhavesu. Evaṃ kāmupādānapaccayā dve kāmabhavā, tadantogadhā ca saññābhavapañcavokārabhavā, dve rūpabhavā, tadantogadhā ca saññābhavaasaññābhavaekavokārabhavapañcavokārabhavā, dve arūpabhavā, tadantogadhā ca saññābhavanevasaññānāsaññābhavacatuvokārabhavāti saddhiṃ antogadhehi cha bhavā. Yathā ca kāmupādānapaccayā saddhiṃ antogadhehi cha bhavā. Tathā sesupādānapaccayāpīti evaṃ upādānapaccayā bhedato saddhiṃ antogadhehi catuvīsati bhavā.

Saṅgahato pana kammabhavaṃ upapattibhavañca ekato katvā kāmupādānapaccayā saddhiṃ antogadhehi eko kāmabhavo. Tathā rūpārūpabhavāti tayo bhavā. Tathā sesupādānapaccayā pīti. Evaṃ upādānapaccayā saṅgahato saddhiṃ antogadhehi dvādasa bhavā. Apica avisesena upādānapaccayā kāmabhavūpagaṃ kammaṃ kammabhavo. Tadabhinibbattā khandhā upapattibhavo. Esa nayo rūpārūpabhavesu. Evaṃ upādānapaccayā saddhiṃ antogadhehi dve kāmabhavā, dve rūpabhavā, dve arūpabhavāti aparena pariyāyena saṅgahato cha bhavā. Kammabhavaupapattibhavabhedaṃ vā anupagamma saddhiṃ antogadhehi kāmabhavādivasena tayo bhavā honti. Kāmabhavādibhedampi anupagamma kammabhavaupapattibhavavasena dve bhavā honti. Kammupapattibhedañcāpi anupagamma upādānapaccayā bhavoti bhavavasena ekova bhavo hotīti evamettha upādānapaccayassa bhavassa bhedasaṅgahāpi viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Ru-Yi Translation

In the section "dependent on clinging, being": "by meaning, by nature, by the point of restating it, by division and inclusion, and by which is a condition for which — the discernment should be understood."

Herein, that which comes to be is "being." It is of two kinds: kamma-being and rebirth-being. As it is said: "being is of two kinds: there is kamma-being, and there is rebirth-being." Herein, kamma itself is being — hence "kamma-being"; likewise, rebirth itself is being — hence "rebirth-being." And here, rebirth comes to be — hence "being." But kamma, just as it is called, on account of being the cause of happiness, "the happy arising of Buddhas," should likewise be understood to be called "being" by a figure of speech naming the cause after its result, on account of being the cause of being. Thus, so far, the discernment by way of meaning should be understood.

By nature, kamma-being, first, is, in brief, volition, and also the states classed as kamma, associated with volition, beginning with covetousness. As it is said: "and what, herein, is kamma-being? The meritorious formation, the demeritorious formation, the imperturbable formation, whether of a limited or an exalted plane — this is called kamma-being. All kamma whatsoever that leads to being is kamma-being." Herein, "the meritorious formation" means the thirteen volitions; "the demeritorious formation," the twelve; "the imperturbable formation," the four volitions. And by "whether of a limited or an exalted plane," the lesser or greater fruitfulness of those same volitions is expressed. But by "all kamma whatsoever that leads to being," the states associated with volition, beginning with covetousness, are expressed.

But rebirth-being, in brief, is the aggregates produced by kamma; by subdivision, it is of nine kinds. As it is said: "and what, herein, is rebirth-being? Sense-sphere being, form-sphere being, formless being, percipient being, non-percipient being, neither-percipient-nor-non-percipient being, one-constituent being, four-constituent being, five-constituent being — this is called rebirth-being." Herein, being called "sensual pleasure" is sense-sphere being; the same method applies to form-sphere and formless being. The being of those possessing perception, or that in which perception exists, is percipient being; the reverse is non-percipient being. Neither a coarse perception, on account of its absence, nor no perception at all, on account of the presence of a subtle one, exists in this being — hence, neither-percipient-nor-non-percipient being. Being intermingled with a single constituent, form, is one-constituent being; or, this being has a single constituent — hence, one-constituent being. The same method applies to four-constituent and five-constituent being. Herein, sense-sphere being consists of five clung-to aggregates; likewise form-sphere being. Formless being consists of four; percipient being, five. Non-percipient being consists of one clung-to aggregate. Neither-percipient-nor-non-percipient being consists of four. One-constituent being and the rest consist of one, four, or five clung-to aggregates respectively. Thus, herein, the discernment by way of nature, too, should be understood.

"By the point of restating it": although, granted, in the exposition of formations it is likewise the meritorious formation and the rest that were spoken of, just as here in the exposition of being — even so, this restatement is genuinely meaningful, since the former passage concerns their being a condition for rebirth-linking here by way of past kamma, while these here concern their being a condition for rebirth-linking in the future by way of present kamma. Or: earlier, in the manner beginning "and what, herein, is the meritorious formation? Wholesome sense-sphere volition," it was said that volition alone constitutes the formations; but here, from the statement "all kamma whatsoever that leads to being," the states associated with volition are meant as well. And earlier, only the kamma that conditions consciousness was called "formations"; here, even that which produces non-percipient being is included. Or, to say no more: in "dependent on ignorance are formations," only the meritorious formation and the rest — wholesome and unwholesome states — were mentioned; but here, in "dependent on clinging, being," since rebirth-being too is included, wholesome, unwholesome, and indeterminate states are all mentioned. Therefore, in every respect, this restatement is indeed meaningful. Thus, herein, the discernment by way of the point of restating it, too, should be understood.

"By division and inclusion": for, conditioned by clinging to sensual pleasure, kamma that produces sense-sphere being is performed — that is kamma-being; the aggregates produced by that are rebirth-being. The same method applies to form-sphere and formless being. Thus, conditioned by clinging to sensual pleasure, there are two sense-sphere beings, together with the percipient being and five-constituent being included within them; two form-sphere beings, together with the percipient being, non-percipient being, one-constituent being, and five-constituent being included within them; and two formless beings, together with the percipient being, neither-percipient-nor-non-percipient being, and four-constituent being included within them — six beings altogether, together with those included within them. And just as, conditioned by clinging to sensual pleasure, there are six beings together with those included within them, so too for the remaining kinds of clinging as their condition. Thus, conditioned by clinging, there are, by way of division, twenty-four beings together with those included within them.

By way of inclusion, however, taking kamma-being and rebirth-being together as one: conditioned by clinging to sensual pleasure, together with those included within it, there is one sense-sphere being. Likewise form-sphere and formless being — three beings in all. So too for the remaining kinds of clinging as their condition. Thus, conditioned by clinging, by way of inclusion, together with those included within them, there are twelve beings. Moreover, without distinction, kamma leading to sense-sphere being, conditioned by clinging, is kamma-being; the aggregates produced by that are rebirth-being. The same method applies to form-sphere and formless being. Thus, conditioned by clinging, together with those included within them, there are two sense-sphere beings, two form-sphere beings, and two formless beings — six beings, by way of inclusion, in this further alternative reckoning. Or, without even approaching the division into kamma-being and rebirth-being, together with those included within them, there are, by way of sense-sphere being and the rest, three beings. Without even approaching the division into sense-sphere being and the rest, there are, by way of kamma-being and rebirth-being, two beings. And without even approaching the division into kamma and rebirth at all, simply as "being conditioned by clinging," by way of being alone, there is just a single being. Thus, herein, the discernment by way of the division and inclusion of being conditioned by clinging, too, should be understood.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The nine-fold classification of rebirth-being is worth mapping against the three-fold one, since both are used freely throughout the tradition: sense-sphere, form-sphere, and formless being are the three broad realms already familiar from the treatise's earlier chapters on meditation, while percipient, non-percipient, and neither-percipient-nor-non-percipient being cut across those realms along a different axis — the presence or refinement of perception itself — and one-, four-, and five-constituent being cut across them along yet a third axis, counting how many of the five aggregates are actually present. The same being can, and typically does, fall under one category from each of these three overlapping systems simultaneously.

The extended justification for restating "formations" under the new name "kamma-being" is a small window into how seriously this commentarial tradition takes apparent redundancy in the canon: rather than dismissing a repeated formula as mere stylistic habit, Buddhaghosa treats it as a puzzle requiring a real answer, and supplies no fewer than three independent, non-competing explanations for why the restatement earns its place.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
kammabhava / upapattibhava

kamma-being / rebirth-being — the two-fold division of "being" that structures this entire section: the volitional act on one hand, and the resultant existence it produces on the other.

ekavokārabhava / catuvokārabhava / pañcavokārabhava

one-constituent, four-constituent, and five-constituent being — a classification of rebirth-being by how many of the five aggregates are present: form alone (non-percipient beings), the four mental aggregates alone (formless beings), or all five together (everywhere else).

Which Clinging Leads to Which Being: Five Worldlings, Five Destinies

A final, sobering point closes the discussion: any clinging whatsoever can lead to any kind of being whatsoever, since an unenlightened worldling reasons no more carefully than a madman. Five vivid character sketches make this concrete — one man drawn by hearsay toward sensual pleasure who ends up in a state of misery through the misconduct his craving provokes; another whose same desire, pursued through good conduct instead, leads to a fortunate rebirth; a third who imagines form- and formless-sphere existence as even more pleasurable and meditates his way there; and two more, driven respectively by annihilationist and eternalist misreadings of the self, and by a naive faith in ascetic rules and vows, each capable of producing kamma leading to any of the three realms of being.

Pali Source

Yaṃ yassa paccayo cevāti yañcettha upādānaṃ yassa paccayo hoti, tatopi viññātabbo vinicchayoti attho. Kiṃ panettha kassa paccayo hoti? Yaṃkiñci yassa kassaci paccayo hotiyeva. Ummattako viya hi puthujjano. So idaṃ yuttaṃ idaṃ ayuttanti avicāretvā yassa kassaci upādānassa vasena yaṃkiñci bhavaṃ patthetvā yaṃkiñci kammaṃ karotiyeva. Tasmā yadekacce sīlabbatupādānena rūpārūpabhavā na hontīti vadanti, taṃ na gahetabbaṃ. Sabbena pana sabbo hotīti gahetabbaṃ.

Seyyathidaṃ – idhekacco anussavavasena vā diṭṭhānusārena vā "kāmā nāmete manussaloke ceva khattiyamahāsālakulādīsu, cha kāmāvacaradevaloke ca samiddhā"ti cintetvā tesaṃ adhigamatthaṃ asaddhammassavanādīhi vañcito "iminā kammena kāmā sampajjantī"ti maññamāno kāmupādānavasena kāyaduccaritādīnipi karoti, so duccaritapāripūriyā apāye upapajjati. Sandiṭṭhike vā pana kāme patthayamāno paṭiladdhe ca gopayamāno kāmupādānavasena kāyaduccaritādīni karoti, so duccaritapāripūriyā apāye upapajjati. Tatrāssa upapattihetubhūtaṃ kammaṃ kammabhavo. Kammābhinibbattā khandhā upapattibhavo. Saññābhavapañcavokārabhavā pana tadantogadhā eva.

Aparo pana saddhammassavanādīhi upabrūhitañāṇo "iminā kammena kāmā sampajjantī"ti maññamāno kāmupādānavasena kāyasucaritādīni karoti. So sucaritapāripūriyā devesu vā manussesu vā upapajjati. Tatrāssa upapattihetubhūtaṃ kammaṃ kammabhavo. Kammābhinibbattā khandhā upapattibhavo. Saññābhavapañcavokārabhavā pana tadantogadhā eva. Iti kāmupādānaṃ sappabhedassa sāntogadhassa kāmabhavassa paccayo hoti.

Aparo "rūpārūpabhavesu tato samiddhatarā kāmā"ti sutvā parikappetvā vā kāmupādānavaseneva rūpārūpasamāpattiyo nibbattetvā samāpattibalena rūpārūpabrahmaloke upapajjati. Tatrāssa upapattihetubhūtaṃ kammaṃ kammabhavo. Kammābhinibbattā khandhā upapattibhavo. Saññā-asaññā-nevasaññānāsaññā-eka-catu-pañcavokārabhavā pana tadantogadhā eva. Iti kāmupādānaṃ sappabhedānaṃ sāntogadhānaṃ rūpārūpabhavānampi paccayo hoti.

Aparo "ayaṃ attā nāma kāmāvacarasampattibhave vā rūpārūpabhavānaṃ vā aññatarasmiṃ ucchinne suucchinno hotī"ti ucchedadiṭṭhiṃ upādāya tadupagaṃ kammaṃ karoti, tassa taṃ kammaṃ kammabhavo. Kammābhinibbattā khandhā upapattibhavo. Saññābhavādayo pana tadantogadhā eva. Iti diṭṭhupādānaṃ sappabhedānaṃ sāntogadhānaṃ tiṇṇampi kāmarūpārūpabhavānaṃ paccayo hoti.

Aparo "ayaṃ attā nāma kāmāvacarasampattibhave vā rūpārūpabhavānaṃ vā aññatarasmiṃ sukhī hoti vigatapariḷāho"ti attavādupādānena tadupagaṃ kammaṃ karoti, tassa taṃ kammaṃ kammabhavo. Tadabhinibbattā khandhā upapattibhavo. Saññābhavādayo pana tadantogadhā eva. Iti attavādupādānaṃ sappabhedānaṃ sāntogadhānaṃ tiṇṇaṃ bhavānaṃ paccayo hoti.

Aparo "idaṃ sīlabbataṃ nāma kāmāvacarasampattibhave vā rūpārūpabhavānaṃ vā aññatarasmiṃ paripūrentassa sukhaṃ pāripūriṃ gacchatī"ti sīlabbatupādānavasena tadupagaṃ kammaṃ karoti, tassa taṃ kammaṃ kammabhavo. Tadabhinibbattā khandhā upapattibhavo. Saññābhavādayo pana tadantogadhā eva. Iti sīlabbatupādānaṃ sappabhedānaṃ sāntogadhānaṃ tiṇṇaṃ bhavānaṃ paccayo hoti. Evamettha yaṃ yassa paccayo hoti, tatopi viññātabbo vinicchayo.

Kiṃ panettha kassa bhavassa kathaṃ paccayo hotīti ce? "Rūpārūpabhavānaṃ, upanissayapaccayo upādānaṃ; sahajātādīhipi taṃ, kāmabhavassāti viññeyyaṃ."

Rūpārūpabhavānaṃ hi, kāmabhavapariyāpannassa ca kammabhave kusalakammasseva, upapattibhavassa cetaṃ catubbidhampi upādānaṃ upanissayapaccayavasena ekadhāva paccayo hoti. Kāmabhave attanā sampayuttākusalakammabhavassa sahajātaaññamaññanissayasampayuttaatthiavigatahetupaccayappabhedehi sahajātādīhi paccayo hoti. Vippayuttassa pana upanissayapaccayenevāti.

Ayaṃ "upādānapaccayā bhavo"ti padasmiṃ vitthārakathā.

Ru-Yi Translation

"And by which is a condition for which": the meaning is that the discernment here should also be understood by way of which clinging is a condition for which being. What, then, is a condition for what here? Anything at all is indeed a condition for anything at all. For a worldling is like a madman: without considering "this is fitting, this is unfitting," he simply aspires to any being whatsoever, by way of any clinging whatsoever, and simply performs any kamma whatsoever. Therefore, if some say that form- and formless being cannot come about through clinging to rules and vows, that should not be accepted. Rather, it should be accepted that any being whatsoever can come about through any clinging whatsoever.

For example: here, someone, by way of hearsay or by following a wrong view, thinks: "sensual pleasures are indeed abundant in the human world, among great warrior-noble and other such families, and in the six sense-sphere heavens" — and, deceived by listening to false teachings in order to obtain them, thinking "by this kamma, sensual pleasures are accomplished," commits bodily misconduct and the like by way of clinging to sensual pleasure; he is reborn in a state of misery through the fulfillment of that misconduct. Or, longing for sensual pleasures visible in this very life, and guarding those already obtained, he commits bodily misconduct and the like by way of clinging to sensual pleasure; he is reborn in a state of misery through the fulfillment of that misconduct. In that case, the kamma that is the cause of his rebirth is kamma-being; the aggregates produced by that kamma are rebirth-being. Percipient being and five-constituent being are simply included within that.

But another, his understanding strengthened by listening to the true teaching and the like, thinking "by this kamma, sensual pleasures are accomplished," commits bodily good conduct and the like by way of clinging to sensual pleasure. He is reborn among gods or human beings through the fulfillment of that good conduct. In that case, the kamma that is the cause of his rebirth is kamma-being; the aggregates produced by that kamma are rebirth-being. Percipient being and five-constituent being are simply included within that. Thus, clinging to sensual pleasure is a condition for sense-sphere being, together with its subdivisions and what is included within it.

Another, having heard, or having imagined for himself, that "in form and formless being, sensual pleasures are even more abundant than there," produces, precisely by way of clinging to sensual pleasure, the form- and formless-sphere attainments, and, by the power of that attainment, is reborn in the form- or formless-sphere Brahmā world. In that case, the kamma that is the cause of his rebirth is kamma-being; the aggregates produced by that kamma are rebirth-being. Percipient being, non-percipient being, neither-percipient-nor-non-percipient being, and one-, four-, and five-constituent being are simply included within that. Thus, clinging to sensual pleasure is a condition even for form- and formless being, together with their subdivisions and what is included within them.

Another, taking up the annihilationist view that "this self is well and truly cut off when cut off, in either a sense-sphere state of attainment or one or another of the form- or formless states of being," performs kamma conducive to that. For him, that kamma is kamma-being; the aggregates produced by that kamma are rebirth-being. Percipient being and the rest are simply included within that. Thus, clinging to views is a condition even for all three kinds of being — sense-sphere, form-sphere, and formless — together with their subdivisions and what is included within them.

Another, by way of clinging to a doctrine of self — thinking "this self is happy, free of torment, in one or another of a sense-sphere state of attainment or the form- or formless states of being" — performs kamma conducive to that. For him, that kamma is kamma-being; the aggregates produced by that are rebirth-being. Percipient being and the rest are simply included within that. Thus, clinging to a doctrine of self is a condition for all three kinds of being, together with their subdivisions and what is included within them.

Another, by way of clinging to rules and vows — thinking "this rule or vow, when fulfilled, brings one to the fulfillment of happiness in one or another of a sense-sphere state of attainment or the form- or formless states of being" — performs kamma conducive to that. For him, that kamma is kamma-being; the aggregates produced by that are rebirth-being. Percipient being and the rest are simply included within that. Thus, clinging to rules and vows is a condition for all three kinds of being, together with their subdivisions and what is included within them. Thus, herein, the discernment by way of which is a condition for which should also be understood.

But how, here, is clinging a condition for which being? "Clinging is a condition for form- and formless being by the decisive support condition; it should be understood that it is also a condition for sense-sphere being by the conascence and other conditions."

For, with respect to form- and formless being, and with respect to the wholesome kamma alone within the kamma-being included in sense-sphere being, and with respect to rebirth-being — for all these, this fourfold clinging is a condition in one way only, by the decisive support condition. But in sense-sphere being, for the unwholesome kamma-being associated with itself, it is a condition by the conascence and other conditions, of the kinds: conascence, mutuality, dependence, association, presence, non-disappearance, and root. But for what is dissociated from it, it is a condition solely by the decisive support condition.

This is the detailed discussion of the phrase "dependent on clinging, being."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

These five character sketches form a small gallery of human folly, and Buddhaghosa's comparison of the ordinary worldling to a madman is not incidental color — it is the argument itself. The point being refuted is a tidy, over-systematic claim that each clinging maps neatly onto its own proper destination (sensual clinging to sensual rebirth, and so on). Real human motivation, the chapter insists, is messier than that: the very same clinging to sensual pleasure can lead a person to hell through misconduct or to heaven through virtue, and even something as apparently narrow as clinging to rules and vows can, in an ascetic seeking release through austerity, become the engine driving him all the way into the formless attainments. Confusion, not consistency, is the worldling's defining trait.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
ummattaka

a madman — the chapter's chosen image for the ordinary, unenlightened worldling, who pursues any being whatsoever through any clinging whatsoever without ever weighing what is truly fitting.

samiddhatara

more abundant, more prosperous — the comparative term the third worldling uses to imagine that sensual pleasures exist in even greater abundance within the form- and formless-sphere realms, motivating him to meditate his way toward a Brahmā-world rebirth purely out of a subtler form of sensual craving.

Being Conditions Birth: Proof from Twins

The tenth and eleventh links receive their briefest treatment yet, since birth, aging, and death were already analyzed at length back in the Truths chapter. Buddhaghosa adds only one new argument here — a clean empirical proof that kamma-being, specifically, is what determines the exact quality of a new birth, drawn from the simple observation that even twins, sharing identical parents and identical external circumstances of conception, are still born with visibly different fortunes.

Pali Source

Bhavapaccayā jātītiādīsu jātiādīnaṃ vinicchayo saccaniddese vuttanayeneva veditabbo. Bhavoti panettha kammabhavova adhippeto. So hi jātiyā paccayo, na upapattibhavo. So ca pana kammapaccayaupanissayapaccayavasena dvedhā paccayo hotīti.

Tattha siyā – kathaṃ panetaṃ jānitabbaṃ bhavo jātiyā paccayoti ce? Bāhirapaccayasamattepi hīnapaṇītatādivisesadassanato. Bāhirānaṃ hi janakajananīsukkasoṇitāhārādīnaṃ paccayānaṃ samattepi sattānaṃ yamakānampi sataṃ hīnapaṇītatādiviseso dissati. So ca na ahetuko sabbadā ca sabbesañca abhāvato, na kammabhavato aññahetuko tadabhinibbattakasattānaṃ ajjhattasantāne aññassa kāraṇassa abhāvatoti kammabhavahetukova. Kammaṃ hi sattānaṃ hīnapaṇītatādivisesassa hetu. Tenāha bhagavā "kammaṃ satte vibhajati yadidaṃ hīnappaṇītatāyā"ti. Tasmā jānitabbametaṃ "bhavo jātiyā paccayo"ti.

Yasmā ca asati jātiyā jarāmaraṇaṃ nāma, sokādayo vā dhammā na honti. Jātiyā pana sati jarāmaraṇañceva, jarāmaraṇasaṅkhātadukkhadhammaphuṭṭhassa ca bālajanassa jarāmaraṇābhisambandhā vā tena tena dukkhadhammena phuṭṭhassa anabhisambandhā vā sokādayo ca dhammā honti. Tasmā ayampi jāti jarāmaraṇassa ceva sokādīnañca paccayo hotīti veditabbā. Sā pana upanissayakoṭiyā ekadhāva paccayo hotīti.

Ayaṃ "bhavapaccayā jātī"tiādīsu vitthārakathā.

Ru-Yi Translation

In the section "dependent on being, birth" and the rest, the discernment regarding birth and the rest should be understood in just the way already stated in the discussion of the truths. "Being," herein, means specifically kamma-being; for it is this that is the condition for birth, not rebirth-being. And it is a condition in two ways: by the kamma condition and by the decisive support condition.

Here it might be asked: how, then, is this to be known — that "being is a condition for birth"? By observing a distinction of inferiority, superiority, and the like even when the external conditions are complete and equal. For, even when the external conditions — the father, the mother, the sperm and blood, nutriment, and the rest — are complete and equal, a distinction of inferiority, superiority, and the like is seen even among a hundred twin beings. And this is not uncaused, since it does not exist always and for everyone; nor is it caused by anything other than kamma-being, since no other cause is found within the internal continuity of the beings it produces — it is caused precisely by kamma-being. For kamma is the cause of the distinction of inferiority, superiority, and the like among beings. Hence the Blessed One said: "kamma divides beings, that is, into the inferior and the superior." Therefore this should be known: "being is a condition for birth."

And since, when birth is absent, there is no such thing as aging-and-death, nor do the states of sorrow and the rest come to be — but when birth is present, there is aging-and-death, and, for a foolish person touched by the painful state called aging-and-death, whether connected with aging-and-death itself or connected with this or that painful state that touches him, the states of sorrow and the rest come to be. Therefore it should be understood that this birth, too, is a condition for aging-and-death and for sorrow and the rest. But it is a condition in one way only, by way of the decisive support condition.

This is the detailed discussion of "dependent on being, birth" and the rest.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The twins argument is a small masterpiece of empirical reasoning within a text that otherwise relies heavily on scripture and etymology: it isolates kamma as a cause by the standard method of holding every other variable constant. If two beings share literally every external circumstance of conception — same parents, same biological material, presumably the same nutriment and care — and still turn out with visibly different fortunes, something internal to each being must be doing the differentiating work, and nothing else remains available to do that work except each one's own individual store of past kamma. It is worth noting that Buddhaghosa restricts this whole discussion specifically to kamma-being, not rebirth-being — it is the volitional act of the past life, not the resultant aggregates of the present one, that actually conditions a future birth.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
yamaka

twins — the key empirical case cited to prove kamma's causal role: identical external conception conditions, yet visibly different outcomes, ruling out chance and isolating each individual's own past kamma as the differentiating factor.

hīnappaṇītatā

the distinction of inferiority and superiority — hīna (inferior) + paṇīta (superior) + -tā (abstract-noun suffix); the exact canonical phrase the Buddha uses for what kamma "divides beings" into, quoted here as the scriptural warrant for the twins argument.

The Wheel of Becoming: No Discernible Beginning, No Agent, No Experiencer

With every individual link now fully explained, Buddhaghosa steps back to characterize the whole twelve-factor chain as a single "wheel of becoming," and proves four remarkable claims about it at once. First, ignorance — the chain's own apparent starting point — is itself shown to be produced by sorrow, lamentation, and the other factors named at the chain's end, since each of those states demonstrably arises from one of the four taints, one of which is the taint of ignorance itself; the wheel has no genuine starting point at all, only a place where any given telling of it happens to begin. Second, since this circularity never terminates, the wheel is said to have "no discernible beginning" — and an apparent objection, that calling ignorance a starting point contradicts this, is resolved by clarifying that ignorance is named first only because it is the principal factor to be cut, the way seizing a snake's head disables the whole snake, not because it is a true first cause. Third, the wheel operates with no maker and no one who experiences its results — only the twelve factors themselves, conditioning one another. Fourth, every single factor in it is void in a full twelvefold sense — impermanent, foul, painful, and not-self, in both its general and its individual nature.

Pali Source

Yasmā panettha sokādayo avasāne vuttā, tasmā yā sā avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārāti evametassa bhavacakkassa ādimhi vuttā, sā, "sokādīhi avijjā, siddhā bhavacakkamaviditādimidaṃ; kārakavedakarahitaṃ, dvādasavidhasuññatāsuññaṃ." Satataṃ samitaṃ pavattatīti veditabbaṃ.

Kathaṃ panettha sokādīhi avijjā siddhā, kathamidaṃ bhavacakkaṃ aviditādi, kathaṃ kārakavedakarahitaṃ, kathaṃ dvādasavidhasuññatāsuññanti ce? Ettha hi sokadomanassupāyāsā avijjāya aviyogino, paridevo ca nāma mūḷhassāti tesu tāva siddhesu siddhā hoti avijjā. Apica "āsavasamudayā avijjāsamudayo"ti vuttaṃ. Āsavasamudayā cete sokādayo honti.

Kathaṃ? Vatthukāmaviyoge tāva soko kāmāsavasamudayā hoti. Yathāha – "tassa ce kāmayānassa, chandajātassa jantuno; te kāmā parihāyanti, sallaviddhova ruppatī"ti. Yathā cāha – "kāmato jāyati soko"ti.

Sabbepi cete diṭṭhāsavasamudayā honti. Yathāha – "tassa 'ahaṃ rūpaṃ mama rūpa'nti pariyuṭṭhaṭṭhāyino rūpavipariṇāmaññathābhāvā uppajjanti sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā"ti.

Yathā ca diṭṭhāsavasamudayā, evaṃ bhavāsavasamudayāpi. Yathāha – "yepi te devā dīghāyukā vaṇṇavanto sukhabahulā uccesu vimānesu ciraṭṭhitikā, tepi tathāgatassa dhammadesanaṃ sutvā bhayaṃ santāsaṃ saṃvegamāpajjantī"ti. Pañca pubbanimittāni disvā maraṇabhayena santajjitānaṃ devānaṃ viya.

Yathā ca bhavāsavasamudayā, evaṃ avijjāsavasamudayāpi. Yathāha – "sa kho so, bhikkhave, bālo tividhaṃ diṭṭheva dhamme dukkhaṃ domanassaṃ paṭisaṃvedetī"ti.

Iti yasmā āsavasamudayā ete dhammā honti, tasmā ete sijjhamānā avijjāya hetubhūte āsave sādhenti. Āsavesu ca siddhesu paccayabhāve bhāvato avijjāpi siddhāva hotīti. Evaṃ tāvettha sokādīhi avijjā siddhā hotīti veditabbā.

Yasmā pana evaṃ paccayabhāve bhāvato avijjāya siddhāya puna avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā, saṅkhārapaccayā viññāṇanti evaṃ hetuphalaparamparāya pariyosānaṃ natthi. Tasmā taṃ hetuphalasambandhavasena pavattaṃ dvādasaṅgaṃ bhavacakkaṃ aviditādīti siddhaṃ hoti.

Evaṃ sati avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārāti idaṃ ādimattakathanaṃ virujjhatīti ce. Nayidaṃ ādimattakathanaṃ. Padhānadhammakathanaṃ panetaṃ. Tiṇṇannaṃ hi vaṭṭānaṃ avijjā padhānā. Avijjāggahaṇena hi avasesakilesavaṭṭañca kammādīni ca bālaṃ palibodhenti. Sappasiraggahaṇena sesasappasarīraṃ viya bāhaṃ. Avijjāsamucchede pana kate tehi vimokkho hoti. Sappasiracchede kate palibodhitabāhāvimokkho viya. Yathāha – "avijjāyatveva asesavirāganirodhā saṅkhāranirodho"tiādi. Iti yaṃ gaṇhato bandho, muccato ca mokkho hoti, tassa padhānadhammassa kathanamidaṃ, na ādimattakathananti. Evamidaṃ bhavacakkaṃ aviditādīti veditabbaṃ.

Tayidaṃ yasmā avijjādīhi kāraṇehi saṅkhārādīnaṃ pavatti, tasmā tato aññena "brahmā mahābrahmā seṭṭho sajitā"ti evaṃ parikappitena brahmādinā vā saṃsārassa kārakena, "so kho pana me ayaṃ attā vado vedeyyo"ti evaṃ parikappitena attanā vā sukhadukkhānaṃ vedakena rahitaṃ. Iti kārakavedakarahitanti veditabbaṃ.

Yasmā panettha avijjā udayabbayadhammakattā dhuvabhāvena, saṃkiliṭṭhattā saṃkilesikattā ca subhabhāvena, udayabbayapaṭipīḷitattā sukhabhāvena, paccayāyattavuttittā vasavattanabhūtena attabhāvena ca suññā. Tathā saṅkhārādīnipi aṅgāni. Yasmā vā avijjā na attā, na attano, na attani, na attavatī. Tathā saṅkhārādīnipi aṅgāni. Tasmā dvādasavidhasuññatāsuññametaṃ bhavacakkanti veditabbaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now, since sorrow and the rest are spoken of at the end here, that same ignorance which was spoken of at the beginning of this wheel of becoming, as "dependent on ignorance are formations" — it should be understood that: "ignorance is established by sorrow and the rest; this wheel of becoming has no discernible beginning; it is devoid of an agent or an experiencer; it is void with a twelvefold voidness" — and occurs constantly and continuously.

How, then, is ignorance established by sorrow and the rest here; how is this wheel of becoming without a discernible beginning; how is it devoid of an agent and an experiencer; how is it void with a twelvefold voidness? Here: sorrow, displeasure, and despair are never separate from ignorance, and lamentation, indeed, belongs only to one who is deluded; so, once these are established, ignorance too is thereby established. Moreover, it was said: "from the arising of the taints comes the arising of ignorance." And these — sorrow and the rest — arise from the arising of the taints as well.

How so? First, sorrow at the loss of an object of sensual desire arises from the arising of the taint of sensual desire. As it is said: "if, for a person set on sensual pleasure, whose desire has arisen, those sensual pleasures diminish, he is afflicted as though pierced by a dart." And as it is said: "from sensual pleasure sorrow is born."

All of these also arise from the arising of the taint of views. As it is said: "for one who remains obsessed with 'I am form, form is mine,' through the change and alteration of form, there arise sorrow, lamentation, pain, displeasure, and despair."

And just as they arise from the arising of the taint of views, so too from the arising of the taint of being. As it is said: "even those gods who are long-lived, beautiful, abounding in happiness, dwelling long in lofty mansions — even they, on hearing the Tathāgata's teaching of the Dhamma, fall into fear, dread, and a sense of urgency" — just like gods terrified with the fear of death upon seeing the five preliminary signs of their own impending fall.

And just as they arise from the arising of the taint of being, so too from the arising of the taint of ignorance. As it is said: "that fool, bhikkhus, experiences threefold pain and displeasure right here in this very life."

Thus, since these states arise from the arising of the taints, their own establishment thereby establishes the taints, which are themselves the cause of ignorance. And, the taints being established, since ignorance's being a condition for them depends on their being present, ignorance too is thereby established. Thus, so far, it should be understood that ignorance is established by sorrow and the rest.

And since, ignorance being thereby established by its dependence on being a condition for sorrow and the rest in this way, there is, once again, "dependent on ignorance are formations, dependent on formations, consciousness," and so on — there is no end to this succession of cause and effect. Therefore this twelve-factored wheel of becoming, occurring by way of this connection of cause and effect, is thereby established as having no discernible beginning.

If it should be said that this being so, the statement "dependent on ignorance are formations" contradicts the claim of no discernible beginning — this is not a mere statement of a beginning. Rather, it is a statement of the principal factor. For ignorance is the principal factor among the three rounds. By seizing hold of ignorance, the remaining round of defilements, and kamma and the rest, entangle the fool — just as, by seizing a snake's head, the rest of the snake's body entangles the arm. But when ignorance is cut off, liberation from them comes about — just as, when the snake's head is severed, the entangled arm is freed. As it is said: "it is precisely through the total fading away and cessation of ignorance that the cessation of formations comes about," and so on. Thus, this is a statement of that principal factor, whose seizing entails bondage and whose release entails freedom — not a mere statement of a beginning. Thus, this wheel of becoming should be understood to have no discernible beginning.

And since it is by causes such as ignorance that formations and the rest occur, therefore this wheel of becoming is devoid of any maker of saṃsāra other than these — such as a Brahmā, imagined as "a Great Brahmā, the highest, the creator" — and devoid of any experiencer of pleasure and pain other than these — such as a self, imagined as "that self of mine which speaks, which feels." Thus it should be understood as "devoid of an agent and an experiencer."

And since, here, ignorance is void of permanence, on account of being subject to arising and passing away; void of beauty, on account of being defiled and conducive to defilement; void of pleasure, on account of being oppressed by arising and passing away; and void of a self in the sense of a controlling agent, on account of occurring in dependence on conditions — and likewise the other factors, the formations and the rest — or, since ignorance is not a self, does not belong to a self, is not within a self, and does not possess a self — and likewise the other factors, the formations and the rest — therefore this wheel of becoming should be understood as "void with a twelvefold voidness."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The proof that ignorance is "established by sorrow and the rest" closes a circle that began all the way back near the chapter's opening, when Buddhaghosa first noted that the standard formula's closing terms — sorrow, lamentation, pain, displeasure, despair — exist only to show that the wheel has no gap. Here that observation is put to full philosophical use: since each of these closing terms can independently be traced back to one of the four taints (sensual desire, views, being, and ignorance itself), and since ignorance is itself defined as arising from the taints, the very last words of the standard formula loop directly back into its very first word. There is no privileged starting line to be found anywhere in the twelve factors — only the place a given telling happens to enter the circle, exactly as the vine-gatherers' simile from early in the chapter already showed for the four directions of teaching.

The snake's-head image resolves what could otherwise look like a flat contradiction — how can a "beginningless" wheel still name ignorance first? — with genuine elegance: ignorance is not a first cause in time, but the one weak point in the whole structure where cutting is actually effective, the way a snake handler seizes the head specifically because that is where the danger, and the leverage, both concentrate, not because the head came into existence before the rest of the snake.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
āsava

taint — the four deep-seated defilements (sensual desire, views, being, and ignorance) whose "arising" is shown here to underlie sorrow and the other closing terms of the standard formula, closing the circle back to ignorance itself.

aviditādi

"having no discernible beginning" — a + vidita (known, discerned) + ādi (beginning); the technical description of the wheel of becoming's temporal structure, distinguished carefully here from ignorance's rhetorical role as the formula's "principal factor."

kārakavedakarahita

devoid of an agent and an experiencer — kāraka (doer, maker) + vedaka (experiencer, feeler) + rahita (devoid of); the wheel's third defining property, ruling out both a creator-god behind saṃsāra and a self who undergoes its results.

Two Roots, Three Times: Ignorance and Craving

The wheel is now analyzed along a further axis: its twelve factors reduce to two "roots" — ignorance, which anchors the chain looking backward into the past, and craving, which anchors it looking forward into the future — and distribute across three times: two factors belonging to the past, eight to the present, and two to the future.

Pali Source

Evañca viditvā puna, "tassāvijjātaṇhā, mūlamatītādayo tayo kālā; dve aṭṭha dve eva ca, sarūpato tesu aṅgāni."

Tassa kho panetassa bhavacakkassa avijjā taṇhā cāti dve dhammā mūlanti veditabbā. Tadetaṃ pubbantāharaṇato avijjāmūlaṃ vedanāvasānaṃ, aparantasantānato taṇhāmūlaṃ jarāmaraṇāvasānanti duvidhaṃ hoti. Tattha purimaṃ diṭṭhicaritavasena vuttaṃ, pacchimaṃ taṇhācaritavasena. Diṭṭhicaritānaṃ hi avijjā, taṇhācaritānañca taṇhā saṃsāranāyikā. Ucchedadiṭṭhisamugghātāya vā paṭhamaṃ, phaluppattiyā hetūnaṃ anupacchedappakāsanato, sassatadiṭṭhisamugghātāya dutiyaṃ, uppannānaṃ jarāmaraṇappakāsanato. Gabbhaseyyakavasena vā purimaṃ, anupubbapavattidīpanato, opapātikavasena pacchimaṃ, sahuppattidīpanato.

Atītapaccuppannānāgatā cassa tayo kālā. Tesu pāḷiyaṃ sarūpato āgatavasena "avijjā, saṅkhārā cā"ti dve aṅgāni atītakālāni. Viññāṇādīni bhavāvasānāni aṭṭha paccuppannakālāni. Jāti ceva jarāmaraṇañca dve anāgatakālānīti veditabbāni.

Ru-Yi Translation

Having understood this, further — "ignorance and craving are its root; the past and the rest are its three times; two, eight, and two again are its factors, respectively, by their own specific nature, among these times."

Of this wheel of becoming, ignorance and craving should be understood as its two root factors. This is twofold: rooted in ignorance, drawing back to the past and ending with feeling; and rooted in craving, extending forward to the future and ending with aging-and-death. Herein, the former is spoken of by way of one of views as one's habitual temperament, the latter by way of one of craving. For ignorance leads the round for those of a views-temperament, and craving leads it for those of a craving-temperament. Or: the first is for the uprooting of the annihilationist view, since it discloses the uninterrupted continuity of causes through to the arising of their result; the second is for the uprooting of the eternalist view, since it discloses the aging-and-death of what has already arisen. Or: the former is by way of one born from a womb, since it shows a successive occurrence; the latter is by way of one spontaneously born, since it shows a simultaneous arising.

And its three times are the past, present, and future. Among these, by way of what has come down in the canonical text under its own specific heading, two factors — "ignorance" and "formations" — are the past time; the eight — consciousness and the rest, ending with being — are the present time; and the two — birth and aging-and-death — are the future time. This should be understood.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The pairing of the ignorance-rooted and craving-rooted readings with, respectively, the annihilationist and eternalist views is a small but precise piece of doctrinal engineering: tracing the chain back from feeling to ignorance emphasizes that a genuine, uninterrupted causal history stands behind the present moment — countering the annihilationist's claim that nothing meaningfully connects past and present — while tracing it forward from craving to aging-and-death emphasizes that whatever has already arisen is bound to age and pass away — countering the eternalist's claim that something in the chain persists unchanged. Two halves of one circle, aimed at two different wrong views.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
diṭṭhicarita / taṇhācarita
