a views-temperament / a craving-temperament — two of the classic personality types this tradition recognizes, used here to explain why the standard formula can be told with either ignorance or craving as its emphasized root, depending on which kind of listener is being addressed.

Three Connections, Four Sections, Twenty Modes, Three Rounds

A further numerological analysis, drawn directly from the Paṭisambhidāmagga, breaks the wheel into three "connections" where a cause becomes an effect or an effect becomes a new cause; four inclusive "sections" bounded by those connections; twenty distinct "modes," reckoned as five causes in the past producing a fivefold effect now, and five causes now producing a fivefold effect in the future; and finally three "rounds" — of defilement, of kamma, and of result — which keep the whole wheel spinning for as long as the round of defilement itself remains uncut.

Pali Source

Puna, "hetuphalahetupubbaka-tisandhicatubhedasaṅgahañcetaṃ; vīsati ākārāraṃ, tivaṭṭamanavaṭṭhitaṃ bhamati". Itipi veditabbaṃ.

Tattha saṅkhārānañca paṭisandhiviññāṇassa ca antarā eko hetuphalasandhi nāma. Vedanāya ca taṇhāya ca antarā eko phalahetusandhi nāma. Bhavassa ca jātiyā ca antarā eko hetuphalasandhīti evamidaṃ hetuphalahetupubbakatisandhīti veditabbaṃ.

Sandhīnaṃ ādipariyosānavavatthitā panassa cattāro saṅgahā honti. Seyyathidaṃ – avijjāsaṅkhārā eko saṅgaho. Viññāṇanāmarūpasaḷāyatanaphassavedanā dutiyo. Taṇhupādānabhavā tatiyo. Jātijarāmaraṇaṃ catutthoti. Evamidaṃ catubhedasaṅgahanti veditabbaṃ.

"Atīte hetavo pañca, idāni phalapañcakaṃ; idāni hetavo pañca, āyatiṃ phalapañcakanti."

Etehi pana vīsatiyā ākārasaṅkhātehi arehi vīsatiākārāranti veditabbaṃ. Tattha atīte hetavo pañcāti avijjā saṅkhārā cāti ime tāva dve vuttā eva. Yasmā pana avidvā paritassati, paritassito upādiyati, tassupādānapaccayā bhavo. Tasmā taṇhupādānabhavāpi gahitā honti. Tenāha "purimakammabhavasmiṃ moho avijjā, āyūhanā saṅkhārā, nikanti taṇhā, upagamanaṃ upādānaṃ, cetanā bhavoti ime pañca dhammā purimakammabhavasmiṃ idha paṭisandhiyā paccayā"ti.

Tattha purimakammabhavasminti purime kammabhave, atītajātiyaṃ kammabhave kariyamāneti attho. Moho avijjāti yo tadā dukkhādīsu moho, yena mūḷho kammaṃ karoti, sā avijjā. Āyūhanā saṅkhārāti taṃ kammaṃ karoto yā purimacetanāyo, yathā "dānaṃ dassāmī"ti cittaṃ uppādetvā māsampi saṃvaccharampi dānupakaraṇāni sajjentassa uppannā purimacetanāyo. Paṭiggāhakānaṃ pana hatthe dakkhiṇaṃ patiṭṭhāpayato cetanā bhavoti vuccati. Ekāvajjanesu vā chasu javanesu cetanā āyūhanā saṅkhārā nāma. Sattame bhavo. Yā kāci vā pana cetanā bhavo. Sampayuttā āyūhanā saṅkhārā nāma. Nikanti taṇhāti yā kammaṃ karontassa phale upapattibhave nikāmanā patthanā, sā taṇhā nāma. Upagamanaṃ upādānanti yaṃ kammabhavassa paccayabhūtaṃ "idaṃ katvā asukasmiṃ nāma ṭhāne kāme sevissāmi ucchijjissāmī"tiādinā nayena pavattaṃ upagamanaṃ gahaṇaṃ parāmasanaṃ, idaṃ upādānaṃ nāma. Cetanā bhavoti āyūhanāvasāne vuttā cetanā bhavoti evamattho veditabbo.

Idāni phalapañcakanti viññāṇādivedanāvasānaṃ pāḷiyaṃ āgatameva. Yathāha – "idha paṭisandhi viññāṇaṃ, okkanti nāmarūpaṃ, pasādo āyatanaṃ, phuṭṭho phasso, vedayitaṃ vedanā, ime pañca dhammā idhūpapattibhavasmiṃ purekatassa kammassa paccayā"ti. Tattha paṭisandhi viññāṇanti yaṃ bhavantarapaṭisandhānavasena uppannattā paṭisandhīti vuccati, taṃ viññāṇaṃ. Okkanti nāmarūpanti yā gabbhe rūpārūpadhammānaṃ okkanti āgantvā pavisanaṃ viya, idaṃ nāmarūpaṃ. Pasādo āyatananti idaṃ cakkhādipañcāyatanavasena vuttaṃ. Phuṭṭho phassoti yo ārammaṇaṃ phuṭṭho phusanto uppanno, ayaṃ phasso. Vedayitaṃ vedanāti yaṃ paṭisandhiviññāṇena vā saḷāyatanapaccayena vā phassena saha uppannaṃ vipākavedayitaṃ, sā vedanāti evamattho veditabbo.

Idāni hetavo pañcāti taṇhādayo pāḷiyaṃ āgatā taṇhupādānabhavā. Bhave pana gahite tassa pubbabhāgā taṃsampayuttā vā saṅkhārā gahitāva honti. Taṇhupādānaggahaṇena ca taṃsampayuttā, yāya vā mūḷho kammaṃ karoti, sā avijjā gahitāva hotīti. Evaṃ pañca. Tenāha "idha paripakkattā āyatanānaṃ moho avijjā, āyūhanā saṅkhārā, nikanti taṇhā, upagamanaṃ upādānaṃ, cetanā bhavoti ime pañca dhammā idha kammabhavasmiṃ āyatiṃ paṭisandhiyā paccayā"ti. Tattha idha paripakkattā āyatanānanti paripakkāyatanassa kammakaraṇakāle sammoho dassito. Sesaṃ uttānatthameva.

Āyatiṃ phalapañcakanti viññāṇādīni pañca. Tāni jātiggahaṇena vuttāni. Jarāmaraṇaṃ pana tesaṃyeva jarāmaraṇaṃ. Tenāha – "āyatiṃ paṭisandhi viññāṇaṃ, okkanti nāmarūpaṃ, pasādo āyatanaṃ, phuṭṭho phasso, vedayitaṃ vedanā, ime pañca dhammā āyatiṃ upapattibhavasmiṃ idha katassa kammassa paccayā"ti. Evamidaṃ vīsati ākārāraṃ hoti.

Tivaṭṭamanavaṭṭhitaṃ bhamatīti ettha pana saṅkhārabhavā kammavaṭṭaṃ, avijjātaṇhupādānāni kilesavaṭṭaṃ, viññāṇanāmarūpasaḷāyatanaphassavedanā vipākavaṭṭanti imehi tīhi vaṭṭehi tivaṭṭamidaṃ bhavacakkaṃ yāva kilesavaṭṭaṃ na upacchijjati, tāva anupacchinnapaccayattā anavaṭṭhitaṃ punappunaṃ parivattanato bhamatiyevāti veditabbaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

Further — "this wheel, with its three connections of cause-to-effect and effect-to-cause, its inclusion into four sections, its twenty modes as spokes, and its three rounds, revolves without remaining fixed" — this, too, should be understood.

Herein, the junction between formations and rebirth-linking consciousness is called one cause-to-effect connection. The junction between feeling and craving is called one effect-to-cause connection. The junction between being and birth is one more cause-to-effect connection. Thus this should be understood as having "three connections of cause-to-effect and effect-to-cause."

The determination of these connections' beginnings and endings gives its four inclusive sections. For example: ignorance and formations are one section; consciousness, name-and-form, the sixfold base, contact, and feeling are the second; craving, clinging, and being are the third; birth and aging-and-death are the fourth. This should be understood as its "inclusion into four sections."

"Five causes in the past; a fivefold effect now; five causes now; a fivefold effect in the future."

By these twenty modes, called "spokes," it should be understood as having "twenty modes as spokes." Herein, "five causes in the past": ignorance and formations, these two, first, have already been stated. But since one who does not know the truth is agitated by craving, and one who is agitated clings, and, conditioned by that clinging, being comes to be — therefore craving, clinging, and being are included as well. Hence it is said: "in a past kamma-being, the delusion is ignorance, the exertion is the formations, the longing is craving, the approach is clinging, the volition is being — these five states, in a past kamma-being, are conditions for rebirth-linking here."

Herein, "in a past kamma-being" means: in a former kamma-being — that is, while kamma-being is being performed in a past birth. "The delusion is ignorance": whatever delusion regarding suffering and the rest there was at that time, by which, being deluded, one performs kamma — that is ignorance. "The exertion is the formations": whatever preceding volitions there were for one performing that kamma — as, for instance, the preceding volitions that arose for one who, having formed the thought "I shall give a gift," spends a month or even a year preparing the materials for the gift. But the volition of one actually placing the offering in the hands of the recipients is called "being." Or: the volition occurring in the six impulsions preceding the seventh, single adverting, is called the "exertion, the formations"; in the seventh, it is "being." Or, alternatively: any volition whatsoever is "being"; its associated states are called the "exertion, the formations." "The longing is craving": whatever longing, whatever aspiration for the result — for rebirth-being — there is in one performing kamma, that is called "craving." "The approach is clinging": that approach, grasping, adherence — occurring in the manner of "having done this, I shall enjoy sensual pleasures in such-and-such a place" or "I shall be annihilated," and the like — which serves as a condition for kamma-being, is called "clinging." "The volition is being": the volition already mentioned at the conclusion of the exertion is "being." This is the meaning to be understood.

"A fivefold effect now": this is precisely what is stated in the canonical text, ending with consciousness through feeling. As it is said: "here, the rebirth-linking is consciousness, the descent is name-and-form, the sensitivity is the sense base, the touched is contact, the felt is feeling — these five states, in this rebirth-being here, are conditions for the kamma performed before." Herein, "the rebirth-linking is consciousness": that consciousness which, on account of having arisen by way of linking on to another state of being, is called "rebirth-linking" — that is consciousness. "The descent is name-and-form": that descent of material and formless states into the womb, as though having come and entered — this is name-and-form. "The sensitivity is the sense base": this is said by way of the fivefold sense base — the eye and the rest. "The touched is contact": that which arises touching, having touched an object — this is contact. "The felt is feeling": whatever resultant feeling arises together with the rebirth-linking consciousness, or together with contact conditioned by the sixfold base — that is feeling. This is the meaning to be understood.

"Five causes now": craving and the rest that have come down in the canonical text are craving, clinging, and being. But once being is taken, the formations preceding it, or associated with it, are thereby also taken. And by taking craving and clinging, their associated states are taken; and that ignorance by which, deluded, one performs kamma, is thereby also taken. Thus, five. Hence it is said: "here, on account of the maturity of the sense bases, the delusion is ignorance, the exertion is the formations, the longing is craving, the approach is clinging, the volition is being — these five states, in this present kamma-being, are conditions for rebirth-linking in the future." Herein, "here, on account of the maturity of the sense bases" shows the delusion present at the time of performing kamma for one whose sense bases have matured. The rest is plain in meaning.

"A fivefold effect in the future": consciousness and the other four states already described. These are expressed by taking "birth" as their collective name. Aging-and-death is simply the aging-and-death of those same states. Hence it is said: "in the future, the rebirth-linking is consciousness, the descent is name-and-form, the sensitivity is the sense base, the touched is contact, the felt is feeling — these five states, in a future rebirth-being, are conditions for the kamma performed here." Thus this wheel has twenty modes as spokes.

"It revolves without remaining fixed, with its three rounds": herein, formations and being are the round of kamma; ignorance, craving, and clinging are the round of defilement; consciousness, name-and-form, the sixfold base, contact, and feeling are the round of result. By these three rounds, this wheel of becoming has three rounds; and, for as long as the round of defilement is not cut off, on account of its conditions remaining uninterrupted, it does not remain fixed, but simply keeps revolving, turning again and again. This should be understood.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The three-round summary at the close of this section is, in many ways, the single most useful map this entire chapter offers for holding the whole twelve-factor teaching in mind at once: defilement (ignorance, craving, clinging) produces kamma (formations, being), kamma produces result (consciousness through feeling), and result, through the ignorance and craving still latent within it, feeds straight back into more defilement — a genuine, closed causal loop with three distinct kinds of link in it, not a simple one-directional chain. Practically speaking, this is also the map that shows exactly where intervention is possible: since only the round of defilement, once cut, actually stops the wheel from turning, the entire practice of the path can be understood as an effort directed at precisely that one round, rather than at kamma or result directly.

The twenty-mode breakdown — five past causes, five present effects, five present causes, five future effects — gives the wheel a clean, repeatable rhythm: cause, then effect; cause, then effect; endlessly. The specific technical definitions supplied here for "the exertion is the formations" and "the volition is being" (via the example of a month spent preparing a gift, versus the single volition of the moment the gift is actually placed in the recipient's hands) are a rare moment of genuinely everyday, relatable illustration amid otherwise highly abstract terminology.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
kilesavaṭṭa / kammavaṭṭa / vipākavaṭṭa

the round of defilement / of kamma / of result — the three interlocking cycles that together constitute the wheel of becoming; only the first of the three, once cut, actually stops the wheel from continuing to turn.

āyūhanā

exertion — the technical name given here to the preparatory volitions leading up to a kammically decisive act, illustrated by the volitions of someone spending months preparing materials for a gift before the single, climactic volition of actually giving it.

Truths, Functions, and Warding Off Wrong Views

Three further analytical passes follow in quick succession. First, each link of the chain is mapped onto the four noble truths, showing that the wheel is, in effect, the truths of suffering and its origin playing out link by link, over and over. Second, every factor is shown to have a genuine double life: it performs its own distinctive activity, and, simultaneously, serves as the condition for whatever comes next — ignorance both bewilders and gives rise to formations, feeling both tastes its object and gives rise to craving, and so through all twelve. Third, each individual link is shown to specifically ward off one particular wrong view a careless observer might otherwise fall into — the idea of a fixed agent, of a soul migrating from body to body, of a solid, undivided self, and so on down the chain.

Pali Source

Tayidamevaṃ bhamamānaṃ, "saccappabhavato kiccā, vāraṇā upamāhi ca; gambhīranayabhedā ca, viññātabbaṃ yathārahaṃ."

Tattha yasmā kusalākusalaṃ kammaṃ avisesena samudayasaccanti saccavibhaṅge vuttaṃ, tasmā avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārāti avijjāya saṅkhārā dutiyasaccappabhavaṃ dutiyasaccaṃ. Saṅkhārehi viññāṇaṃ dutiyasaccappabhavaṃ paṭhamasaccaṃ. Viññāṇādīhi nāmarūpādīni vipākavedanāpariyosānāni paṭhamasaccappabhavaṃ paṭhamasaccaṃ. Vedanāya taṇhā paṭhamasaccappabhavaṃ dutiyasaccaṃ. Taṇhāya upādānaṃ dutiyasaccappabhavaṃ dutiyasaccaṃ. Upādānato bhavo dutiyasaccappabhavaṃ paṭhamadutiyasaccadvayaṃ. Bhavato jāti dutiyasaccappabhavaṃ paṭhamasaccaṃ. Jātiyā jarāmaraṇaṃ paṭhamasaccappabhavaṃ paṭhamasaccanti evaṃ tāvidaṃ saccappabhavato viññātabbaṃ yathārahaṃ.

Yasmā panettha avijjā vatthūsu ca satte sammoheti, paccayo ca hoti saṅkhārānaṃ pātubhāvāya. Tathā saṅkhārā saṅkhatañca abhisaṅkharonti, paccayā ca honti viññāṇassa. Viññāṇampi vatthuñca paṭivijānāti, paccayo ca hoti nāmarūpassa. Nāmarūpampi aññamaññañca upatthambheti, paccayo ca hoti saḷāyatanassa. Saḷāyatanampi savisaye ca pavattati, paccayo ca hoti phassassa. Phassopi ārammaṇañca phusati, paccayo ca hoti vedanāya. Vedanāpi ārammaṇarasañca anubhavati, paccayo ca hoti taṇhāya. Taṇhāpi rajjanīye ca dhamme rajjati, paccayo ca hoti upādānassa. Upādānampi upādāniye ca dhamme upādiyati, paccayo ca hoti bhavassa. Bhavopi nānāgatīsu ca vikkhipati, paccayo ca hoti jātiyā. Jātipi khandhe ca janeti tesaṃ abhinibbattibhāvena pavattattā, paccayo ca hoti jarāmaraṇassa. Jarāmaraṇampi khandhānaṃ pākabhedabhāvañca adhitiṭṭhati, paccayo ca hoti bhavantarapātubhāvāya sokādīnaṃ adhiṭṭhānattā. Tasmā sabbapadesu dvedhā pavattikiccatopi idaṃ viññātabbaṃ yathārahaṃ.

Yasmā cettha avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārāti idaṃ kārakadassananivāraṇaṃ. Saṅkhārapaccayā viññāṇanti attasaṅkantidassananivāraṇaṃ. Viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpanti "attā"tiparikappitavatthubhedadassanato ghanasaññānivāraṇaṃ. Nāmarūpapaccayā saḷāyatanantiādi attā passati…pe… vijānāti, phusati, vedayati, taṇhiyati, upādiyati, bhavati, jāyati, jīyati, mīyatītievamādidassananivāraṇaṃ. Tasmā micchādassananivāraṇatopetaṃ bhavacakkaṃ viññātabbaṃ yathārahaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

This wheel, revolving in this way — "by its arising from the truths, by function, by warding off wrong views, by similes, and by the divisions of its profound method — should be understood, as appropriate."

Herein, since it is said in the Analysis of the Truths that wholesome and unwholesome kamma, without distinction, is the truth of origin — therefore, in "dependent on ignorance are formations," the formations, arising from ignorance, are the second truth arising from the second truth. Consciousness, arising from the formations, is the first truth arising from the second truth. Name-and-form and the rest, ending with resultant feeling, arising from consciousness and the rest, are the first truth arising from the first truth. Craving, arising from feeling, is the second truth arising from the first truth. Clinging, arising from craving, is the second truth arising from the second truth. Being, arising from clinging, is both the first and second truths arising from the second truth. Birth, arising from being, is the first truth arising from the second truth. Aging-and-death, arising from birth, is the first truth arising from the first truth. Thus, so far, this should be understood, as appropriate, by way of its arising from the truths.

Herein, since ignorance both bewilders beings with respect to their objects and is a condition for the appearance of the formations; and, likewise, the formations both fashion the conditioned and are a condition for consciousness; and consciousness, too, both cognizes its object and is a condition for name-and-form; and name-and-form, too, both mutually supports its own components and is a condition for the sixfold base; and the sixfold base, too, both occurs in its own respective domain and is a condition for contact; and contact, too, both touches its object and is a condition for feeling; and feeling, too, both experiences the flavor of its object and is a condition for craving; and craving, too, both delights in enticing things and is a condition for clinging; and clinging, too, both grasps at things to be grasped and is a condition for being; and being, too, both scatters beings among the various destinations and is a condition for birth; and birth, too, both generates the aggregates, on account of occurring by way of their production, and is a condition for aging-and-death; and aging-and-death, too, both presides over the breaking up and ripening of the aggregates and is a condition for the appearance of another state of being, on account of presiding over sorrow and the rest — therefore, in every one of these terms, this too should be understood, as appropriate, by way of its twofold function.

And since, herein, "dependent on ignorance are formations" wards off the view of a fixed agent; "dependent on formations, consciousness" wards off the view of a self transmigrating; "dependent on consciousness, name-and-form" wards off the perception of compactness, by showing the diversity of things imagined as "self"; and such statements as "dependent on name-and-form, the sixfold base" and the rest ward off such views as "a self sees … cognizes, touches, feels, thirsts, clings, comes to be, is born, ages, dies" — therefore this wheel of becoming, too, should be understood, as appropriate, by way of its warding off of wrong views.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The mapping onto the four truths reveals something easy to miss when reading the twelve links in isolation: the entire chain, from ignorance through aging-and-death, is nothing but the truth of suffering and the truth of its origin, endlessly alternating and feeding each other — there is no third or fourth truth anywhere within it, since cessation and the path belong to a completely different register, the register of what must be developed to stop the wheel, not to the wheel's own internal machinery.

The "warding off wrong views" pass is worth reading as a direct answer to a question a thoughtful skeptic might ask throughout this whole chapter: if there is no fixed self, why does experience feel so unified and continuous? The answer offered here is that each individual link of the twelve-factor chain is specifically doing the work of dissolving one particular piece of that felt unity — showing an agent where none exists, showing transmigration where there is only conditioned succession, showing genuine diversity where a compact "self" is falsely imagined — so that, taken together, the whole formula functions less like a neutral description and more like a therapeutic dismantling of exactly the illusions that make a self seem necessary in the first place.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
ghanasaññā

the perception of compactness — ghana (dense, solid) + saññā (perception); the illusion of a single, unified "self" or "thing," which name-and-form's demonstrated diversity of components is specifically said to dismantle.

nivāraṇa

warding off — the specific corrective function assigned to each link of the chain against one particular wrong view, the organizing theme of this section's third analytical pass.

Two Simile-Chains: The Blind Man's Boil, and the Cocoon, Prince, Magician, and River of Salt Water

The chapter's most celebrated passage now offers two complete, parallel simile-chains covering all twelve links at once. The first traces a single, grimly comic mishap: a blind man stumbles, falls, develops a boil, which bursts, gets rubbed raw, hurts, prompts a desperate search for relief, receives an unsuitable remedy, which aggravates it further, until it finally bursts open again — each stage mapped precisely onto one link of dependent origination. The second offers twelve entirely independent images — a cocoon-spinning caterpillar, a young prince installed by his minister, a magician's illusion, a thriving forest thicket, fire-sticks rubbed together, a burn, salt water that only deepens thirst, a fish taking bait, a sprouting seed, and a tree's inevitable fall — each illuminating one link from a completely different angle.

Pali Source

Yasmā panettha salakkhaṇasāmaññalakkhaṇavasena dhammānaṃ adassanato andho viya avijjā. Andhassa upakkhalanaṃ viya avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā. Upakkhalitassa patanaṃ viya saṅkhārapaccayā viññāṇaṃ. Patitassa gaṇḍapātubhāvo viya viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpaṃ. Gaṇḍabhedapīḷakā viya nāmarūpapaccayā saḷāyatanaṃ. Gaṇḍapīḷakāghaṭṭanaṃ viya saḷāyatanapaccayā phasso. Ghaṭṭanadukkhaṃ viya phassapaccayā vedanā, dukkhassa paṭikārābhilāso viya vedanāpaccayā taṇhā. Paṭikārābhilāsena asappāyaggahaṇaṃ viya taṇhāpaccayā upādānaṃ. Upādiṇṇaasappāyālepanaṃ viya upādānapaccayā bhavo. Asappāyālepanena gaṇḍavikārapātubhāvo viya bhavapaccayā jāti. Gaṇḍavikārato gaṇḍabhedo viya jātipaccayā jarāmaraṇaṃ.

Yasmā vā panettha avijjā appaṭipattimicchāpaṭipattibhāvena satte abhibhavati paṭalaṃ viya akkhīni. Tadabhibhūto ca bālo punabbhavikehi saṅkhārehi attānaṃ veṭheti kosakārakimi viya kosappadesehi. Saṅkhārapariggahitaṃ viññāṇaṃ gatīsu patiṭṭhaṃ labhati pariṇāyakapariggahito viya rājakumāro rajje. Upapattinimittaparikappanato viññāṇaṃ paṭisandhiyaṃ anekappakāraṃ nāmarūpaṃ abhinibbatteti māyākāro viya māyaṃ. Nāmarūpe patiṭṭhitaṃ saḷāyatanaṃ vuddhiṃ virūḷhiṃ vepullaṃ pāpuṇāti subhūmiyaṃ patiṭṭhito vanappagumbo viya. Āyatanaghaṭṭanato phasso jāyati araṇisahitābhimanthanato aggi viya. Phassena phuṭṭhassa vedanā pātubhavati agginā phuṭṭhassa dāho viya. Vedayamānassa taṇhā pavaḍḍhati loṇūdakaṃ pivato pipāsā viya. Tasito bhavesu abhilāsaṃ karoti pipāsito viya pānīye. Tadassupādānaṃ, upādānena bhavaṃ upādiyati āmisalobhena maccho baḷisaṃ viya. Bhave sati jāti hoti bīje sati aṅkuro viya. Jātassa avassaṃ jarāmaraṇaṃ uppannassa rukkhassa patanaṃ viya. Tasmā evaṃ upamāhipetaṃ bhavacakkaṃ viññātabbaṃ yathārahaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

And since, herein, ignorance, on account of not seeing states by way of their specific and general characteristics, is like a blind man. "Dependent on ignorance are formations" is like the blind man's stumbling. "Dependent on formations, consciousness" is like the falling of one who has stumbled. "Dependent on consciousness, name-and-form" is like the appearance of a boil on one who has fallen. "Dependent on name-and-form, the sixfold base" is like the bursting of the boil into a sore. "Dependent on the sixfold base, contact" is like the rubbing of the boil's sore. "Dependent on contact, feeling" is like the pain of that rubbing; "dependent on feeling, craving" is like the desire for a remedy for that pain. "Dependent on craving, clinging" is like the taking of an unsuitable remedy out of that desire for a remedy. "Dependent on clinging, being" is like the application, once taken up, of that unsuitable remedy. "Dependent on being, birth" is like the appearance of an aggravation of the sore through that unsuitable application. "Dependent on birth, aging-and-death" is like the bursting of the sore through its aggravation.

Or again, since, herein, ignorance, by way of non-application and wrong application, overpowers beings just as a film overpowers the eyes; and the fool, overpowered by it, wraps himself up with the formations that lead to renewed being, just as a cocoon-spinning caterpillar wraps itself with strands of its own cocoon; consciousness, taken hold of by the formations, gains a footing among the destinations, just as a young prince, taken in hand by a chief minister, gains a footing in the kingdom; consciousness, by imagining a sign of rebirth, produces at rebirth-linking a manifold name-and-form, just as a magician produces a magic trick; the sixfold base, established in name-and-form, attains growth, increase, and expansion, just as a thicket of forest growth, established in fertile soil, attains growth; contact is born from the impinging of the bases upon their objects, just as fire is born from the vigorous rubbing together of fire-sticks; feeling appears in one touched by contact, just as burning appears in one touched by fire; craving increases in one who feels, just as thirst increases in one who drinks salt water; one thirsting forms a longing for states of being, just as one made thirsty longs for water; that becomes clinging; by clinging, one clings to being, just as a fish, out of greed for the bait, clings to the hook; when being exists, birth comes to be, just as, when a seed exists, a sprout comes to be; for one who is born, aging-and-death is inevitable, just as the falling of a tree that has grown up is inevitable. Therefore, this wheel of becoming, too, should be understood, as appropriate, by way of these similes.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The two chains are worth reading against each other, since they work by opposite strategies. The blind man's boil is a single continuous narrative — one unlucky man's one unbroken bad day, each link simply the next scene in the same unfolding disaster — which makes vivid the chain's relentless, self-perpetuating momentum: every remedy for the last problem only creates the next one, right up to the final rupture that echoes the very fall that started everything. The second chain abandons narrative continuity entirely in favor of twelve independent, unrelated images, each chosen because it captures something specific and different about its one link's own particular character — the prince-and-minister image, for instance, captures consciousness's dependent, installed authority rather than any innate sovereignty, while the magician's illusion captures name-and-form's convincing but ultimately conjured appearance of solidity. Together, the two chains model two equally valid ways of holding the whole teaching in the mind at once: as a single continuous story, and as twelve separate, vivid points of insight.

The salt-water image for craving deserves particular attention, since it captures something the earlier feeling-conditions-craving section already established analytically: craving does not resolve the thirst that produces it, but deepens it with every attempt at satisfaction, the way drinking salt water only intensifies genuine thirst rather than quenching it — a compact restatement, in a single image, of why craving-driven pursuit of pleasure is structurally self-defeating.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
kosakārakimi

a cocoon-spinning caterpillar — kosakāra (cocoon-maker) + kimi (worm, caterpillar); the image for a fool wrapping himself in the very formations that will produce his own renewed rebirth, entirely by his own unwitting activity.

māyākāra

a magician, an illusionist — the image for consciousness at rebirth-linking, which "produces" a convincing, elaborate name-and-form the way a skilled illusionist produces a convincing trick, real enough to fool the audience yet without any solid, independent substance behind the display.

The Four Kinds of Profundity; the Four Methods

The chapter now returns, near its very close, to a claim it quoted at the very start — that dependent origination is "profound, and profound in its appearance" — and finally makes good on the promise, made many pages ago, to explain what that fourfold profundity actually consists of: profound in meaning (the cause-and-effect relation itself, hard to fathom), profound in nature (exactly how a given cause produces a given effect), profound in teaching (the sheer variety of valid ways it can be presented), and profound in penetration (the intrinsic characteristic of each individual factor, graspable only by genuine insight). A final, fourfold set of interpretive methods then shows how the very same twelve-factor formula can be read correctly, as an unbroken continuity that avoids both eternalism and annihilationism, or misread, by over-emphasizing either its unity or its diversity, into exactly those same two extremes.

Pali Source

Yasmā ca bhagavatā atthatopi dhammatopi desanatopi paṭivedhatopi gambhīrabhāvaṃ sandhāya "gambhīro cāyaṃ, ānanda, paṭiccasamuppādo gambhīrāvabhāso cā"ti vuttaṃ, tasmā gambhīrabhedatopetaṃ bhavacakkaṃ viññātabbaṃ yathārahaṃ.

Tattha yasmā na jātito jarāmaraṇaṃ na hoti, na ca jātiṃ vinā aññato hoti, itthañca jātito samudāgacchatīti evaṃ jātipaccayasamudāgataṭṭhassa duravabodhanīyato jarāmaraṇassa jātipaccayasambhūtasamudāgataṭṭho gambhīro. Tathā jātiyā bhavapaccaya…pe… saṅkhārānaṃ avijjāpaccayasambhūtasamudāgataṭṭho gambhīro. Tasmā idaṃ bhavacakkaṃ atthagambhīranti ayaṃ tāvettha atthagambhīratā. Hetuphalañhi atthoti vuccati. Yathāha – "hetuphale ñāṇaṃ atthapaṭisambhidā"ti.

Yasmā pana yenākārena yadavatthā ca avijjā tesaṃ tesaṃ saṅkhārānaṃ paccayo hoti, tassa duravabodhanīyato avijjāya saṅkhārānaṃ paccayaṭṭho gambhīro. Tathā saṅkhārānaṃ…pe… jātiyā jarāmaraṇassa paccayaṭṭho gambhīro, tasmā idaṃ bhavacakkaṃ dhammagambhīranti ayamettha dhammagambhīratā. Hetuno hi dhammoti nāmaṃ. Yathāha – "hetumhi ñāṇaṃ dhammapaṭisambhidā"ti.

Yasmā cassa tena tena kāraṇena tathā tathā pavattetabbattā desanāpi gambhīrā, na tattha sabbaññutaññāṇato aññaṃ ñāṇaṃ patiṭṭhaṃ labhati. Tathāhetaṃ katthaci sutte anulomato, katthaci paṭilomato, katthaci anulomapaṭilomato, katthaci vemajjhato paṭṭhāya anulomato vā paṭilomato vā, katthaci tisandhicatusaṅkhepaṃ, katthaci dvisandhitisaṅkhepaṃ, katthaci ekasandhidvisaṅkhepaṃ desitaṃ, tasmā idaṃ bhavacakkaṃ desanāgambhīranti ayaṃ desanāgambhīratā.

Yasmā cettha yo so avijjādīnaṃ sabhāvo, yena paṭividdhena avijjādayo sammā salakkhaṇato paṭividdhā honti, so duppariyogāhattā gambhīro, tasmā idaṃ bhavacakkaṃ paṭivedhagambhīraṃ. Tathā hettha avijjāya aññāṇādassanasaccāsampaṭivedhaṭṭho gambhīro, saṅkhārānaṃ abhisaṅkharaṇāyūhanasarāgavirāgaṭṭho, viññāṇassa suññataabyāpāraasaṅkantipaṭisandhipātubhāvaṭṭho, nāmarūpassa ekuppādavinibbhogāvinibbhoganamanaruppanaṭṭho, saḷāyatanassa adhipatilokadvārakhettavisayibhāvaṭṭho, phassassa phusanasaṅghaṭṭanasaṅgatisannipātaṭṭho, vedanāya ārammaṇarasānubhavanasukhadukkhamajjhattabhāvanijjīvavedayitaṭṭho. Taṇhāya abhinanditajjhosānasaritālatānadītaṇhāsamuddaduppūraṭṭho, upādānassa ādānaggahaṇābhinivesaparāmāsaduratikkamaṭṭho, bhavassa āyūhanābhisaṅkharaṇayonigatiṭhitinivāsesukhipanaṭṭho, jātiyā jāti sañjāti okkanti nibbatti pātubhāvaṭṭho, jarāmaraṇassa khayavayabhedavipariṇāmaṭṭho gambhīroti ayamettha paṭivedhagambhīratā.

Yasmā panettha ekattanayo, nānattanayo, abyāpāranayo, evaṃdhammatānayoti cattāro atthanayā honti, tasmā nayabhedatopetaṃ bhavacakkaṃ viññātabbaṃ yathārahaṃ.

Tattha avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā, saṅkhārapaccayā viññāṇanti evaṃ bījassa aṅkurādibhāvena rukkhabhāvappatti viya santānānupacchedo ekattanayo nāma. Yaṃ sammā passanto hetuphalasambandhena santānassa anupacchedāvabodhato ucchedadiṭṭhiṃ pajahati. Micchā passanto hetuphalasambandhena pavattamānassa santānānupacchedassa ekattagahaṇato sassatadiṭṭhiṃ upādiyati.

Avijjādīnaṃ pana yathāsakaṃlakkhaṇavavatthānaṃ nānattanayo nāma. Yaṃ sammā passanto navanavānaṃ uppādadassanato sassatadiṭṭhiṃ pajahati. Micchā passanto ekasantānapatitassa bhinnasantānasseva nānattaggahaṇato ucchedadiṭṭhiṃ upādiyati.

Avijjāya saṅkhārā mayā uppādetabbā, saṅkhārānaṃ vā viññāṇaṃ amhehīti evamādibyāpārābhāvo abyāpāranayo nāma. Yaṃ sammā passanto kārakassa abhāvāvabodhato attadiṭṭhiṃ pajahati. Micchā passanto yo asatipi byāpāre avijjādīnaṃ sabhāvaniyamasiddho hetubhāvo, tassa aggahaṇato akiriyadiṭṭhiṃ upādiyati.

Avijjādīhi pana kāraṇehi saṅkhārādīnaṃyeva sambhavo khīrādīhi dadhiādīnaṃ viya, na aññesanti ayaṃ evaṃdhammatānayo nāma. Yaṃ sammā passanto paccayānurūpato phalāvabodhā ahetukadiṭṭhiṃ akiriyadiṭṭhiñca pajahati. Micchā passanto paccayānurūpaṃ phalappavattiṃ aggahetvā yato kutoci yassa kassaci asambhavaggahaṇato ahetukadiṭṭhiñceva niyatavādañca upādiyatīti.

Ru-Yi Translation

And since it was said by the Blessed One, with reference to its profundity by way of meaning, by way of its nature as cause, by way of teaching, and by way of penetration: "profound, Ānanda, is this dependent origination, and profound is its appearance" — therefore this wheel of becoming, too, should be understood, as appropriate, by way of the divisions of its profundity.

Herein: since it is not the case that aging-and-death does not occur in dependence on birth; nor does it occur, apart from birth, from anything else; and yet it does arise in just this way, from birth — the sense in which aging-and-death arises and comes about through the condition of birth, being difficult to understand, is profound in this way. Likewise, the sense in which birth arises through the condition of being … and so on down to the sense in which the formations arise and come about through the condition of ignorance is profound. Therefore this wheel of becoming is "profound in meaning" — this, so far, is its profundity of meaning. For "meaning" is said to be cause and effect together. As it is said: "knowledge with respect to cause and effect is the discrimination of meaning."

And since the manner in which, and the state in which, ignorance is a condition for this or that formation is difficult to understand, the sense in which ignorance is a condition for the formations is profound. Likewise, the sense in which the formations … and so on down to the sense in which birth is a condition for aging-and-death is profound. Therefore this wheel of becoming is "profound in its nature as cause" — this, herein, is its profundity of nature. For "dhamma" is a name for a cause. As it is said: "knowledge with respect to a cause is the discrimination of the underlying condition."

And since this teaching must be presented in this or that way, for this or that reason, the teaching of it, too, is profound — no knowledge other than omniscient knowledge can find a footing there. For, accordingly, it is taught, in one discourse, in forward order; in another, in reverse order; in another, both forward and reverse; in another, starting from the middle, either forward or in reverse; in one place with three connections and four sections; in another with two connections and three sections; in another with one connection and two sections. Therefore this wheel of becoming is "profound in its teaching" — this is its profundity of teaching.

And since, herein, that intrinsic nature of ignorance and the rest, by penetrating which ignorance and the rest are rightly penetrated according to their true characteristic, is profound on account of being hard to fathom — therefore this wheel of becoming is "profound in penetration." For, herein: the sense of ignorance's non-penetration of the truths through unknowing and non-seeing is profound; the sense of the formations' fashioning, exerting, taking-fire, and fading is profound; the sense of consciousness's voidness, non-involvement, non-transmigration, and appearance at rebirth-linking is profound; the sense of name-and-form's arising together, its separability-yet-inseparability, its bending toward its object, and its deformability is profound; the sense of the sixfold base's dominance, its being a world, a door, a field, and an object-possessor is profound; the sense of contact's touching, impinging, and coincidence-of-conditions is profound; the sense of feeling's experiencing the flavor of an object, its pleasure, pain, and neutrality, and its being felt yet lifeless is profound. The sense of craving's being delighted in and clung to, its being like a creeper, a river, an ocean hard to fill, is profound; the sense of clinging's taking up, grasping, adherence, and misapprehension hard to overcome is profound; the sense of being's exertion, fashioning, its being flung into a womb, a destination, a station, and an abode is profound; the sense of birth's being a birth, a coming-to-birth, a descent, a production, and an appearance is profound; the sense of aging-and-death's destruction, decay, breaking up, and alteration is profound — this, herein, is its profundity of penetration.

And since, herein, there are four methods of meaning — the method of unity, the method of diversity, the method of non-involvement, and the method of "thus is its nature" — therefore this wheel of becoming, too, should be understood, as appropriate, by way of the divisions of method.

Herein, "dependent on ignorance are formations; dependent on formations, consciousness," and so on — the uninterrupted continuity, like a seed's progression through sprout-hood and so forth to become a tree, is called "the method of unity." One who sees this rightly abandons the annihilationist view, through understanding the uninterrupted continuity by way of the connection of cause and effect. One who sees it wrongly takes up the eternalist view, by grasping as a strict unity this uninterrupted continuity that occurs by way of the connection of cause and effect.

But the determination of ignorance and the rest according to their own respective characteristics is called "the method of diversity." One who sees this rightly abandons the eternalist view, through seeing the ever-new arising of each factor. One who sees it wrongly takes up the annihilationist view, by grasping as strict diversity — as though belonging to entirely separate continuities — what has, in fact, fallen within a single continuity.

The absence of any such involvement as "I must produce the formations from ignorance" or "we must produce consciousness from the formations" is called "the method of non-involvement." One who sees this rightly abandons the view of a self, through understanding the absence of any agent. One who sees it wrongly takes up the view of non-efficacy of action, by failing to grasp that, even in the absence of any personal involvement, ignorance and the rest still have a causal role established by the fixed nature of things themselves.

But the fact that it is specifically the formations and the rest that arise from causes such as ignorance — just as it is specifically curds and the like that arise from milk and the like — and not anything else, is called "the method of 'thus is its nature.'" One who sees this rightly abandons both the view of no cause and the view of non-efficacy of action, through understanding that the effect corresponds to its condition. One who sees it wrongly, failing to grasp that the occurrence of an effect corresponds to its condition, takes up both the view of no cause and the doctrine of strict determinism, by grasping that anything whatsoever can arise from anything at all, indiscriminately.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The fourfold-profundity passage finally cashes out a promise made at the very start of the chapter, in the section refuting "mere arising" as a theory of dependent origination — Buddhaghosa there simply asserted that dependent origination possesses a "profound method" not found in mere arising, without yet explaining what that meant. Coming back to it here, at the chapter's close, after ninety paragraphs of demonstration, the four kinds of profundity read almost as a retrospective table of contents for everything the chapter has just done: profundity of meaning is what all the etymological and definitional work established: profundity of nature is what the twenty-four-conditions section established in exhaustive technical detail; profundity of teaching is what the vine-gatherers' simile and the discussion of forward, reverse, and middle-outward orderings established; and profundity of penetration is what the individual characteristic-function-manifestation-proximate-cause treatment of each factor established.

The four methods, and their paired correct and incorrect readings, function as a final safety check against every wrong view the chapter has spent itself refuting: taken too literally as strict continuity, the unity method collapses into eternalism; taken too literally as strict separateness, the diversity method collapses into annihilationism; taken to mean there is no causal structure at all, the non-involvement method collapses into denying that actions have consequences; and taken to mean anything at all could cause anything else, the "thus is its nature" method collapses into either denying causation altogether or embracing a rigid, hopeless determinism. Correctly held, each of the four methods instead cuts cleanly toward the middle way this whole chapter, and indeed the whole Visuddhimagga, has been steering toward from its very first page.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
atthapaṭisambhidā / dhammapaṭisambhidā

the discrimination of meaning / of the underlying condition — two of the four analytical knowledges (paṭisambhidā) introduced back in chapter fourteen, invoked here to explain precisely what "meaning" (attha, i.e. effect) and "dhamma" (i.e. cause) technically denote in the profundity analysis.

niyatavāda

the doctrine of strict determinism — niyata (fixed, determined) + vāda (doctrine); the wrong view that results are fixed regardless of conditions, rejected here alongside the "no cause" view as the twin failure modes of misreading the "thus is its nature" method.

Paññābhūminiddesa Complete

The chapter closes as it opened — with an admission of the sheer difficulty of what has just been attempted, and a final call to sustained, mindful practice rather than mere intellectual mastery. Buddhaghosa's own closing verses picture the wheel of becoming split apart by the sword of knowledge, sharpened on the whetstone of concentration; the Buddha's own words to Ānanda, quoted one last time, warn that failure to penetrate this very teaching is what leaves beings "tangled like a skein of thread," unable to escape the round of rebirth. With the ground of wisdom — aggregates, sense bases, elements, faculties, truths, and now, at last, dependent origination — fully covered across four consecutive chapters, the treatise is ready to move from wisdom's foundation to its actual cultivation, beginning with the first of the seven purifications proper: purification of view.

Pali Source

Idañhi atigambhīrato agādhaṃ. Nānānayagahanato duratiyānaṃ. Ñāṇāsinā samādhipavarasilāyaṃ sunisitena, "bhavacakkamapadāletvā, asanivicakkamiva niccanimmathanaṃ; saṃsārabhayamatīto, na koci supinantarepyatthi."

Vuttampi hetaṃ bhagavatā – "gambhīro cāyaṃ, ānanda, paṭiccasamuppādo gambhīrāvabhāso ca. Etassa cānanda, dhammassa ananubodhā appaṭivedhā evamayaṃ pajā tantākulakajātā kulāgaṇṭhikajātā muñjapabbajabhūtā apāyaṃ duggatiṃ vinipātaṃ saṃsāraṃ nātivattatī"ti. Tasmā attano vā paresaṃ vā hitāya ca sukhāya ca paṭipanno avasesakiccāni pahāya, "gambhīre paccayākārappabhede idha paṇḍito; yathā gādhaṃ labhethevamanuyuñje sadā satoti."

Iti sādhujanapāmojjatthāya kate visuddhimagge
Paññābhāvanādhikāre
Paññābhūminiddeso nāma
Sattarasamo paricchedo.

Ru-Yi Translation

For this is unfathomable, on account of being exceedingly profound; hard to cross, on account of the denseness of its manifold methods. With the sword of knowledge, thoroughly sharpened on the excellent stone of concentration — "having split apart this wheel of becoming, ever grinding on like a thunderbolt's own wheel — no one at all, even in a dream, has gone beyond the fear of saṃsāra without doing so."

For this was said by the Blessed One: "profound, Ānanda, is this dependent origination, and profound is its appearance. And it is through not awakening to, and not penetrating, this Dhamma, Ānanda, that this generation has become like a tangled skein, like a knotted ball of thread, like matted rushes and reeds, unable to pass beyond states of misery, bad destinations, ruin, and the round of rebirth." Therefore, one practicing for one's own welfare and happiness, or for that of others, having set aside all other tasks — "let the wise person here, ever mindful, constantly apply himself to this profound array of the mode of conditions, that he may find a footing therein."

Thus, in the Path of Purification, composed for the joy of good people,
in the section on the development of wisdom,
the discussion called "The Description of the Ground of Wisdom"
constitutes the seventeenth chapter.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The chapter's closing image — knowledge as a sword, concentration as the whetstone on which it must first be sharpened before it can split the wheel apart — makes a point about method that the whole treatise has been building toward since its very first chapter's discussion of virtue and concentration as necessary preludes to wisdom: dependent origination cannot be dismantled by mere intellectual analysis alone, however thorough (and this chapter's analysis has certainly been thorough); it yields only to insight sharpened by genuine meditative stability. The thunderbolt-wheel image for the wheel of becoming's own relentless grinding motion is a striking, almost violent counterpart to the gentler wheel-of-becoming metaphors used earlier in the chapter, underscoring just how much force, both destructive and liberating, this final chapter attributes to the teaching it has spent itself explaining.

With this seventeenth chapter's completion, the entire "ground" of wisdom — the six categories named back in chapter fourteen's own architecture (aggregates, sense bases, elements, faculties, truths, and now dependent origination) — stands fully accounted for, four chapters and many hundreds of pages after that architecture was first announced. The treatise's own logic now turns from foundation to construction proper: with sīla-visuddhi and citta-visuddhi (virtue and concentration) already complete from Part One, and the "ground" of wisdom now complete as well, only the five remaining purifications — the actual "body" of wisdom's development — remain, beginning with diṭṭhivisuddhi, purification of view.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
tantākulakajāta

"become like a tangled skein of thread" — tanta (a woven thread, a warp) + ākulaka (tangled, confused); the Buddha's own image, quoted here, for what befalls a generation that fails to penetrate dependent origination — hopelessly knotted, unable to find a way out of saṃsāra.

Paññābhūminiddeso

"The Description of the Ground of Wisdom" — the formal title of this seventeenth chapter, fixed in its own closing colophon, confirming its place as the completion of wisdom's six-part "ground" first announced in chapter fourteen.
